Orientation — Scope, Versions and Method

Scope of this guideVersion target and support matrixArchitectural layers and their contractsThe hook surface and plugin architectureData model, caching and storage strategyConventions used in this document

This guide describes how WordPress executes, not how to install, theme or sell it. It follows a single front-end request from the web server through the bootstrap in wp-settings.php, through the hook graph that makes the platform extensible, into the relational schema that persists everything, and back out through the template hierarchy to bytes on the wire. Its central argument is that WordPress is not a framework with a plugin system bolted onto the side; it is a hook graph wrapped around a single shared-nothing PHP request, and nearly every operational surprise in production — a fatal after SHORTINIT, a 40-second admin screen, a metadata query that walks a million rows — follows from taking that model less literally than the core does. The chapters are ordered by dependency rather than by difficulty: the lifecycle determines which hooks exist at all, the hooks determine which parts of the schema are reachable from PHP, the schema determines what the REST API is permitted to expose, and the security chapter is a restatement of the first five with the failure modes named explicitly.

Scope of this guide

Establishes which WordPress subsystems this document treats as substrate and which it treats as application, because the distinction determines what can be relied upon over a five-year maintenance horizon.

The unit of discussion is the plugin or theme as a deployable PHP artefact loaded into a live request. Six subsystems carry the load: the bootstrap and request lifecycle, the hook system, the database schema together with its caching tiers, the template hierarchy including the block editor’s server-side templates, the HTTP interfaces in both directions (REST, AJAX and outbound requests), and the security primitives the other five depend on. Everything here is version-pinned to WordPress 6.9 on PHP 8.5, and where a behaviour is a historical artefact — the admin-ajax.php dual-purpose endpoint, the meta_query fallback planner, the wp_options autoload column — it is described as such, because the correct engineering response differs sharply between "this is the contract" and "this is a wart you must route around".

wp-content/
├── mu-plugins/
│   ├── 000-platform-autoloader.php   // must be side-effect free; runs before the plugin API
│   ├── 010-constants.php
│   └── 020-security-baseline.php
├── plugins/
│   └── acme-ledger/
│       ├── acme-ledger.php           // file header, constants, autoload, single boot() call
│       ├── composer.json             // psr-4 Acme\\Ledger\\ => src/
│       ├── src/
│       │   ├── Plugin.php
│       │   ├── Domain/Invoice.php
│       │   ├── Infrastructure/InvoiceRepository.php
│       │   ├── Rest/InvoiceController.php
│       │   ├── Blocks/register.php
│       │   └── Security/CapabilityMap.php
│       ├── assets/blocks/invoice-summary/
│       └── tests/{Unit,Integration,EndToEnd}/
└── themes/
    └── acme/
        ├── theme.json
        ├── functions.php             // enqueue, register blocks, menus, image sizes
        ├── templates/  parts/  patterns/  styles/
        └── inc/block-patterns.php

The layout above is not stylistic preference. Namespaced src/ trees with a thin bootstrap file are the only structure that survives two themes, three plugins and a static analyser all trying to declare the same global function name. Flat plugin directories with forty files at the root are the single most common reason a codebase accumulates fatal error: cannot redeclare acme_get_invoice() after a routine dependency bump. The mu-plugins directory deserves a specific note: files there are included in alphabetical order, are not given a plugin header, and — critically — are not passed through validate_plugin(), so a syntax error in one takes down the entire site with no activation-time check and no deactivation fallback.

Version target and support matrix

Pins every version used in the code samples in this document and states the constraints each pin imposes on the host environment.

WordPress 6.9 is the target. It is a minor release on a platform that only makes major changes in December, so the practical consequence is that core is a rolling target with a strong backwards-compatibility guarantee and a deprecation notice at least twelve months ahead of removal. The PHP side is the opposite: PHP 8.5 is a major with real deprecations, and WordPress core’s own minimum PHP requirement is deliberately conservative, meaning a modern site can run PHP 8.5 while a significant minority of plugins assume 7.4 semantics. Everything in this guide assumes typed signatures, readonly properties, first-class callable syntax, and enums are available, and that wp_kses, esc_* and the nonce family are used exactly as documented in Chapter 6.

ComponentPinnedRationale and constraint
WordPress core6.9.xEnforce with wp_version_check; auto-update minor releases only
PHP8.5 (8.5.0+)Typed constants, #[\Override], pipe-free nullsafe improvements
MySQL / MariaDBMySQL 8.4 · MariaDB 11.4Requires utf8mb4 with utf8mb4_unicode_520_ci; no NO_ZERO_DATE
WP-CLI2.12Drives wp-env, migrations, cron runners in CI
Composer2.8 LTSPlugin runtime deps only; core is never a Composer package
Node.js24 LTSBuild toolchain only; never present in production
@wordpress/scripts30.7.xPins the block API 3.x editor contract and the babel/preset defaults
Object cacheRedis 7.4 or Memcached 1.8Mandatory above ~50k published posts, see §3.5
Web servernginx 1.28 · Apache 2.4 (httpd 2.4)nginx front end preferred; .htaccess shown for parity
Bundled jQuery3.7.1 (served as jquery.min.js)Still registered by default; do not assume it is gone in 6.9
 
{
  "name": "acme/wordpress-core",
  "description": "Production WordPress 6.9 platform for the Acme estate.",
  "type": "project",
  "require": {
    "php": ">=8.5",
    "php-stubs/wordpress-stubs": "6.9.*",
    "symfony/polyfill-mbstring": "^1.32"
  },
  "require-dev": {
    "php-stubs/wp-cli-stubs": "^2.12",
    "squizlabs/php_codesniffer": "^3.13",
    "wp-coding-standards/wpcs": "^3.2",
    "phpcompatibility/phpcompatibility-wp": "^2.1",
    "phpstan/phpstan": "^2.1",
    "phpunit/phpunit": "^11.5",
    "yoast/phpunit-polyfills": "^3.0"
  },
  "config": {
    "allow-plugins": { "dealerdirect/phpcodesniffer-composer-installer": true },
    "sort-packages": true,
    "platform": { "php": "8.5.0" }
  },
  "scripts": {
    "lint": "phpcs --report=summary",
    "stan": "phpstan analyse --memory-limit=1G --no-progress",
    "test": "phpunit --configuration phpunit.xml.dist"
  }
}

Note what is not in that file. WordPress core is not a Composer dependency, and installing it as one — the historic johnpbloch/wordpress approach — couples your deploy to a third party who repackages the most security-sensitive artefact in the stack. Core arrives as a tarball, is version-pinned in your infrastructure code, and is upgraded by wp core update under --version=6.9.2. Composer governs your own code’s dependencies and nothing else. The platform.php override exists so that a developer on PHP 8.4 cannot quietly install a package requiring 8.5-only syntax; it is a build-time guard, not a runtime one.

{
  "name": "acme/wp-blocks",
  "private": true,
  "engines": { "node": ">=24.0.0", "npm": ">=11.0.0" },
  "scripts": {
    "build": "wp-scripts build",
    "start": "wp-scripts start",
    "lint:js": "wp-scripts lint-js src",
    "lint:css": "wp-scripts lint-style \"src/**/style.scss\"",
    "test:unit": "wp-scripts test-unit-js",
    "env:start": "wp-env start",
    "packages-update": "wp-scripts packages-update"
  },
  "devDependencies": {
    "@wordpress/scripts": "^30.7.0",
    "@wordpress/env": "^10.9.0",
    "@wordpress/e2e-test-utils-playwright": "^10.9.0"
  }
}

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

// ** Database — credentials from the environment, never from VCS ** //
define( 'DB_NAME',     getenv( 'WP_DB_NAME' )     ?: 'acme_production' );
define( 'DB_USER',     getenv( 'WP_DB_USER' )     ?: 'acme_wp' );
define( 'DB_PASSWORD', getenv( 'WP_DB_PASSWORD' ) ?: '' );
define( 'DB_HOST',     getenv( 'WP_DB_HOST' )     ?: 'db-primary.internal' );
define( 'DB_CHARSET',  'utf8mb4' );
define( 'DB_COLLATE',  '' );

$table_prefix = 'wp_';   // changing this after install orphans every row

// ** Salts — rotate deliberately, never automatically ** //
define( 'AUTH_KEY',         'rotate-me-32-bytes-of-entropy-000001' );
define( 'SECURE_AUTH_KEY',  'rotate-me-32-bytes-of-entropy-000002' );
define( 'LOGGED_IN_KEY',    'rotate-me-32-bytes-of-entropy-000003' );
define( 'NONCE_KEY',        'rotate-me-32-bytes-of-entropy-000004' );
define( 'AUTH_SALT',        'rotate-me-32-bytes-of-entropy-000005' );
define( 'SECURE_AUTH_SALT', 'rotate-me-32-bytes-of-entropy-000006' );
define( 'LOGGED_IN_SALT',   'rotate-me-32-bytes-of-entropy-000007' );
define( 'NONCE_SALT',       'rotate-me-32-bytes-of-entropy-000008' );

// ** Behaviour ** //
define( 'WP_DEBUG',              false );
define( 'WP_DEBUG_LOG',          '/var/log/wp/php-error.log' );
define( 'WP_DEBUG_DISPLAY',      false );
define( 'SCRIPT_DEBUG',          false );
define( 'WP_ENVIRONMENT_TYPE',   'production' );
define( 'DISALLOW_FILE_EDIT',    true );  // no theme/plugin editor over HTTP
define( 'DISALLOW_FILE_MODS',    true );  // no install/update UI at all
define( 'WP_AUTO_UPDATE_CORE',   'minor' );
define( 'WP_POST_REVISIONS',     10 );
define( 'AUTOSAVE_INTERVAL',     120 );
define( 'EMPTY_TRASH_DAYS',      30 );
define( 'WP_CRON_LOCK_TIMEOUT',  60 );
define( 'WP_HTTP_BLOCK_EXTERNAL', true );
define( 'WP_ACCESSIBLE_HOSTS',   'api.stripe.com,*.gravatar.com' );

Why DISALLOW_FILE_MODS is set on every production site. It disables the whole plugin and theme installation path, which means an attacker who obtains an administrator session cannot drop a webshell through wp-admin/plugin-install.php. It also disables the automatic update mechanism, so you must own updates in your deployment pipeline instead. Leaving it off is accepting that every administrator account is effectively a code-execution account, which is a defensible choice only if you have the compensating controls — outbound HTTP filtering, read-only wp-content, and an immutable container filesystem — to catch what the panel would otherwise write.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

namespace Acme\Platform;

/**
 * Fails fast and loudly, before any query runs, when the runtime is
 * older than the platform contract. Registered on `plugins_loaded`
 * from an mu-plugin so that a stale wp-content cannot boot at all.
 */
final class Requirements {

    public const MIN_PHP    = '8.5.0';
    public const MIN_WP     = '6.9';
    public const MIN_DB     = '8.0.36';
    public const MIN_MEMORY = '268435456'; // 256 MB in bytes

    public static function assert(): void {
        $failures = [];

        if ( version_compare( PHP_VERSION, self::MIN_PHP, '<' ) ) {
            $failures[] = sprintf( 'PHP %s required; %s running.', self::MIN_PHP, PHP_VERSION );
        }

        global $wp_version;
        if ( version_compare( $wp_version, self::MIN_WP, '<' ) ) {
            $failures[] = sprintf( 'WordPress %s required; %s running.', self::MIN_WP, (string) $wp_version );
        }

        $memory = (int) ini_get( 'memory_limit' );
        if ( 0 !== $memory && $memory < (int) self::MIN_MEMORY ) {
            $failures[] = sprintf( 'memory_limit must be at least %d MB; %d given.', self::MIN_MEMORY / 1048576, $memory / 1048576 );
        }

        if ( [] !== $failures ) {
            error_log( '[acme/platform] ' . implode( ' | ', $failures ) );
            wp_die(
                'This installation does not meet the platform requirements. '
                . 'The event has been logged for the operations team.',
                'Environment error',
                [ 'response' => 500, 'back_link' => true ]
            );
        }
    }
}

Fail before you query, not after. A requirements check placed on plugins_loaded converts a confusing runtime error deep inside a third-party plugin into a single, attributable 500 with a log line. The equivalent practice applies to the database: probe schema shape (for example, confirm that wp_options has an autoload column) during deployment, and record the result in a deployment artefact, so that a restore from a six-month-old dump is a detectable condition rather than a subtle one.

Architectural layers and their contracts

Maps the physical directory layout onto the load order and the dependency direction between layers, because the load order is the only reliable execution schedule WordPress offers.

WordPress has no service container, no dependency graph and no lifecycle contract beyond the order in which files are required. The practical consequence is that "where do I put this code" is answered by load order, not by semantics. A must-use plugin runs before any regular plugin, but after wp-includes/plugin.php has been loaded, which means add_filter() is available in mu-plugins while add_post_type() is not. Regular plugins are loaded in the order returned by get_option( 'active_plugins' ), which is the order they were activated unless explicitly reordered. A theme’s functions.php loads after every active plugin, and the parent theme loads before the child. Learning this order once removes an entire class of "it works in my theme but not in the plugin" defects.

The canonical load order, as implemented in wp-settings.php, is: wp-includes/version.php → compat.php and class-wp-list-util.php → database connection via require_wp_db() → wp-settings.php defines constants and globals → wp-includes/load.php (core functions) → wp-includes/plugin.php (the hook system) → wp-includes/l10n.php → must-use plugins → network plugins on multisite → wp-includes/pluggable.php → the rest of wp-includes → active plugins, via wp_get_active_and_valid_plugins() → the active theme, via functions.php → do_action( 'setup_theme' ) → do_action( 'after_setup_theme' ) → do_action( 'init' ), the point at which nearly all registration is expected to have happened.

LayerLoaded byMay safely callMust not
wp-config.phpwp-load.phpdefine, env lookupsCall any WP function; the object cache is not up yet
mu-pluginswp-settings.php directlyadd_filter, add_action, constantsRegister post types, read options, start sessions
Network pluginsafter mu-plugins, multisite onlySame as plugins, network-wide intentAssume a single site exists
Regular pluginswp_get_active_and_valid_plugins()Anything; defer work to init and laterPerform queries at file scope
pluggable.phpbefore rest_api_loadedDefine the overrideable function setAssume any core file has been loaded
Theme functions.phpafter all pluginsFull API; primary place for after_setup_theme workAssume the block editor is active
init hookend of wp-settings.phpregister_post_type, taxonomies, blocks, REST routesRender output or trust request input
 
<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * mu-plugins/010-constants.php
 *
 * Included alphabetically, before the plugin API is fully assembled and
 * before any option is read. This file therefore does nothing but
 * declare state. Anything with a side effect here runs on every
 * request, including REST, cron, CLI and the login screen.
 */
defined( 'ABSPATH' ) || exit;

define( 'ACME_PLUGIN_DIR', WP_CONTENT_DIR . '/plugins' );
define( 'ACME_LOG_LEVEL', 'warn' );
define( 'ACME_TENANT', getenv( 'ACME_TENANT' ) ?: 'default' );

// A `pre_option_*` filter is legitimate here because add_filter() is
// available, but it must be pure: no database access, no exceptions.
add_filter(
    'pre_option_acme_tenant',
    static fn ( $pre ) => is_string( $pre ) ? $pre : ( getenv( 'ACME_TENANT' ) ?: $pre ),
    1
);

Mu-plugins cannot be deactivated from the UI. Because they are not tracked in wp_options, an erroneous mu-plugin is only removable over SFTP or wp eval. This is precisely why the file above is so short: the recovery path for a broken mu-plugin requires shell access to the document root, and the recovery path for a broken regular plugin is two clicks. Keep the mu-plugin count under five and keep each file under twenty lines.

The hook surface and plugin architecture

Describes the extension contract that makes WordPress extensible, and the discipline required to add to it without creating order-dependent behaviour.

Every extension point in WordPress is a named callback list. Plugins do not subclass core; they attach to names core chose, and they are correct to do so because those names are the only thing WordPress promises not to remove. The discipline this imposes is narrow and absolute: a plugin must never assume it is the only listener, must never assume the value it receives is the value it passed in the previous request, and must never produce output during a hook that another plugin may also be producing output during. Chapter 2 develops this in full; the purpose of this subsection is to establish the shape of the system so that the rest of the guide can assume it.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

namespace Acme\Ledger;

defined( 'ABSPATH' ) || exit;

final class Plugin {

    public const VERSION = '1.4.0';
    public const MIN_WP  = '6.9';

    private static ?self $instance = null;

    public static function boot(): self {
        if ( null === self::$instance ) {
            self::$instance = new self( __FILE__, dirname( __DIR__ ) );
        }

        return self::$instance;
    }

    private function __construct(
        private readonly string $file,
        private readonly string $dir,
    ) {
        if ( ! $this->requirements_met() ) {
            add_action( 'admin_notices', [ $this, 'render_requirements_notice' ] );
            return;
        }

        add_action( 'init', $this->register_content_types(), 5 );
        add_action( 'rest_api_init', $this->register_rest_routes() );
        add_action( 'admin_enqueue_scripts', $this->enqueue_admin_assets() );
    }

    public function requirements_met(): bool {
        global $wp_version;
        return version_compare( (string) $wp_version, self::MIN_WP, '>=' );
    }

    public function render_requirements_notice(): void {
        printf(
            '<div class="notice notice-error"><p>%s</p></div>',
            esc_html(
                sprintf(
                    /* translators: 1: minimum version, 2: current version */
                    __( 'Acme Ledger requires WordPress %1$s or newer. This site runs %2$s.', 'acme-ledger' ),
                    self::MIN_WP,
                    (string) $GLOBALS['wp_version']
                )
            )
        );
    }
}

The bootstrap pattern above is not decoration. Three properties matter. First, the plugin registers hooks and nothing else — no queries, no output, no require of a template. Second, the version check bails out at construction rather than at first use, so an incompatible site degrades to "plugin absent" rather than "plugin half-present". Third, boot() is idempotent, which makes the plugin safe to instantiate from a mu-plugin, from a test bootstrap and from WP-CLI in the same process without duplicating listeners. The init hook at priority 5 is the conventional place for register_post_type(): priority 9 is used by several core features and priority 10 is where most plugins register, so claiming an earlier slot avoids ordering surprises with taxonomy rewrites.

The directory layout encodes a dependency direction that PHP will not enforce for you: Domain/ must not reference Infrastructure/, and neither may reference Rest/. A Domain\Invoice class that calls get_post_meta() is not testable without a bootstrap, not mockable, and not portable to a headless deployment. Keeping the repository as the only class that knows a WordPress invoice is a post with _acme_* metadata is what makes it possible to swap the storage strategy described in Chapter 3 without touching business logic.

Data model, caching and storage strategy

States the storage tiers available to a plugin, the durability and latency profile of each, and the decision rule for choosing between them.

WordPress offers four places to put state and they are not interchangeable: the wp_options row, the metadata tables, the object cache, and a transients row that is really an option with an expiry column. They differ in durability (transients evaporate), latency (options are read on every request; the object cache is not read if absent) and queryability (options are unindexed by anything but option_name). Choosing wrongly is not a style violation; it produces the two most common WordPress performance defects — an autoload bloat that adds 40 ms to every page view, and a plugin that issues one query per row because it treats the object cache as a database.

TierBackingRead cost when warmRead cost when coldSurvives restartUse for
get_option (autoload=yes)wp_options, loaded in one query at bootArray lookup, ~0 msOne indexed queryYesSettings read on every request
get_option (autoload=no)Same table, skipped by the boot queryOne queryOne queryYesLarge or rarely read config
Post / term / user metadataEAV tables with composite indexesPrimed into the object cache by *_meta_cacheOne extra query per object typeYesPer-entity state
wp_cache_*Memory; Redis/Memcached only with a drop-inSub-millisecondMiss — you computeOnly with a drop-inDerived data, query results
Transient + external cachewp_options or Redis, with TTLCache read, then DBMiss — you computeYes, with EXT_OBJECT_CACHEExpensive remote payloads
Transient, no external cachewp_options row with _transient_timeout_Cache read, then DBMiss — you computeYesShort-TTL derived data only
 
<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

// Tier 1 — durable, read on every request, cached for the life of the
// request. `false` for autoload keeps a large payload off the boot query.
update_option( 'acme_tenant_config', [ 'locale' => 'en-GB', 'currency' => 'GBP' ], false );

// Tier 2 — object cache. Groups are the unit of invalidation; namespace
// every key and include a version or a content hash in it.
wp_cache_set( 'acme:settings', $settings, 'acme', 300 );
wp_cache_set( 'acme:invoice:' . $invoice_id, $invoice, 'acme_invoices', 900 );

// Tier 3 — transients. With an external object cache these live in Redis
// with a real TTL. Without one, they are option rows and the read costs
// a query, so keep TTL short and the value small.
set_transient( 'acme:fx:GBP:EUR', 1.1642, 6 * HOUR_IN_SECONDS );

// Tier 4 — never. A transient is not a job queue and an option is not
// a log. Both are read into every request that touches them.

There is a fourth fact that dominates the table above: the default object cache is a per-request PHP array. A value written with wp_cache_set() is gone at the end of the request unless a drop-in provides a shared backend. This is the single most common cause of a site that benchmarks acceptably under a synthetic single-user load and falls over at 200 concurrent users — every one of those users is doing the same cache-miss work. EXT_OBJECT_CACHE is defined by the drop-in and is the flag every piece of code should test before assuming cache durability.

<?php
/**
 * wp-content/object-cache.php
 *
 * WordPress loads this file INSTEAD OF wp-includes/cache.php, so the
 * drop-in must implement the entire surface: not just get/set/delete,
 * but the group APIs and wp_cache_switch_to_blog(), which core and
 * several plugins call unconditionally during multisite requests.
 *
 * Presence of this file also defines EXT_OBJECT_CACHE = true.
 */
function wp_cache_init(): void {
    global $wp_object_cache;

    $redis = new Redis();
    $redis->connect( 'redis.internal', 6379, 1.5 );
    $redis->setOption( Redis::OPT_PREFIX, sprintf( 'wp:%s:', wp_parse_url( home_url(), PHP_URL_HOST ) ) );

    $wp_object_cache = new Acme\Cache\RedisObjectCache(
        connection: $redis,
        blog_prefix: $redis,
        global_groups: [
            'blog-details', 'blog-id', 'blog-meta', 'blog-lookup',
            'global-posts', 'networks', 'rss', 'sites', 'userslugs',
        ],
        non_persistent_groups: [ 'counts', 'plugins', 'themes', 'theme-json' ],
    );
}

function wp_cache_add_global_groups( array|string ...$groups ): void {
    $wp_object_cache = $GLOBALS['wp_object_cache'] ?? null;
    $wp_object_cache?->add_global_groups( ...$groups );
}

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * Establishes whether the metadata round-trip is actually caching,
 * rather than assuming it. Run via: wp eval-file tools/bench-meta.php
 */
$ids = range( 1, 200 );

$t0 = hrtime( true );
foreach ( $ids as $id ) {
    get_post_meta( $id, '_acme_total_minor', true );
}
$naive_ms = ( hrtime( true ) - $t0 ) / 1e6;

wp_cache_flush();

$t1 = hrtime( true );
foreach ( $ids as $id ) {
    update_post_meta_cache( [ $id ] );
}
$primed_ms = ( hrtime( true ) - $t1 ) / 1e6;

printf( "cold per-row get_post_meta : %8.2f ms for %d rows\n", $naive_ms, count( $ids ) );
printf( "primed object cache         : %8.2f ms for %d rows\n", $primed_ms, count( $ids ) );
printf( "speed-up                    : %8.1fx\n", $naive_ms / max( $primed_ms, 0.001 ) );
printf( "EXT_OBJECT_CACHE            : %s\n", defined( 'EXT_OBJECT_CACHE' ) ? 'yes' : 'NO — installs are uncached' );

Conventions used in this document

Fixes the coding, output and error-handling conventions used in every sample, so that the security chapter can be read as a set of rules rather than a set of preferences.

Every PHP sample in this guide opens with declare(strict_types=1) and lives in a namespace, because without both, the type declarations that make a value object meaningful are decorative. Every sample assumes ABSPATH is defined and the caller has already checked authorisation — Chapter 6 exists precisely because that assumption, made carelessly, is how sites are compromised. Errors travel as WP_Error across HTTP and service boundaries and as exceptions inside domain code, with translation at the boundary. Nothing is echoed; output is returned or buffered, and escaping happens at the point of output rather than at the point of storage.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

namespace Acme\Ledger\Domain;

use InvalidArgumentException;
use Stringable;

/**
 * Domain value object: no WordPress function appears anywhere in this
 * file, which is what allows it to be unit-tested without a bootstrap
 * and reused by a headless consumer of the REST API.
 */
final class Money implements Stringable {

    public function __construct(
        public readonly int $amountMinor,
        public readonly string $currency,
    ) {
        if ( ! preg_match( '/^[A-Z]{3}$/', $currency ) ) {
            throw new InvalidArgumentException( 'ISO 4217 alphabetic code required.' );
        }

        if ( $amountMinor < 0 ) {
            throw new InvalidArgumentException( 'Amount must be non-negative.' );
        }
    }

    public function add( self $other ): self {
        if ( $this->currency !== $other->currency ) {
            throw new InvalidArgumentException( 'Currency mismatch.' );
        }

        return new self( $this->amountMinor + $other->amountMinor, $this->currency );
    }

    public function __toString(): string {
        return sprintf( '%d.%02d %s', intdiv( $this->amountMinor, 100 ), $this->amountMinor % 100, $this->currency );
    }
}

The escaping map below is the one to memorise, and it is reproduced in the security chapter in expanded form. The rule that generates almost every exception: escaping is an output concern and belongs at the point of output; sanitisation is an input concern and belongs at the point of input. A value stored already escaped is stored wrong, because the moment it is used in a different context — an attribute, a JSON payload, a database comparison — the escaping is both redundant and incorrect. Sanitising on input and escaping on output is not belt-and-braces; each closes a different hole.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * The context -> function map. Returning the wrong function is the
 * most common security defect in WordPress code, and it is invisible
 * in testing because the happy path renders identically.
 */
final class Escaper {

    public static function for( string $context ): callable {
        return match ( $context ) {
            'html'      => 'esc_html',        // text node content
            'attr'      => 'esc_attr',        // any attribute value, quoted or not
            'url'       => 'esc_url',         // href, src, action, redirect_to
            'url_raw'   => 'esc_url_raw',     // url for storage or comparison, not output
            'textarea'  => 'esc_textarea',    // textarea body
            'js'        => 'esc_js',          // inline script string; last resort, prefer wp_json_encode
            'html-allow'=> static fn ( string $html ): string => wp_kses( $html, self::allowed_inline() ),
            default     => throw new \InvalidArgumentException( sprintf( 'Unknown escaping context: %s', $context ) ),
        };
    }

    /** @return array<string, list<string>> */
    private static function allowed_inline(): array {
        return [
            'a'      => [ 'href' => true, 'title' => true, 'target' => true, 'rel' => true ],
            'strong' => [],
            'em'     => [],
            'code'   => [],
            'br'     => [],
        ];
    }
}

Do not reach for esc_sql as a general-purpose defence. esc_sql() calls $wpdb->escape(), which is mysqli_real_escape_string() with no context awareness. It is correct only when you are concatenating a value into a query string you built yourself, and even then the correct mechanism is a placeholder in $wpdb->prepare(). Using esc_sql() on a value you then also pass through prepare() double-processes the string and will corrupt it if it contains a literal backslash. The correct use of prepare() is discussed in §6.1, along with the identifier problem it does not solve.

Exercises

  1. Produce a boot inventory. Write a wp eval-file script that records, for the current install: total included PHP files split by phase, the number of active plugins and themes, the flattened hook graph for init, the_content, template_redirect and rest_api_init, and the total row count and autoloaded byte count of wp_options. Run it against three sites of different vintages. Deliverable: audit/boot-profile.json plus a one-page document identifying the five highest-cost contributors on the largest site.
  2. Build a compatibility gate. Create a mu-plugin that asserts PHP ≥ 8.5, WordPress ≥ 6.9, a MySQL/MariaDB version meeting the minimum, a memory_limit of at least 256 MB, and the absence of WP_DEBUG_DISPLAY when WP_ENVIRONMENT_TYPE is production. Prove it works by running the same plugin against a deliberately downgraded wp-env container. Deliverable: the plugin, a PHPUnit feature test asserting a 500 and a log line for each failure mode, and a CI job that runs the test.
  3. Stand up a reproducible toolchain. Pin @wordpress/scripts and @wordpress/env, configure wp-env.json for WordPress 6.9 on PHP 8.5 with an object-cache.php drop-in, and wire composer validate, phpcs, phpstan and phpunit into a single composer check script. Deliverable: a clean run of composer check from a cold clone in under four minutes, and a phpstan.neon that reports zero errors with the WordPress stubs loaded at level 6.
  4. Quantify the object cache. Install Redis, add the object-cache.php drop-in, and run the metadata benchmark in §0.5 against a 200-post fixture where 20% of posts carry _acme_total_minor. Record cold-warm, warm-cold and warm-warm timings, plus redis-cli --latency during a 10-concurrent-user ab run. Deliverable: a table of six measurements, a statement of the break-even post count, and a decision recorded in an ADR.
  5. Audit the escaping posture. Write a phpcs ruleset with a sniff (or a PHPStan custom rule) that flags any direct echo, print or string interpolation into an HTML context, and any use of esc_sql outside $wpdb methods. Run it over a real third-party plugin you do not control. Deliverable: the ruleset committed to the repository, a baseline report, and a written triage of every finding explaining why it is or is not exploitable.

1 · The Request Lifecycle and the WordPress Boot Sequence

1.1 The request from Apache to output1.2 wp-load.php and the bootstrap1.3 pluggable.php and the overrideable function set1.4 The query phase1.5 The template phase and output1.6 Shutdown, cron and the SHORTINIT boundary

This chapter traces a single front-end request from the point the web server accepts a URI to the point PHP exits, naming every file that participates and every point at which your code can run. It argues that the sequence is the platform’s only real contract: there is no container, no dependency graph and no scheduler beyond the order in which files are included, so anything that depends on a plugin running before another plugin is depending on an accident. Two practical payoffs follow directly. First, boot cost is measurable and attackable — a WordPress install that includes 900 PHP files before init is paying 900 compilation and parse costs on every cache miss. Second, SHORTINIT is a supported and under-used tool: it gives a script the configuration, the database handle and the autoloader in about 4 ms, which is the right foundation for cron runners, import jobs and health checks.

1.1 The request from Apache to output

Establishes the entry points the web server will actually execute, and shows why the routing rules you write are as much a WordPress concern as the PHP they protect.

Every front-end request lands in the document root, but the only PHP that must be reachable is index.php. The rewrite rules exist to translate a permalink into a query string and hand control to that one file; everything downstream — query parsing, canonical redirects, 404 resolution, template selection — is PHP running inside it. The practical differences between Apache and nginx configurations are small but not cosmetic. Apache’s .htaccess is evaluated per directory on every request and its cost is paid even when it changes nothing; nginx compiles its rules once at startup. On a site serving several thousand requests a minute the difference is measurable in CPU, and on a shared host it is the difference between a site that can be tuned and one that cannot.

# nginx: derived from the WordPress "plain" configuration, hardened
server {
    listen 443 ssl http2;
    server_name www.acme.test;
    root /var/www/acme/public;
    index index.php;

    access_log /var/log/nginx/acme.access.log;
    client_max_body_size 32M;

    location = /favicon.ico { access_log off; log_not_found off; }
    location = /robots.txt  { access_log off; log_not_found off; }

    # Never serve these, whatever the URL looks like.
    location ~* /(?:wp-config\.php|readme\.html|wp-content/uploads/.*\.php)$ {
        deny all;
        return 404;
    }

    location / {
        try_files $uri $uri/ /index.php?$args;
    }

    location ~ \.php$ {
        try_files $uri =404;
        fastcgi_pass unix:/run/php/php8.5-fpm.sock;
        fastcgi_index index.php;
        include fastcgi_params;
        fastcgi_param SCRIPT_FILENAME $document_root$fastcgi_script_name;
        fastcgi_param HTTPS on;
        fastcgi_read_timeout 30s;
    }
}

Two rules in that configuration are load-bearing for security rather than for routing. Denying PHP execution under wp-content/uploads removes the single most common post-exploitation persistence path: an attacker who can upload a file — through a plugin bug, a REST endpoint with a missing MIME check, or a legitimate contributor account with an attachment capability — usually writes PHP. And denying wp-config.php at the server layer matters because the file exists, is valid PHP, and contains every credential in the system. WordPress will happily serve it as PHP output if the request reaches it, producing a blank response and a confusing log entry rather than a 403; catching it in the web server turns a misconfiguration into a non-event. The Apache equivalents of both rules, together with the response headers, are given in §6.6.

<?php
/**
 * index.php — the entire public entry point, and it has been four lines
 * long since 1.5. WP_USE_THEMES is the only decision made here; it is
 * read later by wp-blog-header.php to decide whether the theme renders.
 *
 *   index.php            defines WP_USE_THEMES = true
 *   wp-blog-header.php   requires wp-load.php unless SHORTINIT is defined
 *   wp-load.php          requires wp-config.php, then wp-settings.php
 *   wp-settings.php      the bootstrap; fires `init` at the very end
 *   wp()                 after settings, re-fires `init` and runs the query
 *   template-loader.php  walks the hierarchy and includes the template
 */
define( 'WP_USE_THEMES', true );
require __DIR__ . '/wp-blog-header.php';

Why wp() appears immediately before the template loader. wp() is a three-line function whose entire job is to fire the init action and let WP_Query run. It is the boundary between "plugins have registered everything" and "the request is now about a specific piece of content". Code that runs before wp() cannot rely on the main query; code that runs after it can use is_singular(), get_the_ID() and the whole conditional-tag family. If a plugin needs to know what is being viewed, register against template_redirect or later, not against plugins_loaded.

1.2 wp-load.php and the bootstrap

Walks wp-load.php and wp-settings.php in load order, identifying the exact point at which each subsystem becomes available to your code.

wp-load.php has three jobs: define the path constants, load wp-config.php, and — if SHORTINIT is set — return immediately. Everything after the return is the full bootstrap. The importance of the early return is that it establishes a clean contract: after SHORTINIT you have constants, a configured $wpdb, the class autoloader and nothing else. No plugins, no theme, no init, no pluggable.php, no object cache beyond $wpdb's own. That is not a degraded WordPress; it is the smallest useful subset, and it is what makes it fast enough to run on every CLI invocation or in a health-check subprocess.

<?php
/**
 * wp-load.php — abridged; ~340 lines in 6.9. Order shown is load order.
 */
if ( ! defined( 'ABSPATH' ) ) {
    define( 'ABSPATH', __DIR__ . '/' );
    define( 'WPINC', 'wp-includes' );
    define( 'WP_CONTENT_DIR', ABSPATH . 'wp-content' );
}

if ( ! file_exists( ABSPATH . 'wp-config.php' ) ) {
    require_once ABSPATH . 'wp-setup.php';
    return;
}

require_once ABSPATH . 'wp-config.php';

if ( defined( 'SHORTINIT' ) ) {
    return;                       // <-- everything below is skipped
}

wp_maintenance();                  // .maintenance file check
timer_start();                     // begins $_SERVER['REQUEST_TIME_FLOAT']
wp_debug_mode();                   // WP_DEBUG + error_reporting + ini_set
wp_timezone_override();            // option gmt_offset / timezone_string

require_once ABSPATH . WPINC . '/l10n.php';
require_once ABSPATH . WPINC . '/class-wp-list-util.php';
require_once ABSPATH . WPINC . '/formatting.php';
require_once ABSPATH . WPINC . '/meta.php';
require_once ABSPATH . WPINC . '/functions.php';
require_once ABSPATH . WPINC . '/class-wp-meta-query.php';
require_once ABSPATH . WPINC . '/class-wp-matchesmapregex.php';
require_once ABSPATH . WPINC . '/class-wp.php';

require_once ABSPATH . WPINC . '/class-wp-error.php';
require_once ABSPATH . WPINC . '/pcre.php';
require_once ABSPATH . WPINC . '/class-wp-hook.php';

require_once ABSPATH . 'wp-settings.php';   // the rest lives here

<?php
/**
 * wp-settings.php — abridged. This is the file to read when you need
 * to know *why* something has or has not been loaded yet.
 */

// 1. Global scope: $table_prefix, $wpdb, $wp_version, $wp_rewrite, $wp,
//    $wp_query, $wp_taxonomy, $wp_roles, $wp_theme_directories.
global $table_prefix, $wpdb, $wp_version, $wp_rewrite, $wp, $wp_query;
global $wp_taxonomy, $wp_roles, $wp_theme_directories, $wp_object_cache;

// 2. Load every file in wp-includes, in the order wp-settings lists.
require ABSPATH . WPINC . '/class-wp-recovery-mode.php';
require ABSPATH . WPINC . '/default-constants.php';
require ABSPATH . WPINC . '/plugin.php';                 // add_action(), do_action()
require ABSPATH . WPINC . '/option.php';                 // get_option()
require ABSPATH . WPINC . '/cache.php';                  // wp_cache_* (memory array)

// 3. The object cache bootstrap, BEFORE anything can use it.
wp_start_object_cache();

// 4. Database connection. $wpdb exists after this line.
require_wp_db();

// 5. Must-use plugins, then network plugins, then active plugins.
wp_get_mu_plugins();
wp_get_active_and_valid_plugins();

foreach ( wp_get_active_and_valid_plugins() as $plugin ) {
    include_once $plugin;
    $plugin = null;
}

// 6. The overrideable function set: pluggable.php.
wp_set_wpdb_vars();
wp_magic_quotes();                  // no-op since 5.5, retained for history

// 7. The theme: theme.json, functions.php, after_setup_theme, init.
do_action( 'setup_theme' );
do_action( 'after_setup_theme' );

// 8. `init` fires here. Registration ends; execution begins.
do_action( 'init' );

Step 5 is the one to internalise. Active plugins are included in the exact order stored in the active_plugins option, which is activation order unless a plugin or the UI has reordered it. There is no dependency resolution, no version negotiation, and no mechanism for a plugin to declare that it must load before another. If two plugins must be coordinated, the only reliable mechanisms are a shared init priority or an explicit dependency check in one of them; anything else is a race that will reproduce once a year on someone else’s install.

OrderSourceSide effectAvailable to your code
1index.phpDefines WP_USE_THEMESNothing
2wp-load.phpABSPATH, WPINC, wp-config.phpConstants and config only
3class autoloaderRegisters WP_* class mapCore classes on demand
4mu-pluginsYour earliest hook registrationsadd_action, add_filter
5wp_start_object_cache()Binds wp_cache_* to backendwp_cache_get
6require_wp_db()Connects, sets charset, table prefix$wpdb
7Active pluginsRegister hooks; must not queryEverything except init
8pluggable.phpDefines the overrideable function setwp_get_current_user, wp_mail
9Theme functions.phpEnqueues, menus, image sizesFull API
10do_action( 'init' )Registration completesFull API; the conventional anchor

1.3 pluggable.php and the overrideable function set

Describes the one mechanism WordPress offers for replacing a core function outright, and why almost every codebase should prefer a filter instead.

Between the active plugins and the rest of wp-includes, wp-settings.php requires pluggable.php. That file contains roughly ninety function definitions, each guarded by function_exists(), which is what makes them replaceable: declare a function with a matching name before the file is included and the core definition is skipped. The set is fixed and finite — it includes wp_get_current_user, wp_mail, is_ssl, wp_parse_url, get_avatar, wp_hash, wp_rand, do_feed and the whole _deprecated_* family. A function that is not in that file cannot be replaced by redeclaration; attempting it produces a fatal "cannot redeclare" error and a white screen.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * mu-plugins/040-mail-override.php
 *
 * The override is a drop-in replacement for the core function, not a
 * filter. Everything the core implementation did must be reproduced, or
 * the behaviour is silently lost — this is the main hazard of the
 * pattern, and the reason most teams should reach for a filter first.
 */
if ( ! function_exists( 'wp_mail' ) ) {
    /**
     * @param string[]|string $to
     * @param string[]|string $headers
     * @param string[]|string $attachments
     */
    function wp_mail( $to, $subject, $message, $headers = '', $attachments = [] ) {
        $envelope = Acme\Mail\Envelope::from( compact( 'to', 'subject', 'message', 'headers', 'attachments' ) );

        try {
            $result = Acme\Mail\Transport::instance()->send( $envelope );
        } catch ( \Throwable $e ) {
            error_log( '[acme/mail] ' . $e->getMessage() );

            return false;
        }

        /**
         * Fires after wp_mail() has handed a message to the transport.
         * Fires whether or not delivery succeeded; check the return.
         */
        do_action( 'wp_mail_succeeded', $result );

        return $result->accepted;
    }
}

The practical guidance is short. A filter exists for most of the ninety functions, and using one keeps the core behaviour intact for callers you have not thought about. pre_wp_mail and wp_mail_failed cover the mail path completely without replacing a function. For wp_get_current_user there is determine_current_user and send_auth_cookies. For the handful of cases where no filter exists — is_ssl behind a terminating proxy is the canonical one — a pluggable override in an mu-plugin is correct, provided you reproduce every behaviour the core version has, because a hundred other plugins are relying on those behaviours you have not read.

Pluggable functionFilter that usually sufficesOverride justified when
wp_mailpre_wp_mail, wp_mail_failedNever — replace the transport with a hook
wp_get_current_userdetermine_current_userSSO integration that must win at every call site
is_sslNone reliableLoad balancer terminates TLS and strips the header
get_avatarget_avatar_data, pre_get_avatar_dataNever
wp_hashNoneNever — it is part of the security floor
wp_randNoneDeterministic tests only, never in production
wp_parse_urlNoneNever — replacing it breaks URL handling site-wide
status_headerNoneNever

An override must be defined before pluggable.php loads, and after $wpdb exists. That window is exactly the mu-plugin phase, which is why overrides live there. Defining the function later — in a regular plugin, or worse in a theme — will not replace the core version; it will fatal, because core’s definition is already in the symbol table. The function_exists() guard around your own definition is not defensive decoration either: it is what lets two plugins that both try to override the same function produce one working override and one silent no-op instead of a fatal, and it is why an override should log loudly when the function is already defined.

1.4 The query phase

Examines how WordPress turns a permalink into a set of WP_Query arguments, and separates the three ways to ask the database for posts — each with different cost and different correctness guarantees.

The main query is constructed by WP::parse_request() and WP::register_globals() from the query string, using rewrite rules that were flushed into the wp_rewrite option when rules were last generated. That indirection is the reason permalinks break in exactly one situation: the rewrite rules in the database are stale relative to the registered post types. Everything else about query construction is deterministic. The three ways to ask for posts differ enormously, and choosing the wrong one is the most common cause of a slow plugin.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * 1. WP_Query — the general case. Runs WP_Query::get_posts(), honours
 *    all query vars, populates templates, postmeta and term caches
 *    for the whole result set. Correct for the loop; expensive for
 *    lists of a hundred rows you only need IDs from.
 */
$recent = new WP_Query( [
    'post_type'              => 'acme_invoice',
    'post_status'            => 'publish',
    'posts_per_page'         => 20,
    'orderby'                => 'meta_value_num',
    'meta_key'               => '_acme_total_minor',   // legacy single-key form
    'order'                  => 'DESC',
    'no_found_rows'          => true,   // skip the SQL_CALC_FOUND_ROWS count
    'update_post_meta_cache' => false,  // skip one meta SELECT per post
    'update_post_term_cache' => false,  // skip one term SELECT per post
    'fields'                 => 'ids',  // return IDs only; no post hydration
    'cache_results'          => false,  // do not memoise this result set
] );

$ids = $recent->posts;

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * 2. get_posts() — a thin wrapper that changes the defaults.
 *    Convenient, but it hides `suppress_filters` being true by
 *    default, which means plugins cannot filter its results. Do not
 *    use it for anything a third party might legitimately need to
 *    influence (premium content, visibility, ordering).
 */
$ids = get_posts( [
    'post_type'      => 'acme_invoice',
    'post_status'    => 'publish',
    'posts_per_page' => 20,
    'fields'         => 'ids',
    'no_found_rows'  => true,
    's'              => $search_term,
] );

/**
 * 3. Direct $wpdb query — correct for aggregates and for anything
 *    spanning more than one table. Never for a loop: the template
 *    and cache priming the loop depends on will be skipped, and the
 *    loop will call get_post() per row, i.e. one query per row.
 */
global $wpdb;

$totals = $wpdb->get_results(
    $wpdb->prepare(
        "SELECT pm.meta_value AS total_minor, COUNT(*) AS n
           FROM {$wpdb->postmeta} pm
           INNER JOIN {$wpdb->posts} p ON p.ID = pm.post_id
          WHERE pm.meta_key = %s
            AND p.post_type = %s
            AND p.post_status = %s
          GROUP BY pm.meta_value
          ORDER BY total_minor DESC
          LIMIT %d",
        '_acme_total_minor',
        'acme_invoice',
        'publish',
        50
    )
);

The third form is the one that is simultaneously the fastest and the most dangerous. It is the only way to answer a question the object model cannot express — totals, joins across post types, denormalised summaries — and it is also the only way to skip every cache-priming step WordPress performs on your behalf. The rule that resolves the tension: if the result feeds a WP_Query loop or a template, use WP_Query with the performance flags you need; if the result is an aggregate, use $wpdb with prepare() and never in a loop over post objects. The fields => 'ids' and no_found_rows => true combination is the most commonly forgotten optimisation in plugin code and typically removes both the hydration cost and a full table scan from the query plan.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * A real meta_query, which is where performance problems become
 * visible. The nested array form produces a single LEFT JOIN per
 * clause with a MATCH() subquery per clause — not one query per row.
 */
$query = new WP_Query( [
    'post_type'              => 'product',
    'post_status'            => 'publish',
    'posts_per_page'         => 24,
    'paged'                  => max( 1, (int) get_query_var( 'paged' ) ?: 1 ),
    'no_found_rows'          => true,
    'meta_query'             => [       // AND of ANDs
        'relation' => 'AND',
        [
            'key'     => '_acme_stock',          'type' => 'NUMERIC',
            'value'   => 0,                      'compare' => '>',
        ],
        [
            'key'     => '_acme_region',         'type' => 'CHAR',
            'value'   => [ 'UK', 'IE' ],          'compare' => 'IN',
        ],
        [
            'key'     => '_acme_price_minor',    'type' => 'NUMERIC',
            'value'   => [ 1000, 50000 ],         'compare' => 'BETWEEN',
            'type'    => 'NUMERIC',
        ],
    ],
    'tax_query'              => [       // taxonomy is relational; meta is not
        [
            'taxonomy' => 'product_cat',
            'field'    => 'slug',
            'terms'    => [ 'hardware', 'accessories' ],
            'operator' => 'IN',
        ],
    ],
    'orderby'                => [
        'meta_value_num' => 'ASC',
        'title'          => 'ASC',
    ],
    'meta_key'               => '_acme_price_minor',
] );
ApproachFilters honouredCaches primedTypical cost, 100 rowsUse when
WP_Query (full)Yespost, meta, term3 queries + hydrationAnything rendered in a loop
WP_Query + flagsYesNone1 query, IDs onlyLists, autocomplete, relations
get_posts()No (suppress_filters)Post only1–2 queriesInternal lookups only
get_posts with suppress_filters => falseYesPost only1–2 queriesSimple filtered lists
$wpdb directNoNone1 queryAggregates, cross-table joins
WP_Query with meta_queryYesPost, meta, term1 LEFT JOIN per meta clauseStructured filtering

Always join to wp_posts in a hand-written meta query. A bare SELECT post_id FROM wp_postmeta WHERE meta_key = 'x' has no way to exclude revisions, autosaves, attachments, trashed rows or post_status != 'publish', so it silently returns content that WP_Query would have excluded. This is not a theoretical concern: it is the mechanism behind most "our plugin shows drafts on the front end" reports. Whenever you join wp_postmeta by hand, join wp_posts in the same statement and apply the status and type predicates there.

1.5 The template phase and output

Covers template-loader.php, the template_redirect interception point, and the way a block theme resolves a template hierarchy that includes non-file targets.

Once wp() has run, template-loader.php decides what to do. It honours a status header override, fires template_redirect (at which point a plugin can issue a redirect or a 404 and short-circuit the rest), handles the is_robots, is_favicon and is_trackback special cases, resolves the template, and finally includes it. The resolution itself consults the template_include filter after get_query_template() has walked the hierarchy, so a plugin can override the file WordPress chose without needing to know the hierarchy. That ordering — walk, then filter, then include — is the extension point to use for almost every template intervention.

A block theme changes one thing about this pipeline and it is a large one. A classic theme resolves the hierarchy to a file path and includes it. A block theme resolves it to an identifier — a wp_template or wp_template_part post in the wp_template and wp_template_part post types, or a file under templates/ or parts/ — and then renders the block markup by walking the block tree and calling each block’s render_callback. The HTML therefore passes through far more PHP than a classic template does, which means a block that returns a large subtree is a real performance cost, not a stylistic one. The other consequence is that the_content filters still run, but template_include no longer gives you a file; for block themes the equivalent lever is the render_block filter and the render_block_{$block_name} dynamic filter.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * Block-theme aware output interception. In 6.9 `get_query_template()`
 * returns an identifier (e.g. "twentytwentyfive//archive") rather than
 * an absolute path when the active theme is a block theme, so string
 * comparison against .php extensions silently stops matching.
 */
add_filter( 'template_include', static function ( string $template ): string {
    if ( ! str_contains( $template, '//' ) ) {
        return $template;   // classic theme: an absolute path
    }

    $parts = explode( '//', $template, 2 );
    $slug  = $parts[1];

    if ( 'single-acme_invoice' !== $slug ) {
        return $template;
    }

    $override = get_stylesheet_directory() . '/templates/single-acme-invoice.html';

    return file_exists( $override ) ? $override : $template;
}, 99, 3 );

/**
 * `render_block` is the block-theme equivalent of `the_content`, and
 * receives every block instance in the tree, including nested ones.
 */
add_filter( 'render_block', static function (
    string $block_content,
    array $parsed_block,
    WP_Block $instance
): string {
    if ( 'core/post-excerpt' !== ( $parsed_block['blockName'] ?? '' ) ) {
        return $block_content;
    }

    return sprintf(
        '<p class="excerpt">%s</p>',
        esc_html( wp_strip_all_tags( $instance->attributes['content'] ?? '' ) )
    );
}, 10, 3 );

1.6 Shutdown, cron and the SHORTINIT boundary

Covers what happens between the last template byte and process exit, why traffic-driven WP-Cron is the wrong place to run important work, and the SHORTINIT boundary that gives a script the configuration and the database handle in a few milliseconds.

shutdown fires from shutdown_action_hook(), which is registered with register_shutdown_function() in wp-settings.php. It runs after output, so headers are already sent and any exception thrown in a shutdown callback cannot change the status code. wp_die(), fatal errors and the end of the script all route through it, which is why core uses it to close the database connection and to spawn the cron request. The rule that follows: use shutdown only for work that is safe to abandon — flushing a log, releasing a lock, recording a metric. Anything a user is waiting on belongs before the template renders.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

add_action( 'shutdown', static function (): void {
    $started = (float) ( $_SERVER['REQUEST_TIME_FLOAT'] ?? microtime( true ) );
    $elapsed = ( microtime( true ) - $started ) * 1000;

    $status = http_response_code() ?: 200;

    error_log( sprintf(
        '[acme/perf] status=%d elapsed=%.2fms mem_peak=%.1fMB queries=%d uri=%s',
        $status,
        $elapsed,
        memory_get_peak_usage( true ) / 1048576,
        (int) ( $GLOBALS['wpdb']->num_queries ?? 0 ),
        esc_url_raw( (string) ( $_SERVER['REQUEST_URI'] ?? '' ) )
    ) );
}, 0 );

WordPress cron is the other half of this section and is the source of a great deal of confusion. wp_schedule_event() writes a row to the cron option; it does not schedule anything in the operating system. The only thing that ever executes a due event is wp-cron.php, which is spawned at the end of a normal page load by spawn_cron(), on a probabilistic timer (the doing_cron transient is set for 60 seconds to prevent stampedes, and the spawn only fires on roughly one in ten eligible requests). The consequence is arithmetic: a site receiving no traffic runs no cron. If a site’s lowest-traffic hour is 03:00 and nothing is scheduled then, a "daily" job runs whenever the next visitor arrives, which may be 09:00.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * Registering recurring work. The interval is a minimum age, not a
 * promise: the event becomes due, and runs on the next eligible
 * request that spawns wp-cron.php — which is traffic-dependent.
 */
add_action( 'init', static function (): void {
    if ( ! wp_next_scheduled( 'acme_reconcile_payments' ) ) {
        wp_schedule_event( time() + HOUR_IN_SECONDS, 'hourly', 'acme_reconcile_payments' );
    }

    if ( ! wp_next_scheduled( 'acme_expire_intent_holds' ) ) {
        wp_schedule_event( time() + 5 * MINUTE_IN_SECONDS, 'acme_five_minutes', 'acme_expire_intent_holds' );
    }
}, 20 );

/**
 * The callback must be idempotent. It may fire twice, may be killed
 * by a timeout halfway through, and may overlap with a manual run
 * from WP-CLI. Take a lock; make every write re-runnable.
 */
add_action( 'acme_reconcile_payments', static function (): void {
    $lock = Acme\Lock::acquire( 'reconcile_payments', 300 );

    if ( ! $lock ) {
        error_log( '[acme/cron] reconcile already running; skipping' );
        return;
    }

    try {
        Acme\Billing\Reconciler::run( batch_size: 500 );
    } finally {
        $lock->release();
    }
} );

# Replace traffic-dependent cron with a real system timer
* * * * * cd /var/www/acme && /usr/local/bin/wp cron event run --due-now --quiet --path=/var/www/acme
*/5 * * * * cd /var/www/acme && /usr/local/bin/wp cron event run --due-now --quiet --path=/var/www/acme

# Verify what is actually scheduled, and when it last ran
wp cron event list --fields=hook,next_run_gmt,recurrence --format=table
wp cron test          # one HTTP round trip to wp-cron.php
wp cron event run acme_reconcile_payments --debug

# One-off tasks: schedule a timestamped event, then forget about it
wp cron event schedule acme_export_ledger 1770000000
wp cron event run acme_export_ledger
wp cron event delete acme_export_ledger

Never rely on WP-Cron for anything time-critical or safety-critical. An event that releases a reservation, expires a discount, rotates a key or sends a legally-required notice must not wait for a visitor. Move it to a system cron invoking WP-CLI, or to a real queue worker, and keep the WordPress-scheduled copy only as a development convenience. Note also that DISABLE_WP_CRON is defined, not set to false — and that once defined, the traffic-driven spawn is suppressed entirely, so a forgotten wp-cron.php line in your system crontab means the schedule silently never runs and nothing will tell you.

The opposite end of the same lifecycle is the SHORTINIT boundary. With SHORTINIT defined before wp-load.php is required, the bootstrap returns immediately after wp-config.php. You have the path constants, the configured $wpdb connecting lazily on first query, the core class autoloader, and nothing else. Specifically absent: add_action() and the whole hook system, get_option() (which needs the object cache and the wp_start_object_cache() call in wp-settings.php), every esc_* and wp_kses() helper, wp_cache_*, current_user_can(), translations, and every plugin and theme. WP_Error and most WP_* classes are present because the autoloader can reach them; that inconsistency is exactly the trap, because an esc_html() call looks identical whether or not it is defined.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * A SHORTINIT context. Use it for health checks, deployment probes and
 * batch jobs that do not need the plugin surface. Measured at roughly
 * 4 ms versus 40–120 ms for a full bootstrap, because no plugin is
 * included and no autoloaded class is instantiated.
 *
 * NOT AVAILABLE HERE: add_action(), get_option(), wp_cache_*,
 * current_user_can(), esc_html(), __(), is_admin(), WP_Query.
 */
define( 'SHORTINIT', true );
require_once __DIR__ . '/../../public/wp-load.php';

global $wpdb;

$started = hrtime( true );

$healthy = (bool) $wpdb->get_var(
    $wpdb->prepare( 'SELECT %d', 1 )
);

$tables = (int) $wpdb->get_var(
    $wpdb->prepare(
        'SELECT COUNT(*) FROM information_schema.tables WHERE table_schema = DATABASE() AND table_name IN (%s, %s, %s)',
        $wpdb->posts,
        $wpdb->postmeta,
        $wpdb->options
    )
);

printf( "db=%s tables=%d/3 boot=%.2fms\n",
    $healthy ? 'ok' : 'fail',
    $tables,
    ( hrtime( true ) - $started ) / 1e6
);

exit( $healthy && 3 === $tables ? 0 : 1 );

Two more legitimate uses of the same mechanism are worth naming. The first is a constant-time check that the salts in wp-config.php have actually been replaced: wp-config-sample.php ships eight placeholder strings, and a production install running on them is exploitable, because the authentication cookies become forgeable. That check needs only a string comparison, so it runs fine under SHORTINIT. The second is a migration script that must run before any plugin has had a chance to read a schema it is about to change — running it under SHORTINIT guarantees no plugin is holding an open transaction or has cached the old shape.

Exercises

  1. Measure the boot sequence. Instrument your own install with the shutdown logger from §1.5 and a timer around the init hook, then record: files included at init, time to init, time to first template byte, and total request time — for an anonymous front-end request, an authenticated admin dashboard request, a REST request and a WP-CLI command. Deliverable: a table of four rows, the top ten slowest-included files by self-time from Xdebug or a sampling profiler, and one concrete change with its measured delta.
  2. Build a real cron. Replace every wp_schedule_event() registration with a system crontab that invokes wp cron event run --due-now every minute, define DISABLE_WP_CRON, and prove the traffic dependency is gone by taking the site to zero visitors for ten minutes and confirming the five-minute event still fires. Deliverable: the crontab, an Ansible or equivalent role fragment that installs it, and a monitoring check that alerts if wp cron event list shows a next-run more than two intervals in the past.
  3. Eliminate the N+1 in a metadata path. Take a real page on your estate that loads 50+ items with per-item metadata, replace the per-item get_post_meta() calls with a single meta_query or a primed update_post_meta_cache(), and measure before and after with a cold object cache. Deliverable: EXPLAIN output for both query shapes, query counts per request before and after, and a p99 measurement from a 20-concurrent-user run.
  4. Prove the SHORTINIT boundary. Write two CLI scripts against the same fixture: one that boots fully and one that uses SHORTINIT. Have each report included file count, wall time and the availability of twelve named functions. Then deliberately call esc_html() under SHORTINIT and capture the exact error. Deliverable: a written contract listing what SHORTINIT guarantees, the measured boot cost of each mode, and a lint rule that rejects SHORTINIT scripts importing a forbidden function list.
  5. Trace a real render. Use the all-hook tracer from §1.2 on a staging copy of a page with a block theme, a query loop and three plugins. Produce a complete timeline from muplugins_loaded to wp_footer, annotated with wall time per hook, and identify every hook fired more than 100 times. Deliverable: the annotated timeline, a ranked list of the five most expensive hooks with their owning callbacks, and one submitted upstream or one plugin patch for a defect you find.

2 · Hooks: Actions, Filters and the Plugin API

2.1 Actions and the add_action signature2.2 Filters and the add_filter signature2.3 Priority, ordering and recursion2.4 Dynamic hooks, hook arrays and the legacy API2.5 Removing, disabling and short-circuiting hooks2.6 Hook hygiene in production codebases

This chapter treats the hook system as the platform’s only extension contract, and treats its consequences as engineering requirements rather than as trivia. Actions notify, filters transform, both are stored in the same WP_Hook structure keyed by priority, and the difference between them is entirely a question of what the return value is allowed to mean. The argument of the chapter is that the hook graph is shared mutable state: a plugin that registers forty callbacks has made forty promises it cannot verify, and the practical discipline — declare dependencies explicitly, namespace your hook names, keep callbacks idempotent, and never assume you are the only listener — is what distinguishes an extension that survives a five-year dependency stack from one that does not.

2.1 Actions and the add_action signature

Fixes the exact contract of add_action() and explains what each of its four parameters does at registration time and at fire time.

add_action() is a thin wrapper over add_filter(): both call the same WP_Hook::add_filter() method, and the only difference is that the filter’s return value is subsequently discarded. The signature is add_action( string $hook_name, callable $callback, int $priority = 10, int $accepted_args = 1 ). Two consequences follow immediately and are routinely misunderstood. First, a value passed through do_action() is delivered by reference to the arguments, so a callback can mutate the first argument and change what every later callback at that hook receives; this is a filter behaviour leaking into actions and is a common source of inter-plugin interference. Second, $accepted_args is enforced at fire time, not registration time, and it is clamped by what do_action() was called with — registering with 4 for a hook fired with two arguments silently gives you two.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * The canonical registration. Note the docblock: WordPress has no
 * type signatures for callbacks, so the parameter contract exists
 * only in documentation and in what the static analyser can infer
 * from a @param-tagged closure assigned to a known property.
 */
final class Acme\Ledger\Webhooks {

    /** @var array<string, array<int, list<callable>>> */
    private array $listeners = [];

    public function register(): void {
        // Fires when an invoice transitions to `paid`. Two arguments:
        // the post ID and the WP_Post object. $accepted_args MUST match
        // the do_action() call or later arguments are never delivered.
        add_action( 'acme_invoice_paid', [ $this, 'on_invoice_paid' ], 10, 2 );

        // Fires before an invoice is sent to a customer. The first
        // argument is an object you are expected to mutate in place.
        add_action( 'acme_before_send_invoice', [ $this, 'prepare_payload' ], 5, 1 );
    }

    public function on_invoice_paid( int $invoice_id, WP_Post $invoice ): void {
        $this->dispatch( 'invoice.paid', [
            'id'      => $invoice_id,
            'total'   => (int) get_post_meta( $invoice_id, '_acme_total_minor', true ),
            'paid_at' => (int) get_post_meta( $invoice_id, '_acme_paid_at', true ),
        ] );
    }
}

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * What `do_action()` actually does, in order. Every one of these
 * steps is a place your code can be surprised by another plugin.
 *
 *   1. if (! did_action($hook)) do_action("all", $hook, ...$args);
 *   2. if (empty($wp_filter[$hook])) { $wp_query->main_vars...; return; }
 *   3. $wp_filter[$hook]->do_action($args);
 *        foreach ($callbacks as $priority => $cbs) {           // ordered
 *            foreach ($cbs as $cb) {
 *                if (! $this->doing_filter($cb)) {
 *                    call_user_func_array($cb['function'], $args);
 *                }
 *            }
 *        }
 *   4. do_action_ref_array("{$hook}_after", $args);  // if registered
 */
add_action( 'acme_invoice_paid', static function ( int $invoice_id, WP_Post $invoice ): void {
    // Actions run in registration order within a priority. If another
    // plugin at priority 10 throws, everything after it is skipped and
    // the exception propagates out of do_action() into core.
    do_action( 'acme_audit_log_write', [
        'actor'  => wp_get_current_user()->ID,
        'action' => 'invoice.paid',
        'object' => $invoice_id,
        'at'     => gmdate( 'c' ),
    ] );
}, 10, 2 );

Two failure modes deserve emphasis because they are invisible in testing. The first is exception propagation: WP_Hook::do_action() does not catch Throwable, so an exception thrown by a callback at priority 10 prevents every callback at priority 10 and above from running, and then propagates into whatever core code fired the action. A plugin that throws inside a save_post callback will prevent the core behaviour registered at priority 10 — including revision storage and the post_transition bookkeeping that other plugins depend on. The second is the all hook on line one: it fires before the real callbacks, which means any tracer or profiler attached to all observes the hook even when nothing is listening to it, and can itself break the request.

Wrap side-effecting callbacks in a Throwable guard, always. It costs three lines and it converts "one plugin’s bug blanks the admin" into "one plugin’s bug is logged and the request continues". This is not defensive programming for its own sake; it is the price of running inside a shared global hook graph where you do not control the other forty listeners. A callback that must not fail should catch, log with enough context to identify the request, and return; a callback that genuinely cannot continue should throw an exception the caller can distinguish, not a bare RuntimeException.

2.2 Filters and the add_filter signature

Distinguishes actions from filters precisely, and explains the pre-filter and short-circuit patterns that let a filter prevent the operation it is attached to.

A filter is a function from a value to a value. apply_filters() calls each callback in priority order, threading the return value of one into the next, and returns the final result. An action is the same call with the return discarded, distinguished in practice only by convention and by whether core reads the return value. The confusion between the two is the origin of a large class of WordPress bug, in which a developer attaches an action to a filter hook expecting their return value to be used, or attaches a filter to an action hook and is surprised that the value they returned is thrown away. The convention is absolute: a hook name that starts with pre_ filters something before core acts on it; a hook name that ends with _input is filtered before validation; one that ends with _output is filtered after escaping and must not be re-escaped.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * A complete filter chain, traced. `the_content` is fired with one
 * argument; each callback at priority 10 receives the previous one's
 * return value.
 *
 *   apply_filters('the_content', $raw)
 *     9   wptexturize        $a = $a
 *     10  wpautop             $a = $a
 *     10  do_blocks           $a = $a     (block parser, adds ~3 ms)
 *     10  shortcode_unautop   $a = $a
 *     10  wp_filter_content_tags  $a = $a
 *     10  convert_smilies     $a = $a
 *     10  wpautop             $a = $a
 *     11  capital_P_dangit    $a = $a
 *     999 acme_ld_tracking    $a = $a . "<!-- acme -->"
 */
add_filter( 'the_content', static function ( string $content ): string {
    // `in_the_loop` is a real guard: some plugins filter the_content
    // for excerpt generation and preview contexts where appending
    // tracking markup would corrupt the output.
    if ( is_admin() && ! wp_doing_ajax() ) {
        return $content;
    }

    if ( ! is_singular( 'post' ) || ! in_the_loop() || ! is_main_query() ) {
        return $content;
    }

    return sprintf(
        '%s<span class="acme-tracked" data-article="%d"></span>',
        $content,
        get_the_ID()
    );
}, 999 );

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * The `pre_` short-circuit pattern. A `pre_$hook` filter fires with
 * the current value; returning anything other than the passed-in
 * default tells core "someone else has already answered this, stop".
 * Returning the default unchanged lets core proceed normally.
 */
add_filter(
    'pre_http_request',
    static function ( $preempt, array $args, string $url ) {
        if ( 'https://api.internal.example/v1/rates' !== $url ) {
            return $preempt;   // not ours; let the API run
        }

        $cached = wp_cache_get( 'acme:fx:rates', 'acme' );

        if ( is_array( $cached ) ) {
            return $cached;    // array|WP_Error: core checks is_wp_error()
        }

        return $preempt;       // false/undefined: core performs the request
    },
    10,
    3
);

/**
 * The corresponding `_output` discipline: `{$hook}_after` receives
 * the final value and is applied LAST. Anything it produces has not
 * been escaped, so it must escape itself.
 */
add_filter( 'redirect_location', static function ( string $location, int $status ): string {
    $allowed = wp_parse_url( home_url( '/' ), PHP_URL_HOST );

    if ( $allowed && wp_parse_url( $location, PHP_URL_HOST ) === $allowed ) {
        return $location;
    }

    error_log( sprintf( '[acme/security] blocked off-site redirect: %s', $location ) );

    return home_url( '/' );
}, 10, 2 );

Note what the second block does not do: it does not sanitise the value before returning it, and it does not validate it with wp_validate_redirect(). A filter attached at the end of the chain receives a value that core has already validated for core’s purposes, and the correct response to a value that fails your own additional policy is to substitute your own safe value, not to escape the attacker’s. Escaping produces a string that is still an attacker’s URL, merely unparseable in a different way. This is the general shape of the correct response to a filter value you do not trust: decide what the safe value is, and return that.

Hook name patternMeaningReturn value that changes control flowExample
pre_$nameShort-circuit before core actsAnything ≠ the passed defaultpre_http_request
$nameTransform a valueEvery callback’s returnthe_content
${$name}_afterInspect or adjust the final valueThe final valuerest_pre_dispatch
$name_inputSanitise before validationThe validated inputwp_insert_post_data
$name_outputFilter an escaped/output valueMust be escaped again by youget_the_excerpt
has_$nameBoolean capability probetrue to claim the capabilityhas_custom_logo
rest_$namespace_$routeShort-circuit a REST responseWP_REST_Response or WP_Errorrest_pre_dispatch

2.3 Priority, ordering and recursion

Explains how callbacks at the same priority are ordered, why the order is a documented accident rather than a contract, and how recursion is prevented.

Within a single priority, callbacks run in registration order, and registration order is activation order, which is a property of how the install was configured rather than of intent. There is one exception that is genuinely specified: WP_Hook sorts each priority’s list by accepted_args in descending order, so callbacks expecting more arguments run before callbacks expecting fewer within the same priority. The practical implication is narrow and easy to get wrong — if you need to run after another plugin at priority 10 and cannot coordinate with it, you have three real options: use a later priority, use a {$hook}_after filter if one exists, or register on a hook that fires later in the lifecycle entirely. Guessing that "later registration wins" is a bet on a detail of core’s data structure.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * The priority landscape you are actually playing in. Choosing 10
 * and hoping is not a strategy; these are the numbers core uses and
 * the numbers plugins actually occupy.
 *
 *   0      remove_all_actions / very early guards, security
 *   1      register_taxonomy (block-style content)
 *   4      `post_created`/`post_updated` (WP 5.6+ taxonomy writes)
 *   5      register_taxonomy (core) — reserved
 *   8      init for late-loading config
 *   9      user_register, register_taxonomy_for_object_type
 *   10     THE DEFAULT: save_post, the_content, wp_footer, enqueue
 *   20     admin_menu, admin_init, admin_enqueue_scripts
 *   30     template_redirect
 *   99     near-last, used by many "final" integrations
 *   999    last, used by analytics and by core for late filters
 */
add_filter( 'acme_invoice_amount', static fn ( int $amount ): int => $amount * 2, 20 );

/**
 * Priority is per hook, not global. Two filters on unrelated hooks
 * at the same priority have nothing to do with each other; the
 * sort is a property of the WP_Hook instance for that name.
 *
 * Deterministic ordering, when you genuinely must run after an
 * uncooperative plugin: wrap their output rather than their slot.
 */
add_filter( 'the_content', static function ( string $content ): string {
    // Extract and re-inject, which is order-independent.
    if ( preg_match_all( '/<div[^>]*class="[^"]*acme-lazy[^"]*"[^>]*>/i', $content, $m ) ) {
        $content = str_replace( $m[0], '', $content );
        $content .= '<script src="' . esc_url( ACME_PLUGIN_URL . 'assets/lazy.js' ) . '"></script>';
    }

    return $content;
}, 10 );

Recursion is guarded structurally rather than by a flag you have to remember. WP_Hook::apply_filters() and do_action() both check doing_filter( $callback_id ) before invoking, and push the id onto the running stack, so a filter that calls the same filter it is attached to does not infinitely recurse — the nested call simply skips that one callback.
