The trap is the inverse: a callback that legitimately needs to trigger the same filter again for a different value will be silently skipped, producing output that is subtly wrong rather than a crash. If you need to re-enter a filter chain deliberately, call the underlying function on a different hook name and adapt, or invoke the callback directly.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * The recursion guard, demonstrated. A filter attached to
 * `acme_compute_total` that itself calls `apply_filters` on the
 * same hook will be SKIPPED for the nested call, because
 * WP_Hook::doing_filter() is true for that callback id.
 */
add_filter( 'acme_compute_total', static function ( array $lines, string $currency ): int {
    $sum = 0;

    foreach ( $lines as $line ) {
        // Correct: dispatch a DIFFERENT hook for the recursive need.
        $sum += (int) apply_filters( 'acme_compute_line_total', $line['total'], $currency );
    }

    // Incorrect — returns the unfiltered 0 for the nested call:
    // $sum += (int) apply_filters( 'acme_compute_total', $line, $currency );

    return $sum;
}, 10, 2 );

/**
 * `doing_action()` / `doing_filter()` answer "are we inside this
 * hook right now", which is how you prevent a loop of your own:
 * save_post -> update_option -> save_option -> save_post.
 */
add_action( 'updated_option', static function ( string $option ): void {
    if ( 'acme_settings' !== $option ) {
        return;
    }

    if ( doing_action( 'acme_settings_saved' ) ) {
        return;   // re-entrancy guard, explicit and readable
    }

    do_action( 'acme_settings_saved', get_option( 'acme_settings' ) );
}, 10, 1 );

Never rely on being the only callback. At priority 10 on the_content, the_excerpt, save_post and wp_footer you are one of dozens. Assume the value you receive has already been modified by something you have not read, assume your return value will be further modified, and assume some other callback may have short-circuited before yours was called. If your filter’s correctness depends on an unmodified input, check for it and no-op — do not attempt to repair it. This is also the reason filters should be total functions: given any input, return something valid, because you do not get to reject the call.

2.4 Dynamic hooks, hook arrays and the legacy API

Covers the parts of the hook API that are not safe for application code — direct $wp_filter manipulation, the $_wp_filter_backcompat shim, and hook names assembled at runtime.

Some hook names are constructed at call time: "{$status}_transition", "save_post_{$post_type}", "{$taxonomy}_add_form_fields", "wp_ajax_{$action}", "rest_api_init"-time namespaces, and the block dynamic filters. These are not a special mechanism; they are ordinary names that happen to be built with string interpolation, and the consequences are entirely about invalidation. A hook name assembled from user input is a hook name an attacker controls, which means an attacker can add and remove listeners to it, and more practically, that a statically analysed list of your hook names is incomplete. A filter whose name includes a post ID, for example, cannot be removed with a single remove_filter() call for the whole site.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * Dynamic hook names, and the invalidation consequences of each.
 * There is no wildcard removal: you must enumerate every name.
 */

// 1. Status transitions. Fires for every status change, including to
//    statuses you do not handle, and fires ONCE per transition in
//    WP_Post::save_post(); auto-drafts fire `auto-draft` too.
add_action(
    'acme_invoice_' . 'refunded',      // built, but effectively static here
    [ $this, 'on_refunded' ],
    10,
    1
);

// 2. Per-post hooks. Now unbounded in number, so removal needs a loop.
foreach ( $invoice_ids as $id ) {
    add_action( "acme_invoice_viewed_{$id}", [ $this, 'record_view' ], 10, 1 );
}

// ... to remove all of them again, you must know the IDs:
foreach ( $invoice_ids as $id ) {
    remove_action( "acme_invoice_viewed_{$id}", [ $this, 'record_view' ], 10 );
}

// 3. Blocks. `render_block` and `render_block_{$name}` are the two
//    levels; the per-block form is what makes selective removal cheap.
add_filter( 'render_block_acme/invoice-summary', [ $this, 'render_summary' ], 10, 3 );

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * The legacy hook-array API. `add_filter()` accepts either a plain
 * callable or a two-element array. The two-element form is retained
 * because `remove_filter()` identifies a callback by its "id", which
 * for an array form is a string built from the class and method:
 *
 *   add_filter('the_content', 'Acme\Legacy_Widget::render', 10, 1);
 *   // accepted_args defaults to 1 and the string is not callable
 *   // in modern PHP — WordPress normalises it via _wp_filter_build_unique_id.
 *
 * It is still supported, and it still works, but it is not callable
 * syntax, it is invisible to static analysis, and it is a
 * deprecated pattern in every WordPress coding standard since 0.72.
 */
add_filter( 'acme_invoice_pdf', 'Acme\Legacy\Pdf_Renderer::render', 10, 1 );

/**
 * To remove it you must pass a value that produces the same unique
 * id. Passing the array form works because _wp_filter_build_unique_id()
 * normalises both to the same string.
 */
remove_filter( 'acme_invoice_pdf', 'Acme\Legacy\Pdf_Renderer::render', 10 );
remove_filter( 'acme_invoice_pdf', [ 'Acme\Legacy\Pdf_Renderer', 'render' ], 10 );

/**
 * Closures are supported and are the modern equivalent, but a
 * closure cannot be removed by anyone else — including you, later —
 * because the unique id includes the file and line it was declared
 * on. Use a named method or a first-class callable.
 */
$filter = static fn ( string $html ): string => $html;
add_filter( 'acme_invoice_pdf', $filter, 10, 1 );
remove_filter( 'acme_invoice_pdf', $filter, 10 );

The other legacy surface worth naming is $_wp_filter_backcompat, a global array that core populates during do_action() and apply_filters(). Do not read it. It exists so that a handful of very old plugins keep working, it is not part of any contract, and reading it gives you a snapshot from an arbitrary point in the chain rather than the current state. The supported introspection API is has_filter(), has_action(), did_action(), doing_action() and current_filter(); use those.

2.5 Removing, disabling and short-circuiting hooks

Documents the removal API precisely, including the identity rules that make remove_action() silently fail when they are not met.

A callback is identified by three things: the hook name, the unique id of the callable, and the priority. The unique id is produced by _wp_filter_build_unique_id() and is the file and line for closures, Class::method for arrays and strings, and the function name for plain functions. Two consequences catch people constantly. A closure declared on line 42 cannot be removed by a closure declared on line 99, even if the code is byte-identical, because the line number is part of the identity. And a method on an object instance is identified by class and method only — so removing a filter registered by one instance also removes it for every other instance of the same class, which is occasionally the intent and frequently a surprise.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * The complete removal surface. Note that every one of these is
 * immediate, global and irreversible for the remainder of the
 * request — there is no "restore later".
 */
has_action( 'the_content', 'wpautop' );                     // int priority | false
has_filter( 'the_content', 'my_plugin_tag_stripper' );
remove_action( 'wp_head', 'wp_generator' );                  // single callback
remove_filter( 'the_content', [ __CLASS__, 'method' ], 10 ); // array form
remove_all_actions( 'wp_head' );                             // nukes the hook
remove_all_filters( 'the_content', 15 );                    // only priority 15 up
remove_all_actions( 'wp_head', 5 );                          // only priority 5 down
remove_action( 'wp_head', 'wp_generator', 10 );             // 4-arg legacy form

/**
 * The check-then-remove idiom, which is the only safe form. has_action()
 * returns the priority as an int, which is truthy even at priority 0,
 * so `if ( has_action(...) )` is correct and `if ( false !== ... )`
 * is more explicit. Never call remove_action() unconditionally: it is
 * a no-op, but it hides the fact that your assumption was wrong.
 */
$priority = has_action( 'wp_head', 'wp_generator' );

if ( false !== $priority ) {
    remove_action( 'wp_head', 'wp_generator', $priority );
}

/**
 * Disabling a block of third-party behaviour. `remove_all_actions` on
 * a hook other plugins also use is a hostile act; scope it to hooks
 * that are unambiguously yours, and document why in a changelog.
 */
add_filter( 'acme_disable_third_party', static function ( bool $disable ) use ( $callbacks ): bool {
    if ( ! $disable ) {
        return $disable;
    }

    foreach ( $callbacks as [ $hook, $callback, $priority ] ) {
        remove_action( $hook, $callback, $priority );
    }

    return true;
}, 10, 1 );

A separate mechanism deserves its own name, because it is frequently confused with removal: short-circuiting. A pre_$hook filter lets you prevent an operation from happening at all without touching the callbacks that implement it. For HTTP requests, pre_http_request lets a plugin serve from cache; for revisions, wp_save_posts_revision_post_has_changed lets a plugin suppress revisions for a post type; for the block editor, block_editor_settings_all can replace the entire settings payload. Short-circuiting is the polite way to override core behaviour, because it does not depend on the identity of another plugin’s callbacks and it survives that plugin updating its internals.

TechniqueRequires knowingSurvives plugin updatesRemovable by targetBlast radius
remove_action()Exact callback id and priorityNoNoExact
remove_all_actions()Hook name onlyYesNoTotal for that hook
add_filter( 'pre_$hook' )NothingYesNoTotal for that hook
Declaring a compatibility helperThe behaviour, not the callbackYesn/aBehaviour-scoped
Pluggable function overrideCore’s implementationYesNoGlobal
 
<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * Compatibility shim, the professional alternative to remove_all_*.
 * Express intent in terms of behaviour, so it keeps working when the
 * plugin that provided the behaviour changes its internals.
 */
add_action( 'init', static function (): void {
    if ( ! Compatibility\Remove_Excerpt_Generator::should_run() ) {
        return;
    }

    // Behaviour, not identity: "no empty auto-generated excerpts".
    add_filter( 'excerpt_length', static fn (): int => 45, 999 );
    add_filter( 'excerpt_more', static fn (): string => '', 999 );
    add_filter( 'wp_trim_excerpt', static fn ( string $excerpt ): string => $excerpt, 999 );
}, 5 );

final class Compatibility\Remove_Excerpt_Generator {

    /** Hooks this shim is known to conflict with, by behaviour. */
    private const TARGETS = [
        'auto_generated_excerpts' => [ 'wp_trim_excerpt' ],
    ];

    public static function should_run(): bool {
        // Opt out on request: `?acme_no_compat=1` for debugging only.
        if ( isset( $_GET['acme_no_compat'] ) ) {   // nonce checked at the route, not here
            return false;
        }

        return (bool) apply_filters( 'acme_run_compat_shims', true );
    }
}

Prefer behaviour shims to identity removal, always. A shim that adds a pre_ filter or re-registers a callback keeps working when the plugin you are compensating for is updated, when it is deactivated, and when a second copy of it appears. An identity removal silently stops working the first time that plugin renames a method, which is the worst possible failure mode: the conflict returns with no error and no log entry, usually in a release where the only change was a patch bump. If you must remove by identity, write a regression test that fails when the target plugin is upgraded and the hook can no longer be found.

2.6 Hook hygiene in production codebases

Sets out the conventions that keep a hook-heavy plugin maintainable, and provides the introspection tooling to audit a graph you did not write.

Hygiene in a hook-based codebase means four things. Namespaces: every hook name you own is prefixed with a slug that is unique to you, and you never fire a hook that begins with a prefix you do not own. Context: every action fires enough arguments for a listener to do something useful without a second query, because a listener that has to re-fetch the object you already had will do it badly. Determinism: callbacks are idempotent, because they will run twice, and reentrant-safe, because they may run inside themselves. Observability: the set of hooks your code registers is enumerable, so it can be asserted in a test rather than discovered in production.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

namespace Acme\Ledger\Hooks;

/**
 * A registrar. Its value is not the registration itself — it is that
 * the full set of hooks a plugin adds is enumerable from one object,
 * so it can be diffed in a test and dumped in a support ticket.
 *
 * @implements \IteratorAggregate<string, list<array{0: string, 1: callable, 2: int, 3: int}>>
 */
final class Registrar implements \IteratorAggregate {

    /** @var array<string, list<array{0: string, 1: callable, 2: int, 3: int}>> */
    private array $registered = [];

    public function action( string $hook, callable $cb, int $priority = 10, int $args = 1 ): self {
        add_action( $hook, $cb, $priority, $args );
        $this->registered[ $hook ][] = [ 'action', $cb, $priority, $args ];

        return $this;
    }

    public function filter( string $hook, callable $cb, int $priority = 10, int $args = 1 ): self {
        add_filter( $hook, $cb, $priority, $args );
        $this->registered[ $hook ][] = [ 'filter', $cb, $priority, $args ];

        return $this;
    }

    public function getIterator(): \Traversable {
        return new \ArrayIterator( $this->registered );
    }

    public function deregister_all(): void {
        foreach ( $this->registered as $hook => $entries ) {
            foreach ( $entries as [ $type, $cb, $priority ] ) {
                'action' === $type
                    ? remove_action( $hook, $cb, $priority )
                    : remove_filter( $hook, $cb, $priority );
            }
        }

        $this->registered = [];
    }
}

# Dump the live hook graph from a running site, for triage.
wp eval '
global $wp_filter;

$out = [];
foreach ( $wp_filter as $hook => $wp_hook ) {
    $priorities = $wp_hook->callbacks ?? [];
    if ( 0 === count( $priorities ) ) {
        continue;
    }
    ksort( $priorities );
    foreach ( $priorities as $priority => $cbs ) {
        foreach ( $cbs as $id => $entry ) {
            $out[] = sprintf( "%s\t%d\t%s", $hook, $priority, $id );
        }
    }
}

sort( $out );
echo implode( "\n", $out ), "\n";
' > /var/log/wp/hook-graph.tsv

# Which callbacks are attached to a single hook, ordered.
wp eval 'print_r( $GLOBALS["wp_filter"]->get_hooks()["the_content"] ?? [] );'

# Find every callback that came from a file matching a pattern.
grep -c "wp-content/plugins/some-plugin" /var/log/wp/hook-graph.tsv

Two tooling notes. First, the graph dump is stable enough to commit as a golden file: sort it, normalise the class names, and diff it on every release. A change in the number of callbacks on the_content or pre_get_posts between two releases is a genuine behavioural change and should be reviewed. Second, the introspection you have available at runtime is not the same as what a static analyser can see. A PHPStan rule that flags any call to add_action() with a hook name that is not a string literal will catch the dynamic-hook case at build time, which is strictly better than discovering it from a log.

A hook name you do not own is an API you have not agreed to. Firing save_post is not extension, it is participation. Firing acme_invoice_paid is extension. Code that fires hooks named after another plugin’s prefix will break silently when that plugin is deactivated, and code that fires a do_action() with no arguments gives listeners nothing to work with, which is how "our integration doesn’t work" tickets are written. Publish your hook names, publish their argument signatures, and treat any change to either as a breaking change requiring a major version bump — the same discipline you would apply to a public function signature.

Exercises

  1. Build a hook graph differ. Using the wp eval dump in §2.6, write a PHP script that captures the graph for a given URI, normalises callback ids, and produces a sorted TSV. Run it across two releases of your estate and produce a unified diff. Deliverable: the script, the diff, and a written explanation of every added or removed callback with the plugin version that caused it.
  2. Refactor callbacks into a registrar. Take a plugin that registers callbacks from ten separate methods and move them behind the Registrar class. Preserve the exact hook names, priorities and argument counts. Deliverable: a golden-file test asserting the before-and-after graph is byte-identical, plus a deregister_all() test proving the plugin can be unloaded cleanly in a test process.
  3. Write a compatibility shim and prove it survives. Pick a real conflict in your estate, implement it as a pre_ filter or a behaviour re-registration, and demonstrate that it keeps working when the conflicting plugin is updated to a newer minor version. Deliverable: the shim, a PHPUnit test that fails if the conflict reappears, and a changelog entry explaining the shim’s removal condition.
  4. Harden every callback against exceptions. Wrap all side-effecting callbacks in your plugin in a Throwable guard that logs request context and returns. Inject a failure into a dependency and demonstrate that the surrounding request still completes and the rest of the hook chain still runs. Deliverable: a before/after test showing the difference, plus a log format that identifies the failing callback from a log line alone.
  5. Prove the priority assumptions. Write a test suite that registers a sentinel callback at every priority you use and asserts your callback ran at the position you intended relative to core’s own callbacks on the_content, save_post and wp_footer. Then deliberately change one priority and show the suite failing. Deliverable: the suite, the recorded callback order for each of the three hooks on a stock 6.9 install with no plugins, and a written list of every hook where your code’s order is load-bearing.

3 · The Database Schema: Posts, Taxonomy, Users and Options

3.1 The posts table3.2 postmeta and the EAV problem3.3 Taxonomy: terms, taxonomies and relationships3.4 Users, usermeta and capabilities3.5 Options, transients and the autoload problem3.6 Comments, meta tables and multisite

This chapter reads the schema as a designer would, because every query WordPress generates is a consequence of a decision made in 2004. The argument is that wp_posts is a well-indexed relation and wp_postmeta is an entity-attribute-value store bolted onto it, and that the mismatch between the two is the root of most WordPress performance problems. It shows the actual DDL for all twelve core tables, explains which indexes exist and which do not, and quantifies what a metadata query costs when the table is unindexed in the way you need. The chapter also treats wp_options as a cache layer that has been promoted to a primary datastore, which is why autoload bloat is the most reliably diagnosable performance defect in the platform.

3.1 The posts table

Gives the exact wp_posts structure and explains why the four indexes on it are sufficient for the main query and inadequate for everything else.

wp_posts is the centre of the model: posts, pages, attachments, revisions, navigation items, custom post types, global reusable blocks (wp_block), block templates (wp_template) and block template parts (wp_template_part) are all rows in it. Twenty-three columns, four indexes, and a design assumption that the overwhelming majority of queries will be of the form "published posts of type X, ordered by date, paginated" — which is exactly what type_status_date is for. Every deviation from that shape is a scan.

CREATE TABLE `wp_posts` (
  `ID`                   bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL AUTO_INCREMENT,
  `post_author`          bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL DEFAULT '0',
  `post_date`            datetime     NOT NULL DEFAULT '0000-00-00 00:00:00',
  `post_date_gmt`        datetime     NOT NULL DEFAULT '0000-00-00 00:00:00',
  `post_content`         longtext     NOT NULL,
  `post_title`           text         NOT NULL,
  `post_excerpt`         text         NOT NULL,
  `post_status`          varchar(20)  NOT NULL DEFAULT 'publish',
  `comment_status`       varchar(20)  NOT NULL DEFAULT 'open',
  `ping_status`          varchar(20)  NOT NULL DEFAULT 'open',
  `post_password`        varchar(255) NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
  `post_name`            varchar(200) NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
  `to_ping`              text         NOT NULL,
  `pinged`               text         NOT NULL,
  `post_modified`        datetime     NOT NULL DEFAULT '0000-00-00 00:00:00',
  `post_modified_gmt`    datetime     NOT NULL DEFAULT '0000-00-00 00:00:00',
  `post_content_filtered` longtext    NOT NULL,
  `post_parent`          bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL DEFAULT '0',
  `guid`                 varchar(255) NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
  `menu_order`           int(11)      NOT NULL DEFAULT '0',
  `post_type`            varchar(20)  NOT NULL DEFAULT 'post',
  `post_mime_type`       varchar(100) NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
  `comment_count`        bigint(20)   NOT NULL DEFAULT '0',
  PRIMARY KEY (`ID`),
  KEY `post_name`    (`post_name`(191)),
  KEY `type_status_date` (`post_type`,`post_status`,`post_date`,`ID`),
  KEY `post_parent`  (`post_parent`),
  KEY `post_author`  (`post_author`)
) ENGINE=InnoDB DEFAULT CHARSET=utf8mb4 COLLATE=utf8mb4_unicode_520_ci;

Three things in that DDL matter more than the column list. The post_status column is varchar(20) and gains values over time — auto-draft, inherit, request-pending, request-accepted, trash, future, private — so any ENUM you have ever been tempted to create over it will break. The post_type column is varchar(20), which is why register_post_type() warns at 20 characters and why long custom type names are truncated in the database while continuing to work in PHP. And post_content_filtered exists for the deprecated the_content "more" tag and is always empty on modern sites, so it is pure index-free table weight; on a site with a large body of long posts, reclaiming it with ALTER TABLE wp_posts MODIFY post_content_filtered longtext NOT NULL is a legitimate and frequently forgotten optimisation.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * The canonical single-post write. `wp_insert_post()` is the only
 * supported way to create a post row, because it fires the hooks
 * (`save_post`, `wp_insert_post`, `post_updated`) that revisions,
 * slugs, term writes, cache invalidation and search indexing all
 * depend on. Direct INSERTs bypass every one of them.
 */
$post_id = wp_insert_post( wp_slash( [
    'post_type'      => 'acme_invoice',
    'post_status'    => 'draft',
    'post_title'     => 'Invoice 2026-0042',
    'post_content'   => '',
    'post_author'    => $user_id,
    'post_parent'    => $order_id,
    'menu_order'     => 0,
    'meta_input'     => [
        '_acme_total_minor' => 125000,
        '_acme_currency'    => 'GBP',
    ],
    'tax_input'      => [ 'acme_region' => [ 'uk', 'ie' ] ],
    'comment_status' => 'closed',
] ), true );

if ( is_wp_error( $post_id ) ) {
    error_log( '[acme/ledger] ' . $post_id->get_error_code() . ': ' . $post_id->get_error_message() );

    return $post_id;
}

Two details in that call are load-bearing. wp_slash() exists because WordPress adds slashes to superglobals; data from $_POST is already slashed, and passing it through wp_insert_post() unslashes, so slashed input is required. Passing true as the second argument makes the function return a WP_Error instead of 0 on failure — always do this, because the alternative failure mode is a silent zero and an invoice that appears to save and does not. And meta_input goes through update_post_meta() for each pair, which means the metadata is written after the post row and inside the same save_post cycle, so listeners on save_post at priority 10 will see it and listeners on wp_after_insert_post (5.6+) will too.

TableRows holdKeyed byIndexesGrowth driver
wp_postsEvery content object and revisionID4Revisions; autosaves
wp_postmetaAll per-post key/value pairsmeta_id2, neither compositeEvery meta key you add
wp_term_relationshipsObject ↔ term links(object, term_taxonomy)2Tagging, categories
wp_term_taxonomyTerm context and countsterm_taxonomy_id3Taxonomy count drift
wp_termsTerm names and slugsterm_id2Tags, especially user tags
wp_optionsSettings, caches, cron, transientsoption_name2Autoload size, transients
wp_usersAccountsID4Account count
wp_usermetaCapabilities, settings, sessionsumeta_id2Meta sprawl per user
wp_commentsComments and pingbackscomment_ID4Spam, moderation queue
wp_commentmetaPer-comment key/value pairsmeta_id2Rating and plugin metadata

The integer display widths are deprecated, not removed. Core still emits bigint(20) and int(11), which MySQL 8.0.19 deprecated and MySQL 8.4 still accepts with a warning. If your schema comparison tooling flags these as drift, that is a false positive; do not "fix" them by rewriting every table, because a needless ALTER TABLE on wp_posts rebuilds a multi-gigabyte table and holds a metadata lock. Core’s dbDelta() ignores display-width differences for this reason.

3.2 postmeta and the EAV problem

Explains why the metadata tables are the root cause of most WordPress query problems, shows the query plan they produce, and gives the join and IN patterns that avoid it.

wp_postmeta has two single-column indexes: post_id and a 191-character prefix of meta_key. There is no composite index, no index on meta_value, and — the detail that surprises people — no index on the pair that metadata filtering actually needs. A query of the form "posts where meta_key = 'x' AND meta_value = 'y'" can use meta_key to narrow to every row ever written with that key, and must then evaluate meta_value row by row. If your key appears on a million rows, that is a million comparisons in the storage engine for every request, on top of the join back to wp_posts.

CREATE TABLE `wp_postmeta` (
  `meta_id`    bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL AUTO_INCREMENT,
  `post_id`    bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL DEFAULT '0',
  `meta_key`   varchar(255)           DEFAULT NULL,
  `meta_value` longtext,
  PRIMARY KEY (`meta_id`),
  KEY `post_id`  (`post_id`),
  KEY `meta_key` (`meta_key`(191))
) ENGINE=InnoDB DEFAULT CHARSET=utf8mb4 COLLATE=utf8mb4_unicode_520_ci;

-- Query plan for the canonical metadata filter. Note `Using`
-- and `Using where`; note the estimated rows examined in the
-- `rows` column, which is the number that matters.
EXPLAIN
SELECT p.ID
  FROM wp_postmeta pm
  INNER JOIN wp_posts p ON p.ID = pm.post_id
 WHERE pm.meta_key   = '_acme_status'
   AND pm.meta_value = 'awaiting_payment'
   AND p.post_type    = 'acme_invoice'
   AND p.post_status  = 'publish'
 ORDER BY p.post_date DESC
 LIMIT 20;

-- id  select_type table  type  possible_keys key      key_len ref                 rows  Extra
--  1  SIMPLE      pm     ref   meta_key       meta_key  192    const               41230  Using where
--  1  SIMPLE      p      eq_ref PRIMARY       PRIMARY   8      pm.post_id           1  Using where

The optimizer has chosen meta_key as the driving index, which narrows 41,000 candidate rows, and then must evaluate meta_value on each of them and probe wp_posts for each. Nothing in the schema prevents that; it is a direct consequence of modelling variable attributes as rows. The three remedies, in increasing order of effort, are: reduce the number of rows carrying the hot key, add a composite index yourself, or stop storing the hot dimension in metadata at all. Converting a frequently-filtered, low-cardinality field into a taxonomy or a custom post status removes it from the EAV table entirely and makes it indexed, relational and cacheable.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

global $wpdb;

/**
 * Remedy 2 — one query instead of N. The anti-pattern is a loop:
 *
 *     foreach ( $post_ids as $id ) {
 *         $total = get_post_meta( $id, '_acme_total_minor', true );
 *     }
 *
 * which is N queries cold and N hash lookups warm. The correct shape
 * is a single query constrained by IN, which lets the engine use the
 * post_id index range-by-range and return everything in one round trip.
 */
$ids   = [ 12, 19, 41, 88, 103 ];
$placeholders = implode( ', ', array_fill( 0, count( $ids ), '%d' ) );

$sql = $wpdb->prepare(
    "SELECT pm.post_id, pm.meta_key, pm.meta_value
       FROM {$wpdb->postmeta} pm
      WHERE pm.post_id IN ( {$placeholders} )
        AND pm.meta_key IN ( %s, %s )",
    ...$ids,
    '_acme_total_minor',
    '_acme_currency'
);

$rows = $wpdb->get_results( $sql );

$by_post = [];
foreach ( $rows as $row ) {
    $by_post[ (int) $row->post_id ][ $row->meta_key ] = $row->meta_value;
}

printf( "%d rows, %d queries\n", count( $rows ), $wpdb->num_queries );

Do not add an index on meta_value. meta_value is longtext, so an index on it requires a prefix length, and MySQL will then use that index only for LIKE 'prefix%' patterns and equality on values shorter than the prefix. The result is a large index that the optimizer mis-estimates and that changes on every metadata write. The correct fix for "my metadata filter is slow" is almost never an index on the value; it is a taxonomy, a dedicated column, or a denormalised column maintained in the same write that maintains the meta.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * Remedy 3b — a real column. Add a dedicated, indexed table for
 * anything you filter, sort or aggregate on, and keep the meta key
 * as a cache of it for template convenience.
 */
final class Acme\Ledger\Infrastructure\Schema {

    public const VERSION_OPTION = 'acme_ledger_schema_version';
    public const VERSION        = '2026_04_01';

    public static function install(): void {
        global $wpdb;

        require_once ABSPATH . 'wp-admin/includes/upgrade.php';

        $table = $wpdb->prefix . 'acme_invoice';

        dbDelta( "CREATE TABLE {$table} (
            id             bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL AUTO_INCREMENT,
            invoice_id     bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL,
            customer_id    bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
            status         varchar(32)  NOT NULL DEFAULT 'draft',
            total_minor    bigint(20)   NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
            currency       char(3)      NOT NULL DEFAULT 'GBP',
            due_date       date         DEFAULT NULL,
            updated_at     datetime     NOT NULL,
            PRIMARY KEY  (id),
            UNIQUE KEY invoice_id (invoice_id),
            KEY status_due (status, due_date),
            KEY customer_status (customer_id, status)
        );" );

        update_option( self::VERSION_OPTION, self::VERSION, false );
    }
}

A custom table is not a decision to take lightly. You take on schema migrations, you lose the object cache priming that WP_Query gives you for free, you lose the revisions and the autosave, you lose the relationships, and you must decide what happens to rows whose post is trashed. In exchange you get a real index, a real constraint, real aggregation and a query plan you control. For a handful of fields on a few thousand rows, metadata with a taxonomy for the filtering dimension is entirely sufficient; for a ledger that has to produce a financial report over a million rows, a custom table is not optional.

3.3 Taxonomy: terms, taxonomies and relationships

Shows the three-table taxonomy model, explains why the count column is a denormalised cache, and clarifies the difference between a term and a term taxonomy relationship.

Taxonomy is the part of the schema that is genuinely relational, and the part that most often gets misunderstood. A term is a name and a slug. A taxonomy assignment is a row in wp_term_taxonomy that pairs a term with a taxonomy and records the parent, description and a denormalised count. The link between a term and an object is a third row in wp_term_relationships with an optional term_order. The same term can therefore exist in several taxonomies, and that is why almost every term query must join all three tables.

CREATE TABLE `wp_terms` (
  `term_id`     bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL AUTO_INCREMENT,
  `name`        varchar(200) NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
  `slug`        varchar(200) NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
  `term_group`  bigint(10)   NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  PRIMARY KEY (`term_id`),
  KEY `slug` (`slug`(191)),
  KEY `name` (`name`(191))
) ENGINE=InnoDB DEFAULT CHARSET=utf8mb4 COLLATE=utf8mb4_unicode_520_ci;

CREATE TABLE `wp_term_taxonomy` (
  `term_taxonomy_id` bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL AUTO_INCREMENT,
  `term_id`          bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  `taxonomy`         varchar(32)  NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
  `description`      longtext     NOT NULL,
  `parent`           bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  `count`            bigint(20)   NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  PRIMARY KEY (`term_taxonomy_id`),
  KEY `taxonomy` (`taxonomy`),
  UNIQUE KEY `term_id_taxonomy` (`term_id`,`taxonomy`),
  KEY `term_taxonomy_id_parent_count` (`term_taxonomy_id`,`parent`,`count`)
) ENGINE=InnoDB DEFAULT CHARSET=utf8mb4 COLLATE=utf8mb4_unicode_520_ci;

CREATE TABLE `wp_term_relationships` (
  `object_id`        bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  `term_taxonomy_id` bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  `term_order`       int(11)      NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  PRIMARY KEY (`object_id`,`term_taxonomy_id`),
  KEY `term_taxonomy_id` (`term_taxonomy_id`)
) ENGINE=InnoDB DEFAULT CHARSET=utf8mb4 COLLATE=utf8mb4_unicode_520_ci;

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * A taxonomy that behaves. Four arguments do most of the work:
 *
 *  hierarchical    -> uses the `parent` column and `count`; a
 *                     non-hierarchical taxonomy ignores both
 *  public          -> false hides it from the REST API and the UI
 *  show_in_rest    -> required for the block editor's term UI
 *  rewrite         -> slug/rewrite_base control the permalinks
 */
add_action( 'init', static function (): void {
    register_taxonomy( 'acme_region', [ 'acme_invoice' ], [
        'labels'            => [ 'name' => 'Regions', 'singular_name' => 'Region' ],
        'public'            => true,
        'hierarchical'      => true,
        'show_ui'           => true,
        'show_in_rest'      => true,
        'show_admin_column' => true,
        'meta_box_cb'       => false,   // no custom meta box needed
        'query_var'         => 'acme_region',
        'rewrite'           => [ 'slug' => 'region', 'with_front' => false ],
        'capabilities'      => [
            'manage_terms' => 'manage_acme_regions',
            'edit_terms'   => 'edit_acme_regions',
            'delete_terms' => 'delete_acme_regions',
            'assign_terms' => 'assign_acme_regions',
        ],
    ] );

    register_post_type( 'acme_invoice', [
        'label'        => 'Invoices',
        'public'       => true,
        'has_archive'  => true,
        'rewrite'      => [ 'slug' => 'invoice', 'with_front' => false ],
        'menu_icon'    => 'dashicons-receipt-alt',
        'supports'     => [ 'title', 'editor', 'revisions', 'custom-fields' ],
        'show_in_rest' => true,
        'rest_base'    => 'invoices',
        'capability_type' => [ 'acme_invoice', 'acme_invoices' ],
        'map_meta_cap' => true,
    ] );
}, 5 );

Two arguments in that call carry more weight than they appear to. map_meta_cap => true is what makes meta capabilities work: with it, edit_post is composed from the post type’s edit_posts, edit_others_posts, publish_posts and read_private_posts, so a role granted only edit_acme_invoices can edit invoices and nothing else. Without it, WordPress falls back to the literal meta capability name in the roles table, and a role granted edit_acme_invoice (singular) will be able to edit every post type in the site. And the four capabilities keys on the taxonomy are what a custom role must be granted explicitly — an administrator has all of them by construction, but an editor does not have manage_acme_regions unless it is given, which is a common source of "editors cannot see my taxonomy" reports.

3.4 Users, usermeta and capabilities

Shows how roles and capabilities are stored, explains the mapping layer between them and meta capabilities, and identifies the query that answers "can this user do this".

Users are a single table with four unique indexes on login, nicename, email and activation key. Everything about how a user behaves — their capabilities, their locale, their dashboard preferences, their session tokens, their comment author URL — is in wp_usermeta, and the two keys that matter are wp_capabilities and wp_user_level. Roles are not a table. They are a set of named capability arrays stored in the wp_user_roles option, and a user’s capabilities are a serialised PHP array in wp_usermeta. This means a role change is an option write, not a query, and it means there is no foreign key anywhere in the user model.

CREATE TABLE `wp_users` (
  `ID`                    bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL AUTO_INCREMENT,
  `user_login`            varchar(60)  NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
  `user_pass`             varchar(255) NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
  `user_nicename`         varchar(50)  NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
  `user_email`            varchar(100) NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
  `user_url`              varchar(100) NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
  `user_registered`       datetime     NOT NULL DEFAULT '0000-00-00 00:00:00',
  `user_activation_key`   varchar(255) NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
  `user_status`           int(11)      NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  `display_name`          varchar(250) NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
  PRIMARY KEY (`ID`),
  KEY `user_login_key`         (`user_login`(191)),
  KEY `user_nicename`          (`user_nicename`(191)),
  KEY `user_email`             (`user_email`(191)),
  KEY `user_activation_key`    (`user_activation_key`(191))
) ENGINE=InnoDB DEFAULT CHARSET=utf8mb4 COLLATE=utf8mb4_unicode_520_ci;

CREATE TABLE `wp_usermeta` (
  `umeta_id`    bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL AUTO_INCREMENT,
  `user_id`     bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL DEFAULT '0',
  `meta_key`    varchar(255)          DEFAULT NULL,
  `meta_value`  longtext,
  PRIMARY KEY (`umeta_id`),
  KEY `user_id`  (`user_id`),
  KEY `meta_key` (`meta_key`(191))
) ENGINE=InnoDB DEFAULT CHARSET=utf8mb4 COLLATE=utf8mb4_unicode_520_ci;

The capability check is a PHP operation, not a query, and this surprises people who expect a relational answer. WP_User::has_cap() loads the user’s wp_capabilities meta, walks the role definitions from the wp_user_roles option, and evaluates the requested capability, delegating to map_meta_cap() when the capability starts with an underscore-prefixed meta form. The consequence for your code is that a capability check costs a user-object load and an array walk, and that "does this user have this capability" is answered entirely from the object cache once the user is warm. It also means the check cannot be expressed in SQL without reimplementing the mapping logic, and it means current_user_can() is the only correct way to ask.

CapabilityGrants, when mappedCorrect useWrong use
edit_postEdit the specific post in the objectRedirect check on a loaded postAnywhere — it is a meta capability
edit_postsEdit own posts of any editable typeGate an admin list tableGate a single foreign post
edit_others_postsEdit any user’s postsGate a moderation queueGate your own settings screen
edit_published_postsEdit already-published postsGate the "edit" link in the listGate creation
publish_postsMove content to publishGate a "publish" actionGate reading a draft
read_private_postsRead private posts of the typeGate a single-post readGate the list of others' drafts
delete_postsTrash own postsGate a delete buttonGate a bulk action
manage_categoriesCreate, edit, delete termsGate the term UIGate assigning a term
upload_filesUpload an attachmentGate a media pickerGate a file download

Never gate on a role name. in_array( 'editor', (array) $user->roles ) is wrong for four independent reasons: it hard-codes a role name that a site owner may have renamed, it ignores every capability granted outside roles, it ignores meta capabilities entirely — so an editor without edit_others_posts would pass your check and then fail inside core, or worse, pass your check and your code would then perform an operation core would have refused. Capability checks are the interface; roles are one implementation of it, and the only implementation on a site with a custom role plugin is a different one. current_user_can() is the interface.

3.5 Options, transients and the autoload problem

Explains the autoload column, the two-query boot load, and why a single oversized autoloaded option is the most reliably diagnosable cause of a slow WordPress site.

On every request, wp_not-loaded.php is not involved — instead wp_load_alloptions() issues a single SELECT option_name, option_value FROM wp_options WHERE autoload IN ('yes','on','auto-on','auto') and the result is primed into the object cache and the $alloptions array. Every autoloaded option is therefore parsed on every request, on every page, including requests that need none of them. The autoload column gained new values in 5.6 and 6.6 to allow large values to be excluded, which is the correct remedy for the problem and is very rarely applied.

CREATE TABLE `wp_options` (
  `option_id`    bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL AUTO_INCREMENT,
  `option_name`  varchar(191)  NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
  `option_value` longtext      NOT NULL,
  `autoload`     varchar(20)   NOT NULL DEFAULT 'yes',
  PRIMARY KEY (`option_id`),
  UNIQUE KEY `option_name` (`option_name`),
  KEY `autoload` (`autoload`)
) ENGINE=InnoDB DEFAULT CHARSET=utf8mb4 COLLATE=utf8mb4_unicode_520_ci;

-- The query that runs on literally every request.
EXPLAIN SELECT option_name, option_value
          FROM wp_options
         WHERE autoload IN ( 'yes', 'on', 'auto-on', 'auto' );
-- Using index; ~1.1 MiB, 412 rows. Parsed 412 times per request.

-- The diagnostic. Sorted by bytes, not by name.
SELECT option_name,
       autoload,
       LENGTH( option_value )                    AS bytes,
       COUNT(*) OVER ( ORDER BY LENGTH( option_value) DESC ) AS rank_no
  FROM wp_options
 WHERE autoload IN ( 'yes', 'on', 'auto-on', 'auto' )
 ORDER BY bytes DESC
 LIMIT 25;

Typical culprits, in the order they appear on real sites: a WooCommerce or Yoast transients table mirrored into options; a page-builder plugin storing its entire document tree per template; a site that has been running for years with one option per form field per form; and — most insidiously — a plugin that calls update_option() with the third argument defaulted to true for a value that is several hundred kilobytes. Any option over roughly 150 KB should not be autoloaded. Core’s own guidance, and the reason the autoload column was widened from a yes/no enum to a varchar(20), is that values above that threshold belong on a page and almost never on every page.

Transients live in the same table and inherit the same behaviour, with a crucial difference. set_transient() writes _transient_$key, or _transient_timeout_$key as well when a TTL is given. The autoload value is yes for a transient with no expiry and yes — not no — for one with an expiry, which is a detail that surprises people: an expiring transient is still autoloaded, because core wants the chance to fire transient_timeout_$key and delete the expired row. With an external object cache, the entire transient layer bypasses the database and none of this applies. Without one, every transient is a table row, and a plugin that writes a thousand of them is quietly filling wp_options.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * What set_transient() actually does, without an external object
 * cache. With one, all three branches are replaced by a Redis SET
 * with EX and none of these rows are ever created.
 */
set_transient( 'acme:fx:GBP:EUR', 1.1642, 6 * HOUR_IN_SECONDS );

// -> UPDATE/INSERT `_transient_acme:fx:GBP:EUR`      autoload = 'yes'
// -> UPDATE/INSERT `_transient_timeout_acme:fx:GBP:EUR` autoload = 'yes'

$value = get_transient( 'acme:fx:GBP:EUR' );
// -> SELECT both rows; compares time() with the timeout; deletes if stale

/**
 * Consequence: a colon in a transient name produces a *separate*
 * option row for the timeout. Do not use colons if you care about
 * `wp option list` being legible, and never assume a transient is
 * durable — on a site with an object cache it is not in the
 * database at all, and `wp option get` will not find it.
 */

/**
 * Cleanup that is safe to run even though core's own cleanup is
 * passive: delete expired rows for a prefix you own.
 */
global $wpdb;

$wpdb->query( $wpdb->prepare(
    "DELETE o FROM {$wpdb->options} o
      INNER JOIN {$wpdb->options} t
              ON t.option_name = CONCAT( '_transient_timeout_', SUBSTRING( o.option_name, 12 ) )
     WHERE o.option_name LIKE %s
       AND t.option_value < %d",
    $wpdb->esc_like( '_transient_acme:%' ) . '%',
    time()
) );

Prefer wp_cache_* over transients for derived data, and always pass false as the autoload argument to update_option() unless you have measured a need. The decision rule is about what happens when the cache is cold. If recomputing the value is expensive and the value is valid for hours, it is a transient. If recomputing is cheap and the value is valid for the request, it is the object cache. If the value must be read on every request and must be correct, it is an autoloaded option, and you should be able to say why in one sentence. A value that is genuinely per-site configuration and genuinely read everywhere is small; a value that is large is data, and data does not belong in an option row that is parsed before your plugin has executed.

3.6 Comments, meta tables and multisite

Covers the comment tables, the four network tables multisite adds, and the schema differences that a plugin must respect if it is to work on both.

Comments are a straightforward parent-child table with an approval status and a comment_type that distinguishes comment, pingback, trackback and — since 5.5 — comment subtypes registered by plugins. The important structural point is that comment_approved is a varchar(20), not a boolean, because WordPress 2.7+ supports per-comment moderation states of 1, 0, spam, trash and unapproved. Any query that writes comment_approved = 1 is correct; any query that writes comment_approved = 0 and assumes "unapproved" is correct for a plugin that uses unapproved as a distinct state.

CREATE TABLE `wp_comments` (
  `comment_ID`           bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL AUTO_INCREMENT,
  `comment_post_ID`      bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL DEFAULT '0',
  `comment_author`       tinytext      NOT NULL,
  `comment_author_email` varchar(100)  NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
  `comment_author_url`   varchar(200)  NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
  `comment_author_IP`    varchar(100)  NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
  `comment_date`         datetime      NOT NULL DEFAULT '0000-00-00 00:00:00',
  `comment_date_gmt`     datetime      NOT NULL DEFAULT '0000-00-00 00:00:00',
  `comment_content`      text          NOT NULL,
  `comment_karma`        int(11)       NOT NULL DEFAULT '0',
  `comment_approved`     varchar(20)   NOT NULL DEFAULT '1',
  `comment_agent`        varchar(255)  NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
  `comment_type`         varchar(20)   NOT NULL DEFAULT 'comment',
  `comment_parent`       bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL DEFAULT '0',
  `user_id`              bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL DEFAULT '0',
  PRIMARY KEY (`comment_ID`),
  KEY `comment_post_ID`          (`comment_post_ID`),
  KEY `comment_approved_date_gmt` (`comment_approved`,`comment_date_gmt`),
  KEY `comment_parent`           (`comment_parent`),
  KEY `comment_author_email`     (`comment_author_email`(191))
) ENGINE=InnoDB DEFAULT CHARSET=utf8mb4 COLLATE=utf8mb4_unicode_520_ci;

CREATE TABLE `wp_commentmeta` (
  `meta_id`    bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL AUTO_INCREMENT,
  `comment_id` bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL DEFAULT '0',
  `meta_key`   varchar(255)          DEFAULT NULL,
  `meta_value` longtext,
  PRIMARY KEY (`meta_id`),
  KEY `comment_id` (`comment_id`),
  KEY `meta_key`   (`meta_key`(191))
) ENGINE=InnoDB DEFAULT CHARSET=utf8mb4 COLLATE=utf8mb4_unicode_520_ci;

Multisite changes the schema by adding four tables and by making the meaning of several APIs context-dependent. Every table is prefixed wp_ as usual, but the core tables are per site — each site has its own posts, options and users — while four network tables are global: wp_site and wp_sitemeta for the network itself, wp_blogs for each site row, and wp_signups for pending registrations and invites. Network-activated plugins live in the sunrise.php bootstrap, and is_multisite() plus get_sites() and switch_to_blog() are the APIs a plugin must use to be correct on both topologies.

CREATE TABLE `wp_blogs` (
  `blog_id`      bigint(20)   NOT NULL AUTO_INCREMENT,
  `site_id`      bigint(20)   NOT NULL DEFAULT '0',
  `domain`       varchar(200) NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
  `path`         varchar(100) NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
  `registered`   datetime     NOT NULL DEFAULT '0000-00-00 00:00:00',
  `last_updated` datetime     NOT NULL DEFAULT '0000-00-00 00:00:00',
  `public`       tinyint(2)   NOT NULL DEFAULT '1',
  `archived`     tinyint(2)   NOT NULL DEFAULT '0',
  `mature`       tinyint(2)   NOT NULL DEFAULT '0',
  `spam`         tinyint(2)   NOT NULL DEFAULT '0',
  `deleted`      tinyint(2)   NOT NULL DEFAULT '0',
  `lang_id`      int(11)      NOT NULL DEFAULT '0',
  PRIMARY KEY (`blog_id`),
  KEY `domain` (`domain`(191)),
  KEY `path`   (`path`(100))
) ENGINE=InnoDB DEFAULT CHARSET=utf8mb4 COLLATE=utf8mb4_unicode_520_ci;

CREATE TABLE `wp_site`  ( `id` int(11) NOT NULL AUTO_INCREMENT, `domain` varchar(200) NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
                          `path` varchar(100) NOT NULL DEFAULT '', `public` tinyint(2) NOT NULL DEFAULT '1',
                          PRIMARY KEY (`id`), KEY `domain` (`domain`(200)), KEY `path` (`path`(100)) );

CREATE TABLE `wp_sitemeta` ( `meta_id` bigint(20) NOT NULL AUTO_INCREMENT, `site_id` bigint(20) NOT NULL DEFAULT '0',
                             `meta_key` varchar(255) DEFAULT NULL, `meta_value` longtext,
                             PRIMARY KEY (`meta_id`), KEY `site_id` (`site_id`), KEY `meta_key` (`meta_key`(191)) );

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * Writing code that is correct on both topologies. The rule is that
 * the blog id is part of every cache key and part of every option
 * name, and that switch_to_blog() is always paired with a restore in
 * a finally block, because an unbalanced switch corrupts the rest
 * of the request in ways that are extremely hard to diagnose.
 */
add_action( 'acme_reindex_site', static function ( int $site_id ): void {
    $switched = false;

    if ( is_multisite() && get_current_blog_id() !== $site_id ) {
        switch_to_blog( $site_id );
        $switched = true;
    }

    try {
        $query = new WP_Query( [
            'post_type'      => 'acme_invoice',
            'post_status'    => 'any',
            'posts_per_page' => 500,
            'fields'         => 'ids',
            'no_found_rows'  => true,
        ] );

        update_post_meta_cache( $query->posts );

        foreach ( $query->posts as $id ) {
            wp_cache_set(
                sprintf( 'acme:invoice:%d:%d', $site_id, $id ),   // site id in the key
                Acme\Ledger\Domain\Invoice::from_meta( get_post_meta( $id ) ),
                'acme_invoices',
                3600
            );
        }
    } finally {
        if ( $switched ) {
            restore_current_blog();   // not optional, not deferred
        }
    }
}, 10, 1 );

Exercises

  1. Audit the schema against the real thing. Dump SHOW CREATE TABLE for all twelve core tables from a live 6.9 install, diff them against the DDL in this chapter, and explain every difference you find — including the display-width deprecations and any plugin-added indexes. Deliverable: the diff, a written explanation per difference, and a decision on which differences to treat as acceptable drift.
  2. Prove the EAV cost. Create a fixture of 200,000 posts with a metadata pair on each, then compare the runtime and EXPLAIN plan of the same filter before and after adding a (meta_key, post_id) composite index, and again after converting the filtering dimension to a taxonomy. Deliverable: three EXPLAIN outputs, wall-clock timings at 10, 100 and 1000 rows examined, and a written conclusion about the break-even row count.
  3. Fix an autoload bloat. Find the twenty largest autoloaded options on a real site, determine for each whether it is read on every request, and convert the ones that are not. Record the autoloaded row count and total byte length before and after. Deliverable: the migration code, a before/after measurement of the wp_load_alloptions() query, and a nightly health check that alerts if any single autoloaded option exceeds 150 KB.
  4. Move a hot field off the metadata table. Take a plugin that filters a custom post type on a low-cardinality metadata key, convert that key to a taxonomy with map_meta_cap-equivalent permission handling, and keep a synchronised metadata copy for template convenience. Deliverable: a migration that backfills in batches of 1,000, a dual-read period with a reconciliation report showing zero divergence, and a before/after EXPLAIN.
  5. Write the multisite compatibility layer. Take a single-site plugin that caches a query result and a plugin that registers a post type, and make both correct on a five-site network including per-site custom capabilities. Deliverable: both patches, a PHPUnit integration test that runs the same assertions under wp-env single-site and multisite, and a proof that no cache key collides across sites.

4 · The Template Hierarchy and the Theme System

4.1 Template hierarchy resolution4.2 The Loop and WP_Query in themes4.3 Block templates and template parts4.4 Block registration and dynamic blocks4.5 Block patterns and theme.json4.6 Child themes and safe overrides

This chapter explains how a resolved request becomes HTML, first for classic themes and then for block themes, and treats the two as one system with two resolution strategies rather than as competing eras. The argument is that the hierarchy is an ordered list of candidates, that the ordering is a contract you can rely on but only if you do not add speculative entries to it, and that the block editor has not replaced any of it — it has moved the templates from PHP files into the database and replaced function calls with block render callbacks. For a plugin author the practical consequences are concrete: where to hook, which filters intercept template selection, and how to register a block that is fast, translatable and safe to render on both a classic and a block theme.

4.1 Template hierarchy resolution

Enumerates the hierarchy for a single post and explains the resolution algorithm, the template_include extension point, and the 404 fallback.

Resolution is a linear walk. locate_template() builds a list of candidate filenames for the current query type, walks it in order, and returns the first that exists in the child theme and then the parent. The list is not a heuristic; it is exhaustive and ordered by specificity, and it is extended rather than changed when new query types appear. A plugin that needs to influence it should filter the candidate list ({$type}_template) to insert an entry, or filter the result (template_include) to replace it. Doing the former is better when you want child-theme overridability; doing the latter is better when you are substituting a template outright.

# Hierarchy for is_single() on post_type=post. Read top to bottom.
single-{post_type}-{slug}.php
single-{post_type}-{id}.php
single-{post_type}.php
single-{slug}.php
single-{id}.php
single.php                                   // the usual target
singular.php
index.php                                   // the universal fallback

# Hierarchy for a post type archive
archive-{post_type}-{slug}.php
archive-{post_type}.php
archive.php
index.php

# Hierarchy for a taxonomy term archive
taxonomy-{taxonomy}-{term}.php
taxonomy-{taxonomy}.php
taxonomy.php
archive.php
index.php

# Hierarchy for a single term (term.php is rare and usually wrong)
single-{taxonomy}-{term}.php
taxonomy-{taxonomy}-{term}.php
single.php
singular.php
index.php

The 404 case is the one with a real trap. When nothing matches, WordPress sets is_404 and re-runs the resolution for 404.php, then index.php. The trap is that a template can be responsible for setting the 404 itself, and the common bug is a theme that does an internal rewrite — a search, a "did you mean", a redirect to the first post of a type — and leaves is_404 set. The result is a page that renders with 404 status and the template of the resolved type, which no amount of caching will fix and which every accessibility audit will flag. The rule is that if you resolve a request to a different query, you must call $wp_query->set_404() and status_header( 404 ) yourself, or you must clear the flag; you cannot leave the two inconsistent.

Query conditionFirst candidateUsual override fileFilter to use
Single postsingle-{slug}.phpsingle.phpsingle_template
Pagepage-{slug}.phppage.php / singular.phppage_template
Category / tag / termtaxonomy-{tax}-{term}.phparchive.phptaxonomy_template
Authorauthor-{nicename}.phpauthor.phpauthor_template
Post type archivearchive-{type}.phparchive.phparchive_template
Searchsearch.phpsearch.phpsearch_template
404404.php404.php404_template

Template names are part of a plugin’s public surface, whether you intended them or not. A post type registered with 'has_archive' => 'invoices' creates a public URL space, and every hierarchy entry above becomes a potential file that a child theme or a page builder might create. If a plugin intends to supply templates itself, it must do so through {$type}_template and it must document the filenames; if it does not, it should be aware that a third party may already own them. Two plugins registering the same post type slug is a real collision, and core will not warn — it will simply serve whichever theme file happens to exist.

4.2 The Loop and WP_Query in themes

Explains what the Loop actually does mechanically, and gives the pre_get_posts pattern for adding a filter without a second query.

The Loop is not a language construct. It is a call to have_posts(), which delegates to the global $wp_query, which walks a cursor over a materialised array of WP_Post objects plus a primed object cache. the_post() populates the globals the template tag functions read. Two consequences follow. Modifying the main query is done by altering its arguments before it runs, not by re-running it afterwards; and any secondary query should be a separate WP_Query instance, never by touching the global.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * The Loop, annotated. Everything in a classic template is either
 * this loop, a template tag reading the globals it sets, or output.
 */
get_header();

if ( have_posts() ) :

    while ( have_posts() ) :
        the_post();            // advances the cursor, sets $post etc.

        printf(
            '<article id="%s" class="%s">',
            esc_attr( get_the_ID() ),
            esc_attr( implode( ' ', get_post_class() ) )
        );

        the_title( '<h2><a href="%s">', '</a></h2>' );  // 5.5+ args

        if ( has_post_thumbnail() ) {
            the_post_thumbnail( 'large', [ 'loading' => 'lazy' ] );
        }

        the_excerpt();
        comments_template();

        echo '</article>';

    endwhile;

    the_posts_pagination( [ 'mid_size' => 2, 'prev_text' => __( 'Newer', 'acme' ) ] );

else :

    get_template_part( 'template-parts/content', 'none' );

endif;

get_footer();

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * `pre_get_posts` is the only correct place to change a query.
 * It runs before the SQL is built, so a filter added here costs
 * nothing extra; a filter added on `the_posts` costs a full query
 * plus a full re-hydration of the result set.
 */
add_action( 'pre_get_posts', static function ( WP_Query $query ): void {
    if ( is_admin() || ! $query->is_main_query() ) {
        return;
    }

    if ( ! $query->is_post_type_archive( 'acme_invoice' ) ) {
        return;
    }

    if ( isset( $_GET['acme_status'] ) ) {
        $status = sanitize_key( wp_unslash( $_GET['acme_status'] ) );

        if ( 'overdue' === $status ) {
            $query->set( 'meta_query', [
                [
                    'key'     => '_acme_due_date',
                    'value'   => current_time( 'Y-m-d' ),
                    'compare' => '<',
                    'type'    => 'DATE',
                ],
                [
                    'key'     => '_acme_status',
                    'value'   => 'unpaid',
                    'compare' => '=',
                ],
            ] );
        }
    }

    $query->set( 'posts_per_page', 24 );
    $query->set( 'no_found_rows', ! $query->get( 'paged' ) ? false : true );
}, 10, 1 );

Never modify the main query with a posts_ or found_posts filter. the_posts fires after the query has run, so changing the array there means the pagination, the found rows and the post caches all describe a result set that no longer exists. A very common symptom is a page that says "Page 2 of 1" and a sitemap that disagrees with the archive. If you must filter the result set — and occasionally you must — use pre_get_posts to change the query, and if that is genuinely impossible, use the_posts to reduce the set while leaving the count alone and accepting that pagination will be approximate.

4.3 Block templates and template parts

Shows how a block theme stores its templates, how they are overridden, and the API for a plugin to supply one.

In a block theme, a template is a block document stored as a wp_template post with post_type of wp_template and a taxonomy of wp_theme, whose taxonomy term is the theme slug. A file under templates/ with the same slug and the same hierarchy position is equivalent and is the source of truth when both exist: core reads the file and writes it into the database on first use, and thereafter the database copy wins, which is why "I edited the template file and nothing happened" is such a common report. A template part is the same thing with wp_template_part, distinguished by a wp_template_part_area term — header, footer, sidebar or uncategorized.

// wp-content/themes/acme/templates/single-acme_invoice.html
<!-- wp:template-part {"slug":"header","tagName":"header"} /-->

<!-- wp:group {"tagName":"main","layout":{"type":"constrained"},
     "style":{"spacing":{"blockGap":"var:preset|spacing|6"}}} -->
<main class="wp-block-group">
    <!-- wp:post-featured-image {"align":"wide","style":{}} /-->

    <!-- wp:post-title {"level":1,"className":"is-style-lede"} /-->

    <!-- wp:post-content {"layout":{"type":"constrained"},
        "style":{"typography":{"lineHeight":"1.6"}}} /-->

    <!-- wp:acme/invoice-summary {"showVatBreakdown":true} /-->

    <!-- wp:post-terms {"term":"acme_region","className":"is-style-pill"} /-->

    <!-- wp:acme/payment-history {"pageSize":5} /-->
</main>
<!-- /wp:group -->

<!-- wp:template-part {"slug":"footer","tagName":"footer"} /-->

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * A plugin supplying a template, without taking over the theme.
 * `get_block_templates` is the discovery filter; returning a post
 * object built by `wp_insert_post()` registers the override for
 * every request without writing to the database.
 */
add_filter( 'get_block_templates', static function (
    array $templates,
    WP_Theme $theme,
    array $query
): array {
    if ( 'acme' !== $theme->get_stylesheet() || ! is_admin() ) {
        return $templates;
    }

    $wanted = $query['post_type'] ?? 'wp_template';

    if ( 'wp_template' !== $wanted || ! in_array( 'single', $query['slugs'] ?? [], true ) ) {
        return $templates;
    }

    $existing = get_block_template( 'acme//single', $wanted );

    if ( null !== $existing ) {
        return $templates;   // the site owner has customised it; win
    }

    $templates[] = Acme\Ledger\Blocks\InvoiceTemplate::build();

    return $templates;
}, 10, 3 );

/**
 * The corresponding content filter, for when you need to change
 * an existing template rather than add one.
 */
add_filter( 'get_block_file_template', static function (
    ?WP_Block_Template $block_template,
    string $id,
    string $template_type
): ?WP_Block_Template {
    if ( null !== $block_template || 'wp_template' !== $template_type ) {
        return $block_template;
    }

    if ( ! str_starts_with( $id, 'acme//' ) ) {
        return $block_template;
    }

    return Acme\Ledger\Blocks\InvoiceTemplate::build();
}, 10, 3 );

The rule that catches everyone is the precedence one. Specificity beats generic beats index, and a template stored in the database — which is what the Site Editor creates — beats a file on disk for the same slug. A plugin that ships templates/single-acme_invoice.html into a theme directory it does not own is therefore fragile, and a plugin that filters get_block_templates must yield to an existing template, because a site that has deliberately customised the template in the Site Editor will silently lose those customisations the first time the plugin ships an update.

4.4 Block registration and dynamic blocks

Walks through block.json, server-side registration and a dynamic render callback, and states the rules that keep a block fast and safe.

Since 5.8, a block’s metadata lives in block.json and register_block_type() reads it, including render_callback by convention when a render.php sits beside it. The editor half is built with @wordpress/scripts; the server half is plain PHP. The contract between them is the attributes object, and it is a contract in both directions: the editor serialises only declared attributes into the block comment delimiters, and the server receives only those. An undeclared attribute is silently dropped, which is the reason a block that "works in the editor and renders nothing on the front end" almost always has a mismatch between the two attribute sets.

{
  "$schema": "https://schemas.wp.org/trunk/block.json",
  "apiVersion": 3,
  "name": "acme/invoice-summary",
  "title": "Invoice Summary",
  "category": "widgets",
  "icon": "receipt-alt",
  "description": "Displays the payable total, VAT breakdown and payment state of an invoice.",
  "textdomain": "acme-ledger",
  "supports": {
    "html": false,
    "align": [ "wide", "full" ],
    "spacing": { "margin": true, "padding": true },
    "color": { "background": true, "text": true, "gradients": false },
    "typography": { "fontSize": true, "lineHeight": true },
    "anchor": true
  },
  "attributes": {
    "invoiceId":   { "type": "number", "default": 0 },
    "showVat":     { "type": "boolean", "default": true },
    "showDueDate": { "type": "boolean", "default": true },
    "variant":     { "type": "string", "default": "card",
                     "enum": [ "card", "inline", "minimal" ] },
    "align":       { "type": "string" },
    "className":   { "type": "string" }
  },
  "usesContext": [ "postId", "postType" ],
  "editorScript": "file:./index.js",
  "editorStyle":  "file:./index.css",
  "viewScript":   "file:./view.js",
  "style":        "file:./style.css"
}

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

namespace Acme\Ledger\Blocks\InvoiceSummary;

use WP_Block;

/**
 * Server registration. With block.json present, `render` is the
 * convention and is wired automatically when render.php exists in
 * the same directory; passing it explicitly costs nothing and makes
 * the dependency visible to a reader who has not opened the file.
 */
add_action( 'init', static function (): void {
    register_block_type(
        __DIR__,
        [
            'render_callback' => [ Renderer::class, 'render' ],
        ]
    );
} );

final class Renderer {

    /**
     * Every attribute arrives as the declared type with the declared
     * default, so there is no `isset()` dance — but the values are
     * still attacker-controlled, because any client can POST a block
     * comment. Validate against the enum, cast the number, and treat
     * the post ID as untrusted until wp_verify_post() says otherwise.
     */
    public static function render( array $attributes, string $content, WP_Block $block ): string {
        $invoice_id = absint( $attributes['invoiceId'] ?? 0 );
        $variant    = in_array( $attributes['variant'] ?? '', [ 'card', 'inline', 'minimal' ], true )
            ? $attributes['variant']
            : 'card';

        if ( 0 === $invoice_id ) {
            return self::placeholder( __( 'No invoice selected.', 'acme-ledger' ) );
        }

        $post = get_post( $invoice_id );

        if ( ! $post instanceof WP_Post
            || 'acme_invoice' !== $post->post_type
            || ! current_user_can( 'read_post', $invoice_id ) ) {
            return self::placeholder( __( 'Invoice unavailable.', 'acme-ledger' ) );
        }

        $summary = SummaryFactory::from_post( $post );

        return sprintf(
            '<div %s>%s</div>',
            get_block_wrapper_attributes( [ 'class' => 'acme-summary is-' . $variant ] ),
            self::markup( $summary, (bool) ( $attributes['showVat'] ?? true ) )
        );
    }
}

The block in block.json above declares "html": false in supports, which tells the editor to use a React element rather than a save-time string. That is the correct choice for any block that computes markup, and choosing incorrectly is a common migration mistake: a block with a save() function that produces HTML cannot use server-side rendering, and switching to a dynamic block later invalidates every post that already contains it, leaving the editor showing "this block contains unexpected or invalid content". Decide the rendering model at registration time, because changing it is a content migration.

// src/index.js — the editor half. Built with `wp-scripts build`.
import { __ } from '@wordpress/i18n';
import { registerBlockType } from '@wordpress/blocks';
import { InspectorControls, useBlockProps } from '@wordpress/block-editor';
import { PanelBody, ToggleControl, SelectControl } from '@wordpress/components';
import { useSelect } from '@wordpress/data';
import { useEntityProp } from '@wordpress/core-data';

registerBlockType( 'acme/invoice-summary', {
    edit( { attributes, setAttributes, clientId } ) {
        const { invoiceId } = attributes;
        const postId = useSelect(
            ( select ) => select( 'core/editor' ).getCurrentPostId(),
            []
        );

        const [ meta, setMeta ] = useEntityProp(
            'postType', 'acme_invoice', 'meta', postId
        );

        return (
            <>
                <InspectorControls>
                    <PanelBody title={ __( 'Summary', 'acme-ledger' ) }>
                        <SelectControl
                            label={ __( 'Variant', 'acme-ledger' ) }
                            value={ attributes.variant }
                            options={ [
                                { label: __( 'Card', 'acme-ledger' ), value: 'card' },
                                { label: __( 'Inline', 'acme-ledger' ), value: 'inline' },
                            ] }
                            onChange={ ( variant ) => setAttributes( { variant } ) }
                        />
                        <ToggleControl
                            label={ __( 'Show VAT breakdown', 'acme-ledger' ) }
                            checked={ attributes.showVat }
                            onChange={ ( showVat ) => setAttributes( { showVat } ) }
                        />
                    </PanelBody>
                </InspectorControls>

                <div { ...useBlockProps() }>
                    <p>{ __( 'Summary of the selected invoice.', 'acme-ledger' ) }</p>
                </div>
            </>
        );
    },
    save() {
        return null;   // server-rendered: no save-time markup
    },
} );

A block render callback is a public, unauthenticated endpoint. Anyone can render a page containing your block, and a block that reads a post ID attribute will be asked to render whatever ID is put in the block comment. That is why current_user_can( 'read_post', $id ) belongs in the callback, not just in the editor: the editor check protects the editor, and the callback check protects every anonymous visitor who loads a page with the block cached. The related trap is performance — a block whose callback runs get_post_meta() for an ID that is not the current post will, on a cold cache, add two queries per block instance, and a page with a query loop of twenty posts containing a summary block each will add forty.

4.5 Block patterns and theme.json

Covers pattern registration, the categories patterns are grouped into, and the theme.json settings that every theme and many plugins now need to declare.

A pattern is a block document with a title and a category, registered server-side so that it appears in the inserter. Registration is cheap, the content is stored as a PHP string or a file, and the category must be one that exists — core registers featured, buttons, columns, embed, gallery, header, hero, query, services, social-links, text and footers at priority 11, and a plugin registering a new category must do so before its own patterns are registered. A pattern that references a block the current theme does not have will render as an empty comment in the editor, which is the second most common block-editor support ticket after "invalid content".

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * Registering a plugin's patterns. `register_block_pattern` is
 * idempotent by slug, so calling it on every request is correct
 * and cheap — the pattern is registered in memory only, never
 * written to the database.
 */
add_action( 'init', static function (): void {
    if ( ! WP_Block_Type_Registry::get_instance()->is_registered( 'core/query' ) ) {
        return;   // the block editor is not active
    }

    register_block_pattern_category( 'acme-billing', [
        'label'       => __( 'Acme Billing', 'acme-ledger' ),
        'description' => __( 'Layouts for invoices, statements and payment history.', 'acme-ledger' ),
    ] );

    register_block_pattern( 'acme-billing/invoice-detail', [
        'title'       => __( 'Invoice detail with totals and payment history', 'acme-ledger' ),
        'description' => __( 'A two-column layout showing the summary block beside a history table.', 'acme-ledger' ),
        'categories'  => [ 'acme-billing', 'acme-ledger' ],
        'postTypes'   => [ 'acme_invoice', 'page' ],
        'blockTypes'  => [ 'core/post-content' ],
        'viewportWidth' => 1280,
        'content'     => file_get_contents( __DIR__ . '/patterns/invoice-detail.php' ),
        'keywords'    => [ 'invoice', 'billing', 'total' ],
    ] );
}, 11 );

{
  "$schema": "https://schemas.wp.org/trunk/theme.json",
  "version": 3,
  "settings": {
    "appearanceTools": true,
    "useRootPaddingAwareAlignments": true,
    "layout": { "contentSize": "760px", "wideSize": "1200px" },
    "color": {
      "defaultPalette": false,
      "defaultGradients": false,
      "custom": true,
      "palette": [
        { "slug": "ink",       "color": "#101418", "name": "Ink" },
        { "slug": "paper",     "color": "#fbfaf7", "name": "Paper" },
        { "slug": "brand",     "color": "#0b5fff", "name": "Brand" },
        { "slug": "positive",  "color": "#0a7c42", "name": "Positive" },
        { "slug": "critical",  "color": "#b3261e", "name": "Critical" },
        { "slug": "muted",     "color": "#5c6670", "name": "Muted" }
      ],
      "gradients": [
        { "slug": "brand-fade", "gradient": "linear-gradient(135deg,#0b5fff 0%,#6ea8ff 100%)",
          "name": "Brand fade" }
      ]
    },
    "typography": {
      "fluid": true,
      "fontFamilies": [
        { "slug": "sans", "name": "Sans",  "fontFamily": "Inter, system-ui, sans-serif" },
        { "slug": "serif","name": "Serif", "fontFamily": "Source Serif 4, Georgia, serif" }
      ],
      "fontSizes": [
        { "slug": "small", "name": "Small", "size": "0.875rem" },
        { "slug": "medium","name": "Medium","size": "1rem" },
        { "slug": "large", "name": "Large", "size": "1.5rem" },
        { "slug": "x-large","name":"Extra", "size": "2.25rem", "fluid": false }
      ]
    },
    "spacing": { "units": [ "px", "rem", "%", "vw" ], "defaultSpacingSizes": false }
  },
  "styles": {
    "color": { "background": "var(--wp--preset--color--paper)", "text": "var(--wp--preset--color--ink)" },
    "typography": { "fontFamily": "var(--wp--preset--font-family--sans)", "lineHeight": "1.6" },
    "spacing": { "blockGap": "1.5rem", "padding": { "left": "1rem", "right": "1rem" } }
  }
}

Two things about theme.json are routinely misunderstood. First, it is not a theme-exclusive file: a plugin may ship one, and if it does, the plugin’s version is merged with the theme’s using the site-level settings from the database as the base, with the theme taking precedence over the plugin. A plugin that ships a theme.json with "defaultPalette": true can therefore change the palette a theme deliberately defined — set defaultPalette and defaultGradients to false and supply your own slugs if you must define colours. Second, version: 3 is not optional in 6.9; omitting it produces a deprecation notice, and settings.typography.fluid plus fluid font sizes are what make typography scale with the viewport rather than at a fixed rem.

4.6 Child themes and safe overrides

Explains the child theme resolution order for assets, templates and functions, and identifies the overrides that silently stop working.

A child theme inherits the parent’s templates, styles and PHP. Assets resolve in one direction only: the child’s style.css is enqueued first, then the parent’s, which is why the standard handle-based enqueue in a child’s functions.php is a wp_enqueue_style with the parent’s handle and a dependency on the child’s. PHP resolves in the opposite order — the parent’s functions.php loads before the child’s — which is what allows a child to filter what the parent registered, and what makes the recommended pattern of defining the parent handle in a variable filterable from the child work at all.

// wp-content/themes/acme-child/style.css — the header is mandatory
/*
Theme Name:   Acme Child
Theme URI:    https://acme.test/themes/acme-child
Description:  Brand layer for the Acme estate. Markup, queries and
              behaviour all live in the parent; this theme changes
              presentation only.
Author:       Acme Platform
Template:     acme                    // REQUIRED: the parent slug
Version:      1.4.0
Text Domain:  acme-child
*/

<?php
/**
 * wp-content/themes/acme-child/functions.php
 *
 * Runs AFTER the parent's functions.php, so anything the parent
 * registered can be filtered here. `Template:` in style.css is what
 * makes this file load at all.
 */
add_action( 'after_setup_theme', static function (): void {
    remove_action( 'wp_head', 'wp_generator' );
    add_action( 'wp_head', 'wp_resource_hints', 2 );
