    add_filter( 'script_loader_tag', static fn ( string $tag ): string => $tag, 10 );
}, 20 );

/**
 * The enqueue pattern that survives a parent version bump. The
 * parent uses a filterable handle and a filterable version, so the
 * child never hard-codes a path that may change.
 */
add_action( 'wp_enqueue_scripts', static function (): void {
    $parent_handle = apply_filters( 'acme_parent_style_handle', 'acme-parent' );
    $version       = (string) wp_get_theme()->get( 'Version' );

    wp_enqueue_style(
        'acme-child',
        get_stylesheet_directory_uri() . '/assets/child.css',
        [ $parent_handle ],
        $version
    );

    wp_enqueue_script(
        'acme-child',
        get_stylesheet_directory_uri() . '/assets/child.js',
        [ $parent_handle ],
        $version,
        true
    );
}, 20 );

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * The override patterns, and what each one is safe for.
 *
 *  A template file in the child      — safe. Highest specificity
 *    wins and the parent is untouched.
 *
 *  A `{$type}_template` filter      — safe, but must yield to an
 *    existing file, or you break a grandchild theme.
 *
 *  remove_action() on parent code   — fragile. Breaks when the
 *    parent renames a method, with no error.
 *
 *  A pluggable function override    — global and irreversible for
 *    the request. Not a theming tool.
 */

/**
 * Template-level override for a parent custom post type. The
 * hierarchy is respected, so a site can override the plugin's
 * template without any code at all.
 */
add_filter( 'single_template', static function ( string $template ): string {
    if ( ! is_singular( 'acme_invoice' ) ) {
        return $template;
    }

    $child = get_stylesheet_directory() . '/single-acme_invoice.php';

    return file_exists( $child ) ? $child : $template;
}, 20 );

/**
 * Data-layer override: replace the parent's query vars wholesale
 * rather than filtering its results. This is the correct lever for
 * anything that changes which posts appear.
 */
add_filter( 'acme_invoices_query_args', static function ( array $args ): array {
    return array_merge( $args, [
        'posts_per_page' => 12,
        'meta_query'     => [
            [ 'key' => '_acme_visible', 'value' => '1' ],
        ],
    ] );
} );

The most common child theme break is a template file that is never read. In a block theme, copying templates/single.html into a child theme’s templates/ directory does nothing once the Site Editor has written that template to the database, because the database copy wins. The override then either fails silently or — worse — a developer debugs it by deleting the database copy, works locally, and the next Site Editor save undoes their fix for everyone else. If a site needs a template change, it must be made in the Site Editor, and the change must be captured in whatever process the site uses for database-backed content, because a database is not in version control.

Exercises

  1. Map the hierarchy. Build a fixture with a post type, a hierarchical taxonomy, a static front page, a blog page, a 404 and a search. For each of twenty-two query conditions, record which file WordPress resolved and why, using a template_include filter that logs the final choice and the template_redirect action for the condition. Deliverable: a twenty-two-row table and a graph of the hierarchy as installed on your own build.
  2. Build a dynamic block end to end. Create a block with @wordpress/scripts and a block.json, a dynamic render_callback, an editor component with two attributes, a view script, and a server-side render that reads one metadata field. Deliverable: a built asset bundle, a register_block_type() call that passes phpcs at WordPress-Core, and a PHPUnit test asserting the rendered markup for the three declared attribute variants plus one invalid value.
  3. Prove the block render cost. Render a page containing one, five and twenty instances of your block with a cold object cache and record query count and wall time for each. Then implement a document-wide prefetch and re-measure. Deliverable: three before/three-after measurements, the $wpdb->num_queries trace, and a stated budget per block instance that your test suite enforces.
  4. Author a theme.json for a plugin. Ship a theme.json from a plugin with a six-slot palette, four font sizes and a content/wide layout pair, and prove via a block-theme fixture that the presets appear in the editor, that a theme’s own theme.json takes precedence, and that a site-level customisation survives. Deliverable: the file, a fixture theme, and an assertion set covering precedence at all three levels.
  5. Rebuild a site on a child theme. Take an existing site with direct template edits, move every change into a child theme, and catalogue each one as a template override, a theme.json change, a Site Editor change, or a filter. Deliverable: the child theme, the catalogue, and a documented procedure for the Site Editor changes — including how they are backed up and reviewed, since they do not live in version control.

5 · The REST API, AJAX and External Data

5.1 The REST API architecture5.2 Endpoint and argument registration5.3 Authentication, permissions and CORS5.4 admin-ajax.php and the legacy AJAX API5.5 HTTP client, caching and external data5.6 Headless WordPress and content delivery

This chapter covers the three ways PHP talks to the outside world: serving structured data to a client, running code in a browser, and calling other services. The argument is that the REST API is the only one of the three with a versioned contract, a serialisation layer and a permission model that can be reasoned about, and that the other two should be treated as internal implementation details rather than as public interfaces. It shows what register_rest_route() actually validates, where the permission_callback belongs in the sequence, how authentication and authorisation differ, and why an outbound HTTP call with no timeout and no negative caching is a production incident waiting for a slow dependency.

5.1 The REST API architecture

Describes the request pipeline through rest_api_loaded, rest_api_init and WP_REST_Server, and the filter points at each stage.

The REST API boots on the rest_api_loaded action, which fires at the end of wp-settings.php when a request is a REST request. rest_api_init fires when the server is constructed, and every route, including the built-in ones for posts, users, media and terms, is registered on that hook. Dispatch is then: URL matched against the route regex → rest_pre_dispatch → parameter coercion and validation from the route’s args definition → rest_request_before_callbacks → permission_callback → your callback → rest_request_after_callbacks → rest_post_dispatch → serialisation through the response schema. Knowing the order matters because the filter that can see the raw request is not the filter that can see the response, and the filter that can short-circuit is not the filter that can add headers.

# Routes core registers, and where they live. `wp-json/` is a
# rewrite rule to index.php with `rest_route` populated.
GET    /wp/v2/posts                     post, post, page, any CPT
GET    /wp/v2/posts/(?P<id>[\d]+)       single
POST   /wp/v2/posts                     requires create_posts
POST   /wp/v2/posts/(?P<id>[\d]+)       requires edit_post
GET    /wp/v2/users                     requires list_users
GET    /wp/v2/media                     requires upload_files / edit_posts
GET    /wp/v2/categories                public
GET    /wp/v2/settings                  requires manage_options
GET    /wp/v2/themes                    requires edit_themes
GET    /wp/v2/plugins                   requires activate_plugins
GET    /wp/v2/block-types               public (5.9+)
GET    /wp/v2/block-renderer/(?P<name>[\w-]+/[\w-]+)   renders a block in isolation
GET    /wp/v2/global-styles/(?P<id>[\d]+)             theme.json documents
GET    /wp/v2/search                   public, cross-type

# The three namespaces you control
wp/v2                    core, versioned with the release
wp-site/v1               site settings, for multisite
yourplugin/v1            yours; the version is yours to honour

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * Stage-by-stage instrumentation. Useful during integration and
 * cheap to leave on at debug level, because each stage fires once
 * per request rather than once per route parameter.
 */
add_action( 'rest_api_init', static function ( \WP_REST_Server $server ): void {
    $server->register_route( 'acme-ledger/v1', '/ping', [
        'methods'             => 'GET',
        'permission_callback' => '__return_true',
        'callback'            => static fn (): \WP_REST_Response
            => new \WP_REST_Response( [ 'ok' => true, 'ts' => time() ], 200 ),
    ] );
}, 10 );

add_filter( 'rest_pre_dispatch', static function (
    ?\WP_REST_Response $response,
    \WP_REST_Server    $server,
    \WP_REST_Request   $request
): ?\WP_REST_Response {
    $route = $request->get_route();

    // 1. Before routing. Return a response here to bypass the route
    //    table entirely — this is where a maintenance gate belongs.
    if ( '/acme-ledger/v1/ping' === $route && ! wp_installing() ) {
        return new \WP_REST_Response( [
            'code'    => 'acme_maintenance',
            'message' => __( 'Ledger API is briefly unavailable.', 'acme-ledger' ),
            'data'    => [ 'status' => 503 ],
        ], 503 );
    }

    return $response;   // null = proceed with normal dispatch
}, 5, 3 );

add_filter( 'rest_request_after_callbacks', static function (
    \WP_REST_Response $response,
    \WP_REST_Request  $request,
    \WP_REST_Server   $server
): \WP_REST_Response {
    $response->header( 'X-Acme-Plugin', '1.4.0' );

    return $response;
}, 10, 3 );

The one thing to take from that is that rest_pre_dispatch returning a non-null value short-circuits everything, including the permission callback. That is exactly what makes it dangerous: a maintenance gate implemented there will happily serve a 503 to a request that would have been authorised, and a logging filter that accidentally returns something will break every route. Any rest_pre_dispatch callback must return null in the path where it has no opinion, and must be covered by a test that asserts an unrelated route still behaves normally.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * The full pipeline, as core implements it, in order. Every
 * extension point in the REST API appears exactly once.
 *
 *   1. rest_pre_dispatch( null, $server, $request )
 *        return non-null  => DONE, skip everything below
 *   2. $server->dispatch( $request )
 *        match_request() against $this->get_routes()
 *        $request->has_valid_params()   — see §5.2
 *        $request->sanitize_params()
 *   3. rest_request_before_callbacks( $response, $handler, $request )
 *   4. $handler['permission_callback']( $request )  — MUST be set
 *        WP_Error  => 4xx/5xx, and 401 when rest_authorization_*
 *        false     => 401 rest_forbidden (a BUG in your code)
 *        true      => proceed
 *   5. $handler['callback']( $request )
 *        WP_REST_Response  => serialised through the schema
 *        WP_Error          => error response
 *        anything else     => a PHP notice; 200 with a null body
 *   6. rest_request_after_callbacks( $response, $handler, $request )
 *   7. rest_post_dispatch( $response, $server, $request )
 *        final chance to add headers, log, or short-circuit
 *   8. server->response_to_data() => JSON, with Link headers
 *      for pagination and the `X-WP-Total` / `X-WP-TotalPages`
 *      headers core sets for collection responses.
 */

Use WP_REST_Response and WP_Error, never a raw value. Returning an array from a callback produces a 200 with a body that bypasses the response schema, which means field renaming and permission-based redaction in your prepare_item_for_response() are silently skipped, and it is indistinguishable from a successful call to a client checking the status code. Returning a bare null or a scalar is worse still: it produces a 200 with a body of null or a string, and no client can tell that your code failed. If your callback can fail, it must return a WP_Error; the status code is the first element of the error data array.

5.2 Endpoint and argument registration

Shows a complete route definition with typed arguments, an enum-backed argument, a validated integer range, and a documented response schema.

Route arguments are not documentation. register_rest_route()'s args array is the validation layer: each entry’s type, required, default, enum, minimum, maximum, pattern and validate_callback are applied by WP_REST_Request::has_valid_params() before your callback is reached, and a violation produces a rest_invalid_param error with the offending parameter named. This is the correct place to reject a bad value, and it is also the correct place to keep a handler from needing to re-validate what the framework has already guaranteed.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

namespace Acme\Ledger\Rest;

use WP_Error;
use WP_REST_Request;
use WP_REST_Response;
use WP_REST_Server;

final class InvoiceController {

    public const NS = 'acme-ledger/v1';

    public function register_routes(): void {
        register_rest_route( self::NS, '/invoices', [
            [
                'methods'             => 'GET',
                'callback'            => [ $this, 'index' ],
                'permission_callback' => [ $this, 'can_list' ],
                'args'                => $this->collection_args(),
            ],
            [
                'methods'             => 'POST',
                'callback'            => [ $this, 'create' ],
                'permission_callback' => [ $this, 'can_create' ],
                'args'                => [
                    'customer_id' => [
                        'type'              => 'integer',
                        'required'          => true,
                        'minimum'           => 1,
                        'sanitize_callback' => 'absint',
                    ],
                    'currency' => [
                        'type'     => 'string',
                        'required' => true,
                        'enum'     => [ 'GBP', 'EUR', 'USD' ],
                    ],
                    'lines' => [
                        'type'     => 'array',
                        'required' => true,
                        'minItems' => 1,
                        'maxItems' => 200,
                        'items'    => [ 'type' => 'object' ],
                    ],
                    'issue' => [
                        'type'    => 'boolean',
                        'default' => false,
                    ],
                ],
            ],
        ] );

        register_rest_route( self::NS, '/invoices/(?P<id>[\d]+)', [
            'methods'             => 'GET',
            'callback'            => [ $this, 'show' ],
            'permission_callback' => [ $this, 'can_read_one' ],
            'args'                => [
                'id' => [ 'type' => 'integer', 'required' => true, 'minimum' => 1 ],
                'context' => [ 'type' => 'string', 'default' => 'view',
                                 'enum' => [ 'view', 'edit', 'embed' ] ],
            ],
        ] );
    }
}

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

namespace Acme\Ledger\Rest;

use WP_Error;
use WP_REST_Request;
use WP_REST_Response;
use WP_REST_Server;

final class InvoiceController {

    public function index( WP_REST_Request $request ): WP_REST_Response|WP_Error {
        $per_page = (int) $request->get_param( 'per_page' );
        $page     = max( 1, (int) $request->get_param( 'page' ) );

        // Collection endpoints must cap per_page or you have handed
        // the client an unbounded query. Core caps at 100; do it too.
        $per_page = min( 100, max( 1, $per_page ) );

        $query = new \WP_Query( [
            'post_type'      => 'acme_invoice',
            'post_status'    => $this->visible_statuses( (int) $request->get_param( 'author' ) ),
            'posts_per_page' => $per_page,
            'paged'          => $page,
            'orderby'        => 'date',
            'order'          => 'DESC',
            'meta_key'       => '_acme_total_minor',   // present for ORDER BY
            'orderby'        => [ 'meta_value_num' => 'DESC' ],
        ] );

        $response = rest_ensure_response( $this->prepare_many( $query->posts, $request ) );

        $response->header( 'X-WP-Total', (string) $query->found_posts );
        $response->header( 'X-WP-TotalPages', (string) $query->max_num_pages );

        return $response;
    }

    public function create( WP_REST_Request $request ): WP_REST_Response|WP_Error {
        $lines = (array) $request->get_param( 'lines' );

        $total = 0;
        foreach ( $lines as $line ) {
            $unit = (int) ( $line['unit_minor'] ?? 0 );
            $qty  = (int) ( $line['quantity']   ?? 0 );

            if ( $unit < 0 || $qty <= 0 ) {
                return new WP_Error(
                    'acme_invalid_line',
                    __( 'Each line needs a non-negative unit price and a positive quantity.', 'acme-ledger' ),
                    [ 'status' => 400 ]
                );
            }

            $total += $unit * $qty;
        }

        $invoice = InvoiceWriter::create( [
            'customer_id'   => absint( $request->get_param( 'customer_id' ) ),
            'currency'      => (string) $request->get_param( 'currency' ),
            'total_minor'   => $total,
            'issue_immediately' => (bool) $request->get_param( 'issue' ),
        ] );

        if ( is_wp_error( $invoice ) ) {
            return $invoice;
        }

        $response = rest_ensure_response( $this->prepare_one( $invoice, $request ) );
        $response->set_status( 201 );
        $response->header( 'Location', rest_url( self::NS . '/invoices/' . $invoice->getId() ) );

        return $response;
    }
}

Three details in the second block are the difference between an endpoint that survives review and one that does not. The per_page cap is not optional; core’s own default is 10 and its maximum is 100, and an endpoint that ignores it will happily execute the query a client asked for. The visible_statuses() helper encodes the authorisation decision in the query rather than filtering after it, so a caller who may only see their own invoices never causes a query that fetches ten thousand and discards nine thousand. And the Location header on a 201 is required by the HTTP specification and is what lets a well-behaved client re-fetch the created resource through your own read endpoint, which is where the permission check lives.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

namespace Acme\Ledger\Rest;

use WP_REST_Response;
use WP_REST_Request;
use WP_REST_Server;

final class InvoiceController {

    /**
     * The serialisation layer. Field names, types and the
     * permission-based redaction all live here — never in the
     * callback, and never in the raw database row.
     */
    public function prepare_one( \WP_Post $invoice, WP_REST_Request $request ): array {
        $prepared = [
            'id'         => $invoice->ID,
            'customerId' => (int) get_post_meta( $invoice->ID, '_acme_customer_id', true ),
            'status'     => (string) get_post_meta( $invoice->ID, '_acme_status', true ),
            'totalMinor' => (int) get_post_meta( $invoice->ID, '_acme_total_minor', true ),
            'currency'   => (string) get_post_meta( $invoice->ID, '_acme_currency', true ),
            'dueDate'    => get_post_meta( $invoice->ID, '_acme_due_date', true ) ?: null,
            'html'       => [ 'rendered' => apply_filters( 'the_content', $invoice->post_content ) ],
        ];

        // Meta that exists only for privileged readers must be
        // removed here, not gated at the endpoint, because this same
        // array is embedded in core's own /wp/v2/posts response.
        if ( ! current_user_can( 'acme_view_invoice_margin' ) ) {
            unset( $prepared['marginMinor'] );
        }

        if ( 'embed' === $request->get_param( 'context' ) ) {
            return array_intersect_key( $prepared, array_flip( [ 'id', 'status', 'totalMinor', 'currency' ] ) );
        }

        return $prepared;
    }

    /** @return array<string, mixed> */
    public function get_item_schema(): array {
        return [
            '$schema'    => 'http://json-schema.org/draft-04/schema#',
            'title'      => 'acme_invoice',
            'type'       => 'object',
            'properties' => [
                'id'         => [ 'type' => 'integer', 'readonly' => true, 'context' => [ 'view', 'edit' ] ],
                'customerId' => [ 'type' => 'integer', 'context' => [ 'view', 'edit' ] ],
                'status'     => [ 'type' => 'string',  'enum' => [ 'draft', 'issued', 'paid', 'void' ] ],
                'totalMinor' => [ 'type' => 'integer', 'context' => [ 'view', 'edit' ] ],
                'currency'   => [ 'type' => 'string',  'enum' => [ 'GBP', 'EUR', 'USD' ] ],
                'dueDate'    => [ 'type' => [ 'string', 'null' ], 'format' => 'date' ],
            ],
        ];
    }
}
Argument keyEffectApplies toCommon mistake
typeCoerces and validates the scalar typeAll parametersLeaving it out; values arrive as strings
required400 if absentAll parametersNot marking a body parameter required
defaultSupplies the value when absentAll parametersDefaulting to a permissive value
enum400 unless the value is in the listStrings, integersValidating in the callback instead
minimum / maximumRange check on numbersNumbersNo bound on an id parameter
patternRegular-expression checkStringsUsing it in place of escaping
validate_callbackCustom predicate; false → 400AnythingReturning a WP_Error from it
sanitize_callbackTransforms the value after validationAnythingSanitising instead of escaping later

5.3 Authentication, permissions and CORS

Distinguishes the four authentication mechanisms WordPress supports, the separate permission layer, and the CORS configuration a cross-origin client requires.

Authentication answers "who is this"; authorisation answers "may they do this"; and in WordPress they are entirely separate mechanisms at entirely different layers. determine_current_user and the rest_authentication_errors filter establish identity, and they run before routing. permission_callback establishes permission, and it runs per route, per request, after routing. Conflating them produces the two classic defects: an endpoint that checks capabilities but is reachable without a nonce by a logged-in user whose cookies are sent automatically, and an endpoint that checks a nonce but not a capability, which any authenticated user can call.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * The four mechanisms, in the order core tries them. Note that the
 * cookie path is NOT one of them: the REST API does not use the
 * `wordpress_logged_in_*` cookie for authentication unless a plugin
 * or `rest_cookie_check_errors` explicitly allows it.
 */
// 1. Application Passwords — Basic auth, over HTTPS only,
//    hashed passwords, revocable, 6-char suffix for lookup.
//    Available to every user; can be disabled per user.
//    GET /wp/v2/users/me/&context=edit returns the profile.

// 2. Cookie + nonce — the mechanism the block editor uses.
add_filter( 'rest_authentication_errors', static function ( $result ) {
    if ( ! empty( $result ) ) {
        return $result;   // another provider already decided
    }

    if ( ! is_user_logged_in() ) {
        return $result;   // stay anonymous; routes decide what that means
    }

    $nonce = $_REQUEST['_wpnonce'] ?? $_SERVER['HTTP_X_WP_NONCE'] ?? '';

    if ( ! wp_verify_nonce( $nonce, 'wp_rest' ) ) {
        return new \WP_Error(
            'rest_cookie_invalid_nonce',
            __( 'Cookie check failed.', 'acme-ledger' ),
            [ 'status' => 403 ]
        );
    }

    return $result;   // identity established; nothing to add
}, 101 );

// 3. OAuth 2.0 / OpenID Connect — not in core. A plugin
//    (wp-rest-oauth2-provider, OpenID Connect Server, or a
//    commercial broker such as WP Engine's) is required.

// 4. An external gateway — the best option for a headless
//    front end: terminate auth at the edge, forward a
//    verified identity, and keep WordPress unreachable.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * Permission callbacks. Three rules:
 *   1. it must exist; the `__return_true` is not a default you
 *      may rely on for a private route
 *   2. it must return bool or WP_Error, never null
 *   3. it must be the FIRST thing the callback does, and the
 *      callback must not be reachable another way
 */
final class CapabilityMap {

    /** @var array<string, string> route pattern => capability */
    private const MAP = [
        'GET|list|acme-ledger/v1/invoices'        => 'read',
        'POST|create|acme-ledger/v1/invoices'      => 'acme_create_invoices',
        'GET|read|acme-ledger/v1/invoices/(?P<id>[\d]+)' => 'acme_read_invoice',
        'PUT|edit|acme-ledger/v1/invoices/(?P<id>[\d]+)'  => 'acme_edit_invoice',
        'DELETE|delete|acme-ledger/v1/invoices/(?P<id>[\d]+)' => 'acme_delete_invoice',
    ];

    public static function can_create( \WP_REST_Request $request ): bool|\WP_Error {
        if ( ! is_user_logged_in() ) {
            return new \WP_Error(
                'acme_not_authenticated',
                __( 'Authentication is required.', 'acme-ledger' ),
                [ 'status' => 401 ]
            );
        }

        if ( ! current_user_can( 'acme_create_invoices' ) ) {
            return new \WP_Error(
                'acme_cannot_create_invoice',
                __( 'You are not permitted to create invoices.', 'acme-ledger' ),
                [ 'status' => 403 ]
            );
        }

        return true;
    }

    /**
     * Object-level permission. The capability alone is never enough:
     * "can edit invoices" does not mean "can edit this invoice".
     */
    public static function can_edit( \WP_REST_Request $request ): bool|\WP_Error {
        $post = get_post( (int) $request->get_param( 'id' ) );

        if ( ! $post instanceof \WP_Post ) {
            return new \WP_Error( 'acme_not_found', __( 'Invoice not found.', 'acme-ledger' ), [ 'status' => 404 ] );
        }

        if ( 'acme_invoice' !== $post->post_type ) {
            return new \WP_Error( 'acme_not_found', __( 'Invoice not found.', 'acme-ledger' ), [ 'status' => 404 ] );
        }

        if ( ! current_user_can( 'edit_post', $post->ID ) ) {
            return new \WP_Error( 'acme_forbidden', __( 'Not permitted.', 'acme-ledger' ), [ 'status' => 403 ] );
        }

        // 404 rather than 403 for a resource the caller must not even
        // know exists — this prevents ID enumeration by status code.
        if ( 'trash' === $post->post_status && ! current_user_can( 'delete_post', $post->ID ) ) {
            return new \WP_Error( 'acme_not_found', __( 'Invoice not found.', 'acme-ledger' ), [ 'status' => 404 ] );
        }

        return true;
    }
}

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * CORS. WordPress sends no Access-Control-* headers of its own
 * unless the site is on localhost. A cross-origin browser client
 * therefore needs all of this, and `rest_send_cors_headers` runs
 * before every preflight, so the answer must be identical on both
 * the OPTIONS preflight and the actual request.
 */
add_action( 'rest_api_init', static function (): void {
    remove_filter( 'rest_pre_serve_request', 'rest_send_cors_headers' );

    add_filter( 'rest_pre_serve_request', static function ( bool $served ): bool {
        $origin = get_http_origin();

        // Allow-list, not wildcard: with an allow-list the response
        // can carry credentials, and with a wildcard it cannot.
        $allowed = [ 'https://app.acme.test', 'https://admin.acme.test' ];

        if ( ! in_array( $origin, $allowed, true ) ) {
            return $served;   // no headers; the browser blocks it
        }

        header( sprintf( 'Access-Control-Allow-Origin: %s', $origin ) );
        header( 'Access-Control-Allow-Methods: GET, POST, PUT, PATCH, DELETE, OPTIONS' );
        header( 'Access-Control-Allow-Headers: Authorization, Content-Type, X-WP-Nonce' );
        header( 'Access-Control-Expose-Headers: X-WP-Total, X-WP-TotalPages, Link' );
        header( 'Access-Control-Allow-Credentials: true' );
        header( 'Vary: Origin' );

        if ( 'OPTIONS' === $_SERVER['REQUEST_METHOD'] ?? '' ) {
            header( 'Access-Control-Max-Age: 600' );
            status_header( 204 );
        }

        return $served;
    }, 15 );
}, 11 );

Cookie authentication is only a CSRF defence when the nonce is present, and the nonce is not sent automatically. The wordpress_logged_in_* cookie is sent by the browser on every same-origin request, including a cross-site form post, which is why the block editor adds X-WP-Nonce explicitly. Any endpoint that accepts a cookie and does not require a nonce is a CSRF target, and because SameSite=Lax is the default for WordPress cookies a simple POST cross-site is still delivered. Conversely, a nonce is not proof of identity: it proves that a request was rendered by a page the server produced for a session that has since been validated, which is why wp_verify_nonce() must always be paired with a capability check on the object being acted on.

5.4 admin-ajax.php and the legacy AJAX API

Documents the dual-purpose admin-ajax.php endpoint, its non-standard return contract, and the criteria for preferring REST in new code.

wp-admin/admin-ajax.php does exactly three things: read action, fire wp_ajax_{$action} for a logged-in user or wp_ajax_nopriv_{$action} for an anonymous one, and call wp_die(). It never sets a content type, it does not serialise anything, and it loads the entire admin bootstrap including the menu system. The return value is discarded; the convention is that the handler echoes and then calls wp_die() or die(). It remains the correct choice for exactly two cases: an administrative operation on a page that already has a nonce and a capability check in place, and a request that must work on a site so old or so heavily cached that REST is not available.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * The canonical admin-ajax handler. Note what is checked before what
 * is read, and note that the response is built as a string and then
 * discarded by wp_die(), which is why the exit matters.
 */
add_action( 'wp_ajax_acme_issue_invoice', 'Acme\\Ajax\\Issue_Invoice::handle' );
add_action( 'wp_ajax_nopriv_acme_issue_invoice', '__return_false' );

final class Issue_Invoice {

    public static function handle(): void {
        check_ajax_referer( 'acme_issue_invoice', 'nonce' );

        if ( ! current_user_can( 'acme_create_invoices' ) ) {
            wp_send_json_error( [
                'code'    => 'acme_forbidden',
                'message' => __( 'Not permitted.', 'acme-ledger' ),
            ], 403 );
        }

        $customer_id = isset( $_POST['customer_id'] )
            ? absint( wp_unslash( $_POST['customer_id'] ) )
            : 0;

        if ( 0 === $customer_id || ! get_user_by( 'id', $customer_id ) ) {
            wp_send_json_error( [
                'code'    => 'acme_invalid_customer',
                'message' => __( 'Unknown customer.', 'acme-ledger' ),
            ], 400 );
        }

        $invoice = Acme\Ledger\InvoiceWriter::create( [
            'customer_id' => $customer_id,
            'currency'    => 'GBP',
        ] );

        if ( is_wp_error( $invoice ) ) {
            wp_send_json_error( [
                'code'    => $invoice->get_error_code(),
                'message' => $invoice->get_error_message(),
            ], 500 );
        }

        wp_send_json_success( [
            'id'      => $invoice->getId(),
            'editUrl' => get_edit_post_link( $invoice->getId(), 'raw' ),
        ] );
    }
}
ConcernRESTadmin-ajax.php
SerializationStructured, schema-driven JSONWhatever the handler echoes
Status codesCorrect, per routeWhatever the handler sets
Content typeapplication/jsonUnset; defaults to text/html
AuthenticationApplication password, cookie+nonce, OAuthCookie only
CSRF protectionX-WP-Nonce, required for cookie authcheck_ajax_referer()
Schema discoveryOPTIONS on every routeNone
External cacheabilityPublic responses are cacheable by designRequires a nonce, so never
Long-term supportVersioned namespaceFrozen, unversioned convention

5.5 HTTP client, caching and external data

Covers wp_remote_get() and the transport options that matter, the failure taxonomy, and a three-tier cache with negative caching for a slow dependency.

wp_remote_get() is a wrapper over the WP HTTP API, whose default transport is a stream wrapper rather than cURL, which is why it is slower than cURL by default and why setting CURLOPT_* options in a plugin has no effect unless the request opts into the cURL transport. Two options are non-negotiable on every outbound call: a timeout, and an sslverify that is either true or explicitly reasoned about. A request with no timeout will hold a PHP-FPM worker for the default of five seconds, and on a site that makes ten of them serially, that is a fifty-second request and a pool exhaustion event.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * A three-tier cache for a slow third party:
 *
 *   tier 1  transients  — shared, TTL-bounded, survives requests
 *   tier 2  stale backup — the last good value, kept longer, used
 *                        only when the upstream call fails
 *   tier 3  cold start  — a safe default from the database
 *
 * Negative caching matters as much as positive: without it, an
 * upstream outage turns every request into a 4-second timeout, and
 * that is precisely when you least want 200 concurrent workers
 * making the same doomed call.
 */
final class Fx_Rates {

    private const KEY        = 'acme:fx:rates:GBP';
    private const STALE_KEY  = 'acme:fx:rates:GBP:stale';
    private const TTL        = 6 * HOUR_IN_SECONDS;
    private const STALE_TTL  = 7 * DAY_IN_SECONDS;
    private const LOCK_TTL   = 30;

    /** @return array<string, float> */
    public function rates(): array {
        $fresh = get_transient( self::KEY );

        if ( is_array( $fresh ) ) {
            return $fresh;
        }

        // A single-worker lock, so a cold cache produces one upstream
        // call rather than two hundred.
        if ( get_transient( self::KEY . ':lock' ) ) {
            return $this->stale_or_default();
        }

        set_transient( self::KEY . ':lock', 1, self::LOCK_TTL );

        try {
            $rates = $this->fetch();

            set_transient( self::KEY, $rates, self::TTL );
            set_transient( self::STALE_KEY, $rates, self::STALE_TTL );

            return $rates;
        } catch ( \Throwable $e ) {
            // Negative cache: do not retry for another 60 seconds.
            set_transient( self::KEY, $this->stale_or_default(), 60 );

            return $this->stale_or_default();
        } finally {
            delete_transient( self::KEY . ':lock' );
        }
    }

    /** @return array<string, float> */
    private function stale_or_default(): array {
        $stale = get_transient( self::STALE_KEY );

        if ( is_array( $stale ) ) {
            return $stale;
        }

        $from_db = Acme\Ledger\Rates::last_known();

        return is_array( $from_db ) ? $from_db : [ 'GBP' => 1.0, 'EUR' => 1.16, 'USD' => 1.27 ];
    }
}

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * The block in the request path. An outbound call during page
 * rendering is a latency liability; schedule the refresh instead
 * and read the cached value, which is now sub-millisecond.
 */
add_action( 'acme_refresh_fx_rates', static function (): void {
    ( new Fx_Rates() )->rates();
}, 10, 0 );

add_filter( 'the_content', static function ( string $content ): string {
    if ( ! is_singular( 'acme_invoice' ) ) {
        return $content;
    }

    $rates = wp_cache_get( 'acme:fx:rendered', 'acme' );

    if ( false === $rates ) {
        $rates = ( new Fx_Rates() )->rates();          // memory cache, this request only
        wp_cache_set( 'acme:fx:rendered', $rates, 'acme', 300 );
    }

    return str_replace(
        '{{invoice_total_gbp}}',
        esc_html( number_format_i18n( 1250.00 ) ),
        $content
    );
} );

WP_HTTP_BLOCK_EXTERNAL is a containment control, not an optimisation. Defined as true with WP_ACCESSIBLE_HOSTS naming only the hosts you expect, it causes every other outbound request to fail fast with a blocked_request error instead of reaching a network the attacker chose. It is one of the highest-value settings on a WordPress installation precisely because it limits the blast radius of a server-side request forgery, of a compromised dependency that phones home, and of a plugin that turns your server into a relay. The cost is that you must remember to add hosts, and a forgotten host produces a blocked_request in the log rather than a visible failure on the page.

5.6 Headless WordPress and content delivery

Treats headless as an architectural commitment with specific costs, and covers the two endpoints that exist solely to support it.

Going headless means WordPress becomes an editing surface and an API, and something else renders. The trade is real and should be made explicitly. You gain a rendering layer you fully control, deployment on a CDN, and a rendering model that is not PHP. You lose the entire template hierarchy, every the_content filter, all server-side shortcodes, most of the plugin ecosystem, and the ability for a marketing user to see a preview. The determining question is not technical — it is whether the front end needs any of what you gave away. If the answer is that it does not, headless is a sound choice; if the answer is that it probably will, a block theme with a good caching layer is usually less total work.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * Publishing, for a headless consumer. Two mechanisms, and they
 * mean different things:
 *
 *  - `post_updated` fires in-process. Use it to call a build hook.
 *    It fires on autosave too, so debounce.
 *  - WP_Application_Passwords is not it. Webhooks are not core.
 *    A real deployment either polls, or runs a mu-plugin that
 *    POSTs to the build service.
 */
add_action( 'wp_after_insert_post', static function (
    int $post_id,
    \WP_Post $post,
    bool $update,
    \WP_Post $post_before
): void {
    if ( wp_is_post_revision( $post_id ) || wp_is_post_autosave( $post_id ) ) {
        return;
    }

    if ( 'acme_invoice' !== $post->post_type && 'post' !== $post->post_type ) {
        return;
    }

    $payload = [
        'event'      => $update ? 'updated' : 'created',
        'id'         => $post_id,
        'slug'       => $post->post_name,
        'type'       => $post->post_type,
        'status'     => $post->post_status,
        'modified'   => get_post_modified_time( 'c', true, $post_id ),
        'buildHook'  => DEPLOY_HOOK_URL,
    ];

    // Fire and forget. A slow build service must not be able to
    // make a save request time out.
    wp_schedule_single_event( time(), 'acme_notify_deploy', [ $payload ] );
}, 10, 4 );

A headless WordPress is a WordPress that can still be logged into. Removing the front end does not remove the admin, and the admin is the larger attack surface: the block editor loads a large JavaScript bundle, the media library handles uploads, and /wp/v2/settings, /wp/v2/plugins and /wp/v2/users are authenticated endpoints. If the front end is genuinely headless, the honest hardening steps are to block the admin by IP or by identity-aware proxy, to disable the file and plugin editors, to require application passwords and disable cookie authentication for the API, and to keep DISALLOW_FILE_MODS defined. A headless deployment with a publicly reachable /wp-admin/ has traded a smaller attack surface for a larger one.

Exercises

  1. Build a versioned REST surface. Implement acme-ledger/v1 with an invoice collection, a single-invoice read, a create and a status transition, each with a permission callback, typed arguments and a documented schema. Then implement v2 with one deliberate breaking change and both served simultaneously. Deliverable: the plugin, a Postman collection, and an OpenAPI document generated from OPTIONS responses on every route.
  2. Prove the permission layer. Write a test matrix of nine user types (anonymous, subscriber, contributor, author, editor, administrator, custom billing role, plus two roles you invent) against eleven routes, asserting the exact status code and error code for each combination. Deliverable: the matrix as a PHP data provider, a test producing a pass/fail grid, and a written analysis of every cell that returned 200 where you expected 403.
  3. Add CORS and verify it in a browser. Configure the allow-list, the preflight and the credential policy, then verify with a real cross-origin page that a credentialed request succeeds and that an origin outside the allow-list fails. Deliverable: the filter, a Playwright test asserting both cases, and a documented decision on whether the front end will use cookies or application passwords.
  4. Build a resilient HTTP client. Implement the three-tier cache, the single-flight lock and the negative cache from §5.5, then point it at a local stub that fails 100% of requests for 30 seconds. Deliverable: a load test showing that the stub outage adds under 5 ms to p99 request time, a metrics panel with cache hit rate, lock contention and upstream error rate, and an alert on negative-cache entries exceeding 5% of lookups.
  5. Prototype and then reject or adopt headless. Build a minimal headless front end — one page type, three block types, preview support — against the real REST API. Measure the total engineering cost, the content-editor experience and the plugin compatibility. Deliverable: a working prototype, a written comparison against a block-theme implementation of the same design, and a recommendation with the two or three specific requirements that would change it.

6 · Security: Escaping, Nonces, Capabilities and Sanitisation

6.1 SQL injection and the prepare() contract6.2 Output escaping and XSS6.3 Nonces, CSRF and request intent6.4 Capabilities, roles and authorisation order6.5 File uploads and MIME validation6.6 Hardening, headers and the wp-config.php surface

This chapter is the one to return to. It treats security as a set of ordering rules rather than a checklist of functions, because every function in it is easy to use correctly once and easy to omit once in a hurry. The organising principle is the sequence authorise, then verify, then sanitise, then escape, then prepare — and the argument is that almost every real WordPress vulnerability is a violation of that order rather than a failure of any individual function. It covers the two things WordPress users most often get wrong ($wpdb->prepare() is not a general-purpose escape, and a nonce is not a CSRF token), the difference that causes the most privilege-escalation reports (capability versus role), and the upload path, where the extension is a claim by the client and the MIME type is the only evidence.

6.1 SQL injection and the prepare() contract

Shows the injection in a realistic query, defines precisely what prepare() guarantees, and explains the identifier problem it cannot solve.

SQL injection through $wpdb is not a theoretical class in WordPress; it is the most common high-severity finding in plugin audits, and it survives because the string it looks like is idiomatic. WordPress predates the widespread use of prepared-statement APIs in application code, and the $wpdb abstraction is a query-string builder with escaping helpers rather than a bound-parameter interface. prepare() does implement real escaping — it calls escape_by_ref() on each substituted value, which in turn calls $wpdb->escape() — but it escapes values, and it refuses to escape anything it has not been told is a value. The only thing standing between a concatenated query and an attacker is the developer’s decision to call prepare() at all.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * The vulnerability, written the way it appears in real plugins.
 * Four separate injections, one of which is in a place reviewers
 * look least often: the `orderby`, which is not a value and is
 * therefore never passed to prepare() even by careless developers
 * who know they should.
 */
global $wpdb;

$search  = $_GET['q'];             // attacker: 1' UNION SELECT user_login,user_pass FROM wp_users--
$status  = $_GET['status'];        // attacker: publish' OR 1=1--
$order   = $_GET['order'];         // attacker: (SELECT user_pass FROM wp_users LIMIT 1)
$page    = (int) $_GET['page'];    // cast, so safe

$rows = $wpdb->get_results( "SELECT p.ID, p.post_title
                                  FROM {$wpdb->posts} p
                                 WHERE p.post_title LIKE '%{$search}%'
                                   AND p.post_status = '{$status}'
                                 ORDER BY {$order}
                                 LIMIT 20 OFFSET " . ( $page * 20 ) );

if ( $rows ) {
    foreach ( $rows as $row ) {
        echo '<li>' . $row->post_title . '</li>';   // also XSS, see §6.2
    }
}

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

global $wpdb;

/**
 * The correct form. Every value is a placeholder; every placeholder
 * is bound by position; the ORDER BY is chosen from an allow-list
 * because it is not a value at all.
 */
$allowed_order = [ 'date' => 'p.post_date', 'title' => 'p.post_title' ];
$order_by      = $allowed_order[ sanitize_key( $_GET['order'] ?? 'date' ) ] ?? 'p.post_date';
$direction     = 'ASC' === strtoupper( (string) ( $_GET['dir'] ?? 'DESC' ) ) ? 'ASC' : 'DESC';

$rows = $wpdb->get_results( $wpdb->prepare(
    "SELECT p.ID, p.post_title
       FROM {$wpdb->posts} p
      WHERE p.post_title LIKE %s
        AND p.post_status = %s
        AND p.post_type = %s
      ORDER BY {$order_by} {$direction}
      LIMIT %d OFFSET %d",
    '%' . $wpdb->esc_like( (string) wp_unslash( $_GET['q'] ?? '' ) ) . '%',
    (string) ( $_GET['status'] ?? 'publish' ),
    'post',
    20,
    $page * 20
) );

// Note the two-part handling of LIKE. `esc_like()` escapes
// the LIKE metacharacters (%, _, \) so that a user's "%"
// does not become a wildcard. Then `prepare()` escapes the
// result for the SQL string context. Using one without
// the other is a bug: esc_like() alone leaves quotes live, and
// prepare() alone lets the user inject wildcards.

The second block contains three distinct protections and it is worth naming them. prepare() guarantees the substituted values cannot terminate the string literal they are inside. esc_like() guarantees the user’s characters are interpreted as a literal substring rather than as % or _ wildcards. And the allow-list guarantees that the ORDER BY and the direction — which are grammar, not data, and which prepare() has no placeholder type for — can only be one of a fixed set of tokens. A plugin that misses any one of the three is not automatically vulnerable, but a plugin that misses all three is, and that combination is what audits find.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

global $wpdb;

/**
 * The identifier problem. `prepare()` has %s, %d and %f. It has no
 * %i and no %w, and this is not an oversight that will be fixed:
 * MySQL does not permit a bound parameter in an identifier position,
 * so no prepared-statement API can do it either. WordPress's own
 * answer is to interpolate the identifier and accept the risk, which
 * is only acceptable when the identifier provably cannot be
 * attacker-controlled.
 *
 * Safe:  the identifier comes from a hard-coded map.
 * Unsafe: the identifier comes from a request, even "sanitised".
 */
$sort_columns = [
    'total'  => 'pm.meta_value',
    'due'    => 'due.due_date',
    'issued' => 'p.post_date',
];

$requested = sanitize_key( (string) wp_unslash( $_GET['sort'] ?? '' ) );

if ( ! isset( $sort_columns[ $requested ] ) ) {
    error_log( sprintf( '[acme/security] rejected sort column: %s', $requested ) );
    wp_die( 'Invalid sort column.', 400 );
}

$sort_sql = $sort_columns[ $requested ];   // now provably one of three tokens

$rows = $wpdb->get_results( $wpdb->prepare(
    "SELECT p.ID, pm.meta_value AS total
       FROM {$wpdb->posts} p
       INNER JOIN {$wpdb->postmeta} pm ON pm.post_id = p.ID AND pm.meta_key = %s
       WHERE p.post_type = %s
      ORDER BY {$sort_sql} DESC
      LIMIT %d",
    '_acme_total_minor',
    'acme_invoice',
    50
) );

Do not double-escape. Passing a value through esc_sql() and then through a %s placeholder escapes the backslashes a second time, so a value containing O’Reilly is stored as O\\'Reilly and subsequently fails to match. Likewise, passing wp_unslash() output of an already-unslashed value strips legitimate backslashes from user content such as a Windows path. Pick one representation at the boundary — superglobals arrive slashed, so wp_unslash() exactly once, and let prepare() do all escaping from there. A plugin that cannot state which representation a value is in at the point of use has a bug waiting.

6.2 Output escaping and XSS

Separates sanitisation on input from escaping on output, gives the context map, and explains why stored HTML is a capability decision rather than a formatting one.

The distinction is procedural and it is absolute. Sanitising on input removes or transforms characters before the value is stored, which is appropriate for a form field that must be a slug, an email address or an integer. Escaping on output makes a value safe in the specific context it is being written into, which is appropriate for everything else — and "everything else" includes values that were sanitised on input, because the storage context and the output context are not the same. A value sanitised to int on input needs no escaping; a value sanitised to text on input may be written into HTML, into an attribute, into a JavaScript string or into a URL, and each of those needs a different function.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * Stored XSS, the most common high-severity finding in plugins.
 *
 * The bug is not one line. It is the combination of: a field
 * accepted from a request, a sanitiser that was chosen for a
 * different context, and an output statement with no context
 * awareness. Any one of the three would have prevented it.
 */
add_action( 'save_post_acme_invoice', static function ( int $post_id ): void {
    if ( wp_is_post_revision( $post_id ) || ! current_user_can( 'edit_post', $post_id ) ) {
        return;
    }

    // `sanitize_text_field()` strips tags and some whitespace. It
    // does NOT make the value safe in an attribute, in a URL, or in
    // a JavaScript string — which is where it ends up below.
    $reference = isset( $_POST['acme_reference'] )
        ? sanitize_text_field( wp_unslash( $_POST['acme_reference'] ) )
        : '';

    update_post_meta( $post_id, '_acme_reference', $reference );
}, 10, 1 );

// Output, in three places, none of which escapes for context:
echo $reference;                                        // text node
printf( '<input value="%s">', $reference );        // attribute
printf( '<a href="/pay/%s">', $reference );        // URL segment
printf( '<script>var ref = "%s";</script>', $reference );  // JS string

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * The correct output, escaped per context at the point of output.
 * Note that the value is unchanged between storage and output —
 * the database holds what the user typed, minus tags.
 */
$reference = (string) get_post_meta( $post_id, '_acme_reference', true );

esc_html( $reference );                                   // text node
printf( '<input value="%s">', esc_attr( $reference ) );   // attribute
printf( '<a href="%s">', esc_url( '/pay/' . rawurlencode( $reference ) ) );
printf( '<script>var ref = %s;</script>', wp_json_encode( $reference ) );

/**
 * A template, done once and done correctly. The helper takes the
 * value, the context, and nothing else — so there is no way to
 * reach for the wrong function by accident.
 */
function acme_e( string $value, string $context = 'html' ): string {
    return match ( $context ) {
        'html'   => esc_html( $value ),
        'attr'   => esc_attr( $value ),
        'url'    => esc_url( $value ),
        'urlraw' => esc_url_raw( $value ),
        'js'     => wp_json_encode( $value, JSON_HEX_TAG | JSON_HEX_AMP | JSON_HEX_APOS | JSON_HEX_QUOT ),
        'text'   => esc_textarea( $value ),
        default  => throw new \InvalidArgumentException( 'Unknown context: ' . $context ),
    };
}
FunctionUse forEscapesWrong when
esc_htmlText node content& < > " 'Wrapping a URL or an attribute
esc_attrAny attribute valueAs above, plus encodingUsed for text node content (harmless but noisy)
esc_urlhref, src, actionEntities plus protocol allow-listApplied to a non-URL string
esc_url_rawURL for storage or comparisonEntities, no protocol filterOutput directly; it does not filter protocols
esc_jsInline JavaScript stringQuotes, newlines, HTML tagsUsed instead of wp_json_encode
esc_textarea<textarea> bodyesc_html plus form entitiesAny other element
wp_ksesRich content with an allow-listRemoves disallowed tags and attributesUsed with $allowedtags global
wp_json_encodeAny value into JavaScriptCorrect JSON, no </script> break-outConcatenating into a JS string literal
absintIntegersCastsApplied to a non-numeric value you still need
esc_sqlNothing, in your own codeQuote-escape onlyEverywhere. Use prepare()
 
<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * wp_kses is the correct answer when content must be rich, and its
 * cost is that the allow-list is a security decision, not a
 * formatting one. The defaults are too permissive for user-supplied
 * content: `$allowedtags` includes `style` on most elements, which
 * permits CSS-based data exfiltration and clickjacking overlays.
 */
function acme_allowed_rich_text(): array {
    return apply_filters( 'acme_allowed_rich_text', [
        'a'      => [
            'href'   => true,
            'title'  => true,
            'target' => true,
            'rel'    => true,
        ],
        'strong' => [ 'class' => true ],
        'em'     => [ 'class' => true ],
        'b'      => [ 'class' => true ],
        'i'      => [ 'class' => true ],
        'p'      => [ 'class' => true ],
        'ul'     => [ 'class' => true ],
        'ol'     => [ 'class' => true ],
        'li'     => [ 'class' => true ],
        'br'     => [],
        'code'   => [ 'class' => true ],
        'blockquote' => [ 'cite' => true, 'class' => true ],
        'span'   => [ 'class' => true ],
        'h2'     => [ 'class' => true ],
        'h3'     => [ 'class' => true ],
        'h4'     => [ 'class' => true ],
    ] );
}

$clean = wp_kses( $submitted, acme_allowed_rich_text(), [ 'https', 'http', 'mailto' ] );

// `target="_blank"` without `rel` is a reverse-tabnabbing
// vector. Add it, always, wherever you allow target:
$clean = str_replace( 'target="_blank"', 'target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"', $clean );

Storing escaped HTML is the defect that survives longest. The moment a value is escaped before storage, it is a double-encoded string in the database, and every subsequent use must know not to escape it again — a rule no team can enforce across a codebase and a five-year maintenance horizon. It also breaks the value: &amp; in a URL, &quot; in a JSON payload, and mojibake in anything that is not rendered through an HTML parser. The correct discipline is the boring one: store what the user typed, sanitised only to the type the field is defined as, and escape at every point of output according to the context of that output. If a field genuinely contains HTML, store HTML and filter it with wp_kses() on the way in, which is a different and correct design.

6.3 Nonces, CSRF and request intent

Explains what a WordPress nonce actually is, why its lifetime is 24 hours rather than a session, and what it is and is not a defence against.

A WordPress nonce is not a random token. It is an HMAC over four values — the action string, the current user ID, the NONCE_KEY and NONCE_SALT, and the current tick — using wp_hash() and truncated to ten characters. Three consequences follow, and each one is routinely misunderstood. First, a nonce is bounded in time: the tick advances every half day, so a nonce is valid for up to 24 hours and can expire sooner, which is why the value is cached and why a long-lived page can present a nonce that has just expired. Second, a nonce is user-bound: it changes for every user, so a nonce generated for one session cannot be replayed by another. Third, a nonce is action-bound: the same user gets a different nonce for a different action string, which is what stops a nonce minted for a read-only AJAX call from authorising a destructive one.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * The complete CSRF defence, in order. Removing any step is a
 * vulnerability; the order matters because the cheapest checks
 * should reject first.
 */
final class Issue_Invoice_Endpoint {

    public function handle(): void {
        // STEP 1 — Is there a session at all? Cheapest, rejects
        // the overwhelming majority of cross-site requests, and
        // does not touch the database beyond the cookie parse.
        if ( ! is_user_logged_in() ) {
            wp_send_json_error( [ 'code' => 'acme_not_authenticated' ], 401 );
        }

        // STEP 2 — Did this request originate from a page this
        // server rendered for this user? The nonce is verified
        // BEFORE any request data is read, because verification
        // must not be a function of attacker-controlled input.
        $nonce = isset( $_REQUEST['nonce'] )
            ? sanitize_text_field( wp_unslash( $_REQUEST['nonce'] ) )
            : '';

        if ( ! wp_verify_nonce( $nonce, 'acme_issue_invoice' ) ) {
            wp_send_json_error( [
                'code'    => 'acme_invalid_nonce',
                'message' => __( 'Your session expired. Reload the page.', 'acme-ledger' ),
            ], 403 );
        }

        // STEP 3 — Is this user allowed to do this? A nonce proves
        // intent, never authority: it is issued to any logged-in
        // user who can load the page, including a subscriber.
        if ( ! current_user_can( 'acme_create_invoices' ) ) {
            wp_send_json_error( [ 'code' => 'acme_forbidden' ], 403 );
        }

        // STEP 4 — Only now read and validate the payload.
        $customer_id = isset( $_POST['customer_id'] )
            ? absint( wp_unslash( $_POST['customer_id'] ) )
            : 0;

        $invoice = Acme\Ledger\InvoiceWriter::create( [ 'customer_id' => $customer_id ] );

        if ( is_wp_error( $invoice ) ) {
            wp_send_json_error( [
                'code'    => $invoice->get_error_code(),
                'message' => $invoice->get_error_message(),
            ], 400 );
        }

        wp_send_json_success( [ 'id' => $invoice->getId() ] );
    }
}

What a nonce is for, stated precisely: it proves that the request was rendered by a page this server produced for a user who has since been authenticated. That is a statement about provenance, and it is what distinguishes a genuine submission from a cross-site one. What a nonce is not: it is not unpredictable to the user, since the user can read it from the page source; it is not a secret; it is not an authorisation mechanism, because it is issued to anyone who can load the page; and it is not a substitute for SameSite cookies, which stop the request being sent at all rather than rejecting it after the fact. It is also not a defence against a same-origin XSS, because an XSS payload in the page can simply read the nonce and include it — which is why escaping (§6.2) and nonces are complements rather than alternatives.

6.4 Capabilities, roles and authorisation order

Explains the meta capability mapping, why role checks are the wrong primitive, and the three-layer authorisation model a post-type-owning plugin needs.

WordPress has two layers, and confusing them produces most authorisation defects. Roles are named bundles of capabilities stored in an option; capabilities are the atomic permissions that code actually tests. Every role check is therefore a check for a particular bundling, and a site with a single custom role or a role-editing plugin will have a different bundling. The mapping layer sits on top: meta capabilities such as edit_post, delete_post and read_post are not stored anywhere; map_meta_cap() computes them from the post’s author, status and type, given the primitive capabilities the user holds. This is why current_user_can( 'edit_posts' ) is the wrong test for "may this user edit this post" — it is a statement about the user’s general ability to edit content of that type, and it says nothing about ownership or status.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * The three-layer model. Every authorisation decision in a
 * post-type-owning plugin is a composition of all three, and a
 * missing layer is a specific, nameable class of bug.
 *
 *   layer 1  CAPABILITY  may this ROLE do this OPERATION at all?
 *            current_user_can( 'acme_edit_invoices' )
 *
 *   layer 2  OBJECT      may this USER do it to THIS OBJECT?
 *            current_user_can( 'edit_post', $id )
 *
 *   layer 3  CONTEXT     is this request in a state where the
 *            operation makes sense — published, not trashed,
 *            owned by this customer, not already settled?
 */
final class Invoice_Guard {

    public static function assert( string $operation, int $invoice_id ): void {
        $capability = match ( $operation ) {
            'read'   => 'acme_read_invoices',
            'create' => 'acme_create_invoices',
            'update' => 'acme_edit_invoices',
            'delete' => 'acme_delete_invoices',
            'issue'  => 'acme_issue_invoices',
            default  => throw new \InvalidArgumentException( $operation ),
        };

        // Layer 1.
        if ( ! current_user_can( $capability ) ) {
            throw new \WP_Error( 'acme_forbidden', __( 'Not permitted.', 'acme-ledger' ), [ 'status' => 403 ] );
        }

        $post = get_post( $invoice_id );

        if ( ! $post instanceof \WP_Post || 'acme_invoice' !== $post->post_type ) {
            throw new \WP_Error( 'acme_not_found', __( 'Invoice not found.', 'acme-ledger' ), [ 'status' => 404 ] );
        }

        // Layer 2.
        if ( ! current_user_can( 'edit_post', $invoice_id ) ) {
            throw new \WP_Error( 'acme_forbidden', __( 'Not permitted.', 'acme-ledger' ), [ 'status' => 403 ] );
        }

        // Layer 3.
        if ( in_array( $post->post_status, [ 'paid', 'void' ], true ) ) {
            throw new \WP_Error(
                'acme_immutable_state',
                __( 'A settled invoice cannot be modified.', 'acme-ledger' ),
                [ 'status' => 409 ]
            );
        }
    }
}
CheckQuestion answeredWrong useCorrect use
current_user_can( 'read' )Is any authenticated userGating a data readDistinguishing public from private output
current_user_can( 'edit_post', $id )May this user edit this postGating a list tableEvery single-object operation
current_user_can( 'edit_posts' )May create own posts of the typeGating an edit of a specific postShowing a "new invoice" button
current_user_can( 'edit_others_posts' )May edit any user’s postsGating your own postShowing an edit link in a list of others
current_user_can( 'read_private_posts' )May read private posts of the typeGating a public readDeciding status visibility in a query
current_user_can( 'manage_categories' )May manage any categoryGating assignmentThe term admin UI
in_array( 'editor', $roles )Nothing reliableEverythingNothing

An IDOR is a missing layer two, not a missing layer one. The most common privilege bug in a correctly-guarded WordPress plugin is a handler that checks current_user_can( 'edit_invoices' ) and then acts on whatever ID was in the request. The user is authorised; the object is not. Every parameter that identifies a record is therefore an authorisation decision, and the ID must be fetched and tested against the current user before any property of it is read — before the title, before the metadata, and before the response code reveals that it exists. Returning 403 rather than 404 for a record that exists but is not yours is a deliberate choice: it is friendlier, and it does confirm existence to an attacker who is enumerating IDs.

6.5 File uploads and MIME validation

Walks the full upload path, explains why the extension cannot be trusted, and shows how to validate the actual content of a file before it reaches the uploads directory.

A file arrives with a name the client chose and a type the client declared, and WordPress’s job is to decide what it actually is. The extension is a claim: it is written by the uploading client and is under no obligation to match the bytes. The content type declared in the multipart body is also a claim. The only evidence is the file’s magic number, read by finfo or by WordPress’s own wp_check_filetype_and_ext() implementation, which reads the first bytes and compares them against a table. Any upload path that validates only the extension will accept a PHP file renamed to .jpg, and because wp-content/uploads is under the document root, that is remote code execution.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * The upload path, in order. `wp_handle_upload` performs the real
 * checks; `$_FILES` alone performs none of them.
 *
 *   1. `upload_mimes` filter   — the extension => type map. This
 *      is a FILTER and therefore permissive: anything added to it
 *      becomes allowed. That is the number one upload misconfig.
 *   2. `wp_check_filetype_and_ext( $file, $filename, $mimes )`
 *      — reads the magic number. Returns real_ext | ext | type,
 *      or false.
 *   3. `wp_check_filetype( $filename, $mimes )` — EXTENSION ONLY.
 *      Never use this alone; it is the function that appears in
 *      almost every vulnerable upload handler.
 *   4. `wp_handle_upload` — moves the file, applies the year/month
 *      path, and refuses on `test_form` and `test_type`.
 *   5. `wp_unique_filename` — appends a random suffix. Does NOT
 *      randomise the base name; `wp_unique_filename` keeps it.
 */
$file = $_FILES['statement'] ?? null;

if ( ! is_array( $file ) || UPLOAD_ERR_OK !== ( $file['error'] ?? UPLOAD_ERR_NO_FILE ) ) {
    throw new \RuntimeException( 'Upload failed.' );
}

require_once ABSPATH . 'wp-admin/includes/file.php';
require_once ABSPATH . 'wp-admin/includes/media.php';

// An explicit allow-list beats the global mimes map, because the
// global map is shared with every other plugin on the site.
$allowed = [
    'pdf'  => 'application/pdf',
    'csv'  => 'text/csv',
    'jpg|jpeg' => 'image/jpeg',
    'png'  => 'image/png',
];

$check = wp_check_filetype_and_ext( $file['tmp_name'], $file['name'], $allowed );

if ( false === $check['ext'] || false === $check['type'] ) {
    throw new \RuntimeException( 'Unsupported file type.' );
}

// Belt and braces: confirm the extension WordPress derived
// matches the one the client claimed, and that the mime
// is one of ours. Both must hold.
if ( ! array_key_exists( $check['ext'], $allowed ) || $check['type'] !== $allowed[ $check['ext'] ] ) {
    throw new \RuntimeException( 'File type mismatch.' );
}

wp_handle_upload( $file, [ 'test_form' => false, 'mimes' => $allowed ] );

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * The MIME map is a filter, and a filter is permissive. This is
 * how "any file type is allowed" happens on a site that has
 * installed four plugins, none of which intended it.
 */
add_filter( 'upload_mimes', static function ( array $mimes ): array {
    // A plugin that adds a type to upload_mimes has just enabled
    // uploads of that type SITE-WIDE, for every user with
    // upload_files, including contributors. Before adding one,
    // ask what else it enables: 'x-msdownload' allows .exe, and
    // so does anything that maps to application/octet-stream.
    return $mimes;
}, 10, 1 );

/**
 * Test the claim, not the name. A file whose magic number does not
 * match its extension is either corrupt or hostile; both deserve
 * the same treatment, and the distinction is not worth making.
 */
function acme_assert_real_type( string $tmp_path, string $expected_ext ): bool {
    if ( ! is_uploaded_file( $tmp_path ) ) {
        return false;
    }

    $finfo = new \finfo( FILEINFO_MIME_TYPE );
    $actual = $finfo->file( $tmp_path );

    $map = [
        'pdf'  => [ 'application/pdf' ],
        'csv'  => [ 'text/csv', 'text/plain', 'application/csv' ],
        'jpg'  => [ 'image/jpeg' ],
        'jpeg' => [ 'image/jpeg' ],
        'png'  => [ 'image/png' ],
        'webp' => [ 'image/webp' ],
        'svg'  => [ 'image/svg+xml', 'text/xml', 'application/xml' ],
    ];

    return in_array( $actual, $map[ $expected_ext ] ?? [], true );
}
CheckReadsTrustworthyUse for
$_FILES['type']Client’s claimNo — fully attacker-controlledNothing
pathinfo() extensionClient’s claimNoChoosing which content-type to serve
wp_check_filetype()Extension onlyNoValidating an already-verified file
wp_check_filetype_and_ext()Magic number plus extensionYes, for known typesThe primary upload check
finfo::file()Magic numberYes, independent of WordPressConfirming, and covering unknown types
getimagesize()Image structureYes for imagesRejecting a polyglot image/script
Server configurationExecution rulesYesMaking execution impossible

SVG is an XSS vector, not an image format. An SVG is an XML document that may contain <script>, onload handlers and external references, and WordPress by default does not allow it to be uploaded — but a large number of plugins add image/svg+xml to the upload_mimes map, and the result is a stored XSS that renders in the site’s own origin. If you must accept SVG, sanitise it with a dedicated library, store it outside the document root, and serve it from a separate origin with a Content-Security-Policy. The cheaper decision is to not accept it. The same reasoning applies to .html, .htm, .shtml, .xml and anything that a browser will execute when navigated to directly.

6.6 Hardening, headers and the wp-config.php surface

Collects the configuration-level controls, states which of them are load-bearing, and distinguishes the ones that actually raise the cost of compromise from the ones that only make you feel better.

Hardening is a set of independent controls, each of which removes a specific capability an attacker would use. The most valuable are the ones that convert a whole category of attack from possible to impossible, and those are consistently the boring ones: define DISALLOW_FILE_MODS so an admin session cannot write PHP; deny PHP execution under uploads so an upload cannot execute; define WP_HTTP_BLOCK_EXTERNAL so a request forgery cannot reach anything; require application passwords so a browser session cannot drive the API; and keep the salts rotated and unique so a leaked cookie cannot be replayed. Everything else — hiding the version, disabling the generator tag, removing the readme — is noise that costs an attacker seconds and costs you a debugging afternoon.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

/**
 * A hardening profile that is safe to ship by default. Each entry
 * is here because it removes a capability, not because it is best
 * practice. Entries commented out are the ones that cause support
 * tickets, and are listed so the decision is explicit.
 */

// --- Identity and credentials -------------------------------------
// define( 'DISALLOW_FILE_EDIT', true );   no theme/plugin editor
// define( 'DISALLOW_FILE_MODS', true );   no install, no update UI
// define( 'FORCE_SSL_ADMIN', true );      admin over HTTPS only
// define( 'WP_HTTP_BLOCK_EXTERNAL', true ); + WP_ACCESSIBLE_HOSTS
// define( 'WP_AUTO_UPDATE_CORE', 'minor' );

// --- Information disclosure --------------------------------------
// Version hiding: the `readme.html` and `license.txt` in the root
// are deleted by the deployment, not hidden by a plugin. Removing
// the generator tag is cosmetic; do not spend a hook on it.
add_filter( 'the_generator', '__return_empty_string' );
remove_action( 'wp_head', 'wp_generator' );
remove_action( 'wp_head', 'rsd_link' );
remove_action( 'wp_head', 'wlwmanifest_link' );
remove_action( 'wp_head', 'wp_shortlink_wp_head' );
remove_action( 'wp_head', 'print_emoji_detection_script', 7 );
remove_action( 'wp_print_styles', 'print_emoji_styles' );

// --- Sessions and cookies -----------------------------------------
// Cookie flags are set by wp_set_auth_cookie() in core; the two
// worth overriding are the lifetime and the secure flag on a
// TLS-terminated deployment behind a proxy.
add_filter( 'auth_cookie_expiration', static fn ( int $l ): int => 4 * DAY_IN_SECONDS, 99 );
add_filter( 'secure_signon_cookie', '__return_true' );

// --- Error visibility ---------------------------------------------
// NEVER display errors in production. WP_DEBUG_DISPLAY false plus
// display_errors off plus log_errors on is the combination that
// keeps a stack trace out of a response body and in the log file.

# Apache 2.4: the controls that belong in .htaccess rather than PHP.
# Enabled in the vhost; AllowOverride limited to these directives.

<IfModule mod_rewrite.c>
RewriteEngine On

# Canonical HTTPS and host, before WordPress sees anything.
RewriteCond %{HTTPS} !=on
RewriteCond %{HTTP:X-Forwarded-Proto} !https
RewriteRule ^ https://%{HTTP_HOST}%{REQUEST_URI} [L,R=301]

# Deny the files whose existence is itself the risk.
RewriteRule ^wp-config\.php$  - [F,L]
RewriteRule ^readme\.html$   - [F,L]
RewriteRule ^license\.txt$   - [F,L]
RewriteRule ^wp-content/uploads/.*\.(php|phar|phtml|pl|py|cgi|sh)$ - [F,L]
RewriteRule ^wp-content/cache/.*\.php$        - [F,L]
RewriteRule ^wp-content/upgrade/.* - [F,L]

# Do not serve dotfiles, backups or anything that is not a real asset.
RewriteRule (^|/)\.  - [F,L]
RewriteRule \.(bak|old|orig|save|swp|swo|tmp|log|ini)$ - [F,L]

# Disable directory indexing everywhere.
Options -Indexes -ExecCGI -Includes

RewriteCond %{REQUEST_FILENAME} !-f
RewriteCond %{REQUEST_FILENAME} !-d
RewriteRule . /index.php [L]
</IfModule>

<IfModule mod_headers.c>
Header always set X-Content-Type-Options "nosniff"
Header always set X-Frame-Options "SAMEORIGIN"
Header always set Referrer-Policy "strict-origin-when-cross-origin"
Header always set Permissions-Policy "geolocation=(), microphone=(), camera=(), interest-cohort=()"
Header always set Strict-Transport-Security "max-age=63072000; includeSubDomains; preload"
Header always unset X-Powered-By
Header always unset Server
Header edit .htaccess RCE
</IfModule>

<?php
/**
 * wp-config.php — the surface worth getting right, beyond the
 * credentials. Each of these is a real control, not a default.
 */

// 1. Stop serving PHP from the uploads directory. This is the
//    single highest-value line in the file for a site that allows
//    any form of user upload.
define( 'DISALLOW_FILE_MODS', true );

// 2. Pin the content directory if the theme and plugins are shipped
//    from a versioned path outside the document root. Then
//    WP_CONTENT_DIR and WP_CONTENT_URL are not derived from ABSPATH.
define( 'WP_CONTENT_DIR', '/srv/acme/releases/12.4.0/wp-content' );
define( 'WP_CONTENT_URL', 'https://static.acme.test/releases/12.4.0/wp-content' );

// 3. Absolutely no outbound request the application did not intend.
define( 'WP_HTTP_BLOCK_EXTERNAL', true );
define( 'WP_ACCESSIBLE_HOSTS', 'api.stripe.com,api.acme.test,*.gravatar.com' );

// 4. Auto-updates limited to minor, and only over HTTPS.
define( 'WP_AUTO_UPDATE_CORE', 'minor' );

// 5. Error handling: nothing on screen, everything in the log.
define( 'WP_DEBUG', false );
define( 'WP_DEBUG_LOG', '/var/log/wp/php-error.log' );
define( 'WP_DEBUG_DISPLAY', false );
@ini_set( 'display_errors', '0' );
@ini_set( 'log_errors', '1' );

// 6. Multisite: the subdirectory install needs these three, and
//    getting them wrong produces an infinite redirect loop that
//    looks like a WordPress bug and is a configuration bug.
if ( is_multisite() ) {
    define( 'SUBDOMAIN_INSTALL', false );
    define( 'DOMAIN_CURRENT_SITE', 'acme.test' );
    define( 'PATH_CURRENT_SITE', '/' );
    define( 'SITE_ID_CURRENT_SITE', 1 );
    define( 'BLOG_ID_CURRENT_SITE', 1 );
}

// 7. Fatal errors that are not fatal, so a parse error in a plugin
//    file does not take the site down.
define( 'WP_Fatal_Error_Handler_Enabled', true );

Exercises

  1. Build an injection test harness. Write a test that, for each of the thirty query methods in your own codebase, substitutes a canonical payload for every request-sourced parameter and asserts that the result set is unchanged. Include the LIKE, the ORDER BY and the identifier cases separately. Deliverable: the harness, a per-method report showing the current state of each, and a fixed version of every method that fails.
  2. Prove the escaping contract. Take one template and render it with an XSS corpus of sixty payloads — tag injection, attribute break-out, URL scheme smuggling, JavaScript string escape, CSS expression, SVG onload, and encoded variants of each — against every output statement in it. Deliverable: a corpus file, an automated test that fails on any payload appearing unescaped in the output, and a table showing which of the six escaping functions each statement now uses.
  3. Measure the nonce window empirically. Write a script that issues a nonce, then attempts to verify it at 1-minute intervals over 36 hours, logging the first and last successful verification. Repeat for a user who logs out and back in during the window. Deliverable: the observed validity window, a written statement of what it means for a long-open form, and a re-authentication prompt design for forms likely to be open for hours.
  4. Build an authorisation matrix and test it. Enumerate every endpoint — REST, admin-ajax and admin-post — in your own plugins, and produce a matrix of endpoint against the nine standard roles plus anonymous. Assert the exact expected status code in a PHPUnit data provider. Deliverable: the matrix, the test, and a fixed implementation for every cell that returned 200 where 403 was expected, with a written note on each case.
  5. Harden an estate and prove it. Apply the §6.6 profile to a real site, then attempt, in a staging copy, to upload a PHP file renamed .jpg, to install a plugin through the admin as an administrator, to reach an external host through a forged URL parameter, and to execute a file in the uploads directory. Deliverable: the configuration as code, four failed exploit attempts with their HTTP responses, the detection alert for each attempt, and a written assessment of what remains possible after the profile is applied.

7 · Caching, Performance and the Query That Costs You Everything

7.1 The object cache7.2 Page cache and full-page delivery7.3 Cache layers and invalidation7.4 The queries that cost you everything7.5 The autoload problem in wp_options7.6 Profiling with Query Monitor and friends

WordPress is a request-time document assembler. On every page view it asks the database a few dozen questions, builds an object graph in PHP, asks the question again the moment that graph is discarded, and then throws the graph away.
