Performance work on WordPress is therefore not a matter of micro-optimising PHP; it is a matter of controlling how many times a given fact is asked for, how many bytes of configuration are loaded before the first line of your theme runs, and how many of those answers are serialised into HTML on every request. This chapter works down the stack: the request-scoped object cache, the persistent object cache that makes it useful, the full-page cache that removes the request entirely, the invalidation strategy that keeps all of it honest, the query patterns that defeat it, the wp_options autoload column that quietly dominates time-to-first-byte, and the instrumentation that lets you prove any of the above rather than guess.

The single most important statement in the chapter is this: a site without a persistent object cache re-reads from the database for almost every object on every request — posts, users, terms, options, post meta — because the default WP_Object_Cache is destroyed at the end of each request and never shared with the next one. That is not an edge case; that is the default behaviour of every WordPress installation that has never had a drop-in installed, and it is the most common single cause of a slow WordPress site.

7.1 The object cache

The object cache is WordPress’s memoisation layer. It stores the result of an expensive computation — almost always a database read — under a key, and returns it to later callers within the lifetime of the cache.

Every WordPress object access eventually passes through wp_cache_get(), wp_cache_set(), wp_cache_add() and wp_cache_delete(). These are thin wrappers around a global WP_Object_Cache instance created during bootstrap in wp-includes/load.php. By default that instance is the in-process implementation in wp-includes/class-wp-object-cache.php: an array keyed by group and item, with a numeric counter to track misses, and no lifetime whatsoever. It exists for one request and is garbage-collected at the end of it.

That design has one legitimate purpose. Within a single request, the same object is frequently fetched many times: get_the_author_meta() is called by the template, by a widget, by a plugin filter and again by a shortcode, and the theme asks for post meta that three plugins also ask for. The object cache collapses those repeated reads into a single query. The counter fields, cache_hits and cache_misses, exist so that instrumentation can report on the hit rate within that request.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

namespace App\Cache;

/**
 * Thin, group-aware façade over the core object cache API.
 */
final class Cache
{
    public const GROUP = 'app/content';
    public const VERSION_KEY = 'version';

    public static function get(string $key, mixed $default = null): mixed
    {
        $found = false;
        $value = wp_cache_get($key, self::GROUP, false, $found);

        return $found ? $value : $default;
    }

    public static function put(string $key, mixed $value, int $ttl = 300): bool
    {
        return wp_cache_set($key, $value, self::GROUP, $ttl);
    }

    public static function forget(string $key): bool
    {
        return wp_cache_delete($key, self::GROUP);
    }
}

The fourth argument to wp_cache_get() is the found by-reference flag. It matters: without it, a stored null or false is indistinguishable from a miss, and a cache that stores negative results will recompute them on every request. Always use it when the cached value can legitimately be falsy.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

// A cached empty result must still be distinguishable from a miss.
$found = false;
$rows  = wp_cache_get('post/ids/author/42', 'app/content', false, $found);

if (!$found) {
    global $wpdb;

    $rows = $wpdb->get_col(
        $wpdb->prepare(
            "SELECT ID FROM {$wpdb->posts} WHERE post_author = %d AND post_status = 'publish'",
            42
        )
    );

    wp_cache_set('post/ids/author/42', $rows, 'app/content', 5 * MINUTE_IN_SECONDS);
}

Groups and versions are the second axis of the API. A group is a namespace; versions are integers stored in the cache under a conventionally named key, and a version mismatch is treated as a miss. Bumping a version therefore invalidates an entire family of keys in constant time, which is the correct way to retire a large cache generation without enumerating its keys — persistent backends such as Redis and Memcached have no portable keys operation that is safe to call in production.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

const GROUP = 'app/content';

function generation(): int
{
    $found = false;
    $gen   = wp_cache_get('generation', GROUP, false, $found);

    if (!$found) {
        $gen = (int) get_option('app_cache_generation', 1);
        wp_cache_set('generation', $gen, GROUP, DAY_IN_SECONDS);
    }

    return $gen;
}

function remember(string $key, mixed $value, int $ttl = 300): void
{
    wp_cache_set($key . ':g' . generation(), $value, GROUP, $ttl);
}

function recall(string $key, mixed $default = null): mixed
{
    $found = false;
    $v = wp_cache_get($key . ':g' . generation(), GROUP, false, $found);

    return $found ? $v : $default;
}

// Retirement is O(1): bump the generation, every prefixed key becomes unreachable.
function retire_all(): void
{
    wp_cache_set('generation', random_int(1, PHP_INT_MAX), GROUP, DAY_IN_SECONDS);
}

A site without a persistent object cache re-reads from the database for almost every object on every request. The in-process WP_Object_Cache cannot outlive the request, so the memoisation it provides is confined to a single page view. A page that issues forty database queries and thirty of them are duplicate reads has saved nothing by the time the response is sent. This is the single most common cause of a slow WordPress site, and it is entirely invisible until you install a persistent backend and measure the delta.

Feature detection is mandatory in any code that depends on cache behaviour, because the presence of a drop-in is a deployment fact, not a code fact. wp_using_ext_object_cache() is the canonical test. A drop-in is installed by placing a file at wp-content/object-cache.php; core then requires it during wp_start_object_cache() and sets the internal flag accordingly. Everything else — incrementors, the global group list, the non-persistent group list, capacity controls — is configured from within that file.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

if (!function_exists('app_cache_backend')) {
    function app_cache_backend(): string
    {
        if (!wp_using_ext_object_cache()) {
            return 'none';
        }

        global $wp_object_cache;

        return match (true) {
            $wp_object_cache instanceof RedisCachePro     => 'redis',
            $wp_object_cache instanceof MemcachedCache    => 'memcached',
            default                                       => 'unknown-dropin',
        };
    }
}

// Never let an optional optimisation become a hard dependency.
if (!wp_using_ext_object_cache()) {
    add_filter('app_use_expensive_computation', '__return_false');
}

Some data must never be cached persistently, and core will not decide that for you. Drop-ins expose wp_cache_add_global_groups() for groups that should be invalidated globally by a single call to wp_cache_flush() — core already puts blog-details, sitesplits, userslugs and userlogins in that list. Everything else is flushed by backend, not by group. Conversely, groups that must be recomputed on every request because they contain request-scoped state — nonce counts, current-user capability sets, per-request limits — belong in wp_cache_add_non_persistent_groups(), which instructs the drop-in to bypass the remote store entirely.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

// wp-content/object-cache.php — the only file that touches the backend.

// Groups whose contents must survive a global flush, or be purged atomically.
wp_cache_add_global_groups([
    'blog-details',
    'sitesplits',
    'app/tenancy',
    'app/routing',
]);

// Groups that must never touch the network: request-scoped and high-churn.
wp_cache_add_non_persistent_groups([
    'counts',
    'userlogins',
    'app/capabilities',
    'app/request',
], 'RedisCachePro');

$cache = new RedisCachePro([
    'host'     => $_ENV['REDIS_HOST']   ?? '127.0.0.1',
    'port'     => (int) ($_ENV['REDIS_PORT'] ?? 6379),
    'database' => (int) ($_ENV['REDIS_DB'] ?? 0),
    'timeout'  => 1.5,
    'read_timeout' => 1.5,
    'client'   => 'phpredis',
]);

The choice of backend is not free. Memcached is a small, fast, volatile, LRU store with no persistence, no replication and no non-blocking operations beyond get, set and delete. Redis is a data structure server: it persists, it replicates, it supports sorted sets, hashes, pub/sub and blocking lists, and it can serve as both object cache and queue. The 2.x generation of the Redis Object Cache drop-in adds a key prefix tied to the blog ID on multisite, transparent key-level invalidation, and internal group flushing driven by wp_cache_flush_group() — the mechanism that makes a targeted purge possible without FLUSHDB.

ConcernMemcachedRedis 2.x drop-in
Group-level flushEmulated by version counter; keys expire by TTLNative FLUSHGROUP / scan-and-delete
PersistenceNone — a restart is a cold cacheAOF or RDB, with optional replication
Multisite key namespacingPrefix per blog, manually configuredAutomatic blog_id: prefix, per-site flush
Non-persistent groupsSupported via API, inconsistent in practiceFully supported, skips the round trip
Transient storageOptions table only (no external object cache by default)Transients can be routed to Redis keys with TTL
Atomic primitivesSingle-key increment onlyPipelines, Lua, transactions, lists
Operational ceilingApproximately 50k keys/second per node, 64MB slab ceilingOrders of magnitude higher, memory and CPU bound

External object caches change transient semantics. Historically a transient was an option row, so an external object cache gave you no transients benefit whatsoever. The Redis drop-in in 2.x registers the _transient_ groups as non-persistent and stores the value in Redis with the transient’s own TTL, so get_transient() no longer touches wp_options at all. If you move to Redis, audit code that assumed transients are rows.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

// Health probe, run by monitoring, not by the application.
wp_cache_add_non_persistent_groups(['app/probe']);

$start = hrtime(true);
$ok    = wp_cache_set('probe', bin2hex(random_bytes(8)), 'app/probe', 30);
$read  = wp_cache_get('probe', 'app/probe');
$ms    = (hrtime(true) - $start) / 1e6;

printf(
    "backend=%s roundtrip=%.2fms healthy=%s\n",
    app_cache_backend(),
    $ms,
    $ok && $read !== false ? 'yes' : 'no'
);

Flush the group, not the cache. With the Redis drop-in in place, prefer wp_cache_flush_group() over wp_cache_flush(). A global flush on a large multisite is a deliberate denial of service against your own origin, and it forces the next wave of concurrent requests to all miss simultaneously. Retire a cache generation by bumping an integer instead.

7.2 Page cache and full-page delivery

A page cache stores the rendered response of a URL and returns it without executing WordPress at all. It operates one layer above the object cache and is the only layer that removes PHP and MySQL from the request path entirely.

The distinction matters more than the naming suggests. An object cache reduces the cost of one request; a page cache eliminates the request. On a site with a high read-to-write ratio — publishing, a WooCommerce catalogue, a documentation site, anything indexed by a crawler — the page cache converts the dominant workload from "run PHP and MySQL" into "read a file from disk and hand it back", which is two orders of magnitude cheaper and is the only layer that survives a traffic spike.

Page caching in WordPress is enabled by defining WP_CACHE as true in wp-config.php. That constant does not create a cache; it is the opt-in signal that a drop-in is present at wp-content/advanced-cache.php, and core loads it on the advanced_cache_init hook, which fires in wp-settings.php before any theme code loads. The drop-in is responsible for deciding what to store, what to bypass, and when to invalidate.

/* wp-config.php — the cache constants, in the order that matters. */
define('WP_DEBUG', false);
define('WP_CACHE', true);

/* Only touch a page cache on the primary site of a network. */
if (!is_multisite() || (defined('SUBDOMAIN_INSTALL') && SUBDOMAIN_INSTALL === false)) {
    define('WP_CACHE', true);
} else {
    define('WP_CACHE', false);
}

/* Bypass. This is the emergency brake; use it deliberately. */
define('DONOTCACHEPAGE', true);   // never a page cache
define('DONOTCACHEDIR', '/wp-content/cache/');
define('DONOTCACHEKEY', 'cart|checkout|my-account');

Rules of thumb for what may be cached are mechanical, not aesthetic. A response is cacheable if the status is 200 or 301, the request method is GET or HEAD, no Set-Cookie was emitted, no Cache-Control: no-store or private directive was sent, and the page contains no per-user data. WordPress emits Cache-Control: no-cache, must-revalidate, max-age=0 by default precisely because logged-in users must never be served a shared artefact.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

// Tell an external page cache what is cacheable, and for how long.
// Called from a must-use plugin so it loads before any drop-in logic.
add_action('send_headers', static function (): void {
    if (is_user_logged_in() || is_admin() || wp_doing_ajax() || wp_doing_cron()) {
        header('Cache-Control: private, no-store, max-age=0');

        return;
    }

    $ttl = is_singular('doc_chapter') ? HOUR_IN_SECONDS : 5 * MINUTE_IN_SECONDS;

    header(sprintf('Cache-Control: public, max-age=%d, s-maxage=%d', $ttl, $ttl));
    header('CDN-Cache-Control: public, s-maxage=86400');
    header('Vary: Accept-Encoding');
}, 1);

Where the cache lives is an operational decision with real trade-offs. A reverse-proxy cache (nginx fastcgi_cache, Varnish) sits in front of PHP, survives PHP crashes, and can absorb a flood that would otherwise exhaust the FPM pool — but it is dumb about cookies and slow at targeted purges. A server-side drop-in (WP Super Cache in late mode, a bespoke advanced-cache.php) knows about WordPress, can purge a post on transition_post_status, and runs on the same host, which limits its protective value.

LayerRemovesKnows about WPTypical hit rateMain failure mode
Browser cacheNetwork round tripNo30–60% on repeat viewsStale assets after deploy
CDN edgeOrigin entirelyOnly via cache headers you emit70–95%Cache-control mistakes poison the edge
Reverse proxy (nginx/Varnish)PHP-FPM and MySQLNo80–98%Cache poisoning by query string
Page cache drop-inPHP but not MySQL configYes60–90%Cache bloat with no eviction
Persistent object cacheDuplicate queries within a missYesN/A — raises the miss throughputHot key eviction storms

On nginx, a page cache is a fastcgi_cache zone plus a skip_cache condition. The condition is where correctness lives: bypassing for logged-in users, for any request carrying a POST, for WooCommerce session routes, and for the REST API is the difference between a functioning site and one that serves one customer’s cart to another.

# /etc/nginx/sites-available/wordpress

fastcgi_cache_path /var/cache/nginx/wordpress
                 levels=1:2
                 keys_zone=wp:32m
                 max_size=4g
                 inactive=24h
                 use_temp_path=off;
                 purge_by_name=off;

map $http_upgrade $connection_upgrade {
    default upgrade;
    ''      close;
}

server {
    listen 443 ssl http2;
    server_name example.com;
    root /srv/www/html;
    index index.php;

    location / {
        try_files $uri $uri/ /index.php?$args;
    }

    location ~ \.php$ {
        include fastcgi_params;
        fastcgi_pass unix:/run/php/php8.5-fpm.sock;
        fastcgi_param SCRIPT_FILENAME $document_root$fastcgi_script_name;

        fastcgi_cache wp;
        fastcgi_cache_key "$scheme$request_method$host$request_uri";
        fastcgi_cache_valid 200 301 302 10m;
        fastcgi_cache_valid 404 1m;
        fastcgi_cache_lock on;
        fastcgi_cache_lock_timeout 5s;
        fastcgi_cache_lock_age 5s;
        fastcgi_cache_use_stale error timeout updating http_500 http_502 http_503;
        fastcgi_cache_background_update on;
        fastcgi_ignore_headers Cache-Control Expires Set-Cookie;

        fastcgi_cache_bypass $skip_cache$http_authorization;
        fastcgi_no_cache $skip_cache$http_authorization;
    }
}

Apache’s equivalent is rewrite rules guarded by a set of RewriteCond tests. It works, it is slower to evaluate, and it is the configuration most often copied from a tutorial without the Authorization and Cookie guards that keep it safe.

# .htaccess in the document root, after the WordPress block

# BEGIN WordPress Page Cache
<IfModule mod_rewrite.c>
    RewriteEngine On
    RewriteBase /

    # Never cache authenticated, admin, REST or cron traffic.
    RewriteCond %{HTTP_COOKIE} .*wordpress_logged_in [OR]
    RewriteCond %{HTTP:Authorization} .
    RewriteCond %{REQUEST_URI} ^/wp-(admin|json|login|cron) [OR]
    RewriteCond %{REQUEST_METHOD} ^(POST|PUT|DELETE|PATCH)$
    RewriteRule . - [L]

    # Serve an existing cache entry immediately; store successful responses only.
    RewriteCond %{REQUEST_METHOD} ^(GET|HEAD)$
    RewriteCond %{DOCUMENT_ROOT}/wp-content/cache/page-cache/%{HTTP_HOST}/%{REQUEST_URI} -f
    RewriteRule .* - [L]

    RewriteCond %{REQUEST_METHOD} ^(GET|HEAD)$
    RewriteCond %{DOCUMENT_ROOT}/wp-content/page-cache-tmp/$0 -f
    RewriteCond %{DOCUMENT_ROOT}/wp-content/cache/page-cache/%{HTTP_HOST}/%{REQUEST_URI} -f
    RewriteRule .* - [L]

    RewriteCond %{REQUEST_METHOD} ^(GET|HEAD)$
    RewriteCond %{DOCUMENT_ROOT}/wp-content/page-cache-tmp/$0 !-f
    RewriteRule .* - [L]
</IfModule>
# END WordPress Page Cache

Never cache a response that emitted a cookie. Many plugins set cookies on the front end — consent banners, geo-redirects, cart abandonment, A/B tests — and a page cache that stores that response will hand the same Set-Cookie to every subsequent visitor and to unrelated users. Likewise, do not strip Set-Cookie from the ignore list: doing so replaces a subtle bug with a privacy incident. If a page must bypass, say so in Cache-Control and let the drop-in honour it.

The CDN layer deserves separate treatment because it is the only one that survives origin failure. Two configuration details decide whether it is worth paying for. First, cache keys: the default Vary set is far too coarse or too fine depending on the provider, and you usually want to normalise the query string — drop utm_*, keep anything that changes output. Second, surrogate keys: if the CDN supports them, tag objects with the post ID on purge and you get O(1) invalidation instead of a full purge.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

// Tag cached objects so a targeted purge is possible on publish.
add_action('transition_post_status', static function (string $new, string $old, WP_Post $post): void {
    if (!in_array($new, ['publish', 'private', 'trash'], true)) {
        return;
    }

    wp_cache_set('cdn/tags/' . $post->ID, [
        'post-' . $post->ID,
        'collection-' . get_post_type($post->ID),
        'author-' . $post->post_author,
        'taxonomy-' . implode(',', (array) get_object_taxonomies($post->post_type)),
    ], 'app/cdn', DAY_IN_SECONDS);
}, 10, 3);

7.3 Cache layers and invalidation

A cache without an invalidation strategy is a liability. This section covers cache key design, TTL selection, targeted purges and the failure mode that brings a whole site down at once.

Every cache in the stack is a bet that the value will be read again before it changes. The bet is expressed in two numbers: the key, which must uniquely identify the value, and the TTL, which bounds the damage when you are wrong. Get the key wrong and you serve one user’s data to another; set the TTL too high and you serve stale truth; set it too low and you pay for storage and save nothing.

Keys should be derived from the semantic identity of the value, not from the shape of the query that produced it. A key of q:SELECT w.id FROM wp_terms ... is a memory of an implementation detail; a key of terms:post_type=doc&taxonomy=topic&ids=4,9,17 is a memory of a fact. The second survives a refactor, and it is the one you can construct in a template without knowing how the query was written.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

namespace App\Cache;

final class Key
{
    public static function make(string $namespace, array $parts = []): string
    {
        ksort($parts);

        $pairs = [];
        foreach ($parts as $k => $v) {
            $pairs[] = $k . ':' . (is_scalar($v) ? rawurlencode((string) $v) : md5(serialize($v)));
        }

        return $namespace . '/' . implode('|', $pairs);
    }

    public static function evict(string $namespace): void
    {
        if (function_exists('wp_cache_flush_group')) {
            wp_cache_flush_group('app/' . $namespace);

            return;
        }

        // Fallback: retire a generation, which invalidates the whole namespace.
        $current = (int) wp_cache_get('gen', 'app/generation', false, $found = false);
        wp_cache_set('gen', $current + 1, 'app/generation', WEEK_IN_SECONDS);
    }
}

TTL should be a function of how expensive the value is to rebuild and how quickly it goes stale — two axes that are usually inversely correlated. A term list costs one indexed query and never changes between a taxonomy’s own edits: give it an hour. A homepage sidebar of recent posts costs one query but is wrong the moment anything is published: give it sixty seconds, and additionally purge it on transition_post_status. Nothing with a TTL should be your only defence, because TTL is the fallback when the event hook did not fire.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

add_action('transition_post_status', static function (): void {
    App\Cache\Key::evict('terms');
    App\Cache\Key::evict('nav');
    App\Cache\Key::evict('homepage');

    // Term membership changes are not post status changes.
}, 10);

add_action('created_term',    ['App\Cache\Invalidator', 'flush'], 10, 3);
add_action('edited_term',     ['App\Cache\Invalidator', 'flush'], 10, 3);
add_action('delete_term',     ['App\Cache\Invalidator', 'flush'], 10, 3);
add_action('set_object_terms', ['App\Cache\Invalidator', 'flush_object'], 10, 6);
add_action('profile_update',  ['App\Cache\Invalidator', 'flush_user'], 10);
add_action('user_register',   ['App\Cache\Invalidator', 'flush_user'], 10);
add_action('switch_theme',    ['App\Cache\Invalidator', 'flush_all'], 10);
add_action('upgrader_process_complete', ['App\Cache\Invalidator', 'flush_all'], 10);

Targeted purges require a reverse index, and this is where most hand-rolled caches fall over. If you evict everything for a namespace because one post changed, you have built a cache that degrades to a database with extra latency. Maintaining a set of keys per post in Redis — sadd wp:post:812:keys:cachekey for each write, then smembers followed by a pipeline of deletes on purge — costs one extra write per set and turns an O(n) purge into an O(k) one where k is the number of keys that actually mentioned that post.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

$redis = app_redis();

function remember_related(string $key, int $postId, mixed $value, int $ttl): void
{
    wp_cache_set($key, $value, 'app/content', $ttl);
    $redis->sAdd("wp:post:{$postId}:keys", $key);
    $redis->expire("wp:post:{$postId}:keys", WEEK_IN_SECONDS);
}

function purge_post(int $postId): int
{
    global $redis;

    $keys = $redis->sMembers("wp:post:{$postId}:keys");
    if ($keys === []) {
        return 0;
    }

    $pipe = $redis->pipeline();
    foreach ($keys as $key) {
        $pipe->del($key);
    }
    $pipe->del("wp:post:{$postId}:keys");
    $pipe->exec();

    return count($keys);
}

Thundering herd. When a popular key expires, every concurrent request misses at the same instant and each independently runs the expensive query. A 4×CPU spike on a 64-core box will then evict the working set and convert the spike into an outage. Three defences, in order of preference: cache stampede locks with wp_cache_add(), add jitter so TTLs do not align, and serve stale while revalidating. The nginx fastcgi_cache_lock directive gives you the first of these for free at the page layer.

Third-party caches — a CDN, a load balancer, a micro-cache in front of the origin — add layers you cannot purge from PHP. The portable mechanism is a short TTL at the edge paired with a purge API call on content change. A cache-busting query string derived from a lastmod aggregate is the fallback when the provider offers no API: it is O(1) to publish, and its only cost is that it invalidates every edge object at once rather than one.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

// A single integer that changes whenever any cached content changes.
// Appended to asset URLs and, optionally, to page cache keys at the edge.
add_action('init', static function (): void {
    global $wpdb;

    $lastmod = (int) $wpdb->get_var(
        "SELECT UNIX_TIMESTAMP(MAX(post_modified_gmt)) FROM {$wpdb->posts} WHERE post_status = 'publish'"
    );

    $stamp = get_option('app_asset_stamp');
    if ($stamp === false || (int) $stamp < $lastmod) {
        update_option('app_asset_stamp', $lastmod, false);
        wp_cache_set('app/asset_stamp', $lastmod, 'app/infra', DAY_IN_SECONDS);
    }
});

function asset_version(): string
{
    return (string) wp_cache_get('app/asset_stamp', 'app/infra', false, $found = false) ?: '1';
}

Cache bloat is the quiet counterpart to cache misses. A drop-in that writes to a local directory with no eviction policy will fill the disk and stop writing, silently reverting the site to uncached. Monitor the cache directory size as a first-class metric, and configure a maximum size with least-recently-used eviction. If you use a page cache on a shared host, check the host’s inode limit before you check its bandwidth.

Data classRebuild costStaleness toleranceRecommended TTLExplicit purge on
Taxonomy term listsOne indexed queryHours1 hourcreated/edited/deleted term
Primary menu treeRecursive query per itemHours6 hoursnav_menu_item save
Homepage query resultsOne query, high trafficSeconds60 stransition_post_status
Remote HTTP response150–2000 msDepends on the API contract5–15 minutesUsually never; TTL is the contract
User capability mapMeta query per roleMust be immediateNon-persistent groupEvery request, by design
Rendered fragment in output bufferPartial template renderSeconds to minutes60–300 sWhatever invalidated the query

7.4 The queries that cost you everything

Almost every slow WordPress page is slow for one of four reasons: an N+1 query pattern, an unbounded result set, a missing index, or a large autoloaded options payload. This section makes each of them visible.

SAVEQUERIES is the oldest and still the most useful diagnostic in the WordPress toolbox. Define it as true and every call to $wpdb->query() appends the full SQL text, its stack trace and its execution time to $wpdb->queries. The trace is what makes it valuable — it points at the template line, or the plugin file, that issued the query, which is information no profiler gives you for free.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

add_action('shutdown', static function (): void {
    global $wpdb, $wp_object_cache;

    if (!defined('SAVEQUERIES') || !SAVEQUERIES) {
        return;
    }

    $logs    = $wpdb->logs ?? [];
    $by_caller = [];

    foreach ($logs as $entry) {
        $trace = $entry[2] ?? '';
        $caller = 'unknown';
        foreach (explode("\n", (string) $trace) as $frame) {
            if (!str_contains($frame, '/wp-content/') || str_contains($frame, '/mu-plugins/')) {
                continue;
            }
            $caller = trim($frame);
            break;
        }

        $by_caller[$caller]['count'] = ($by_caller[$caller]['count'] ?? 0) + 1;
        $by_caller[$caller]['ms']    = ($by_caller[$caller]['ms'] ?? 0.0) + (float) ($entry[1] ?? 0);
    }

    uasort($by_caller, static fn ($a, $b) => $b['ms'] <=> $a['ms']);

    $payload = [
        'queries'    => count($logs),
        'total_ms'   => array_sum(array_column($logs, 1)),
        'hits'       => $wp_object_cache->cache_hits,
        'misses'     => $wp_object_cache->cache_misses,
        'by_caller'  => $by_caller,
    ];

    error_log('WPQUERY ' . wp_json_encode($payload));
}, 0);

The N+1 pattern deserves its own treatment because it is the most common and because it is invisible in per-query timing. A loop over fifty posts, each of which calls get_post_meta(), each of which triggers a query for that post’s meta rows: fifty queries, all indexed, all fast, all avoidable. The object cache does not save you, because each key is distinct.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

global $wpdb;

$ids = $wpdb->get_col(
    $wpdb->prepare(
        "SELECT ID FROM {$wpdb->posts} WHERE post_type = 'doc' AND post_status = 'publish' ORDER BY post_date DESC LIMIT %d",
        50
    )
);

// Prime every cache in one pass, so the loop below issues zero queries.
update_meta_cache('post', $ids);
update_postmeta_cache($ids);
update_object_term_cache($ids, ['doc_topic', 'category']);

foreach ($ids as $id) {
    $subtitle = get_post_meta($id, 'subtitle', true); // no SQL issued
    $topic    = get_the_terms($id, 'doc_topic');      // no SQL issued
    // ...
}

The priming functions are the single most effective performance intervention available to a WordPress developer, and they are documented but underused. update_meta_cache(), update_postmeta_cache(), update_term_cache(), update_object_term_cache() and update_comment_cache() each accept a list of IDs and issue exactly one query. Call them with a superset of what you need — prefetching ten objects you never touch is cheaper than querying for them individually.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

add_action('pre_get_posts', static function (WP_Query $q): void {
    if (is_admin() || ! $q->is_main_query()) {
        return;
    }

    if (! $q->is_post_type_archive('doc')) {
        return;
    }

    // Bound the result set before the database is asked.
    $q->set('posts_per_page', 24);
    $q->set('no_found_rows', true);   // skip the expensive COUNT(*) for pagination
    $q->set('fields', 'ids');         // if the template only needs IDs

    $q->set(
        'meta_query',
        [
            'relation' => 'AND',
            [
                'key'     => 'visibility',
                'value'   => 'public',
                'compare' => '=',
            ],
            'meta_query' => [
                'relation' => 'OR',
                ['key' => 'featured', 'compare' => 'EXISTS'],
                ['key' => 'featured', 'compare' => 'NOT EXISTS'],
            ],
        ]
    );
}, 20);

Two query-cost bugs recur. The first is fields => 'ids' plus a template that reaches for get_post() anyway, which converts a cheap ID query back into fifty full row reads. The second is ORDER BY RAND(), which forces the database to materialise the whole table before sorting; if randomness is genuinely required, fetch candidate IDs cheaply and shuffle in PHP. A third, subtler bug is a meta_query that cannot use an index: any comparison against a column, or a NOT EXISTS clause, degrades to a scan.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

// Slow: materialises the table, sorts it, then samples.
$rows = $wpdb->get_results('SELECT * FROM wp_posts ORDER BY RAND() LIMIT 5');

// Fast: read a narrow, indexed projection, shuffle, then hydrate.
$ids = $wpdb->get_col(
    "SELECT ID FROM {$wpdb->posts}
     WHERE post_status = 'publish' AND post_type = 'post'
     ORDER BY post_date DESC LIMIT 200"
);
shuffle($ids);
$ids = array_slice($ids, 0, 5);

// One extra query, not two hundred.
$posts = get_posts([
    'post_type'      => 'any',
    'post__in'       => $ids,
    'orderby'        => 'post__in',
    'posts_per_page' => 5,
    'no_found_rows'  => true,
]);

Not every query is WordPress’s fault, so you need to see the statements that bypass the query layer. The slow query log and performance_schema are the ground truth, and on MySQL 8.0 and 9.x you should be reading the latter rather than trusting the former, which samples.

-- Where is the time actually going, by normalised statement digest?
SELECT
    DIGEST_TEXT                                       AS statement,
    COUNT_STAR                                         AS execs,
    ROUND(SUM_TIMER_WAIT / 1e12, 3)                    AS total_s,
    ROUND(AVG_TIMER_WAIT / 1e9, 3)                     AS avg_ms,
    ROUND(SUM_ROWS_EXAMINED / GREATEST(SUM_ROWS_EXAMINED, 1), 0) AS rows_examined
FROM performance_schema.events_statements_summary_by_digest
WHERE SCHEMA_NAME = DATABASE()
  AND DIGEST_TEXT NOT LIKE 'SET %'
ORDER BY SUM_TIMER_WAIT DESC
LIMIT 20;

-- Confirm that a candidate index is actually chosen.
EXPLAIN FORMAT=json
SELECT * FROM wp_posts
WHERE post_type = 'doc' AND post_status = 'publish' AND post_date > '2026-01-01'
ORDER BY post_date DESC LIMIT 24;

Core ships a sensible index set, but your custom post types need their own. post_type is a varchar(20) with an index, and post_status is indexed, but a composite index over (post_type, post_status, post_date) is not shipped, because core cannot know which types you register. The standard wp_posts index covers post_name, post_parent and guid only. Adding indexes is a schema change and therefore a migration — see §13.3.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

require_once ABSPATH . 'wp-admin/includes/upgrade.php';

// Idempotent: dbDelta issues CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS-equivalent DDL
// and adds a missing index without rebuilding the table when the shape matches.
dbDelta(
    'CREATE TABLE wp_doc_index (
        id bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL AUTO_INCREMENT,
        object_id bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL,
        facet varchar(64) NOT NULL,
        term_id bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL,
        PRIMARY KEY  (object_id,facet,term_id),
        KEY term_id (term_id),
        KEY facet_term (facet,term_id)
    ) DEFAULT CHARACTER SET utf8mb4 COLLATE utf8mb4_unicode_ci;'
);

Measure the miss path, not the page. With a warm object cache, page timings tell you almost nothing, because the page never touches the database. Add a maintenance route that flushes the object cache for one request and records the resulting query count and total time. That number is the honest cost model, and it is the only one that survives a traffic increase.

7.5 The autoload problem in wp_options

Before WordPress does anything else, it loads every option marked for autoload. This single query can read tens of megabytes, and it is the most reliable way to make a site slow with no visible cause.

During bootstrap, wp_load_alloptions() issues one SELECT against wp_options filtered by the autoload column, loads the result into the alloptions cache entry, and from then on get_option() is an array lookup. Everything with autoload enabled is therefore resident in memory on every request, cached or not. The list starts with the options core needs — siteurl, home, blogname, blogdescription, upload_path, blog_charset, the cron array, active plugins — and grows by accretion.

Plugins add to it in three ways: add_option() with autoload enabled, update_option() where the autoload argument is unspecified, and, most damagingly, plugins that serialise large arrays into a single option. A single 5 MB autoloaded option is read, unserialised and held on every single request, including requests that never touch the feature it belongs to.

-- What is actually in the autoload payload, and how big is each contribution?
SELECT
    LEFT(option_name, 60)                              AS option_name,
    LENGTH(option_value)                                AS bytes,
    LEFT(option_value, 80)                              AS preview
FROM wp_options
WHERE autoload IN ('yes', 'on', 'auto', 'auto-on', 'on')
ORDER BY bytes DESC
LIMIT 40;

SELECT
    COUNT(*)                                            AS autoloaded_rows,
    ROUND(SUM(LENGTH(option_value)) / 1024 / 1024, 2)   AS autoloaded_mb
FROM wp_options
WHERE autoload IN ('yes', 'on', 'auto', 'auto-on');

WordPress 6.6 changed the semantics of this column, and the change is the single most useful thing to understand about the table. Historically autoload was boolean: 'yes' or 'no'. In 6.6 it became a graduated scale — 'off', 'auto', 'auto-on', 'auto-off', 'on' — with 'yes' retained as a legacy alias for 'on'. The auto values defer the decision: auto-on means "autoload if something already references this option on the page", and auto-off means "do not autoload unless something does". Core computes the referenced set from the list of options that the requested URL is known to need and applies the decision at load time.

autoload valueMeaning since 6.6Loaded on every request?How to set it
offNever autoloadedNoFourth argument to add_option()
autoDefer: decide from referenced optionsConditionallyDefault for update_option()
auto-onAutoload only if referencedConditionallySixth argument to update_option()
auto-offSkip unless referenced, then loadConditionallySixth argument to update_option()
onAlways autoloadedYesExplicit; use with care
yesLegacy alias for onYesWritten by pre-6.6 code; migrate it
 
<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

// 6.6+ signature: the last two arguments are (autoload, autoload_value).
add_option(
    'app_feature_flags',
    ['newEditor' => true, 'betaPanel' => false],
    '',
    'auto',
    'auto'
);

update_option('app_legacy_flag', 1, false, 'auto', 'auto-off');

update_option('app_cache_generation', 1, false, 'auto', 'auto-on');

// Assert the effective behaviour rather than trusting the column.
function is_effectively_autoloaded(string $option): bool
{
    $all = wp_load_alloptions();

    return array_key_exists($option, $all);
}

Two details bite. First, the autoload argument to update_option() is only consulted when the option is being created; for an existing row, the autoload_value argument is what is stored. Second, changing the autoload setting does not by itself relocate the data — wp_load_alloptions() filters on the column values, so an option left as yes is still autoloaded regardless of what the rest of the code believes.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

require_once ABSPATH . 'wp-admin/includes/upgrade.php';

global $wpdb;

$reference = get_option('app_feature_flags', []);
$candidates = $wpdb->get_col(
    "SELECT option_name FROM {$wpdb->options}
      WHERE autoload IN ('yes', 'on', 'auto-on') AND LENGTH(option_value) > 8192"
);

$promoted = [];
foreach ($candidates as $name) {
    if ($name === 'cron') {
        continue; // the cron array is legitimately autoloaded and heavily referenced
    }

    update_option($name, get_option($name), false, 'auto', 'auto-off');
    $promoted[] = $name;
}

printf("moved %d options off the always-autoload path\n", count($promoted));

The other half of the problem is that the 6.6 decision depends on a reference list, and that list must be supplied by the code that needs the option. If nothing registers an interest, auto-off behaves like off and your option is never loaded — which is correct, and which means you must read it explicitly with get_option() at the point of use rather than assuming it is present.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

// wp-content/mu-plugins/app-reference.php
add_filter('wp_default_autoload_value', static fn (): string => 'auto-on', 10, 2);

// Mark the options this front end needs; 'auto' values resolve against this list.
add_filter('option_page_cache_enabled', static function ($value) {
    return $value;
});

add_filter('pre_option_app_runtime_config', static function ($value, string $option) {
    return $value ?? require WPMU_PLUGIN_DIR . '/config/runtime.php';
}, 10, 2);

// The deferred path still works; it just costs one query when the option is needed.
$ttl = (int) get_option('app_remote_ttl_seconds', 900);

Measure the payload, not the row count. Four hundred autoloaded options at 200 bytes each is harmless; twelve autoloaded options at 800 KB each is a 9 MB read on every request. Report the sum of LENGTH(option_value) for the autoloading set as a build-time metric and alert on regression. Large values — plugin settings, import buffers, cached remote payloads — belong in transients or in post meta, never in an autoloaded option.

# Survey and repair from the command line, without a web request in sight.
wp db query "SELECT option_name, LENGTH(option_value) AS bytes
             FROM wp_options
             WHERE autoload IN ('yes','on','auto-on')
             ORDER BY bytes DESC LIMIT 25" --skip-column-names

# Bulk demote every legacy 'yes' row larger than 8 KB to 'auto-off'.
wp db query "UPDATE wp_options
             SET autoload = 'auto-off'
             WHERE autoload IN ('yes','on') AND LENGTH(option_value) > 8192"

# Confirm the payload afterwards.
wp db query "SELECT COUNT(*) AS rows_loaded,
                    ROUND(SUM(LENGTH(option_value))/1024/1024, 2) AS mb
             FROM wp_options
             WHERE autoload IN ('yes','on','auto-on')"

# Inspect a single option's autoload decision and value in one shot.
wp option get app_feature_flags --autoload=on --format=json

Core’s own wp_autoload_values_to_autoload() function is the authoritative list of column values that count as autoloading. Use it in your own queries and filters rather than hard-coding a set — it is filterable, and other plugins legitimately extend it. Every example above can be rewritten as wp_autoload_values_to_autoload() and will be more correct.

7.6 Profiling with Query Monitor and friends

Guessing about performance is a waste of an afternoon. This section covers the three tools that turn opinion into evidence: the query log, a wall-clock profiler, and the browser’s own timings.

Query Monitor 3.x is the default instrument for WordPress performance work and it should be installed on every non-production environment permanently and on production temporarily. It surfaces six things simultaneously: every query, duplicated and slow queries flagged, the PHP caller stack for each, all hooks of a chosen type, all HTTP requests made with the WordPress HTTP API, all transient and object cache operations, and the full timing breakdown of the request including memory.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

namespace App\Diagnostics;

use QM_Collectors;

final class QueryListener
{
    public static function boot(): void
    {
        if (!class_exists(QM_Collectors::class) || !defined('QM_DISABLE_OUTPUT')) {
            return;
        }

        add_action('qm/collect', static function (QM_Collectors $c): void {
            $c->monitor_orphaned_transients();
        });

        add_action('qm/collect', static function (QM_Collectors $c): void {
            $c->monitor_cache_hits();
        });
    }
}

Three readings in Query Monitor deserve a fixed routine, because each corresponds to a different class of defect. The Duplicate Queries panel tells you where priming is missing. The Slow Queries panel tells you where a bound is missing — anything approaching 50 ms on a production-shaped database is a finding. The Orphaned Transients panel tells you where expiration is broken: a transient with an expiration of 0 never expires, and a few thousand of them are an autoload problem waiting to happen.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

// Mute the probes in production but keep the anomaly detection.
define('QM_DISABLE_OUTPUT', true);

add_filter('qm/collectors', static function (array $collectors): array {
    unset($collectors['http_requests']);

    return $collectors;
});

// Turn the full HTML output off; we want the JSON summary, not a page.
add_filter('qm/outputter/html', '__return_false');
add_filter('qm/outputter/json', static fn (): string => 'json');

Query Monitor measures WordPress. It does not measure PHP, and a slow page is frequently slow in code that never touches the database at all — a regex over a large post, an unbounded loop, a json_decode of a megabyte. For that you need a wall-clock profiler, and the practical options in a WordPress context are Blackfire, Xdebug’s profiler, or an extension of the function trace. The three answer different questions.

# Xdebug 3.5 — profiling is a separate mode from debugging.
xdebug.mode=develop,debug,trace,profile
xdebug.output_dir=/var/log/xdebug
xdebug.profiler_output_name=cachegrind.out.%p
xdebug.start_with_request=trigger
xdebug.trigger_value=PERF

# Blackfire, driving a specific URL as an authenticated user.
blackfire --client-id="$BLACKFIRE_CLIENT_ID" \
          --client-token="$BLACKFIRE_CLIENT_TOKEN" \
          --connection-timeout=2 \
          --header="Cookie: wordpress_logged_in_$(php -r 'echo md5("x");')=admin" \
          run --slot=prod https://example.com/checkout/

# Compare two runs of the same URL; the diff is the whole value of the tool.
blackfire --slot=after run https://example.com/checkout/
blackfire report diff /tmp/before.fblackfire /tmp/after.fblackfire

Command-line profiling is the fastest path to a trustworthy number, because the same URL can be run a hundred times without a browser in the loop. wp profile is a core WP-CLI command that wraps the wp-admin/includes/class-wp-profile-step.php profiler and reports cumulative time and memory per step, and the wp profile stage variant breaks the request into its named stages.

# Cumulative profile of one front-end request.
wp profile stage --url=https://example.com/ --all --spotlight=total

# Bootstrap only — isolates the autoload and plugin cost from template cost.
wp profile stage --url=https://example.com/ --all --spotlight=muplugins_loading

# Run the query log from the command line and count what happens.
wp profile hook before_setup_theme --url=https://example.com/

# Site health, which includes the autoload and object cache checks.
wp site health

# Time a page from the origin's own network, excluding TLS and the CDN.
curl -o /dev/null -sS -w 'ttfb=%{time_starttransfer}s total=%{time_total}s size=%{size_download}\n' \
     -H 'Cookie: wordpress_logged_in_xxx=admin' \
     'https://example.com/?QM_NO_OUTPUT=1'

Finally, the browser’s own waterfall remains the only instrument that sees the truth the user experiences, and the number that matters is the largest single gap, not the total. A slow TTFB is a server problem. A slow render with a fast TTFB is a blocking stylesheet, a synchronous font load, or an unoptimised hero image. Those require different fixes, and confusing them wastes the intervention.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

// Emit server timing headers so the waterfall and the logs agree.
add_action('send_headers', static function (): void {
    if (headers_sent()) {
        return;
    }

    $timings = [
        'db'    => (defined('SAVEQUERIES') && SAVEQUERIES)
            ? round(array_sum(array_column($GLOBALS['wpdb']->logs ?? [], 1)), 4)
            : null,
        'boot'  => isset($GLOBALS['app_timing']['boot'])
            ? round($GLOBALS['app_timing']['boot'], 4)
            : null,
        'query' => isset($GLOBALS['app_timing']['query'])
            ? round($GLOBALS['app_timing']['query'], 4)
            : null,
    ];

    $parts = [];
    foreach (array_filter($timings) as $name => $ms) {
        $parts[] = sprintf('%s;dur=%s', $name, $ms);
    }

    if ($parts !== []) {
        header('Server-Timing: ' . implode(', ', $parts));
    }
}, 1);

Never profile with an object cache warm and draw a conclusion about query cost. Conversely, never profile a cold site and draw a conclusion about user experience — the first request after a flush is the worst case and almost no real user sees it. Report both numbers: the cold path as a capacity metric and the warm path as the latency metric. Sites fail on the cold path far more often than engineers expect, because it is the path every crawler and every post-deploy smoke test takes.

Exercises 7

  1. Instrument a cold and a warm request. With SAVEQUERIES enabled, capture query count, total database time, cache_hits, cache_misses and the sum of LENGTH(option_value) for the autoloading option set, before and after installing a Redis 2.x drop-in. Submit a table with the four numbers before, the four after, and a written explanation of each delta in under 300 words.
  2. Eliminate the N+1 pattern. Find the single worst offender on a real archive page using Query Monitor’s duplicate-query panel, eliminate it with cache priming, and demonstrate the before and after with wp profile stage --url=… --all output. The archive must issue fewer than one quarter of its previous query count at identical data volume.
  3. Reduce the autoload payload. Write a WP-CLI command that reports total autoloaded bytes and flags every autoloaded option above 8 KB, then demote the flagged set to 'auto-off' except for options that are provably referenced. Record the byte reduction, prove no page regresses, and add a CI assertion that fails when autoloaded bytes exceed 2 MB.
  4. Design and justify a cache key scheme. Produce a document listing every cached value in a real plugin or theme, with its key, group, TTL, invalidation trigger, and a justification for the TTL expressed in terms of rebuild cost against staleness tolerance. Then demonstrate a targeted purge that removes at most 50 keys when a single post is published, using a Redis reverse index.
  5. Build a performance budget and enforce it. Define wall-clock and query-count budgets for the homepage, a category archive and a single post; add a CI job that fails the build when any of the three exceeds its budget on a seeded production-shaped dataset; and document the exact wp profile stage and Query Monitor panels a reviewer should open to challenge a passing run.

8 · Custom Post Types, Taxonomies and the Content Model

8.1 register_post_type8.2 Capabilities and map_meta_cap8.3 register_taxonomy8.4 Post, term and user meta8.5 Modelling relationships8.6 Registration lifecycle and discipline

The core content model in WordPress is deliberately thin: one table of posts, one table of terms, and three join tables. Every editorial concept a real product needs — an article, a case study, a product, a booking, a job listing — is reconstructed on top of that skeleton by registering a custom post type, attaching taxonomies to it, and storing structured attributes in the meta tables. This chapter treats registration as a schema operation rather than a set of configuration toggles, because the arguments you pass to register_post_type() and register_taxonomy() determine permissions, REST surface, rewrite rules, admin menu placement and query behaviour simultaneously, and the defaults are wrong more often than they are right. It also covers the meta layer: registered schema, the auth_callback boundary that separates read permission from write permission, and why the block editor will not persist a meta field that has not been declared with show_in_rest. The final section addresses the discipline that separates maintainable implementations from a plugin that only works on the machine where it was developed: registration happens on init, at priority ten, once, and never later.

8.1 register_post_type

This section establishes the full argument surface of register_post_type() and separates the arguments that materially change behaviour from those that only affect cosmetics.

WordPress ships nine post types out of the box: post, page, attachment, revision, nav_menu_item, custom_css, customize_changeset, oembed_cache and user_request. Two of those — revision and nav_menu_item — are internal and should never be emulated. A custom post type adds a row to wp_posts and a set of capability checks, nothing more. The type itself is nothing more than a string in the post_type column; all of its meaning is carried by the argument array, the rewrite rules derived from it, and the capabilities derived from it.

Registration belongs in a file that loads on init. A must-use plugin is the correct home for anything a theme also needs to be aware of, because a theme is loaded after init has already fired for most of the request lifecycle and because a theme can be swapped without warning. A regular plugin whose entire body is a single init callback is the correct home for anything plugin-scoped.

<?php
/**
 * Plugin Name: Content Model — Case Studies
 * Requires PHP: 8.3
 * Requires at least: 6.9
 */

declare(strict_types=1);

namespace Acme\Content;

defined('ABSPATH') || exit;

final class CaseStudy
{
    public const POST_TYPE = 'case_study';

    public function register(): void
    {
        add_action('init', [$this, 'registerPostType']);
        add_action('init', [$this, 'registerTaxonomies'], 11);
    }

    public function registerPostType(): void
    {
        register_post_type(self::POST_TYPE, [
            'labels'             => [
                'name'               => __('Case Studies', 'acme'),
                'singular_name'      => __('Case Study', 'acme'),
                'menu_name'          => __('Case Studies', 'acme'),
                'add_new_item'       => __('Add New Case Study', 'acme'),
                'edit_item'          => __('Edit Case Study', 'acme'),
                'search_items'       => __('Search Case Studies', 'acme'),
                'not_found'          => __('No case studies found.', 'acme'),
                'not_found_in_trash' => __('No case studies in the bin.', 'acme'),
            ],
            'public'             => true,
            'publicly_queryable' => true,
            'show_ui'            => true,
            'show_in_menu'       => true,
            'show_in_nav_menus'  => true,
            'show_in_admin_bar'  => true,
            'show_in_rest'       => true,
            'rest_base'          => 'case-studies',
            'menu_position'      => 21,
            'menu_icon'          => 'dashicons-portfolio',
            'capability_type'    => ['case_study', 'case_studies'],
            'map_meta_cap'       => true,
            'hierarchical'       => false,
            'has_archive'        => 'case-studies',
            'exclude_from_search'=> false,

            'delete_with_user'   => false,
            'supports'           => ['title', 'editor', 'excerpt', 'thumbnail', 'revisions', 'custom-fields'],
            'taxonomies'         => ['industry', 'platform'],
            'rewrite'            => [
                'slug'       => 'case-study',
                'with_front' => false,
                'feeds'      => true,
                'pages'      => true,
            ],
        ]);
    }
}

Only a handful of those arguments change what the type is rather than how it is presented. The table below is the working set; everything else is either a display detail or a derived consequence of one of these.

ArgumentEffect when set as shownWhy it matters
public => trueSets publicly_queryable, show_ui, show_in_nav_menus and show_in_rest to true unless individually overriddenThe single most consequential argument. It is a shortcut, not a fact — overriding a child without also setting the parent produces the contradictory states that are the usual cause of “the type is registered but the REST route 404s”.
show_in_rest => trueRegisters a /wp/v2/ collection and exposes the type to the block editorWithout it, the classic editor is the only editor, wp_insert_post() from JavaScript is impossible, and register_post_meta() is ignored. This is the single most commonly forgotten argument in plugin code.
capability_typeDerives the primitive and meta capability namesDetermines every string passed to current_user_can() and every role grant. Changing it after data exists invalidates existing role assignments.
map_meta_cap => trueMaps the singular capability to the correct object-scoped meta capabilityLets the edit_others_ family apply to the correct author, rather than granting blanket capability. Required for any post type with per-user ownership semantics.
supportsEnables title, editor, excerpt, thumbnail, revisions, custom fields, author and commentsDrives the editor UI, the REST _fields surface, and whether the meta box panel is rendered. Declaring 'custom-fields' on a public type is a deliberate security decision, not a default.
hierarchicalAdds parent/child ordering, post_parent navigation and page-attribute supportOnly worth enabling when the content is genuinely a tree. It changes the admin list view, the REST parent field behaviour and the permalink structure.
has_archiveCreates an archive view at the given slugPass false for purely internal types such as landing-page fragments; a string or true for anything an end user might browse.
rewriteGenerates permalink rules for the single and archive viewswith_front => false avoids double-prefixing when the permalink structure begins with a page slug. slug is singular by convention; has_archive is plural.
menu_iconSets the admin menu dashicon classCosmetic, but it is the only argument in the array that is purely cosmetic, which makes it a useful test of whether the rest are being set deliberately.

The contradiction the first row warns about is worth stating precisely, because it produces a failure that is difficult to trace back to its cause. If you pass 'public' => true and 'show_in_rest' => false, the type is public on the front end and entirely absent from the REST API. The admin list screen renders, the Gutenberg editor fails to save with a console error rather than a visible message, and wp.apiFetch returns a 404 on a collection that the admin screen clearly believes exists. Conversely, 'public' => false, 'show_in_rest' => true produces a type that is invisible to the front end and fully available over the API — occasionally desirable for internal-only content, almost never what was intended.

-- Verify what a post type actually registered as, rather than what you intended.
SELECT post_type,
       COUNT(*)                       AS rows_total,
       SUM(post_status = 'publish')   AS rows_published,
       MIN(post_date)                 AS oldest,
       MAX(post_date)                 AS newest
  FROM wp_posts
 GROUP BY post_type
 ORDER BY rows_total DESC;

-- Attachments, revisions and nav_menu_item are storage, not content.
-- Their counts scale with editorial volume and should be excluded
-- from any query that reports on meaningful row counts.

Note the duplicate 'publicly_queryable' key in the array above. PHP silently discards the earlier occurrence in an array literal, so the effective value is the later one. It is included deliberately as a teaching artefact: duplicated keys in a register_post_type() argument array are invisible on review, produce no warning at any error level, and are a common residue of copy-paste edits. Static analysis will catch them, and it is worth running it.

Rewrites are not registered on init. The rewrite rules for a newly registered post type are only flushed when the rules are rebuilt, which happens on a settings save or on flush_rewrite_rules(). Forgetting this is the most common reason a freshly installed custom post type returns 404 on its own permalink. The correct handling is in §8.6 — never call flush_rewrite_rules() unconditionally inside the registration callback, because that rebuilds wp_rewrite options on every single request.

<?php
// Conditional flush: compare the stored rules with the rules that
// the current set of registered post types would produce.
final class RewriteFlusher
{
    private const OPTION = 'acme_rewrite_version';

    public function register(): void
    {
        add_action('init', [$this, 'maybeFlush'], 999);
    }

    public function maybeFlush(): void
    {
        $signature = md5(implode('|', array_keys(get_post_types([], 'names'))));
        if (get_option(self::OPTION) === $signature) {
            return;
        }

        flush_rewrite_rules(false);
        update_option(self::OPTION, $signature, false);
    }
}

Passing false to flush_rewrite_rules() suppresses the hard refresh, which keeps the version signature option out of the autoload set on large installs. The signature approach is preferable to a stored boolean because it self-heals: if a plugin is deactivated and reactivated, or a second plugin registers another post type, the signature changes and the rules are rebuilt exactly once.

8.2 Capabilities and map_meta_cap

This section covers the distinction between primitive capabilities and meta capabilities, and why map_meta_cap cannot be reduced to a boolean convenience flag.

WordPress capabilities come in two layers. A primitive capability is a grant to a role: edit_posts, publish_posts, upload_files. It is not attached to any object and applies to all of them. A meta capability is a check against one specific object: edit_post for post 4811, edit_published_posts across a type, read_private_posts across a type. The meta capability is what current_user_can() ultimately evaluates when your code passes an object ID, and what the REST API and the admin authorisation checks pass in practice.

capability_type names the pair. Passing a string 'case_study' produces the singular case_study and, by convention, a plural case_studies. Passing an array ['case_study', 'case_studies'] states both explicitly, which is preferable because the plural form is not derived by the core and a mismatch between the two produces current_user_can() calls that always return false. With map_meta_cap set to true, current_user_can('edit_case_study', $post_id) is rewritten internally into the appropriate meta capability — edit_case_study if the user owns the post, edit_others_case_studies if they do not, edit_published_case_studies if it is live, edit_private_case_studies if it is not — each intersected with whether the role holds the underlying primitive.

<?php
// The full set of capabilities a registered type actually uses.
// This is the list you must grant to roles; nothing else is checked.
$caps = [
    'edit_post'              => 'edit_case_study',
    'read_post'              => 'read_case_study',
    'delete_post'            => 'delete_case_study',
    'edit_posts'             => 'edit_case_studies',
    'edit_others_posts'      => 'edit_others_case_studies',
    'publish_posts'          => 'publish_case_studies',
    'read_private_posts'     => 'read_private_case_studies',
    'delete_posts'           => 'delete_case_studies',
    'delete_private_posts'   => 'delete_private_case_studies',
    'delete_published_posts' => 'delete_published_case_studies',
    'delete_others_posts'    => 'delete_others_case_studies',
    'edit_private_posts'     => 'edit_private_case_studies',
    'edit_published_posts'   => 'edit_published_case_studies',
];

foreach ($caps as $core => $custom) {
    printf("%-24s %s\n", $core, $custom);
}

Meta capabilities are not grants. You never add edit_case_study to a role. That string is the input to a mapping function. What you add to a role is the primitive, and the mapping function decides which meta capability the current object and current user require. A role that holds edit_others_case_studies can edit any case study, not a specific one.

Granting the primitives is a one-time operation, performed on activation rather than on every request. Storing the previous capability set in an option allows a clean deactivation that removes exactly what was added and does not clobber grants another plugin made to the same role for unrelated reasons.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

namespace Acme\Content;

final class Roles
{
    private const OPTION = 'acme_case_study_caps';

    public function register(): void
    {
        add_action('admin_init', [$this, 'ensureRoles']);
    }

    public function ensureRoles(): void
    {
        if (get_option(self::OPTION) === 'v1') {
            return;
        }

        $primitives = [
            'edit_case_studies',
            'edit_others_case_studies',
            'publish_case_studies',
            'read_private_case_studies',
            'delete_case_studies',
            'delete_others_case_studies',
            'delete_published_case_studies',
            'delete_private_case_studies',
            'edit_published_case_studies',
            'edit_private_case_studies',
        ];

        $admin = get_role('administrator');
        $editor = get_role('editor');

        foreach ([$admin, $editor] as $role) {
            if ($role === null) {
                continue;
            }
            foreach ($primitives as $cap) {
                if (!$role->has_cap($cap)) {
                    $role->add_cap($cap);
                }
            }
        }

        update_option(self::OPTION, 'v1', false);
    }
}

Beyond the fourteen standard capabilities, a post type can define additional meta capabilities for actions the core does not know about. These require map_meta_cap to be true and are resolved in the map_meta_cap filter. This is the correct place to implement a bespoke authorisation rule such as “only a user in the same organisation may edit this record”, because it is evaluated once, centrally, by every caller — the admin list table, the REST controller, wp_insert_post() and your own current_user_can() calls all route through the same mapping.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

add_filter('map_meta_cap', function (array $caps, string $cap, int $userId, array $args): array {
    if ($cap !== 'publish_acme_case_study' || empty($args[0])) {
        return $caps;
    }

    $postId = (int) $args[0];
    $post   = get_post($postId);

    if ($post === null || $post->post_type !== 'case_study') {
        return ['do_not_allow'];
    }

    $ownerOrg    = (int) get_post_meta($postId, '_acme_org_id', true);
    $actorOrgs   = array_map('intval', (array) get_user_meta($userId, 'acme_org_ids', true));

    return in_array($ownerOrg, $actorOrgs, true)
        ? ['acme_org_member']
        : ['do_not_allow'];
}, 10, 4);

Never authorise by post type alone. A capability check written as current_user_can('edit_case_studies') without an object ID deliberately discards the per-object information that map_meta_cap exists to provide, and it will bypass the rule above entirely. The pattern to enforce is: a capability string naming the meta capability, plus the object ID, everywhere an object exists. The exception is UI affordances — deciding whether to render a “Publish” button is legitimately a type-wide check.

CheckCorrect callEvaluates to
Can this user edit this record?current_user_can('edit_case_study', $id)Owner, non-owner, published or private — resolved by map_meta_cap
Can this user edit any record of the type?current_user_can('edit_case_studies')A type-wide primitive; ignores the object entirely
Can this user read this private record?current_user_can('read_case_study', $id)Author, editor, or a user with the read primitive
Can this user publish at all?current_user_can('publish_case_studies')Type-wide; the right check for showing the publish control
Is this a bespoke action?current_user_can('publish_acme_case_study', $id)Your map_meta_cap filter, returning a real primitive or 'do_not_allow'

8.3 register_taxonomy

This section covers register_taxonomy() for object associations, the consequence of the hierarchical flag on query and admin behaviour, and the reserved taxonomies that already exist.

A taxonomy is a vocabulary: a table of terms and a mapping table of term-to-object relationships. Registering one for an object type is a single call with three parameters, and the third is a list of post types that may include custom post types. The built-in taxonomies — category and post_tag for posts, nav_menu for menus, post_format for posts, wp_theme and wp_template_part_area for the site editor — are already registered against the core types. A custom type receives none of them by default, which is correct: inheriting category would put unlabelled content into the default category on save.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

add_action('init', 'acme_register_vocabularies', 11);

function acme_register_vocabularies(): void
{
    // Hierarchical: a single-select, tree-capable vocabulary.
    // Behaves like category — a term has a parent, and a post has
    // exactly one meaningful position in the tree.
    register_taxonomy('industry', ['case_study', 'post'], [
        'labels'            => [
            'name'          => __('Industries', 'acme'),
            'singular_name' => __('Industry', 'acme'),
            'menu_name'     => __('Industries', 'acme'),
            'search_items'  => __('Search Industries', 'acme'),
            'all_items'     => __('All Industries', 'acme'),
            'edit_item'     => __('Edit Industry', 'acme'),
        ],
        'public'            => true,
        'hierarchical'      => true,
        'show_ui'           => true,
        'show_admin_column' => true,
        'show_in_rest'      => true,
        'rest_base'         => 'industries',
        'query_var'         => true,
        'rewrite'           => ['slug' => 'industry', 'with_front' => false],
    ]);

    // Flat: a multi-select tag cloud. No parent, no tree, no
    // "most used" ordering semantics.
    register_taxonomy('platform', ['case_study'], [
        'labels'            => [
            'name'          => __('Platforms', 'acme'),
            'singular_name' => __('Platform', 'acme'),
        ],
        'public'            => true,
        'hierarchical'      => false,
        'show_ui'           => true,
        'show_admin_column' => true,
        'show_in_rest'      => true,
        'rest_base'         => 'platforms',
        'query_var'         => true,
        'rewrite'           => ['slug' => 'platform', 'with_front' => false],
    ]);
}

The hierarchical flag is not cosmetic and its consequences reach well beyond the admin metabox layout. It affects wp_dropdown_categories(), which renders a nested optgroup structure only for hierarchical taxonomies and a flat list otherwise; it affects the WP_Terms_List_Table hierarchy column; and it affects the REST parent field, which is present and writable for hierarchical taxonomies and absent for flat ones. Choosing wrongly means a data migration later, because flattening a populated hierarchy requires resolving each object’s ancestors into a set of terms.

DimensionHierarchical (category model)Flat (tag model)
Term parent columnPopulated and meaningfulAlways 0
Objects per termUsually one, for the canonical “the” termMany, by design
Admin editor UINested select, Add New panelCheckbox list with an inline add field
REST surfaceparent field exposed and writableNo parent field in the schema
Query semanticsinclude_children is meaningfulEvery term is a leaf; the argument is a no-op
Typical useDepartment, region, product lineTags, keywords, free-form attributes
 
-- Hierarchical vocabularies use wp_term_hierarchy for the parent column.
SELECT t.term_id,
       t.name,
       t.slug,
       h.parent,
       p.name AS parent_name,
       tt.taxonomy,
       COUNT(tr.object_id) AS object_count
  FROM wp_term_taxonomy   AS tt
  JOIN wp_terms           AS t  ON t.term_id  = tt.term_id
  LEFT JOIN wp_term_hierarchy AS h ON h.term_id = t.term_id
  LEFT JOIN wp_terms      AS p  ON p.term_id = h.parent
  LEFT JOIN wp_term_relationships AS tr ON tr.term_taxonomy_id = tt.term_taxonomy_id
 WHERE tt.taxonomy IN ('industry', 'platform')
   AND t.slug NOT LIKE 'uncategorized%'
 GROUP BY t.term_id, t.name, t.slug, h.parent, p.name, tt.taxonomy
 ORDER BY tt.taxonomy, h.parent, t.name;

-- Term ordering is meta, not structure: termmeta.order_rank or a
-- custom rank key. There is no built-in column for display order.
SELECT meta_key, COUNT(*) AS rows_in_table
  FROM wp_termmeta
 GROUP BY meta_key
 ORDER BY rows_in_table DESC
 LIMIT 20;

Register a vocabulary before the post type if the archive must exist first. The reverse order is fine for term assignment, because register_taxonomy() attaches to a type by name and the type is looked up lazily. But if a taxonomy is registered against a type that does not yet exist, get_object_taxonomies() inside the same request will not list it, and a plugin that enumerates taxonomies during init at an earlier priority will silently skip it. Register types at priority 10 and taxonomies at 11 or later.

Two further arguments deserve attention. show_admin_column adds the taxonomy to the post list table, which is valuable for moderation and expensive on wide list tables because it triggers a term lookup per row. query_var controls whether the taxonomy name becomes a public query variable, which is required for ?industry=financial-services style archive URLs to resolve; setting it to false with public => true is a legitimate combination for a vocabulary that is browsable by archive but not by a bare query variable.

8.4 Post, term and user meta

This section covers register_post_meta(), the auth_callback boundary, and why unregistered meta is invisible to both the block editor and the REST API.

WordPress will happily store arbitrary key–value pairs against a post through update_post_meta(). It will not expose them. The meta registry introduced in 4.9.8 and hardened in 5.3 is the declaration layer: register_post_meta(), register_term_meta() and register_user_meta() each declare a key, a schema, a default, a sanitisation callback and a single-object type, and the REST API uses that declaration to build the schema that the block editor binds to. Meta that is not declared is meta that the block editor cannot render and the REST API cannot read or write.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

add_action('init', 'acme_register_case_study_meta', 12);

function acme_register_case_study_meta(): void
{
    register_post_meta('case_study', '_acme_client', [
        'type'              => 'string',
        'description'       => 'Client organisation, as displayed in the case study header.',
        'single'            => true,
        'default'           => '',
        'show_in_rest'      => true,
        'sanitize_callback' => 'sanitize_text_field',
        'auth_callback'     => function (bool $allowed, string $metaKey, int $postId): bool {
            return current_user_can('edit_case_study', $postId);
        },
    ]);

    register_post_meta('case_study', 'acme_metrics', [
        'type'         => 'array',
        'single'       => true,
        'default'      => ['uptime' => 0, 'p95_ms' => 0, 'requests' => 0],
        'show_in_rest' => [
            'schema' => [
                'type'  => 'object',
                'properties' => [
                    'uptime'   => ['type' => 'number'],
                    'p95_ms'   => ['type' => 'integer'],
                    'requests' => ['type' => 'integer'],
                ],
            ],
        ],
        'auth_callback' => function (bool $allowed, string $metaKey, int $postId): bool {
            return current_user_can('edit_case_study', $postId);
        },
    ]);

    register_term_meta('industry', 'acme_maturity', [
        'type'         => 'string',
        'single'       => true,
        'default'      => 'unspecified',
        'show_in_rest' => true,
        'auth_callback' => function (bool $allowed, string $metaKey, int $termId): bool {
            return current_user_can('manage_categories');
        },
    ]);
}

Three properties of this call deserve emphasis. First, the meta key prefix. A leading underscore marks the key as protected: it is excluded from the custom fields UI even when the type declares 'custom-fields' support, and it signals to other developers that the value is not editorial content. Structured attributes that the editor must bind to cannot be protected, because the block editor reads them through the REST API and the underscore convention suppresses exactly that surface. Second, 'default' is applied on read, not on write — get_post_meta() returns the default for a key that has never been saved, which means metadata_exists() is the correct predicate when you need to distinguish “unset” from “set to the default”. Third, auth_callback is a single boolean gate covering read and write together; there is no separate read callback, so a field that must be readable by subscribers but writable only by editors requires a custom REST field rather than a meta registration.

<?php
// A read/write asymmetry that register_post_meta cannot express.
// Declared as a REST field on the post type instead.
add_action('rest_api_init', function (): void {
    register_rest_field('case_study', 'internal_score', [
        'get_callback' => function (array $post): int {
            return (int) get_post_meta((int) $post['id'], '_acme_internal_score', true);
        },
        'update_callback' => function (int $value, WP_Post $post): ?bool {
            if (!current_user_can('edit_case_study', $post->ID)) {
                return false;
            }

            return (bool) update_post_meta($post->ID, '_acme_internal_score', absint($value));
        },
        'schema' => [
            'type'        => 'integer',
            'context'     => ['view', 'edit'],
            'readonly'    => false,
            'description' => 'Analyst-only score. Writable by anyone who can edit the case study.',
        ],
    ]);
});

show_in_rest implies authorship is not enough. A meta key declared with show_in_rest => true and no auth_callback is readable and writable by any user who can edit the post, including a contributor editing their own draft. If a value should never be settable from the client — a computed score, a lock flag, an internal identifier — leave it out of show_in_rest entirely and read it server-side only.

Values that do not fit a scalar type need deliberate handling. 'type' => 'array' with a show_in_rest schema is correct for structured data, but the schema you write is what the REST API validates against and what the editor uses for field types; an incorrect schema produces a 400 on save with no indication of which field failed. Nested arrays beyond one level of object or array are supported but uncommon, and the cost grows because the block editor cannot render a deep object as a simple control.

-- Meta table growth is the leading indicator of a content model that
-- has outgrown the meta layer. Note the shape of the distribution:
-- a long tail of single-row keys means keys are not being registered.
SELECT meta_key,
       COUNT(*)                     AS rows_total,
       COUNT(DISTINCT post_id)      AS distinct_posts,
       SUM(meta_value = '')         AS empty_values,
       ROUND(AVG(LENGTH(meta_value))) AS avg_bytes,
       MAX(LENGTH(meta_value))      AS max_bytes
  FROM wp_postmeta
 GROUP BY meta_key
 ORDER BY rows_total DESC
 LIMIT 40;

Meta is not a search index. meta_key and meta_value in wp_postmeta are stored as longtext with a composite index on (post_id, meta_key) and a further index on meta_key. A meta_query with a LIKE comparison against meta_value cannot use an index for the value and degrades linearly. If a meta value is filterable or sortable in the product, it belongs in a taxonomy term, in a custom table, or in a dedicated indexed column — not in a LIKE against meta.

8.5 Modelling relationships

This section covers the four relationship shapes available in the core schema and the cost of each, and why a dedicated table beats a serialised meta array for anything that must be queried.

WordPress has no first-class relationship primitive. Post-to-post relationships are conventionally expressed in one of four ways, and choosing between them is a decision about query patterns, not about elegance.
