The options are: a meta value holding a comma-separated list of IDs, a meta value holding a serialised array, a pair of reciprocal meta keys written in both directions, or a dedicated table. The first two are cheap to write and expensive to query; the third is expensive to keep consistent; the fourth requires you to own its indexes, its cascading behaviour and its invalidation.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

// The reciprocal-maintenance pattern, done safely.
// A partial failure here is how the data becomes inconsistent, so
// every write is guarded and every delete is mirrored.
final class CaseStudyRelations
{
    public function link(int $caseStudyId, int $productId): bool
    {
        if (get_post_type($caseStudyId) !== 'case_study' || get_post_type($productId) !== 'acme_product') {
            return false;
        }

        // Refuse cycles: walking the reverse edge must terminate.
        if ($this->wouldCreateCycle($caseStudyId, $productId)) {
            return false;
        }

        $ok = add_post_meta($caseStudyId, '_acme_products', $productId, false);
        if ($ok) {
            add_post_meta($productId, '_acme_case_studies', $caseStudyId, false);
        }

        return (bool) $ok;
    }

    public function unlink(int $caseStudyId, int $productId): bool
    {
        $removed = delete_post_meta($caseStudyId, '_acme_products', $productId);

        if ($removed) {
            delete_post_meta($productId, '_acme_case_studies', $caseStudyId);
        }

        return $removed;
    }

    private function wouldCreateCycle(int $from, int $to, int $depth = 0): bool
    {
        if ($from === $to || $depth > 10) {
            return $from === $to;
        }

        foreach ((array) get_post_meta($to, '_acme_products', false) as $next) {
            if ($this->wouldCreateCycle($from, (int) $next, $depth + 1)) {
                return true;
            }
        }

        return false;
    }
}

The reciprocal pattern is defensible for small, low-churn graphs where both directions are genuinely needed in the front end. It is not defensible when the relationship count per object exceeds a few dozen, because every insert is two writes and there is no transaction to make them atomic. A dedicated table is the correct answer for anything with a growth curve, and the cost is smaller than it appears: one table, one composite index, and a small set of accessor functions that replace direct SQL.

-- A dedicated relationship table, created on version activation.
-- The reverse index is mandatory: every read traverses the other
-- direction, and without it the second query in any pair is a scan.
CREATE TABLE wp_acme_case_study_product (
    id            BIGINT UNSIGNED NOT NULL AUTO_INCREMENT,
    case_study_id BIGINT UNSIGNED NOT NULL,
    product_id    BIGINT UNSIGNED NOT NULL,
    sort_order    INT UNSIGNED    NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    created_at    DATETIME        NOT NULL DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP,
    PRIMARY KEY (id),
    UNIQUE KEY case_study_product (case_study_id, product_id),
    KEY product_case_study (product_id, case_study_id, sort_order)
) ENGINE=InnoDB DEFAULT CHARSET=utf8mb4 COLLATE=utf8mb4_unicode_520_ci;

-- Read forward with sort order honoured.
SELECT p.ID,
       p.post_title,
       r.sort_order
  FROM wp_acme_case_study_product AS r
  JOIN wp_posts AS p ON p.ID = r.product_id
 WHERE r.case_study_id = 4811
   AND p.post_status = 'publish'
 ORDER BY r.sort_order ASC, p.post_title ASC
 LIMIT 20;

-- Read reverse for the "related case studies" panel.
SELECT p.ID,
       p.post_title
  FROM wp_acme_case_study_product AS r
  JOIN wp_posts AS p ON p.ID = r.case_study_id
 WHERE r.product_id = 902
   AND p.post_status = 'publish'
 ORDER BY p.post_date DESC
 LIMIT 6;

If a dedicated table is created, three obligations follow and they are non-negotiable. First, the table name must include the blog id on multisite, or a per-site table must be created on site creation — a global table silently collides across ten thousand sites. Second, deletion of either endpoint must cascade, and the core does not do it for you; the deleted_post hook is the only reliable interception point. Third, the table must be included in the database export and health check tooling, which in practice means a documented dbDelta schema and a health_check test that verifies it exists.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

use Automattic\Wpdb\Wpdb;

function acme_get_related_case_studies(int $productId, int $limit = 6): array
{
    global $wpdb;

    $ids = $wpdb->get_col($wpdb->prepare(
        <<<'SQL'
        SELECT r.case_study_id
          FROM {$wpdb->prefix}acme_case_study_product AS r
          JOIN {$wpdb->posts} AS p ON p.ID = r.case_study_id
         WHERE r.product_id = %d
           AND p.post_status = 'publish'
           AND p.post_type = 'case_study'
         ORDER BY r.sort_order ASC
         LIMIT %d
        SQL,
        $productId,
        $limit
    ));

    if ($ids === []) {
        return [];
    }

    $query = new WP_Query([
        'post_type'              => 'case_study',
        'post__in'               => array_map('intval', $ids),
        'orderby'                => 'post__in',
        'posts_per_page'         => $limit,
        'no_found_rows'          => true,
        'update_post_meta_cache' => false,
        'update_post_term_cache' => false,
    ]);

    return $query->posts;
}

// Cascade both endpoints. deleted_post fires for the post and for
// each of its revisions, so the DELETE must be idempotent.
add_action('deleted_post', function (int $postId, WP_Post $post): void {
    global $wpdb;
    $table = $wpdb->prefix . 'acme_case_study_product';

    match ($post->post_type) {
        'case_study'   => $wpdb->query($wpdb->prepare("DELETE FROM {$table} WHERE case_study_id = %d", $postId)),
        'acme_product' => $wpdb->query($wpdb->prepare("DELETE FROM {$table} WHERE product_id = %d", $postId)),
        default        => null,
    };
}, 10, 2);

Note the use of the <<<'SQL' heredoc and the prepare() call. Interpolating $wpdb->prefix is safe because it is a value the site controls, not user input; interpolating a post id is not, and prepare() is what makes the difference. The match in the cascade hook is exhaustive by type and returns null for every other post type, which keeps the hook from touching the table on unrelated deletions — a meaningful saving on a site with a large media library, where deleted_post fires once per attachment.

Hydrate through WP_Query, not through the relationship table. The custom table is the index; wp_posts is the source of truth for titles, statuses, dates and permalinks. Joining out to wp_posts in raw SQL and then permalinking each row by hand duplicates logic that get_permalink() already handles correctly for every post type, including custom rewrite slugs and hierarchical permalinks. Use the custom table to obtain an ordered set of IDs, then let WP_Query resolve them.

8.6 Registration lifecycle and discipline

This section sets out the ordering rules that make a content model reliable across activation, upgrade, multisite and CLI contexts.

Registration is a process-wide side effect. The rules that keep it predictable are short. Register post types and taxonomies on init at the default priority of ten, or at a priority no earlier than ten. Do not register in a plugin bootstrap file, because the bootstrap runs before $wpdb is fully available for rewrite registration and before translations are loaded for the type’s labels. Do not register on admin_init or wp_loaded, because then any code that ran earlier in the request — including cron callbacks, REST route registration, and other plugins' init callbacks at priority ten — has already enumerated the set of post types and will not see yours.

<?php
/**
 * Plugin Name: Acme Content Model
 * Version:     3.2.0
 * Requires PHP: 8.3
 */

declare(strict_types=1);

namespace Acme\Content;

defined('ABSPATH') || exit;

final class Plugin
{
    public static function boot(): void
    {
        $plugin = new self();

        // Priority 10: post types first, because taxonomies and
        // meta registrations resolve the type by name.
        add_action('init', [$plugin, 'registerPostTypes'], 10);
        add_action('init', [$plugin, 'registerTaxonomies'], 11);
        add_action('init', [$plugin, 'registerMeta'], 12);
        add_action('init', [$plugin, 'registerRewrites'], 20);

        // Post-registration wiring: capabilities, REST extras, admin.
        add_action('init', [$plugin, 'registerRoles'], 30);
        add_action('rest_api_init', [$plugin, 'registerRestFields']);
        add_action('admin_init', [$plugin, 'registerAdminColumns']);
        add_filter('the_content', [$plugin, 'renderFooterBlock'], 20);
    }
}

add_action('plugins_loaded', static fn() => Plugin::boot());

The rule that catches teams out is the second-order effect on other plugins. If plugin A at init priority 10 builds a navigation menu or a search index by calling get_post_types(), and plugin B registers its type at priority 11, plugin A’s list is incomplete for the whole request. No error is raised, no warning is logged, and the failure appears only in a specific screen. Registering early and consistently is not a style preference; it is a contract.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

namespace Acme\Content;

use wpdb;

final class Schema
{
    public function register(): void
    {
        add_action('plugins_activated', [$this, 'onActivate']);
        add_action('wp_initialize_site', [$this, 'onNewSite'], 20);
    }

    public function onActivate(): void
    {
        require_once ABSPATH . 'wp-admin/includes/upgrade.php';

        // Multisite: the table must exist on every existing site, and
        // wp_initialize_site only fires for sites created later.
        if (is_multisite()) {
            $sites = get_sites(['fields' => 'ids', 'number' => 0]);
            foreach ($sites as $siteId) {
                switch_to_blog((int) $siteId);
                $this->createTable();
                restore_current_blog();
            }
            return;
        }

        $this->createTable();
    }

    public function onNewSite(WP_Site $site): void
    {
        switch_to_blog((int) $site->blog_id);
        $this->createTable();
        restore_current_blog();
    }

    private function createTable(): void
    {
        global $wpdb;

        $charset = $wpdb->get_charset_collate();
        $table   = $wpdb->prefix . 'acme_case_study_product';

        dbDelta(
            "CREATE TABLE {$table} (
                id BIGINT UNSIGNED NOT NULL AUTO_INCREMENT,
                case_study_id BIGINT UNSIGNED NOT NULL,
                product_id BIGINT UNSIGNED NOT NULL,
                sort_order INT UNSIGNED NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
                created_at DATETIME NOT NULL DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP,
                PRIMARY KEY  (id),
                UNIQUE KEY case_study_product (case_study_id, product_id),
                KEY product_case_study (product_id, case_study_id, sort_order)
            ) {$charset};"
        );
    }
}

# Verify the registered content model from outside PHP.
$ wp post-type list --fields=name,public,show_in_rest,hierarchical,has_archive
+---------------------+--------+------------+--------------+-------------+
| name                | public | show_in_rest | hierarchical | has_archive |
+---------------------+--------+------------+--------------+-------------+
| post                | 1      | 1          | 0            | 0           |
| page                | 1      | 1          | 1            | 0           |
| attachment          | 1      | 1          | 0            | 0           |
| case_study          | 1      | 1          | 0            | 1           |
| acme_product        | 1      | 1          | 0            | 1           |
+---------------------+--------+------------+--------------+-------------+

$ wp taxonomy list case_study --fields=name,hierarchical,public,show_in_rest
+------------+--------------+--------+---------------+
| name       | hierarchical | public | show_in_rest |
+------------+--------------+--------+---------------+
| industry   | 1            | 1      | 1             |
| platform   | 0            | 1      | 1             |
| post_tag   | 0            | 1      | 1             |
+------------+--------------+--------+---------------+

$ wp eval 'print_r( get_post_type_object("case_study")->cap );'
Array
(
    [edit_post]              => edit_case_study
    [read_post]              => read_case_study
    [delete_post]            => delete_case_study
    [edit_posts]             => edit_case_studies
    [edit_others_posts]      => edit_others_case_studies
    [publish_posts]          => publish_case_studies
    [read_private_posts]     => read_private_case_studies
    [delete_posts]           => delete_case_studies
    [delete_private_posts]   => delete_private_case_studies
    [delete_published_posts] => delete_published_case_studies
    [delete_others_posts]    => delete_others_case_studies
    [edit_private_posts]     => edit_private_case_studies
    [edit_published_posts]   => edit_published_case_studies
)

wp post-type list and wp taxonomy list are the fastest way to settle an argument about what a plugin actually registered, because they read the same global objects the rest of the request reads. They also reveal the classic failure directly: a type with show_in_rest reporting 0 is a type the block editor cannot save, and no amount of client-side work will change that.

# Block editor support is verifiable end to end, not by inspection.
$ wp rest get /wp/v2/case-studies?per_page=1 --field=0.id
4821

$ wp rest get /wp/v2/case-studies/4821 --fields=id,slug,acme_client,acme_metrics
{
    "id": 4821,
    "slug": "northwind-logistics",
    "acme_client": "Northwind Logistics",
    "acme_metrics": {"uptime": 99.98, "p95_ms": 142, "requests": 0}
}

# A 400 here means the show_in_rest schema and the stored value disagree.
$ wp rest post /wp/v2/case-studies/4821 --field=acme_metrics='{"uptime":"high"}'
Error: Parameter validation failed.

# Capability inspection for a specific user.
$ wp eval '
$ u = get_user_by("login", "editor");
foreach (["edit_case_studies", "edit_others_case_studies", "manage_options"] as $c) {
    printf("%-28s %s\n", $c, user_can($u, $c) ? "yes" : "no");
}'
edit_case_studies           yes
edit_others_case_studies    yes
manage_options             no

Deactivation does not unregister anything. Post types, taxonomies and meta registrations live in PHP globals for the duration of a request. They exist exactly as long as the plugin is loaded, which means a post type’s rows in wp_posts become invisible the moment the plugin is deactivated — they are not deleted, and the standard list table will not show them. If a type must be protected against accidental deletion, either register an untrashed or before_delete_post guard, or exclude it from bulk operations. Content that is hidden rather than destroyed is recoverable; content that a bulk action deleted is not.

Exercises 8

  1. Register a job_listing post type in a must-use plugin with the full argument set, including capability_type => ['job_listing', 'job_listings'] and map_meta_cap => true. Then prove, using wp eval against two different users holding different role grants, that the fourteen derived capabilities differ between them for a post the first user owns and the second does not. Submit the two capability dumps side by side.
  2. Add a bespoke meta capability approve_acme_job_listing resolved through the map_meta_cap filter so that only users whose acme_approver_ids user meta contains the listing’s author ID may approve it. Demonstrate the filter returning ['do_not_allow'] for a non-approver by asserting current_user_can( 'approve_acme_job_listing', $id ) is false, and document the specific line that would break if map_meta_cap were set to false.
  3. Register the flat taxonomy tech_stack and the hierarchical taxonomy job_function against job_listing. Seed 30 terms and 200 listings, then use the SQL from §8.3 to show that job_function populates wp_term_hierarchy and tech_stack does not. Record the row counts from both before and after flattening one hierarchy.
  4. Declare four meta keys for job_listing — one protected string, one public string with auth_callback, one integer array with a show_in_rest schema, and one term meta on job_function — and confirm through the REST API that each is readable and writable only under the intended capability. Include the JSON body of a deliberately malformed write and the 400 response it produces.
  5. Implement the wp_acme_job_product relationship table with dbDelta, a bidirectional accessor, a cycle guard and a deleted_post cascade that is idempotent under revision deletion. Demonstrate the cycle guard by attempting a link that would close a loop of length three, and show the EXPLAIN output proving the reverse index is used.

9 · The Options API, Transients and Site Options

9.1 Reading and writing options9.2 The autoload column9.3 The transient API9.4 Site options and wp_sitemeta9.5 Configuration that does not belong in the database9.6 Auditing and repairing option bloat

The options table is the most misused storage layer in WordPress, in both directions: it accumulates serialised blobs that were never meant to be cached, and it accumulates flags that were never meant to be stored at all. This chapter covers the three write functions and the precise reason their behaviour differs, the autoload column and the semantic change that arrived in 6.6 when the boolean 'yes'/'no' values were replaced by the three-state 'auto', 'on' and 'off', and the practical consequence that every autoloaded option is loaded on every request regardless of whether the current request touches it. It then covers the transient API, whose behaviour changes fundamentally depending on whether a persistent object cache is present, and site options, which live in a different table with different semantics. The closing sections address the discipline of keeping configuration out of the database entirely, and the queries needed to find the offenders on a large install.

9.1 Reading and writing options

This section establishes the difference between add_option, update_option and delete_option, and the caching and race semantics each one carries.

All three functions ultimately reach the same wp_options row, but they are not interchangeable and the differences are load-bearing. add_option() inserts only if the key does not already exist and returns false otherwise; it is the correct primitive for a default value, because it is the only one that will not overwrite an existing stored value. update_option() writes unconditionally and returns false when the new value is identical to the old. delete_option() removes the row and the autoload record, and fires deleted_option so that object caches can be invalidated.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

namespace Acme\Settings;

defined('ABSPATH') || exit;

final class Defaults
{
    private const KEY = 'acme_api_settings';

    public function register(): void
    {
        // add_option is the correct default-setting primitive: it
        // never clobbers a value an administrator has already edited.
        add_action('admin_init', [$this, 'seedDefaults']);
    }

    public function seedDefaults(): void
    {
        $defaults = [
            'endpoint'   => 'https://api.acme.test/v2',
            'timeout'    => 10,
            'retries'    => 3,
            'cache_ttl'  => 900,
            'verify_ssl' => true,
        ];

        add_option(self::KEY, $defaults, '', false);
    }

    public function all(): array
    {
        $stored = get_option(self::KEY, []);

        if (!is_array($stored)) {
            $stored = [];
        }

        return array_replace($defaultsSnapshot(), $stored);
    }
}

There is a fourth function that is routinely mistaken for one of these three. get_option() with a second argument returns that value when the key is absent, but it does not create anything. The default argument is evaluated on every call, so passing an expensive expression as the default is a per-call cost, not a one-off. The third and fourth arguments — $autoload and $deprecated — control schema-level behaviour rather than data.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

// Reading options correctly, including the failure modes.
$timeout = get_option('acme_request_timeout');

// Wrong: '0', '' and false are all falsy, and get_option returns
// false both for "absent" and for "stored as false".
$timeout = (int) get_option('acme_request_timeout', 10) ?: 10;

// Right: distinguish absence from a deliberately stored falsy value.
if (!isset($settings['timeout'])) {
    $settings['timeout'] = 10;
}

// Right: a value that must never be a string.
$retries = max(0, min(5, (int) get_option('acme_retries', 3)));

// A stored serialised array is already decoded by get_option. Calling
// maybe_unserialize on the result is a common and pointless double
// decode that turns legitimate string values into surprises.
$raw = get_option('acme_blob', []);
$decoded = is_string($raw) ? json_decode($raw, true) : $raw;

Values are stored with type fidelity, not always. Strings, integers, floats and booleans round-trip correctly. Arrays and objects are serialised by maybe_serialize() and transparently unserialised by get_option(). That transparency is the reason option values silently become arrays in code that was written expecting scalars, and it is why get_option() should always be type-checked at the boundary rather than trusted.

Concurrency is the other reason the choice between add_option() and update_option() matters. Both are non-atomic read-modify-write cycles. Two concurrent requests calling update_option() with a value derived from what each read will produce a last-writer-wins result with no error. For genuinely contended keys — counters, locks, rate-limit state — the correct tool is a database-level atomic operation, either a dedicated table with INSERT … ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE, or a transient acting as a lock (see §9.3).

-- Atomic counter increment. Two concurrent requests cannot interleave
-- and lose an increment, which is exactly what an options read-modify-
-- write cycle guarantees they will do.
INSERT INTO wp_acme_counters (name, value, updated_at)
     VALUES ('api_calls', 1, NOW())
ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE
     value      = value + 1,
     updated_at = NOW();

-- Read it back in one round trip rather than add_option + update_option.
SELECT value FROM wp_acme_counters WHERE name = 'api_calls';

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

final class Counter
{
    public function increment(string $name, int $by = 1): int
    {
        global $wpdb;

        $table = $wpdb->prefix . 'acme_counters';

        $wpdb->query($wpdb->prepare(
            "INSERT INTO {$table} (name, value, updated_at)
                  VALUES (%s, %d, NOW())
             ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE
                      value = value + %d,
                      updated_at = NOW()",
            $name,
            $by,
            $by
        ));

        return (int) $wpdb->get_var(
            $wpdb->prepare("SELECT value FROM {$table} WHERE name = %s", $name)
        );
    }
}

Never store a growing log in an option. Options are designed for small, single-valued configuration. A log, a queue, a running counter with history or anything that appends belongs in a table, because every append to an option is a read, a serialise, a write and an object-cache invalidation — and the option row grows without bound, inflating every SELECT that touches it and every backup of the database.

9.2 The autoload column

This section explains what the autoload column does at query time, the three-state model introduced in 6.6, and the difference between 'auto' and 'on'.

Every row in wp_options carries an autoload value that determines whether it is included in the single query WordPress issues on bootstrap. In current core that query is issued by wp_load_alloptions(), which selects a fixed set of columns for all rows whose autoload value places them in the autoload set, ordered so that a short list of “on” rows is returned first and longer “auto” values follow. The result is loaded into a single array held in the alloptions cache, so the cost of an autoloaded option is paid by every request on the site regardless of whether that request has any relationship to the option.

Before 6.6 the column held 'yes' or 'no'. From 6.6 it holds one of three values, and the change is semantic rather than cosmetic: 'on' is the unconditional autoload, 'off' is the unconditional opt-out, and 'auto' means “autoload this value unless it is large enough to be worth keeping out of every request”.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

namespace Acme\Settings;

defined('ABSPATH') || exit;

// 6.6+ third argument is a string, not a boolean.
// Passing true / false still works and maps to 'on' / 'off', but
// the string form is what the type documents and what you should write.
add_option('acme_feature_flags', ['checkout_v2' => true], '', 'auto');
add_option('acme_api_endpoint', 'https://api.acme.test/v2', '', 'on');

// The default when the argument is omitted is 'auto' as of 6.6.
// Writing it explicitly documents the intent at the call site.
update_option('acme_last_crawl', time(), 'off');

// A large computed value should never be autoloaded. 'off' forces
// the opt-out; 'auto' would also opt out above the size threshold,
// but only by accident of the value it happened to be on that day.
update_option('acme_full_index', $largeArray, 'off');
autoloadLoaded on every request?Use forFailure mode if misused
'on'AlwaysSmall values read on nearly every request: site title, active theme, permalink structure, plugin listFront-end latency scales with total bytes, on every page, including admin-ajax and cron
'auto'Yes, unless largeConfiguration that is usually small but occasionally grows — a settings array with optional blocksA value that is small on day one and 2 MB on day ninety is silently promoted into the alloptions set
'off'NoAnything read rarely, written often, or large: caches, indexes, logs, per-request feature stateAn extra query on the pages that do read it — usually cheaper than the alternative
'yes' (legacy)AlwaysPre-6.6 sites; still present in databases not yet migratedNothing, but the value is deprecated and should be normalised
'no' (legacy)NoPre-6.6 sitesSame; the core migration rewrites both values

The distinction between 'auto' and 'off' is worth stating precisely because the two are routinely treated as interchangeable. 'auto' hands the decision to core’s size heuristic at the moment the value is written. 'off' is a statement of intent that survives the value growing. For a settings array that genuinely does grow with the number of enabled modules, 'auto' is defensible and adapts. For a derived index, a cache, a crawl result or anything with a natural size ceiling that is nevertheless large, 'off' is the correct answer and you should not rely on the heuristic to get there.

-- The query that finds the autoload offenders, in order of cost.
-- LENGTH() is the same measure core uses for its own heuristic, so
-- this ranking is the ranking that determines front-end latency.
SELECT option_id,
       option_name,
       autoload,
       LENGTH(option_value)                     AS bytes,
       ROUND(LENGTH(option_value) / 1024, 1)    AS kib,
       LEFT(option_value, 120)                  AS preview
  FROM wp_options
 WHERE autoload IN ('yes', 'on', 'auto', 'off')
   AND option_name NOT LIKE '_transient_%'
   AND option_name NOT LIKE '_site_transient_%'
   AND option_name NOT LIKE '_transient_timeout_%'
   AND option_name NOT LIKE '_site_transient_timeout_%'
   AND option_name NOT LIKE '_transient_group_%'
 ORDER BY (autoload IN ('on', 'yes', 'auto')) DESC, bytes DESC
 LIMIT 30;

The sum of the byte column for rows that are actually autoloaded is the number that matters. On a healthy site it is comfortably under 100 KB. Between 200 KB and 1 MB it is worth investigating; above 1 MB it is a measurable front-end regression on every request, and the fix is almost always to move the largest handful of rows to 'off' rather than to install a cache plugin. Caching makes the symptom go away by serving the same bloated payload from somewhere faster; it does not remove the transfer, the serialisation or the memory.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

// Normalise legacy values and demote the largest rows. Run once,
// deliberately, from WP-CLI — never on every admin page load.
add_action('acme_cli_migrate_autoload', function (): void {
    global $wpdb;

    // Legacy 'yes' and 'no' become 'on' and 'off' respectively.
    $wpdb->query("UPDATE {$wpdb->options} SET autoload = 'on' WHERE autoload = 'yes'");
    $wpdb->query("UPDATE {$wpdb->options} SET autoload = 'off' WHERE autoload = 'no'");

    // Demote anything over 150 KB that is not already off.
    $threshold = 150 * 1024;
    $affected  = $wpdb->query(
        $wpdb->prepare(
            "UPDATE {$wpdb->options}
                SET autoload = 'off'
              WHERE autoload IN ('on', 'auto')
                AND LENGTH(option_value) > %d",
            $threshold
        )
    );

    WP_CLI::success(sprintf('Demoted %d oversized autoloaded options.', (int) $affected));
});

Flipping the autoload column by hand does not update the object cache. A persistent object cache holds the alloptions array in memory. Changing the column behind its back leaves the cache serving the old, bloated set until it expires or the cache is flushed. Whenever you alter autoload values in bulk, follow the UPDATE with wp_cache_delete('alloptions', 'options') and wp_cache_delete('notoptions', 'options'), and run the migration under conditions where the persistent cache is either flushed or bypassed.

9.3 The transient API

This section covers the storage path taken by set_transient() with and without a persistent object cache, and the consequences for atomicity and TTL handling.

A transient is a value with an expiry. The API is three functions — get_transient(), set_transient() and delete_transient() — and their implementation branches on a single condition: whether a persistent object cache drop-in is active. That branch changes everything about the performance characteristics, and it changes the guarantees as well.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

// With NO persistent object cache:
//   set_transient() writes two rows:
//     wp_options: _transient_acme_feed     (the value)
//     wp_options: _transient_timeout_acme_feed (the expiry timestamp)
//   get_transient() SELECTs both, checks the timestamp, and returns
//   false if expired. The rows are NOT deleted on read.
//
// With a persistent object cache:
//   set_transient() writes ONE row:
//     wp_options: _transient_acme_feed     (the value plus the
//                                          4-byte expiry prefix)
//   The timeout lives inside the cached value, so the expiry is
//   enforced by the cache backend's own TTL, not by WordPress.

function acme_fetch_feed(string $url): array|false
{
    $key = 'acme_feed_' . md5($url);
    $hit = get_transient($key);

    if (is_array($hit)) {
        return $hit;
    }

    $response = wp_remote_get($url, ['timeout' => 15]);

    if (is_wp_error($response) || ($response['response']['code'] ?? 0) >= 400) {
        // Store the failure briefly to avoid hammering a dead upstream.
        set_transient($key . '_err', $response instanceof WP_Error ? $response->get_error_code() : 'http', '5 MINUTES');
        return false;
    }

    $items = json_decode((string) wp_remote_retrieve_body($response), true);
    if (!is_array($items)) {
        return false;
    }

    set_transient($key, $items, '1 HOUR');
    delete_transient($key . '_err');

    return $items;
}

Four practical consequences follow. First, without a persistent cache, expired transients accumulate as orphaned rows because nothing deletes them on read; the standard maintenance sweep in delete_expired_transients() exists for exactly this and is run daily by core when the site is not using an external cache. Second, the timeout row is a separate row, so an interrupted write can leave a value row with no timeout row, which the read path treats as a permanent value. Third, transients are not a distributed lock: with an external cache, two requests can both miss, both fetch, and both write, because there is no atomic “set if absent”. Fourth, the key must be 45 characters or fewer after the _transient_ prefix, because the option_name column is indexed at that width — longer keys are silently truncated, and two long keys that share a prefix collide.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

// A working lock built on the transient API, with the race stated
// honestly in the comment rather than papered over.
function acme_acquire_lock(string $name, int $ttl = 300): bool
{
    $key    = 'acme_lock_' . substr(md5($name), 0, 24);
    $holder = get_transient($key);

    if ($holder !== false) {
        return false;
    }

    // Not atomic: two workers can both reach this line. For strict
    // mutual exclusion use INSERT ... ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE
    // against a dedicated table, or Redis SET NX.
    set_transient($key, ['pid' => getmypid(), 'at' => time()], $ttl);

    return true;
}

function acme_release_lock(string $name): void
{
    delete_transient('acme_lock_' . substr(md5($name), 0, 24));
}

// Verify a lock is not stale before acting on its presence.
function acme_lock_age(string $name): ?int
{
    $holder = get_transient('acme_lock_' . substr(md5($name), 0, 24));
    return is_array($holder) ? (int) ($holder['at'] ?? 0) : null;
}

-- Expired transients with no timeout row are the leak that matters:
-- nothing will ever expire them and nothing will ever delete them.
SELECT LEFT(option_name, 60)  AS option_name,
       COUNT(*)               AS orphaned_values,
       ROUND(SUM(LENGTH(option_value)) / 1024, 1) AS total_kib
  FROM wp_options AS o
 WHERE o.option_name LIKE '\_transient\_%'
   AND o.option_name NOT LIKE '\_transient\_timeout\_%'
   AND NOT EXISTS (
        SELECT 1
          FROM wp_options AS t
         WHERE t.option_name = CONCAT('_transient_timeout_',
                                      SUBSTRING(o.option_name, 12))
   )
 GROUP BY option_name
 ORDER BY total_kib DESC
 LIMIT 20;

Site transients are a different namespace. get_site_transient() uses the _site_transient_ prefix in the same wp_options table on a single-site install, and moves to wp_sitemeta only on multisite. That means the two APIs share storage but not key space, and a single-site migration to multisite changes where site transients live without any change in calling code — worth knowing before a lock built on a site transient silently loses its state.

9.4 Site options and wp_sitemeta

This section covers the network-level option API, its table, its lack of an autoload strategy, and the network administration flows that write to it.

get_site_option(), add_site_option(), update_site_option() and delete_site_option() operate on the network when is_multisite() is true, and on wp_options with a _site_ key prefix when it is false. The abstraction is complete enough that calling code need not branch, but the storage model is different: on multisite the data lives in wp_sitemeta, a table with no autoload column at all.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

$networkTheme = get_site_option('acme_allowed_themes', []);       // wp_sitemeta
$siteOption   = get_site_option('acme_site_license', '');            // wp_sitemeta
$uploadQuota  = get_option('acme_upload_quota_mb', 512);              // wp_options

// The default value argument is a plain value, never a callable.
// get_site_option( 'k', null ) is required to distinguish a stored
// null from an absent key, exactly as with get_option.
if (get_site_option('acme_maintenance_mode') === null) {
    add_site_option('acme_maintenance_mode', false);
}

// Every write invalidates the 'site-options' cache group, which on a
// 10 000-site network is a much larger blast radius than an option
// write on a single site.
update_site_option('acme_license_tier', 'enterprise');

-- wp_sitemeta has no autoload column: every site option is read
-- individually, on demand. The cost model is per-read, not per-request.
SELECT meta_id,
       meta_key,
       LENGTH(meta_value) AS bytes,
       LEFT(meta_value, 100) AS preview
  FROM wp_sitemeta
 WHERE meta_key NOT LIKE '\_site_transient\_%'
 ORDER BY bytes DESC
 LIMIT 25;

-- Transient cleanup on multisite, which core does not do globally.
SELECT COUNT(*) AS expired_site_transients
  FROM wp_sitemeta AS m
  JOIN wp_sitemeta AS t
    ON t.meta_key = CONCAT('_site_transient_timeout_',
                           SUBSTRING(m.meta_key, 17))
 WHERE m.meta_key LIKE '\_site_transient\_%'
   AND m.meta_key NOT LIKE '\_site_transient\_timeout\_%'
   AND CAST(t.meta_value AS UNSIGNED) < UNIX_TIMESTAMP();
Aspectget_optionget_site_option
Table (single site)wp_optionswp_options, prefixed _site_
Table (multisite)wp_options per blogwp_sitemeta, network-wide
Autoload control'on' / 'auto' / 'off'None; every read is an individual lookup
Cache groupoptions / alloptionssite-options
Writes fromAny plugin, theme, admin screenNetwork Admin, CLI, or switch_to_blog()-free code
Appropriate forPer-site configuration and stateLicensing, global feature flags, network theme policy

Site options are not a per-site cache. A value that differs on each of ten thousand sites cannot live in wp_sitemeta. The Network Admin screen that presents site options for editing is editing a single network-wide row; if you need per-site values, you need per-site options, and on a large network the correct implementation is a lookup table keyed by blog id rather than a naming convention like acme_setting_{$blog_id} that produces ten thousand option rows and ten thousand cache misses.

9.5 Configuration that does not belong in the database

This section covers the classes of configuration that must be sourced from the filesystem, the environment or constants, and the reasons each belongs there.

Several categories of value must never be stored in wp_options, and the reasons are not stylistic. Secrets cannot be there because the database is exposed to every plugin, to every REST client that reaches the database layer, to every backup and to every replica; a credential that an administrator types into a settings screen will eventually be read by something that logs it. Build-specific values — the production hostname, the staging API key, the environment name — cannot be there because the same database is restored into a different environment and the configuration would travel with it. Operational toggles that must take effect before WordPress loads cannot be there because the bootstrap reads them before the options API is available.

<?php
// wp-config.php — the correct home for deployment-specific values.
// The file is not in the web root's served path on a correct setup,
// and it is version-controlled, so every environment has a copy
// appropriate to itself.
define('DB_NAME', getenv('DB_NAME') ?: 'acme_production');
define('DB_USER', getenv('DB_USER') ?: 'acme');
define('DB_PASSWORD', getenv('DB_PASSWORD') ?: '');

define('AUTH_KEY',         '...32+ random bytes...');
define('SECURE_AUTH_KEY',  '...32+ random bytes...');
define('LOGGED_IN_KEY',    '...32+ random bytes...');
define('NONCE_KEY',        '...32+ random bytes...');
define('AUTH_SALT',        '...32+ random bytes...');
define('SECURE_AUTH_SALT', '...32+ random bytes...');
define('LOGGED_IN_SALT',   '...32+ random bytes...');
define('NONCE_SALT',       '...32+ random bytes...');

$table_prefix = 'wp_';

// Environment, not configuration, is resolved from the environment
// and then expressed as constants the codebase can read.
define('WP_ENVIRONMENT_TYPE', getenv('WP_ENVIRONMENT_TYPE') ?: 'production');
define('WP_DEBUG',            (bool) (getenv('WP_DEBUG') === '1'));
define('WP_DEBUG_LOG',        WP_DEBUG ? '/var/log/wp-debug.log' : false);
define('WP_DEBUG_DISPLAY',    false);
define('SCRIPT_DEBUG',        WP_DEBUG);
define('DISALLOW_FILE_EDIT',  true);
define('DISALLOW_FILE_MODS',  true);
define('AUTOMATIC_UPDATER_DISABLED', true);
define('WP_MEMORY_LIMIT', '256M');
define('WP_MAX_MEMORY_LIMIT', '512M');

// A filesystem location, used for a cache directory, is a path on
// this machine, not a value that should be copied between machines.
define('ACME_CACHE_DIR', __DIR__ . '/../storage/acme-cache');

if (!defined('ABSPATH')) {
    define('ABSPATH', __DIR__ . '/');
}

require_once ABSPATH . 'wp-settings.php';

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

// A thin configuration resolver. The database holds the *editorial*
// choices; the environment holds everything else. This function is the
// only place in the codebase that reads either source for these keys.
namespace Acme\Config;

final class Config
{
    public static function apiKey(): string
    {
        $constant = defined('ACME_API_KEY') ? constant('ACME_API_KEY') : null;
        if (is_string($constant) && $constant !== '') {
            return $constant;
        }

        $env = getenv('ACME_API_KEY');
        if (is_string($env) && $env !== '') {
            return $env;
        }

        // The database is the last resort, and only for a value that
        // is genuinely editorial. A secret stored here is logged by
        // the REST field, by debug output and by every SQL trace.
        return (string) get_option('acme_api_key', '');
    }

    public static function isProduction(): bool
    {
        return (defined('WP_ENVIRONMENT_TYPE') && constant('WP_ENVIRONMENT_TYPE') === 'production')
            || getenv('APP_ENV') === 'production';
    }

    public static function timeout(): int
    {
        return self::isProduction() ? 10 : 30;
    }
}

Constants cannot be filtered; options can. A constant defined in wp-config.php is immutable for the whole request and invisible to apply_filters(), which is exactly why it is right for a secret and wrong for anything a plugin needs to influence. If a value must be filterable and must not live in the database, the correct pattern is a constant holding a file path plus a filter on the resolver — the constant is the anchor, the filter is the extension point.

# Secrets never appear in the database, and never in the repository.
$ wp config create --dbname=acme_production --dbuser=acme \
      --dbpass="$(vault read -field=password secret/acme/db)" \
      --skip-check --force

$ wp config set ACME_API_KEY --type=constant
# Prompts for the value; writes it to wp-config.php, never to the DB.

# Confirm the constant is defined and the option is absent.
$ wp eval '
printf("constant: %s\n", defined("ACME_API_KEY") ? "defined" : "missing");
printf("option:   %s\n", get_option("acme_api_key", null) === null ? "absent" : "PRESENT - migrate it");
printf("env:      %s\n", getenv("ACME_API_KEY") === false ? "unset" : "set");'

# wp-config.php must never be served. Verify the web server, not the app.
$ curl -sI https://acme.test/wp-config.php | head -1
HTTP/2 403

9.6 Auditing and repairing option bloat

This section covers the diagnostic queries for a bloated options table, the repair order, and the instrumentation that prevents recurrence.

Option bloat is cumulative and invisible until it is severe, because each individual write is small and fast. Diagnosing it is a matter of measuring the total autoloaded bytes, identifying which rows contribute, and classifying each offender as a cache, a log, a configuration value or a third-party mistake. The classification is the work; the queries only locate the rows.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

// A single diagnostic that reports the number that matters, the
// contribution breakdown, and the rows that most need attention.
function acme_option_health(): array
{
    global $wpdb;

    $totals = $wpdb->get_row(
        "SELECT
            COUNT(*)                                          AS total_rows,
            SUM(autoload IN ('on', 'auto'))                    AS autoloaded_rows,
            ROUND(SUM(CASE WHEN autoload IN ('on', 'auto')
                           THEN LENGTH(option_value) ELSE 0 END) / 1024, 1) AS autoloaded_kib,
            ROUND(SUM(LENGTH(option_value)) / 1024 / 1024, 1)  AS table_mib
         FROM {$wpdb->options}",
        ARRAY_A
    );

    $byGroup = $wpdb->get_results(
        "SELECT
            CASE
                WHEN option_name LIKE '\_transient\_%'        THEN 'transients'
                WHEN option_name LIKE '\_site_transient\_%'   THEN 'site_transients'
                WHEN option_name LIKE '\_transient\_timeout\_%' THEN 'transient_timeouts'
                WHEN option_name = 'cron'                     THEN 'cron_array'
                WHEN option_name LIKE 'rewrite_rules'         THEN 'rewrite_rules'
                WHEN option_name LIKE '_wp_session_%'         THEN 'sessions'
                WHEN option_name LIKE 'akismet%'              THEN 'akismet'
                WHEN LENGTH(option_value) > 65536            THEN 'large_rows'
                ELSE 'other'
            END            AS option_group,
            COUNT(*)       AS rows_total,
            ROUND(SUM(LENGTH(option_value)) / 1024, 1) AS total_kib,
            ROUND(AVG(LENGTH(option_value)) / 1024, 1) AS avg_kib
         FROM {$wpdb->options}
        GROUP BY option_group
        ORDER BY total_kib DESC",
        ARRAY_A
    );

    return ['totals' => $totals, 'groups' => $byGroup];
}

-- The rewrite_rules option is the single largest legitimate autoload
-- row on most sites. It is expected to be in alloptions; the check is
-- that its size is proportionate to the number of rewrite rules.
SELECT LENGTH(option_value) AS bytes,
       (LENGTH(option_value) - LENGTH(REPLACE(option_value, 'O:', 1))) / 3 AS approx_serialised_objects
  FROM wp_options WHERE option_name = 'rewrite_rules';

-- The cron array grows by one entry per scheduled event. A cron array
-- in the tens of thousands of bytes means events are being scheduled
-- on every request without being unscheduled.
SELECT LENGTH(option_value) AS cron_bytes,
       (LENGTH(option_value) - LENGTH(REPLACE(option_value, 'i:', 1))) / 1 AS approx_events
  FROM wp_options WHERE option_name = 'cron';

-- Options whose names suggest a log, an index or a queue.
SELECT option_name,
       LENGTH(option_value) AS bytes,
       LEFT(option_value, 160) AS preview
  FROM wp_options
 WHERE option_name REGEXP '(log|history|queue|index|items|cache|export|backup|tmp|temp|draft)'
   AND LENGTH(option_value) > 4096
 ORDER BY bytes DESC
 LIMIT 25;

# Locate the code that writes a named option. wp-cli cannot do this, but
# the option's own history in a staging database usually points at it.
$ wp option get acme_full_index --format=json --fields=autoload

# Trace every call site in the codebase.
$ grep -rn "acme_full_index" --include="*.php" .
src/Service/Indexer.php:88:        update_option( 'acme_full_index', $index, 'off' );
src/Service/Indexer.php:142:       update_option( 'acme_full_index', $index, 'off' );
# 142 is inside a loop. That is the bug, and the byte count confirms it.

# The measured, repeatable fix: run the migration under a flush, then
# verify the alloptions total has actually dropped.
$ wp db query "SELECT ROUND(SUM(LENGTH(option_value))/1024,1) AS kib
                 FROM wp_options WHERE autoload IN ('on','auto')"
+kib+
| 64.2 |
+-----+
$ wp cache flush
$ wp db query "SELECT ROUND(SUM(LENGTH(option_value))/1024,1) AS kib
                 FROM wp_options WHERE autoload IN ('on','auto')"
+kib+
| 51.7 |
+-----+

Repair order matters, because an intervention in the wrong sequence can lose data. Demote autoload values first — that is metadata, not content, and it is reversible. Delete orphaned transients second — they are expired by definition and carry no value. Only then consider deleting any row that is genuinely a log or a cache, and only after confirming with the owning code that it will be regenerated. Never run DELETE against wp_options with a LIKE pattern derived from a plugin name you have not read; the core stores critical rows — siteurl, active_plugins, theme_switched, rewrite_rules — alongside third-party rows in the same table.

Prevent recurrence with a health check, not a cron job. Registering a Site Health test that reports the autoloaded byte total and flags any option above a threshold turns this from a periodic fire drill into a dashboard entry. The test runs on demand, in a controlled context, where the result is visible to the person who can act on it — which is a very different reliability profile from a scheduled task that emails a log nobody reads.

Exercises 9

  1. Demonstrate the difference between add_option and update_option on a pre-seeded key, and show that update_option() returns false without writing when the value is unchanged. Extend this to demonstrate the lost-update race with two parallel processes incrementing the same counter, then implement the INSERT … ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE version from §9.1 and show the count is correct under the same contention.
  2. Audit a real wp_options table: report the total autoloaded bytes, the top 30 rows by size, and a per-group breakdown. Then normalise all legacy 'yes'/'no' values to the 6.6 three-state model, demote every row over 150 KB to 'off', flush the object cache, and re-measure. Include the before and after byte totals and the exact list of option names whose autoload value changed.
  3. Add instrumentation that records which code path wrote each option — a filtered add_option / update_option wrapper capturing wp_debug_backtrace() into a table — and use it to identify the largest single contributor to autoloaded bytes in a plugin you have installed. Present the backtrace, the option name, its size and the fix you implemented.
  4. Build a lock on set_transient() and show the non-atomic failure by running twenty concurrent processes that each try to acquire it; count how many succeed. Then implement the same lock against a dedicated table with INSERT … ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE and re-run, showing that the count is exactly one. Document the key-length constraint and demonstrate a collision caused by exceeding it.
  5. Write a Site Health test that reports autoloaded bytes, the count of transients, and the count of orphaned transient values with no timeout row. Have it pass on a healthy database, and have it fail with a specific, actionable message when you insert a 400 KB 'on' option by hand. Include the test file, the passing and failing output, and the threshold values chosen with justification.

10 · Background Work: WP-Cron, Queues and Long Operations

10.1 How WP-Cron actually fires10.2 Scheduling API10.3 Long operations and chunking10.4 Choosing a transport10.5 Action Scheduler

WordPress ships a scheduler that does not run on a timer. wp-cron.php is invoked as a side effect of a page load, spawned by a loopback HTTP request, and only for the specific event whose due time has passed. On a low-traffic site the consequence is that scheduled work does not run at all: no email, no import, no cleanup, no webhook. This chapter covers the mechanics of that mechanism, the scheduling API and its duplicate-detection semantics, the design of a chunked long-running operation with progress persisted outside the request, and the four transports available for background work — HTTP, WP-CLI, a real queue and Action Scheduler — with the criteria that should decide between them. It closes on the command layer, because the most common failure in a plugin’s background architecture is that its only worker is the web request it is trying to avoid.

10.1 How WP-Cron actually fires

This section traces the request-time trigger for wp-cron.php and identifies the traffic threshold below which scheduled work stops running.

There is no daemon. On every front-end request, wp_cron() in wp-includes/cron.php checks the cron option for events whose timestamp is in the past. If one exists and no cron lock is held, it spawns wp-cron.php in a non-blocking loopback request — a second HTTP request to the same site, made with a short timeout so the page is not delayed — and that second request is what actually executes the callbacks. The doing_cron transient is the lock that prevents a thundering herd, and ALLOW_CRON set to false disables the mechanism entirely.

The arithmetic that follows is unforgiving. A site receiving fewer than roughly one request per minute will, on average, not check cron often enough to keep events within their intended window. Below that, hourly events fire whenever traffic happens to occur after the due time — which may be never, on a site whose only traffic is a nightly backup. The core documentation is explicit about this and has been since 4.8, and the fix is a real system cron.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

// wp-cron.php, reduced to the decision path. The loopback request
// is a separate HTTP request against the same site.
function wp_cron() {
    if (defined('DOING_CRON') || wp_installing()) {
        return;
    }

    $crons = wp_get_ready_cron_jobs();
    if (empty($crons)) {
        return;
    }

    // The lock is a transient, so its lifetime is bounded by the
    // object cache or by the transient timeout row.
    if (get_transient('doing_cron') < microtime(true)) {
        // A single reschedule protects against rapid repeat requests.
        wp_schedule_single_event(time(), 'wp_cron');

        $gmt_time = microtime(true);
        $keys     = array_keys($crons);
        $schedules = wp_get_schedules();
        $results  = [];

        foreach ($keys as $key) {
            $schedule = $crons[$key]['schedule'];
            if ($schedule) {
                $result = wp_reschedule_event($key, $schedule, $crons[$key]['args']);
            } else {
                $result = wp_unschedule_event($key, $crons[$key]['args']);
            }
            $results[$key] = $result;
        }

        $pre = apply_filters('pre_cron', null);
        if (null !== $pre) {
            return $pre;
        }

        // The spawn: a non-blocking request to wp-cron.php.
        $cron_request = sprintf(
            '%.22F', microtime(true)
        );
        spawn_cron($gmt_time);
    }
}

The fix is to disable the trigger and let the operating system schedule it. On a single site, a crontab entry running wp-cron.php every minute is the standard answer. On a network install, one entry per site is required, or a single entry that iterates with --url, and the latter is preferable because ten thousand loopback spawns is not a scheduling strategy.

# /etc/cron.d/wp-cron — every minute, single site.
# The flock guards against a slow run overlapping the next tick, which
# happens whenever one batch exceeds 60 seconds.
* * * * * acme-web wp cron --quiet > /dev/null 2>&1
* * * * * root    flock -n /var/lock/wp-cron.lock -c 'wp cron --quiet' >/dev/null 2>&1

# Disable the request-time trigger in wp-config.php.
define('DISABLE_WP_CRON', true);

# Multisite: one entry, iterating the site list. Batch to keep any
# single invocation bounded; --batch-size bounds memory, not time.
*/2 * * * * root /usr/local/bin/wp cron --quiet --url=acme.test
*/2 * * * * root /usr/local/bin/wp cron --quiet --url=acme.com

<?php
// wp-config.php: replace the request-time trigger with a real one.
// DISABLE_WP_CRON stops the loopback; the constant below tells
// admin-ajax and admin-post to fire due events themselves, so a
// background request arriving through those entry points is not
// silently unprocessed.
define('DISABLE_WP_CRON', true);
define('ALTERNATE_WP_CRON', true);

// The event is registered on init at a late priority, because the
// cron array is rebuilt by every other scheduler during that hook.
add_action('init', function (): void {
    if (!wp_next_scheduled('acme_reconcile_daily')) {
        wp_schedule_event(time() + HOUR_IN_SECONDS, 'daily', 'acme_reconcile_daily');
    }
}, 100);

Two crons, one site, is a bug waiting to happen. With DISABLE_WP_CRON unset, a system cron and the loopback both fire. The doing_cron lock usually prevents concurrent execution, but it is a transient, and on a site without a persistent object cache it is stored in wp_options with a 60-second timeout — long enough to overlap on a busy site. Set the constant or add the crontab entry, never both.

TrafficCron reliability with the loopbackRecommended
Under 1 req/minUnreliable; events can be hours or days lateSystem cron, mandatory
1–10 req/minUsually within a few minutes; occasional longer gapsSystem cron, strongly recommended
10–100 req/minGenerally reliable to within a minute or twoSystem cron if predictability matters
Over 100 req/minReliable, but the loopback doubles the request volumeSystem cron; the spawn is pure overhead
Any, with DISABLE_WP_CRONNever fires from a requestSystem cron, or nothing runs at all

10.2 Scheduling API

This section covers the five scheduling functions, the duplicate-detection rules each uses, and the event identity that determines whether a schedule is a repeat or a new registration.

An event is identified by a hash of the hook name, the arguments array and the interval. Two events with the same hook and arguments but different intervals are two distinct events. Two with the same hook, arguments and interval are the same event, and the second wp_schedule_event() call is a no-op. That is the single most important fact about the scheduling API, because it is why the recommended registration pattern is guarded by wp_next_scheduled(): an unguarded call on every init is not creating duplicates, but it is doing a read and a comparison on every request, and it is confusing to anyone reading the code.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

add_action('init', function (): void {
    // Recurring. Identity = hash( hook, args, interval ).
    if (!wp_next_scheduled('acme_sync_catalog')) {
        wp_schedule_event(time() + 300, 'hourly', 'acme_sync_catalog');
    }

    // Recurring with arguments. Arguments are part of the identity,
    // so these are two events, not one event with two configurations.
    if (!wp_next_scheduled('acme_reconcile_account', [42])) {
        wp_schedule_event(time() + 600, 'daily', 'acme_reconcile_account', [42]);
    }

    // One-shot. The timestamp is the earliest execution time, not a
    // delay: a timestamp in the past runs on the next cron pass.
    if (!wp_next_scheduled('acme_send_digest', ['weekly'])) {
        wp_schedule_single_event(time() + WEEK_IN_SECONDS, 'acme_send_digest', ['weekly']);
    }
}, 10);

// wp_next_scheduled accepts the same identity components and returns
// the timestamp of the first matching occurrence, or false.
$next = wp_next_scheduled('acme_reconcile_account', [42]);
if ($next === false) {
    error_log('Reconciliation is not scheduled. Re-registering.');
    wp_schedule_event(time(), 'daily', 'acme_reconcile_account', [42]);
}

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

// Unscheduling. All four are needed to remove a recurring event
// completely, because wp_clear_scheduled_hook removes only the next
// occurrence when the interval is unknown.
function acme_unschedule_all(string $hook, array $args = []): int
{
    $cleared = (int) wp_clear_scheduled_hook($hook, $args);

    // Belt and braces: walk the cron array and remove every remaining
    // occurrence of this identity, whatever its timestamp.
    while (($timestamp = wp_next_scheduled($hook, $args)) !== false) {
        if (!wp_unschedule_event($timestamp, $hook, $args)) {
            break;
        }
        ++$cleared;
    }

    // Individual events, each with its own timestamp, are addressed
    // by wp_unschedule_event directly.
    wp_unschedule_event(time() + 3600, 'acme_legacy_digest', ['daily']);

    return $cleared;
}

// Deactivation is the only reliable moment to remove events, because
// the plugin is still loaded and the cron array is writable.
register_deactivation_hook(__FILE__, function (): void {
    foreach ([10, 20, 30] as $accountId) {
        acme_unschedule_all('acme_reconcile_account', [$accountId]);
    }
    acme_unschedule_all('acme_sync_catalog');
    wp_clear_scheduled_hook('acme_send_digest', ['weekly']);
});

Two subtleties bite regularly. First, a scheduled event does not survive the site being restored to a state in which the cron array predates the schedule; the timestamps in the cron option are absolute, so a database restored from a week-old backup will run every event whose timestamp has passed, all at once, in a single cron pass. Second, the cron option is a single serialised array on every site, so a network with ten thousand sites and a plugin that schedules per site has a scheduling problem that is a database problem.

-- What the cron array actually contains. Parse the serialised PHP
-- in a SELECT by hand only as a last resort; prefer wp crontab.
SELECT option_id,
       LENGTH(option_value) AS bytes,
       updated_at
  FROM wp_options
 WHERE option_name = 'cron';

-- A real schedule, as reported by WP-CLI. This is the ground truth
-- that "it is scheduled" claims should be checked against.
$ wp cron event list --fields=hook,next_run_gmt,recurrence
+-------------------------+---------------------+------------+
| hook                    | next_run_gmt        | recurrence |
+-------------------------+---------------------+------------+
| acme_sync_catalog       | 2026-09-29T14:05:00 | hourly      |
| acme_reconcile_account  | 2026-09-29T09:00:00 | daily       |
| acme_send_digest        | 2026-09-26T09:00:00 |             |
| acme_cleanup_sessions   | 2026-09-30T03:00:00 | daily       |
+-------------------------+---------------------+------------+

$ wp cron event run acme_sync_catalog --due-now
Success: Ran 1 event due now.

$ wp cron test
Success: WP-Cron is working correctly.

Custom intervals need re-registration on every request. A schedule added through the cron_schedules filter is not persisted anywhere. The cron array stores the interval name as a string, and the filter is what turns that name back into a timestamp on every run. A filter registered on admin_init, or only inside an admin context, means that a WP-CLI run or a REST request will see an unknown interval and treat the event as unschedulable — the event is dropped from the array and silently disappears. Register interval filters on init at priority ten or earlier, in the same place the event is scheduled.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

final class Schedules
{
    public function register(): void
    {
        add_filter('cron_schedules', [$this, 'addIntervals']);
        add_action('init', [$this, 'scheduleEvents'], 10);
    }

    public function addIntervals(array $schedules): array
    {
        $schedules['five_minutes'] = [
            'interval' => 5 * MINUTE_IN_SECONDS,
            'display'  => __('Every Five Minutes', 'acme'),
        ];

        $schedules['quarter_hour'] = [
            'interval' => 15 * MINUTE_IN_SECONDS,
            'display'  => __('Every Quarter Hour', 'acme'),
        ];

        return $schedules;
    }

    public function scheduleEvents(): void
    {
        if (!wp_next_scheduled('acme_heartbeat', ['v2'])) {
            wp_schedule_event(time() + MINUTE_IN_SECONDS, 'five_minutes', 'acme_heartbeat', ['v2']);
        }

        // Never reschedule the same event from inside its own callback:
        // the callback runs after the event has already been popped
        // from the array, so this creates a duplicate every run.
        add_action('acme_heartbeat', function (array $args): void {
            if (($args[0] ?? '') !== 'v2') {
                return;
            }
            $this->processHeartbeat();
        }, 10, 1);
    }
}

Guard every callback with a time budget. A cron callback that is allowed to run for as long as it needs will, on the first occasion when the work grows, exceed the loopback timeout, be killed mid-write, and re-run on the next tick against a partially processed state. The fix is not a faster implementation; it is a bounded implementation that can be interrupted and resumed — which is the subject of §10.3.

10.3 Long operations and chunking

This section covers the design of an interruptible batch operation, with progress persisted in an option and a lock preventing concurrent execution.

A long operation is any unit of work whose runtime is not bounded by the request. The characteristics of a correct one are: it is idempotent, it is resumable, it holds a lock, and it records enough state that a killed run leaves the data consistent and the next run knows where to continue. The progress record is the mechanism that makes resumption possible, and it must be written at the end of each chunk, not at the end of the operation, because the end of the operation is precisely the moment that is not guaranteed to arrive.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

namespace Acme\Import;

final class ChunkedImporter
{
    private const STATE  = 'acme_import_state';
    private const LOCK   = 'acme_import_lock';
    private const BUDGET = 8; // seconds; the hard stop for one chunk

    public function register(): void
    {
        add_action('acme_import_chunk', [$this, 'runChunk'], 10, 0);
        add_action('acme_import_start', [$this, 'start'], 10, 1);
    }

    public function start(string $source): void
    {
        $state = [
            'source'     => $source,
            'cursor'     => 0,
            'processed'   => 0,
            'imported'    => 0,
            'skipped'     => 0,
            'failed'      => 0,
            'started_at'  => time(),
            'finished_at' => null,
        ];

        update_option(self::STATE, $state, 'off');
        wp_schedule_single_event(time(), 'acme_import_chunk');
    }

    public function runChunk(): void
    {
        // A lock that is not held by a live process is stale by
        // definition after its TTL; reclaim it rather than deadlocking.
        $lock = get_option(self::LOCK);
        if (is_array($lock) && ($lock['expires'] ?? 0) > time()) {
            return;
        }
        update_option(self::LOCK, ['pid' => getmypid(), 'expires' => time() + 300], 'off');

        try {
            $state   = get_option(self::STATE, []);
            $cursor  = (int) ($state['cursor'] ?? 0);
            $deadline = microtime(true) + self::BUDGET;

            while (microtime(true) < $deadline) {
                $row = $this->fetchRow((int) $state['source'], $cursor);
                if ($row === null) {
                    $state['finished_at'] = time();
                    break;
                }

                $this->processRow($row, $state);
                $cursor++;
                $state['cursor'] = $cursor;

                // Persisted every chunk, never every run: this write is
                // what makes a killed run resumable.
                update_option(self::STATE, $state, 'off');
            }

            if ($state['finished_at'] === null) {
                wp_schedule_single_event(time() + 5, 'acme_import_chunk');
            }
        } finally {
            delete_option(self::LOCK);
        }
    }
}

The properties this design guarantees are worth stating explicitly. Idempotency: processRow() checks for an existing record keyed on the source’s own identifier before writing, so re-running a chunk is safe. Resumability: the cursor is the source’s natural ordering key, not an offset, so a source that changes between runs cannot cause a record to be silently skipped — a row inserted above the cursor is picked up on the next full pass, and a pass at the end with the cursor reset catches everything. Locking: the lock has a TTL and is reclaimed when expired, so a crashed worker does not permanently block the queue. Bounded time: the deadline is checked before each row, not after, so a single pathological row cannot blow the budget.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

private function processRow(array $row, array &$state): void
{
    ++$state['processed'];

    // The natural key makes the whole operation idempotent. Without
    // it, a re-run after a crash creates duplicates.
    $externalId = (string) ($row['id'] ?? '');
    if ($externalId === '') {
        ++$state['failed'];
        return;
    }

    $existing = get_posts([
        'post_type'      => 'case_study',
        'post_status'    => 'any',
        'posts_per_page' => 1,
        'fields'         => 'ids',
        'no_found_rows'  => true,
        'meta_query'     => [[
            'key'   => '_acme_external_id',
            'value' => $externalId,
        ]],
    ]);

    $postId = $existing[0] ?? 0;

    $result = $postId > 0
        ? wp_update_post([
            'ID'           => $postId,
            'post_title'   => sanitize_text_field((string) $row['title']),
            'post_content' => wp_kses_post((string) $row['body']),
            'post_status'  => 'draft',
        ], true)
        : wp_insert_post([
            'post_type'    => 'case_study',
            'post_title'   => sanitize_text_field((string) $row['title']),
            'post_content' => wp_kses_post((string) $row['body']),
            'post_status'  => 'draft',
        ], true);

    if (is_wp_error($result)) {
        ++$state['failed'];
        return;
    }

    update_post_meta((int) $result, '_acme_external_id', $externalId);

    $state[$postId > 0 ? 'skipped' : 'imported']++;
}

# Run the same chunked operation from the command line, where the time
# budget is the only thing that stops it. --time-budget makes the
# bounded design explicit rather than relying on an HTTP timeout.
$ wp acme import run --source=feed.xml --time-budget=60
Success: Processed 1 842 records in 58.4s (1 790 imported, 41 skipped, 11 failed). Cursor 1842.

$ wp acme import run --source=feed.xml --time-budget=60
Success: Processed 1 803 records in 57.9s (12 imported, 1780 skipped, 11 failed). Cursor 3645.
Success: Processed 0 records. Cursor 3645. Cursor 3645 = complete; finished_at set.

$ wp acme import status
+------------------+---------+
| field            | value   |
+------------------+---------+
| source           | feed.xml|
| processed        | 3645    |
| imported         | 1802    |
| skipped          | 1832    |
| failed           | 11      |
| finished_at      | 1759243...|
+------------------+---------+

# The state option must be autoload-off, or every page load on the
# site pays for it. This is a §9.2 rule enforced by code review.
$ wp option get acme_import_state --field=autoload
off

An option is the wrong store for progress on a busy site. Writing the state every chunk is a UPDATE against a row in the table that is read on every request, and on multisite it is a network-wide cache invalidation per chunk. It is correct for a job that runs a few times an hour. For a job running every five minutes, or a job with per-item progress, the state belongs in a dedicated table with a primary key on the job name, so that the write does not contend with the alloptions read and so that concurrent jobs cannot collide on a shared key.

-- Per-item progress in a dedicated table. No contention with alloptions,
-- no serialisation, and history is retained for diagnosis.
CREATE TABLE wp_acme_job_progress (
    id          BIGINT UNSIGNED NOT NULL AUTO_INCREMENT,
    job_name    VARCHAR(64)     NOT NULL,
    item_key    VARCHAR(191)    NOT NULL,
    status      ENUM('pending','done','failed') NOT NULL DEFAULT 'pending',
    attempts    TINYINT UNSIGNED NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    message     TEXT            NULL,
    updated_at  DATETIME        NOT NULL DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP
                              ON UPDATE CURRENT_TIMESTAMP,
    PRIMARY KEY  (id),
    UNIQUE KEY job_item (job_name, item_key),
    KEY job_status (job_name, status, id)
) ENGINE=InnoDB DEFAULT CHARSET=utf8mb4 COLLATE=utf8mb4_unicode_520_ci;

-- Claim a batch atomically. The UPDATE ... LIMIT inside the claim is
-- what prevents two workers from taking the same rows.
START TRANSACTION;
  UPDATE wp_acme_job_progress
     SET status = 'pending', attempts = attempts + 1
   WHERE job_name = 'catalog_sync'
     AND status = 'pending'
   LIMIT 500;
  SELECT item_key FROM wp_acme_job_progress
   WHERE job_name = 'catalog_sync' AND attempts = 1
   ORDER BY id LIMIT 500;
COMMIT;

10.4 Choosing a transport

This section compares the four available transports for background work against durability, latency, ordering and observability requirements.

There are four ways to run work outside the current request, and the choice is determined by how much the operation must be guaranteed rather than by preference. A background HTTP request is fire-and-forget and is the right answer for work that may be lost. A WP-CLI invocation scheduled by the operating system is durable and observable and is the right answer for anything that must complete. A real queue — Redis, RabbitMQ, SQS — is the right answer when throughput, ordering per key, or per-item retry with backoff matters. Action Scheduler is a specific implementation of the third and fourth hybrid: a database-backed queue that ships as a library and needs no infrastructure.

TransportDurableRetriesObservabilityUse when
Background HTTP (wp_remote_post to admin-ajax)NoNone unless you build themLog file onlyThe work is optional and cheap to re-derive: a cache warm, a best-effort notification
WP-CLI from system cronYes, via the OS schedulerManualExit code, stdout, log rotationRecurring maintenance, imports, exports — anything that must complete
Action SchedulerYes, in wp_actionscheduler tablesYes, with backoffAdmin UI, status API, WC admin reportsPer-item async work inside a plugin, with retries and a queue admin
External queue (Redis, RabbitMQ, SQS)Yes, depending on backendYesBackend toolingHigh throughput, strict per-key ordering, or workloads exceeding the database’s write capacity

The background HTTP pattern is worth showing precisely because it is so widely used and so widely wrong. The request is dispatched and the response is never read; if the target endpoint is slow, the loopback is killed at the timeout and the work is lost mid-way with no record that it started. It is acceptable for work that is idempotent and re-derivable, and for nothing else.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

// Acceptable: fire-and-forget for work that can be re-derived.
wp_remote_post(admin_url('admin-ajax.php'), [
    'timeout'   => 0.01, // do not wait; this is the whole point
    'blocking'  => false,
    'body'      => [
        'action'   => 'acme_warm_cache',
        'nonce'    => wp_create_nonce('acme_warm'),
        'post_id'  => $postId,
    ],
]);

add_action('wp_ajax_acme_warm_cache', function (): void {
    check_ajax_referer('acme_warm', 'nonce');

    $postId = absint($_POST['post_id'] ?? 0);
    if ($postId <= 0 || !current_user_can('edit_post', $postId)) {
        wp_send_json_error(['message' => 'not permitted'], 403);
    }

    // Non-blocking means nothing waits for this, including the
    // function that scheduled it. Bound it anyway.
    wp_schedule_single_event(time(), 'acme_warm_cache_chunk', [$postId]);
    wp_send_json_success(['scheduled' => true]);
}, 10, 0);

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

// The durable pattern for work that must complete: a command, run by
// the operating system, with an exit code that means something.
namespace Acme\CLI;

use WP_CLI;

final class ImportCommand
{
    /**
     * Imports the customer catalog in bounded batches.
     *
     * ## OPTIONS
     *
     * [--source=<path>]
     * : Path to the catalog export. Defaults to the last downloaded file.
     *
     * [--batch-size=<n>]
     * : Rows to process per transaction. Defaults to 500.
     *
     * [--time-budget=<seconds>]
     * : Stop cleanly after this many seconds. Defaults to 50, leaving
     * headroom inside a 60-second cron tick.
     *
     * [--dry-run]
     * : Report what would change without writing.
     */
    #[\\WP_CLI\\CommandMethod('acme import catalog')]
    public function __invoke(array $args, array $assoc): void
    {
        $source     = $assoc['source']     ?? $this->defaultSource();
        $batchSize  = max(1, (int) ($assoc['batch-size'] ?? 500));
        $budget     = max(5, (int) ($assoc['time-budget'] ?? 50));
        $dryRun     = isset($assoc['dry-run']);
        $deadline   = microtime(true) + $budget;
        $processed  = 0;
        $written    = 0;
        $cursor     = 0;

        if (!is_readable($source)) {
            WP_CLI::error(sprintf('Source not readable: %s', $source));
        }

        $handle = fopen($source, 'rb');
        if ($handle === false) {
            WP_CLI::error('Unable to open source.');
        }

        WP_CLI::log(sprintf('Importing %s (batch %d, budget %ds)', $source, $batchSize, $budget));

        while (microtime(true) < $deadline) {
            $batch = $this->readBatch($handle, $cursor, $batchSize);
            if ($batch === []) {
                break;
            }

            $results = $this->upsertBatch($batch, $dryRun);
            $written += count(array_filter($results));
            $processed += count($batch);
            $cursor += count($batch);

            WP_CLI::debug(sprintf('cursor=%d written=%d', $cursor, $written));

            if ($dryRun) {
                WP_CLI::line(sprintf('  would upsert %d rows', count($batch)));
            }
        }

        fclose($handle);

        WP_CLI::success(sprintf(
            'Processed %d rows, wrote %d, stopped at cursor %d%s',
            $processed,
            $written,
            $cursor,
            $cursor > 0 && $this->hasMore($handle) ? ' (incomplete; re-run to continue)' : ''
        ));
    }
}

The --time-budget default of fifty seconds is not arbitrary. A system cron tick is sixty seconds; leaving ten seconds of headroom means the next tick cannot start while the previous run is still going, and the flock guard from §10.1 never has to reject a run. The cursor is reported on exit so that an operator can see whether the run completed, and the command is safe to re-run because every write is an upsert keyed on the source’s identifier.

# Crontab for the durable worker, with jitter so ten thousand sites
# do not all hit the upstream API on the same second.
17 2,8,14,20 * * * root  wp acme import catalog --time-budget=50
37 3,9,15,21 * * * root  sleep $((RANDOM % 300)) && wp acme import catalog --time-budget=50

# Verify the command is registered and its help is complete.
$ wp help acme import catalog
USAGE
  wp acme import catalog [--source=<path>] [--batch-size=<n>] [--time-budget=<seconds>] [--dry-run]

# Run it in the foreground while watching the database.
$ wp acme import catalog --dry-run --batch-size=200
Importing /var/www/catalog.xml (batch 200, budget 50s)
  would upsert 200 rows
  would upsert 200 rows
Success: Processed 400 rows, wrote 0, stopped at cursor 400.

Give the command a --dry-run and a --time-budget from the start. Both are cheap to add before there is data and expensive to retrofit once an operator has run the command destructively. The dry run should report the same numbers the real run would write, so that the two can be compared.

10.5 Action Scheduler

This section covers the Action Scheduler library, why WooCommerce ships it, and the three tables and six states its queue uses.

Action Scheduler is a library, not a service. It stores its queue in three tables in the site’s own database, processes it in batches driven by ordinary page requests, and ships with an admin interface.
