WooCommerce bundles it because scheduled order work — recurring payments, subscription renewals, abandoned cart cleanups, action-scheduler cleanup — is precisely the workload it was written for, and because WooCommerce needs that queue to exist whether or not an external queue is available on the host.

The queue model is: an action is a hook name plus an argument array plus a schedule; an action group is a named batch within a hook, which is how thousands of identical actions are managed together; a schedule is when it should run. Each action moves through a state machine, and the state is what makes the library’s guarantees possible.

StateMeaningWho moves it
pendingDue in the future, or due now and not yet claimedThe queue runner, on claim
in-progressClaimed by a runner; the claim is time-boundedThe runner on completion, or the stale-claim reaper
completeRan to completion; terminalThe runner
failedRan and threw, or exhausted its retriesThe runner; retained for diagnosis
canceledUnscheduled before running; terminalas_unschedule_action() or a group cancellation
trashDeleted but retained for the retention periodThe cleanup routine, after the retention window
 
<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

use function ActionScheduler\as_enqueue_async_action;
use function ActionScheduler\as_enqueue_recurring_action;
use function ActionScheduler\as_next_scheduled_action;
use function ActionScheduler\as_unschedule_all_actions;
use function ActionScheduler\as_has_scheduled_action;

// Single async action. as_has_scheduled_action() prevents the
// duplicate that enqueuing on every save would otherwise create.
function acme_schedule_reindex(int $postId): void
{
    $group = sprintf('acme_reindex_post_%d', $postId);

    if (as_has_scheduled_action('acme_reindex_post', [$postId], $group)) {
        return;
    }

    as_enqueue_async_action(
        'acme_reindex_post',
        [$postId],
        $group
    );
}

// Recurring with an interval in seconds and a unique identifier, so
// re-registering on init is a no-op rather than a second schedule.
as_enqueue_recurring_action(
    time() + HOUR_IN_SECONDS,
    HOUR_IN_SECONDS,
    'acme_prune_search_index',
    ['batch' => 1000],
    'acme_maintenance'
);

add_action('acme_reindex_post', function (int $postId): void {
    if (get_post_status($postId) !== 'publish') {
        return;
    }

    update_post_meta($postId, '_acme_indexed_at', time());
    acme_index_document($postId);
}, 10, 1);

-- The three tables, and the indexes that make claim-batching viable.
-- The claim query is an UPDATE with a LIMIT inside a transaction, so
-- the (status, scheduled_date_gmt) composite index is load-bearing.
SELECT table_name, table_rows, ROUND(data_length / 1024 / 1024, 1) AS data_mib
  FROM information_schema.tables
 WHERE table_schema = DATABASE()
   AND table_name LIKE 'wp_actionscheduler%';
+-----------------------------+------------+-----------+
| table_name                  | table_rows | data_mib  |
+-----------------------------+------------+-----------+
| wp_actionscheduler_actions  |      18422 |       9.4 |
| wp_actionscheduler_groups   |       3180 |       0.2 |
| wp_actionscheduler_logs     |      44190 |      14.1 |
+-----------------------------+------------+-----------+

-- Queue depth by state. A growing 'pending' count means the runner
-- is not keeping up; a growing 'in-progress' count means runners are
-- dying mid-action.
SELECT status, COUNT(*) AS actions,
       MIN(scheduled_date_gmt) AS oldest_scheduled
  FROM wp_actionscheduler_actions
 GROUP BY status
 ORDER BY actions DESC;
+-----------+---------+--------------------+
| status    | actions | oldest_scheduled   |
+-----------+---------+--------------------+
| complete  | 402211  | 2026-03-02 04:00:00|
| pending   |    1843 | 2026-09-29 14:00:00|
| in-progress|      4 | 2026-09-29 14:05:00|
| failed    |     217 | 2026-09-28 22:14:00|
| canceled  |    1090 | 2026-06-11 09:00:00|
| trash     |   18422 | 2026-04-01 00:00:00|
+-----------+---------+--------------------+

WooCommerce ships Action Scheduler rather than using WP-Cron because of one specific requirement: the number of scheduled actions. A store with two million orders has two million order records and a correspondingly large volume of asynchronous work. WP-Cron stores its entire schedule in a single serialised option, so a queue that size is not a scheduling problem, it is a row that cannot be loaded. Action Scheduler uses a table per action, which means an index, which means the claim query is a range scan over a few hundred rows rather than a full deserialisation of millions.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

use ActionSchedulerWP_CLI\Command as AS_CLI_Command;

final class AcmeSchedulerCommands
{
    /**
     * Reports Action Scheduler queue depth by status.
     *
     * ## OPTIONS
     *
     * [--group=<group>]
     * : Limit the report to one action group.
     */
    #[\\WP_CLI\\CommandMethod('acme queue report')]
    public function report(array $args, array $assoc): void
    {
        global $wpdb;

        $table = $wpdb->prefix . 'actionscheduler_actions';
        $group = $assoc['group'] ?? null;

        $rows = $group
            ? $wpdb->get_results($wpdb->prepare(
                "SELECT status, COUNT(*) AS n FROM {$table}
                  WHERE group_id = %s GROUP BY status", $group), ARRAY_A)
            : $wpdb->get_results(
                "SELECT status, COUNT(*) AS n FROM {$table} GROUP BY status", ARRAY_A);

        $rows = wp_list_pluck($rows, 'n', 'status');
        ksort($rows);

        foreach ($rows as $status => $count) {
            WP_CLI::log(sprintf('%-12s %8d', $status, $count));
        }

        $pending = (int) ($rows['pending'] ?? 0);
        if ($pending > 5000) {
            WP_CLI::warning(sprintf('%d pending actions: the runner is behind.', $pending));
        }
    }
}

# Action Scheduler ships a CLI. These are its own commands, not ours.
$ wp action-scheduler run --batch-size=500
[2026-09-29T14:05:00+00:00] Running Action Scheduler
[2026-09-29T14:05:11+00:00] Processed 500 of 1843 pending actions.
Success: Batch complete.

$ wp action-scheduler status
+----------------+-----------+--------------------------------+
| status         | actions   | oldest_scheduled                |
+----------------+-----------+--------------------------------+
| pending        | 1343      | 2026-09-29T14:00:00+00:00       |
| in-progress    | 4         | 2026-09-29T14:05:00+00:00       |
| complete       | 402211    | 2026-03-02T04:00:00+00:00       |
| failed         | 217       | 2026-09-28T22:14:00+00:00       |
+----------------+-----------+--------------------------------+

# Stale claims are reclaimed automatically after the claim TTL. Force
# it when a process was killed and the queue must move now.
$ wp action-scheduler migrate --status=in-progress --force
Success: 4 actions migrated back to pending.

Do not run the Action Scheduler queue from system cron on a busy site without a lock. The runner is safe to run concurrently — claims are transactional — but two runners on one site still double the write load against wp_actionscheduler_actions for no throughput gain. Use flock, or run the runner on one host and disable the async request-time trigger on the others.

Exercises 10

  1. Prove the WP-Cron traffic threshold empirically. On a site with DISABLE_WP_CRON unset, register an hourly event, then simulate zero traffic for an hour and confirm it has not run; then generate one request and confirm it fires. Repeat with DISABLE_WP_CRON set and a system cron entry, and submit both timelines with timestamps.
  2. Implement acme_import_chunk as in §10.3 and demonstrate resumability by killing the process mid-run with SIGKILL from a separate shell, then re-running and showing that no record is duplicated and no record is skipped. Include the acme_import_state option contents at the moment of the kill and after resumption.
  3. Replace the option-based progress record from exercise 2 with the wp_acme_job_progress table, including the transactional claim query. Run four concurrent workers against the same job and prove that the union of the claimed batches is exactly the pending set with no overlap, using SELECT COUNT(*), COUNT(DISTINCT item_key).
  4. Ship a WP-CLI command with --batch-size, --time-budget and --dry-run, wire it into a system crontab with an flock guard, and schedule a monitor that alerts when the reported cursor has not advanced between two consecutive runs. Show the command’s --help output and the monitor’s alert output on a deliberately stalled run.
  5. Install Action Scheduler as a library, enqueue a recurring action with a unique identifier and a per-post group, then demonstrate: (a) that repeated enqueue calls on save do not create duplicates, (b) that a thrown exception moves the action to failed with the exception message in the log, and (c) that as_unschedule_all_actions() with a group removes exactly that group. Include the state-by-state table from wp action-scheduler status after each step.

11 · Internationalisation, Translation and RTL

11.1 Text domains and loading11.2 The string functions11.3 Languages, locales and script translations11.4 RTL and logical CSS11.5 Dates, times, numbers and currency11.6 Extracting and maintaining the POT

Internationalisation in WordPress is two separable concerns that are routinely conflated. The first is internationalisation — the practice of writing code that can be translated: marking every user-facing string, loading the right domain, and never concatenating a translated fragment with a variable. The second is localisation — the practice of presenting data in the conventions of a locale: dates, times, numbers, currency, plurals, and reading direction. Translating a string and escaping it are also two different operations performed at two different moments, and collapsing them produces output that is either vulnerable or visibly broken. This chapter covers the five string functions and when each applies, the text domain conventions for themes and plugins and the loading order that changed in 6.7, script translations for the block editor, the locale and RTL APIs, and the tooling that keeps a .pot file honest as the codebase changes.

11.1 Text domains and loading

This section covers text domain naming, the domain that a theme and a plugin are each responsible for, and the loading order that determines whether a string is translatable at all.

The text domain is the key under which translations are stored. It must be unique across everything that can be loaded, it must be stable for the lifetime of the product, and it must match the directory the translation files are found in. For a theme, the convention is the stylesheet directory name, and the translations live in get_template_directory() . '/languages' — note the template directory, not the stylesheet directory, because a child theme inherits the parent’s translations and must not define its own domain.

<?php
/**
 * Theme functions.php — the text domain is the theme's
 * directory slug, and the domain is loaded by WordPress
 * automatically for a theme present in wp-content/themes.
 *
 * No load_theme_textdomain() call is needed. Core has loaded
 * wp-content/themes/acme/languages/acme-{locale}.mo by the time
 * after_setup_theme fires.
 */

declare(strict_types=1);

add_action('after_setup_theme', function (): void {
    add_theme_support('title-tag');
    add_theme_support('post-thumbnails');
    add_theme_support('html5', ['search-form', 'gallery', 'caption', 'style', 'script']);
    add_theme_support('responsive-embeds');
    add_theme_support('align-wide');
    add_theme_support('custom-logo', [
        'height'      => 120,
        'width'       => 320,
        'flex-width' => true,
        'flex-height'=> true,
    ]);

    // A child theme uses the parent's domain, and only extends the
    // parent's strings rather than introducing its own.
    add_theme_support('translate');
});

// Strings in templates and functions use the parent's slug.
printf(
    <<<'HTML'
    <section class="related">
      <h2>%s</h2>
      <a href="%s">%s</a>
    </section>
    HTML,
    esc_html__( 'Related case studies', 'acme' ),
    esc_url( get_term_link( $term ) ),
    esc_html( $term->name )
);

A plugin owns a different domain — its slug or vendor prefix — and is responsible for loading its own translations. Until 6.7 this was done with load_plugin_textdomain() called from init, and the ordering problem was well known: if another plugin called load_textdomain() or load_plugin_textdomain() before yours, the just-in-time loading path for translations was disabled and only explicitly loaded files were consulted. From 6.7 the recommended pattern is to declare the translation path as soon as the plugin file is included, before any hook runs.

<?php
/**
 * Plugin Name:       Acme Content Model
 * Text Domain:       acme-content
 * Domain Path:       /languages
 */

declare(strict_types=1);

defined('ABSPATH') || exit;

// 6.7+ : register the translation path immediately, at file scope,
// before init and before any other plugin has had a chance to
// change the loading behaviour. This replaces the
// load_plugin_textdomain() call on init.
add_action('init', function (): void {
    // Only required for languages that are NOT installed for the
    // site as a whole. A site running a translated WordPress core
    // gets core's languages directory automatically.
    if (function_exists('determine_locale')) {
        $locale = determine_locale();
    } else {
        $locale = is_admin() ? get_user_locale() : get_locale();
    }

    load_plugin_textdomain(
        'acme-content',
        false,
        dirname(plugin_basename(__FILE__)) . '/languages'
    );
}, 1);

// Strings are marked with this domain everywhere in the plugin.
$label = __( 'Reconcile account', 'acme-content' );
$url   = add_query_arg( 'action', 'reconcile', admin_url( 'admin-post.php' ) );

determine_locale() is the right locale source on 6.7+. It returns the user’s locale when one is set on a per-user basis, the site’s locale when it is not, and it respects the WPLANG constant. The older pattern of is_admin() ? get_user_locale() : get_locale() returns the site locale in the admin when a user has chosen a different one, which is exactly the case where a plugin’s own admin strings are being mistranslated.

Three failures account for most “my strings are not translating” reports. The domain in the __() call does not match the domain that was loaded — usually because the plugin was renamed but the strings were not. The .mo file is present but at the wrong path, usually because Domain Path is missing from the plugin header and the default is not where the file was uploaded. And the string is not marked at all, because it was built by concatenation and there is no way for a translator to reorder it.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

// Concatenation destroys the sentence. A translator cannot reorder
// "3" into the middle of "You have %d new messages" in every language,
// because the argument is not part of the translatable string.
$bad  = sprintf( __( 'You have %d new messages', 'acme-content' ), $count );
$bad  = __( 'Hello', 'acme-content' ) . ', ' . $user->first_name . '!';
$bad  = sprintf( __( 'Showing %1$s of %2$s results', 'acme-content' ), $from, $to );

// A placeholder is part of the string; the translator moves it.
$good = sprintf(
    /* translators: %d: number of unread messages. */
    _n( '%d new message', '%d new messages', $count, 'acme-content' ),
    $count
);

// Context disambiguates, and appears in the .pot as a note the
// translator can act on.
$good = sprintf(
    /* translators: %s: taxonomy term name, e.g. "Financial services". */
    __( 'Case studies in %s', 'acme-content' ),
    $term->name
);

// A named placeholder is unambiguous across all languages and does
// not depend on the order arguments happen to be passed in.
$good = sprintf(
    __( 'Published %1$s in %2$s', 'acme-content' ),
    esc_html( get_the_date( 'j F Y' ) ),
    esc_html( $category->name )
);
ProductDomainTranslation directoryLoaded by
ThemeDirectory slug of the parent theme, e.g. acmewp-content/themes/acme/languagesCore, automatically; no call required
Child themeThe parent’s slug, never the child’sInherited from the parentCore; the child adds no domain of its own
PluginPlugin slug or vendor prefix, e.g. acme-contentwp-content/plugins/acme-content/languagesYour code, at file scope or on init at priority 1
Must-use pluginIts own slug, e.g. acme-muwp-content/mu-plugins/acme-mu/languagesYour code; the directory is not scanned automatically
Drop-inIts own slug, e.g. acme-cacheAlongside the drop-in fileYour code; drop-ins load before plugins

Use a translatable text domain that includes the vendor. acme-content is a better domain than content or acme alone, because the unprefixed forms collide with other publishers’ plugins in the same language. The WordPress.org guidelines require the slug to be unique across the directory, and the vendor prefix is the only mechanism that guarantees it.

11.2 The string functions

This section distinguishes translation from escaping, and sets out which of the five string functions to use in which output context.

There are five functions, and the difference between them is not about translation — all five look up the same translation. The difference is about what they do with the result. __() returns it. _e() echoes it. esc_html__() escapes it for HTML text content and returns it. esc_html_e() escapes it for HTML text content and echoes it. esc_attr__() escapes it for an HTML attribute value and returns it. Translation and escaping are separate concerns: escaping depends on the output context, and it must happen at the point of output, where the context is known — not at the point of retrieval, where it is not.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

// The rule: translate first, escape at output, in the right context.

// Plain text content: escape for HTML text.
echo esc_html__( 'Search', 'acme-content' );
printf( '<p>%s</p>', esc_html( __( 'No results found.', 'acme-content' ) ) );

// An attribute value: escape for an attribute, not for text.
printf(
    '<input type="search" name="q" placeholder="%s" />',
    esc_attr__( 'Search case studies', 'acme-content' )
);

// A URL: the translation may contain a slug, the URL does not.
printf(
    '<a class="more" href="%s">%s</a>',
    esc_url( get_term_link( $term ) ),
    esc_html__( 'Read more', 'acme-content' )
);

// JavaScript: escape for a JS string, then for an HTML script block.
wp_add_inline_script(
    'acme-frontend',
    sprintf(
        'window.acmeConfig = %s;',
        wp_json_encode( [
            'endpoint' => rest_url( 'acme/v1/search' ),
            'label'    => __( 'Search', 'acme-content' ),
        ] )
    )
);

// Never echo a translated string raw. A translator is a trusted
// party, but "trusted" is not "safe": a bad translation containing
// <script> would otherwise execute.
$title = __( 'Report', 'acme-content' );  // NOT escaped yet
echo $title;                                // wrong: unescaped output
echo esc_html( $title );                    // right: escaped at output

The subtlety that catches experienced developers is a value which is already escaped. esc_html__() is correct for a string literal. For a value that arrived from the database — a post title, a term name — the correct function is esc_html() on the raw value, because the translator did not produce it and the plugin cannot assume it is already escaped. Escaping twice produces &amp;nbsp; and a visibly mangled string; escaping zero times is an injection. Neither is acceptable, and the rule is that translation functions apply to literals and escaping functions apply to data.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

namespace Acme\Frontend;

final class CardRenderer
{
    public function render( \WP_Post $post ): string
    {
        // From the database: escape, never translate.
        $title = esc_html( get_the_title( $post ) );
        $excerpt = esc_html( wp_trim_words( (string) $post->post_excerpt, 24 ) );

        // A literal in this file: translate, then escape.
        $cta = esc_html__( 'View case study', 'acme-content' );
        $meta = sprintf(
            /* translators: %s: client organisation name. */
            esc_html__( 'Client: %s', 'acme-content' ),
            $title
        );

        $url = esc_url( (string) get_permalink( $post ) );

        return sprintf(
            <<<'HTML'
            <article class="card" data-post="%1$d">
              <h3><a href="%2$s">%3$s</a></h3>
              <p class="card__excerpt">%4$s</p>
              <p class="card__meta">%5$s</p>
              <a class="card__cta" href="%2$s">%6$s</a>
            </article>
            HTML,
            (int) $post->ID,
            $url,
            $title,
            $excerpt,
            $meta,
            $cta
        );
    }
}

_e() and esc_html_e() are the same trap with different outcomes. _e() is never correct in modern WordPress: there is no output context for which raw echoing is safe. The WordPress coding standards mark it as deprecated for new code. If you need to echo, echo the result of esc_html__() or of esc_attr__() or of esc_html_e()’s explicit sibling — but returning a value and letting the caller escape it is the pattern that scales, because it keeps the escaping decision at the point where the context is known.

Two more functions are part of the same family and are needed for any interface with dynamic text. _n() selects between singular and plural based on a count, and _nx() does the same with a context argument for disambiguation. Both accept a $domain as their last argument. Neither escapes; the result is escaped at output like any other translated string.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

$count  = (int) $query->found_posts;
$domain = 'acme-content';

// A plural form needs the count as an int, not a string. Passing
// "1" instead of 1 makes PHP's === comparison against 1 fail and the
// singular form is never selected in some locales.
$label = sprintf(
    /* translators: %s: formatted number of case studies. */
    esc_html__( 'Showing %s case studies', 'acme-content' ),
    esc_html( number_format_i18n( $count ) )
);

// _n with context, for a string that is ambiguous without it.
$rows = sprintf(
    /* translators: %s: number of rows in a data table. */
    _nx( '%s row', '%s rows', $count, 'data table row count', $domain ),
    esc_html( number_format_i18n( $count ) )
);

// Some languages have more than two plural forms. _n() delegates to
// the Plural Forms rules in the .po header, so the singular/plural
// pair above is the correct declaration even where a language has
// six categories: the extra categories fall back to the plural form.
$status = $count === 0
    ? esc_html__( 'No results', 'acme-content' )
    : $rows;

Some languages have no plural. Japanese, Chinese, Korean, Vietnamese and Thai have a single plural form. Other languages — Polish, Russian, Arabic, Czech, Irish — have between three and six. _n() handles all of these through the Plural-Forms header in the .po file, and the only thing the developer must get right is passing the singular and the plural in the order the API expects and using a real integer for the count.

FunctionReturnsEchoesEscapesUse in
__()YesNoNoAny context, followed by an explicit escape at output
_e()NoYesNoNowhere; deprecated
esc_html__()YesNoHTML textText nodes, and the default for literals
esc_html_e()NoYesHTML textText nodes, when a template needs to echo directly
esc_attr__()YesNoAttribute valuetitle, alt, aria-label, placeholder

11.3 Languages, locales and script translations

This section covers wp_set_script_translations, the distinction between a locale and a language, and the way translation files are found on disk.

Strings that originate in JavaScript are a separate population with a separate pipeline. A PHP string is extracted by the .pot generator from PHP source; a JavaScript string is extracted from source files listed in a JSON file, and loaded into the browser by a script that fetches the translation file from the REST API. The PHP side of that is a single call, and the two arguments it takes are the ones that are usually wrong.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

// The handle must match the handle used in wp_register_script().
// The path must be the directory containing the .json files, relative
// to the plugin root, with a trailing slash. Both are common errors
// and both fail silently: the strings simply stay in English.
add_action('init', function (): void {
    wp_register_script(
        'acme-frontend',
        plugins_url( 'build/frontend.js', __DIR__ ),
        ['wp-i18n', 'wp-api-fetch'],
        '3.2.0',
        true
    );

    wp_set_script_translations(
        'acme-frontend',                       // script handle
        'acme-content',                        // text domain
        plugins_url( 'languages', __DIR__ ) . '/' // path, trailing slash
    );
}, 5);

// The JavaScript side, with @wordpress/i18n. The domain must match
// the second argument above, character for character.
add_action( 'init', function (): void {
    wp_add_inline_script(
        'acme-frontend',
        sprintf( 'window.acmeL10n = %s;', wp_json_encode( [
            'placeholder' => __( 'Search case studies…', 'acme-content' ),
            'retry'       => __( 'Try again', 'acme-content' ),
            'noResults'   => __( 'Nothing matched your search.', 'acme-content' ),
        ] ) )
    );
}, 20 );

// frontend.js — built with @wordpress/scripts (Node 24). The domain
// argument to every call must match wp_set_script_translations().
import { __, sprintf, _n, isRTL } from '@wordpress/i18n';
import { sprintf as sprintfRTL } from '@wordpress/i18n';

const domain = 'acme-content';

export function renderSearchBox( container ) {
    const label = __( 'Search case studies…', domain );
    const hint = sprintf(
        /* translators: %d: number of indexed documents. */
        _n(
            'Searching %d document',
            'Searching %d documents',
            window.acmeL10n.documentCount,
            domain
        ),
        window.acmeL10n.documentCount
    );

    container.setAttribute( 'aria-label', label );
    container.setAttribute( 'placeholder', label );
    container.textContent = hint;
}

// isRTL() reflects the site locale, not the document direction of
// the single string being rendered.
document.documentElement.dataset.direction = isRTL() ? 'rtl' : 'ltr';

// The same string in both directions needs no separate variant: the
// browser's bidi algorithm handles it if the container is marked up
// correctly. A translation file may still contain an RTL form for
// languages that need one, and __() will use it when present.

The translation JSON files are named by locale and, optionally, by script and by md5 of the JavaScript file’s contents. WordPress requests /wp-json/wp/v1/translations/<script-handle>?locale=<locale>&load= from the browser; the CRC is of the registered script’s URL path, and it lets a translation file be invalidated when the script changes. The files themselves are generated by wp i18n make-json from the extracted .pot, and they are what a WordPress.org language pack actually ships.

# The file layout a plugin must produce for script translations.
wp-content/plugins/acme-content/
  languages/
    acme-content.pot
    acme-content-fr_FR.po
    acme-content-fr_FR.mo
    acme-content-fr_FR-frontend.js       # generated
    acme-content-de_DE-frontend.js       # generated
    acme-content-ja-frontend-6f3a91c2.js # generated with a crc suffix

# The JSON keys are md5 of the source file path, relative to the
# project root and with a leading slash. This is what makes the
# extractor find the file, so an absolute path here means the
# translation is silently ignored at runtime.
/ {
    "translation-revision-date": "2026-09-20T10:14:00+00:00",
    "generator": "WP-CLI 2.12.0",
    "domain": "messages",
    "locale_data": {
        "messages": {
            "": {
                "domain": "messages",
                "lang": "fr_FR",
                "plural-forms": "nplurals=2; plural=(n > 1);"
            }
        }
    }
}

A locale is not a language. de_DE is German as spoken in Germany; de_CH is the same language with different date formats, a different currency and a different decimal separator. WordPress uses the full locale for date and number formatting and for which translation file is loaded. Plugins that branch on substr( $locale, 0, 2 ) to decide behaviour are discarding everything the locale carries except the language, and the ones that get it wrong in practice are currency, date order and the decimal separator.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

// The locale, resolved the way core resolves it.
$locale = determine_locale();          // per-user, then site, then WPLANG

// Language, if you genuinely need it — rare, and worth a comment.
$language = substr( $locale, 0, 2 );   // 'de' from 'de_CH'

// Never do this: it hard-codes formatting for one country and breaks
// for every other locale the site is translated into.
$price = number_format( $amount, 2, '.', ',' );           // wrong
$date  = date( 'd/m/Y', $timestamp );                     // wrong

// Do this instead: let the locale decide.
$price = wp_format_price( $amount, [ 'currency' => 'EUR' ] );
$date  = wp_date( 'j F Y', $timestamp );

// And for anything the user typed or a language is written in.
$formatted = number_format_i18n( 1234.5, 2 );             // 1,234.50 or 1.234,50

Available formats come from CLDR, not from PHP. IntlDateFormatter and NumberFormatter are used when the intl extension is loaded, and the core polyfills are used when it is not. This means output can differ between two servers running the same code if one has intl and the other does not — a real and frequently reported cause of “the date format is different on staging”. Verify with wp eval 'var_dump( extension_loaded( "intl" ) );' on every environment that must render identically.

11.4 RTL and logical CSS

This section covers is_rtl(), the loading of rtl.css, and the CSS logical properties that eliminate the need for a stylesheet override entirely.

Right-to-left support in WordPress is a combination of three mechanisms. The is_rtl() function reports the direction from the site locale. The rtl.css file, if present in a theme or plugin stylesheet directory, is loaded in addition to the stylesheet when the locale is RTL. And CSS logical properties allow a stylesheet to be direction-agnostic, which removes the override file for any component written that way.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

// In PHP, branch on direction when the layout genuinely differs.
if ( is_rtl() ) {
    $classes[] = 'is-rtl';
} else {
    $classes[] = 'is-ltr';
}

printf(
    '<div class="%s" dir="%s">%s</div>',
    esc_attr( implode( ' ', $classes ) ),
    esc_attr( is_rtl() ? 'rtl' : 'ltr' ),
    esc_html__( 'Filter results', 'acme-content' )
);

// In JavaScript, isRTL() from @wordpress/i18n.
document.documentElement.classList.toggle( 'is-rtl', isRTL() );

// The block editor needs the same direction so that its own UI is
// correct. Core applies this on html[dir], and the theme must not
// hard-code left or right anywhere in the layout.
printf( '<html %s>', get_language_attributes() );
// outputs lang="ar" dir="rtl" when the locale is right-to-left

CSS logical properties are the substantive part. A declaration using margin-inline-start is resolved against the writing direction, so the same rule produces the mirrored result in both directions with no override. This is not a convenience: it removes an entire class of defect in which a stylesheet override is written by hand and later diverges from the property it was overriding.

/* Physical properties: every one of these needs an RTL override. */
.card {
    margin-left: 1.5rem;
    padding-right: 2rem;
    border-left: 3px solid var(--accent);
    text-align: left;
    float: right;
    background-position: right center;
}

/* Logical properties: the same component, direction-agnostic. */
.card {
    margin-inline-start: 1.5rem;
    padding-inline-end: 2rem;
    border-inline-start: 3px solid var(--accent);
    text-align: start;
    /* float has no logical equivalent; use grid or flex instead. */
    background-position: right center;
}

.toolbar {
    display: flex;
    gap: 0.75rem;
    /* No float, so no direction-specific rule at all. */
    justify-content: flex-start;
}

.badge {
    inset-inline-end: 0;
    /* Physical fallback first, then the logical property, for the
       6% of browsers still without logical inset support. */
    right: 0;
    inset-inline-end: 0;
}

/* Grid and flexbox resolve row and column in the writing direction,
   which is what makes a two-column layout mirror for free. */
.layout {
    display: grid;
    grid-template-columns: minmax(0, 2fr) minmax(0, 1fr);
    grid-template-areas:
        "main aside";
}

@media (prefers-reduced-motion: no-preference) {
    .card { transition: transform 200ms cubic-bezier(0.2, 0, 0, 1); }
}

Four CSS properties have no logical equivalent and are the usual source of RTL defects. float, clear, text-align: left|right (use start|end) and directional background-position keywords all need explicit handling. Replace float with flexbox or grid wherever possible, which eliminates the problem structurally rather than by override. Where a float is genuinely required, an rtl.css override is the correct answer.

/* rtl.css — only for what logical properties cannot express.
   Loaded automatically by WordPress when the site locale is RTL,
   so the theme must not enqueue it conditionally by hand. */
.float-right-widget {
    float: right;
}

.float-right-widget {
    float: left;
}

.carousel__caption {
    text-align: right;
    left: 0;
}

.carousel__caption {
    text-align: left;
    right: 0;
    left: auto;
}

/* Gradients and transforms need mirroring explicitly. */
.slide-in {
    transform: translateX(0);
}

.slide-in {
    transform: translateX(0);
}

.slide-in--visible {
    transform: translateX(-24px);
}

.slide-in--visible {
    transform: translateX(24px);
}

/* Caret and pointer affordances. */
.pagination .next::after {
    content: "\2192";        /* rightwards arrow */
}

.pagination .next::after {
    content: "\2190";        /* leftwards arrow */
}

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

// A bidi isolate is required wherever user-generated text is
// embedded in a sentence whose direction is set by the template.
function acme_localised_post_title( \WP_Post $post ): string
{
    return sprintf(
        /* translators: %s: post title, possibly in a different script. */
        esc_html__( 'Read: %s', 'acme-content' ),
        // The U+2068/U+2069 isolates keep the embedded run from
        // reordering the punctuation of the surrounding sentence.
        "\u{2068}" . esc_html( get_the_title( $post ) ) . "\u{2069}"
    );
}

// A user name, an address or any value that may be in a script with a
// different direction must be isolated, or an Arabic display name in
// an English sentence moves the sentence's own punctuation.
$line = sprintf(
    esc_html__( 'Submitted by %s on %s', 'acme-content' ),
    "\u{2068}" . esc_html( $user->display_name ) . "\u{2069}",
    esc_html( wp_date( 'j F Y', $submitted ) )
);
PhysicalLogical equivalentDirection-aware
margin-left / margin-rightmargin-inline-start / margin-inline-endYes
padding-top / padding-bottompadding-block-start / padding-block-endYes, for vertical writing modes too
border-leftborder-inline-startYes
left / right (position offsets)inset-inline-start / inset-inline-endYes, 92% global support
text-align: lefttext-align: startYes
float: rightNoneNo — use flex or grid
direction: ltrNoneNo — set it only for genuinely forced content

11.5 Dates, times, numbers and currency

This section distinguishes date_i18n from wp_date and shows why the former is incorrect for anything a user sees.

Two date functions exist and they do different things. wp_date() formats a Unix timestamp in the site’s configured timezone and locale, and it is correct for user-facing output. date_i18n() predates the timezone API and, by default, treats a bare integer as a local timestamp — meaning the site’s own timezone, not UTC — which produces output that is wrong by the site’s UTC offset whenever the caller has already normalised to UTC. date_i18n() is retained for backward compatibility and must not be used for new code.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

// Wrong: date_i18n() assumes the integer is already in the site
// timezone, so a UTC timestamp renders shifted by the offset.
$wrong = date_i18n( 'j F Y', 1759243200 );   // site TZ = Europe/London

// Right: wp_date() takes a Unix timestamp and the site's timezone.
$right = wp_date( 'j F Y', 1759243200 );

// A timestamp you already hold in UTC — the common case when
// reading from the database — must be declared as such.
$fromDb = (int) get_post_meta( $postId, '_acme_completed_utc', true );
echo esc_html( wp_date( 'j F Y H:i', (int) $fromDb ) );

// For a date the user typed, never reformat a string. Parse it
// first, in the format it was entered in.
$entered = '29/09/2026';
$parsed  = DateTimeImmutable::createFromFormat( '!d/m/Y', $entered, wp_timezone() );
$errors  = DateTimeImmutable::getLastErrors();

if ( $parsed === false || ( is_array( $errors ) && $errors['warning_count'] > 0 ) ) {
    echo esc_html__( 'That date is not valid.', 'acme-content' );
} else {
    echo esc_html( wp_date( 'j F Y', $parsed->getTimestamp() ) );
}

wp_date() is preferred over both date_i18n() and PHP’s date() because it resolves the timezone from WordPress’s own configuration rather than from the server, and because it applies the locale to month and weekday names. It is also the function that is correct for recurrence: a recurring event that fires at 02:30 local time on the day the clocks change must be recomputed from the local time each time, not by adding 86 400 seconds to the previous timestamp.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

$tz = wp_timezone();

// An instant: unambiguous, stored and compared correctly.
$issuedAt = wp_date( 'c', time() );                 // ISO 8601 with offset

// A date and time as the user sees it, in the site timezone.
$display = wp_date( 'j F Y \a\t H:i', $issuedAt );

// A duration, phrased the way a person would phrase it.
$elapsed = human_time_diff( $issuedAt, time() );
$phrase  = sprintf(
    /* translators: %s: human-readable time difference, e.g. "3 days". */
    esc_html__( 'Updated %s ago', 'acme-content' ),
    esc_html( $elapsed )
);

// Recurrence across a DST boundary: recompute the local time, do not
// add seconds. Europe/London moves on the last Sunday in March.
$next = new DateTimeImmutable( 'next 02:30', $tz );
echo esc_html( $next->format( 'c' ) );

// Compare in the same zone. A timestamp from get_gmt_from_date()
// and one from time() are in different zones and subtract wrongly.
$a = (int) get_gmt_from_date( get_post_field( 'post_date', 42 ) );
$b = (int) get_gmt_from_date( get_post_field( 'post_modified', 42 ) );
$lag = human_time_diff( $b, $a );

// Numbers and currency.
$total  = number_format_i18n( $amount, 2 );                    // locale separators
$price  = wp_format_price( $amount, [ 'currency' => 'EUR' ] );
$bytes  = size_format( $bytes, $decimals = 0 );                 // translated units
$pct    = sprintf( '%s%%', number_format_i18n( $ratio * 100, 1 ) );

-- Localization in SQL: MySQL has no locale awareness, so sorting
-- and number parsing done in the database will not match what the
-- user sees. MySQL 8.0 collations are locale-independent (utf8mb4
-- _0900_ai_ci) and wp_posts.post_title is indexed with one of them.
--
-- The consequence is that a title search is case- and
-- accent-insensitive, which is usually what is wanted, and
-- collation-insensitive to a locale the user does not read, which
-- sometimes is not.
SELECT ID, post_title
  FROM wp_posts
 WHERE post_type = 'case_study'
   AND post_status = 'publish'
   AND post_title LIKE '%' & '%nordwind%'
   AND post_date >= DATE_SUB( NOW(), INTERVAL 1 YEAR )
 ORDER BY post_date DESC
 LIMIT 10;

-- Do not do date arithmetic on localised strings in SQL. Store
-- timestamps or DATETIME in UTC and format in PHP, where the
-- locale is available.
SELECT
    SUM(CASE WHEN post_date >= UTC_TIMESTAMP() THEN 1 ELSE 0 END) AS today,
    COUNT(*)                                                        AS total
  FROM wp_posts
 WHERE post_type = 'case_study'
   AND post_status = 'publish';

Number formatting and arithmetic are separate. number_format_i18n() produces a string for display and must never be parsed back into a number — (float) '1.234,56' is 1.234 in any locale. Keep numbers as integers or floats until the moment of output, use wc_format_decimal() for form round-tripping, and never store a formatted string in a numeric column or a numeric string in a text column that is later parsed for display.

11.6 Extracting and maintaining the POT

This section covers wp i18n make-pot, the conventions the extractor depends on, and the CI check that keeps the .pot current.

The .pot file is the template from which translators work. It is generated, never hand-edited, and the only correct way to produce it is wp i18n make-pot with the right arguments. The three that matter are the source directory, the destination, and the domain — because the domain in the generated file must match the domain in every __() call, or the translations will not be applied at runtime.

# Generate the template. --domain must match every __( 'x', 'domain' ) call.
$ wp i18n make-pot . languages/acme-content.pot \
      --domain=acme-content \
      --package-name="Acme Content Model" \
      --team="Acme Engineering <engineering@acme.test>" \
      --bug-report="https://acme.test/support" \
      --exclude=build,vendor,node_modules,tests/data \
      --max-line-length=120 \
      --headers='{"X-Generator":"WP-CLI 2.12.0"}'

# Count what was found. A drop in this number between releases is
# almost always a refactor that stopped marking a string.
$ wp i18n make-pot . languages/acme-content.pot --domain=acme-content
$ grep -c '^msgid ' languages/acme-content.pot
248

# Compile to .mo for the source locale (English) — a no-op in
# practice, but it validates the file.
$ wp i18n make-mo languages/acme-content.pot languages/acme-content.mo
Success: Created languages/acme-content.mo.

# Extract JavaScript strings for script translations. The JSON file
# lists the files, and the paths inside it must match exactly.
$ wp i18n make-json languages/acme-content.pot languages/ \
      --no-purge \
      --relative-to=build/ \
      --domains=acme-content
Success: Created languages/acme-content-{locale}-frontend.js

The extractor is a static analyser, and its judgements are mechanical. It recognises the string functions by name, including those imported under an alias, and it does not follow a function through a variable. This produces two classes of false negative that account for most missing strings in a .pot: a wrapper function that calls __() internally, and a string stored in a variable before being passed.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);

// Missed by the extractor: the string is passed to __(…) through a
// variable, so the analyser cannot see the literal.
$label = 'Save case study';
$out   = __( $label, 'acme-content' );   // NOT extracted

// Extracted correctly: the literal is a direct argument.
$out = __( 'Save case study', 'acme-content' );

// A wrapper defeats static analysis unless the wrapper itself is
// annotated for the extractor.
function acme_label( string $key ): string
{
    return match ( $key ) {
        'save'    => __( 'Save case study', 'acme-content' ),
        'cancel'  => __( 'Cancel', 'acme-content' ),
        'discard' => __( 'Discard changes', 'acme-content' ),
    };
}

// Dynamic strings — a name that changes at runtime — are not
// translatable. Give the pattern a stable set of formats instead.
foreach ( $results as $result ) {
    $template = $result->failed
        ? __( 'Could not process %1$s: %2$s', 'acme-content' )
        : __( 'Processed %s', 'acme-content' );
    $line = sprintf( $template, esc_html( $result->name ), esc_html( $result->reason ) );
}

# composer.json — the i18n tools are dev dependencies and are not
# shipped in the release artefact. Composer 2.8 with --no-dev.
{
    "name": "acme/acme-content",
    "type": "wordpress-plugin",
    "require": {
        "php": ">=8.3"
    },
    "require-dev": {
        "wp-cli/i18n-command": "^2.12",
        "php-stubs/wordpress-stubs": "^6.9",
        "phpstan/phpstan": "^2.1"
    },
    "config": {
        "platform": { "php": "8.5.0" },
        "sort-packages": true,
        "optimize-autoloader": true
    },
    "scripts": {
        "pot": "wp i18n make-pot . languages/acme-content.pot --domain=acme-content --exclude=vendor,node_modules,tests",
        "mo":  "wp i18n make-mo languages/acme-content.pot languages/acme-content.mo",
        "json":"wp i18n make-json languages/acme-content.pot languages/ --no-purge --relative-to=build/ --domains=acme-content",
        "i18n": ["@pot", "@mo", "@json"],
        "lint": "phpstan analyse --level=8 src/",
        "dist": ["@i18n", "@lint", "composer install --no-dev --optimize-autoloader"]
    }
}

# The CI check. Failing the build on a stale template is cheaper than
# discovering it in a translator's review queue.
name: i18n

on: [push, pull_request]

jobs:
  pot:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4

      - name: Set up PHP 8.5
        uses: shivammathur/setup-php@v2
        with:
          php-version: '8.5'
          extensions: intl, mbstring, mysqli
          tools: wp-cli

      - name: Install WP-CLI i18n command
        run: composer global require wp-cli/i18n-command:^2.12 --no-interaction

      - name: Generate and compare
        run: |
          cp languages/acme-content.pot /tmp/baseline.pot
          wp i18n make-pot . /tmp/regenerated.pot \
             --domain=acme-content \
             --exclude=vendor,node_modules,tests
          if ! diff -q /tmp/baseline.pot /tmp/regenerated.pot; then
            echo "::error::The POT file is out of date. Run 'composer i18n' and commit the result."
            diff -u /tmp/baseline.pot /tmp/regenerated.pot | head -100
            exit 1
          fi

      - name: Verify no unmarked strings in templates
        run: |
          if grep -rnE '>[^<]*[A-Z][a-z]+ [a-z]+[</]' templates/ \
               | grep -vE 'esc_(html|attr|html__|attr__|url)|__\(|_e\(' ; then
            echo "::error::Possible untranslated literal in templates."
            exit 1
          fi

Regenerating the .pot is a release task, not a per-commit task, because the line numbers in the generated file change on every unrelated edit and a translator working from a noisy diff stops trusting it. The CI check above therefore compares the committed file against a fresh generation and fails if they differ, which enforces the discipline without producing churn in review. Adding the translator comment on the line immediately above a string containing a placeholder is not optional: it is the only mechanism by which a translator learns what the %s is supposed to be, and its absence is the single most common cause of a bad translation.

# The three checks worth having, in the order they catch problems.

# 1. Text domain consistency: every domain used must be one the
#    plugin actually loads.
$ wp eval '
$domains = [];
$it = new RecursiveIteratorIterator( new RecursiveDirectoryIterator( __DIR__ . "/src" ) );
foreach ( $it as $file ) {
    if ( $file->getExtension() !== "php" ) { continue; }
    $src = file_get_contents( $file->getPathname() );
    if ( preg_match_all( "/(?:__|_e|_n|_x|esc_html__|esc_attr__|esc_html_e)\\s*\\(\\s*(?:\x27|\")(.+?)(?:\x27|\")\\s*,\\s*(?:\x27|\")([a-z0-9-]+)(?:\x27|\")/", $src, $m, PREG_SET_ORDER ) ) {
        foreach ( $m as $hit ) { $domains[ $hit[2] ][] = $file->getFilename(); }
    }
}
ksort( $domains );
foreach ( $domains as $d => $files ) { printf("%-20s %d call sites\n", $d, count( $files )); }
'
acme-content        184 call sites
acme-admin          22 call sites

# 2. Placeholder parity: the placeholders in a translation must match
#    the placeholders in the source, or sprintf() silently produces
#    a malformed string.
$ wp i18n update-po languages/acme-content.pot languages/fr_FR.po --no-import-date
Success: Updated 2 of 248 strings.

# 3. Translator comments present wherever a placeholder exists.
$ grep -B1 -A1 '%s\|%d\|%1\$s' languages/acme-content.pot | grep -c 'translators:'
241   # 241 of 248 strings with a placeholder have a comment

Ship the .pot, never a .po, in the plugin archive. A .po is a translation in progress and belongs in a translation platform — GlotPress, Weblate, Transifex. Shipping one from your repository invites two translators editing the same file and produces a merge conflict that neither can resolve. The .pot is a template, it has no translations in it, and language packs are built from it by the platform.

Exercises 11

  1. Audit a plugin’s own codebase for string hygiene: run the domain-consistency check from §11.6 and produce a list of every __() call whose domain does not match the loaded domain, plus every call where the string reaches __() through a variable. Fix all of them, regenerate the .pot, and submit the before/after string counts.
  2. Prove the difference between translation and escaping with a rendered example: output a post title containing <script>alert(1)</script> and an accented title through each of the five string functions, and document which produce a visible defect and which produce a vulnerability. Include the HTTP response body for each case and a W3C validator verdict for the two that are well-formed.
  3. Register a text domain that is already in use by a WordPress.org plugin, load a translation for it, and show that the strings resolve to the other plugin’s translation. Then implement the fix — a vendor-prefixed domain — and demonstrate the correct resolution. Include the get_translations_for_domain() output before and after.
  4. Register a script with wp_set_script_translations and demonstrate each of its three failure modes: a wrong handle, a missing trailing slash on the path, and a mismatched domain in the JavaScript __() call. For each, show the network request to /wp-json/wp/v1/translations/ and the resulting 404 or untranslated string, then the corrected request.
  5. Build a bidirectionally complete component: one rtl.css override for the four properties with no logical equivalent, and the same component rewritten with logical properties and grid, requiring no override at all. Render it under ar and en_US with screenshots, and add a bidi-isolation test for an Arabic display name inside an English sentence, showing the punctuation position before and after the U+2068/U+2069 isolates.

12 · Testing, Debugging and the WordPress Debug Toolkit

12.1 WP_DEBUG and the debug configuration12.2 Logging and error reporting12.3 Unit testing with the WordPress test suite12.4 Integration testing against a real database12.5 Fixtures, factories and data isolation12.6 Profiling and diagnostic tools

WordPress is unusually testable and unusually untested. The core test suite is mature, the factory API is comprehensive, and a developer with a PHP background will find the harness familiar. Projects skip it for one reason: the first attempt requires bootstrapping an entire WordPress installation, and the documentation assumes familiarity with a build system the reader does not have.

This chapter covers the debug configuration and what each constant actually changes, structured logging in a platform whose default error handler writes to the browser, the two test layers — a pure unit test with a mock bootstrap and an integration test against a real database — integration tests exercising the database, REST and cron, factory-based fixture generation with cleanup that holds when the execution order changes, and the diagnostic tools that make a slow suite tolerable.

12.1 WP_DEBUG and the debug configuration

Six constants control debugging behaviour. Four of them can take the site down if set on production, and one is the reason a stack trace has ever appeared in a search result.

WP_DEBUG defaults to false and, when true, turns on error_reporting at E_ALL, display_errors, and the constant itself. Core’s wp_debug_mode() reads it, or the WP_DEBUG_MODE constant if you prefer an explicit value of default, log or all: default logs everything and displays only unsuppressed errors, log logs everything and displays nothing, all does both including notices. On production log is the only defensible value, and even that depends on WP_DEBUG_LOG pointing somewhere the web server can write. SCRIPT_DEBUG is a separate concern: it affects what users receive, loading the unminified core JavaScript and every dependency, and leaving it on in production produces a site that works perfectly and feels slow.

The PHP side must agree with the WordPress side, because a WP_DEBUG_DISPLAY of false with a php.ini that has display_errors=On still prints the error. The four settings that must be consistent are error_reporting, display_errors, log_errors and error_log; the production combination is to report everything, display nothing, and log to a file only the PHP user can read. Memory is a debug constant in all but name — WP_MAX_MEMORY_LIMIT raises WordPress’s default of 40 MB when the ini value is lower, and a media-heavy import dying at 40 MB with an unhelpful message is the commonest configuration defect in a development environment.

/* wp-config.php — local development. Never on production. */
define( 'WP_DEBUG', true );
define( 'WP_DEBUG_MODE', 'default' );      // 'default' | 'log' | 'all'
define( 'WP_DEBUG_DISPLAY', true );
define( 'WP_DEBUG_LOG', __DIR__ . '/wp-content/debug.log' );
define( 'SCRIPT_DEBUG', true );
define( 'WP_ENVIRONMENT_TYPE', 'local' );

/* wp-config.php — production. */
define( 'WP_DEBUG', false );
define( 'WP_DEBUG_MODE', 'log' );         // never 'all' in production
define( 'WP_DEBUG_DISPLAY', false );
define( 'WP_DEBUG_LOG', '/var/log/php/wordpress-error.log' );
define( 'SCRIPT_DEBUG', false );
define( 'WP_ENVIRONMENT_TYPE', 'production' );

/* php.ini, production */
error_reporting = E_ALL
display_errors = Off
log_errors = On
error_log = /var/log/php/wordpress-error.log
memory_limit = 256M
max_execution_time = 30

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);
final class Environment
{
    public static function apply(): void
    {
        $map = [
            'local'      => ['WP_DEBUG' => true,  'WP_DEBUG_MODE' => 'default', 'WP_DEBUG_DISPLAY' => true,  'SCRIPT_DEBUG' => true],
            'ci'         => ['WP_DEBUG' => true,  'WP_DEBUG_MODE' => 'all',     'WP_DEBUG_DISPLAY' => false, 'SCRIPT_DEBUG' => false],
            'staging'    => ['WP_DEBUG' => false, 'WP_DEBUG_MODE' => 'log',     'WP_DEBUG_DISPLAY' => false, 'SCRIPT_DEBUG' => false],
            'production' => ['WP_DEBUG' => false, 'WP_DEBUG_MODE' => 'log',     'WP_DEBUG_DISPLAY' => false, 'SCRIPT_DEBUG' => false],
        ];
        $type = getenv('APP_ENV') ?: 'local';
        if (!isset($map[$type])) {
            throw new \RuntimeException("Unknown APP_ENV: {$type}");
        }
        foreach ($map[$type] as $constant => $value) {
            defined($constant) || define($constant, $value);
        }
    }
}
add_action('muplugins_loaded', static function (): void {
    $host  = (string) wp_parse_url(home_url(), PHP_HOST);
    $local = ['localhost', '127.0.0.1', '::1', 'wp.test', 'app.test'];
    if (defined('WP_DEBUG') && WP_DEBUG && !in_array($host, $local, true)) {
        error_log("CRITICAL: WP_DEBUG is on at host {$host}");
        if (defined('WP_DEBUG_DISPLAY') && WP_DEBUG_DISPLAY) {
            add_action('admin_notices', static function (): void {
                printf('<div class="notice notice-error"><p>%s</p></div>',
                    esc_html__('WP_DEBUG_DISPLAY is on on a non-local host. Disable it now.', 'app'));
            });
        }
    }
}, 1);
wp eval 'printf("WP_DEBUG=%s MODE=%s DISPLAY=%s ENV=%s PHP=%s MEM=%s\n",
    var_export(WP_DEBUG, true), WP_DEBUG_MODE, var_export(WP_DEBUG_DISPLAY, true),
    defined("WP_ENVIRONMENT_TYPE") ? WP_ENVIRONMENT_TYPE : "undefined",
    PHP_VERSION, ini_get("memory_limit"));'
wp config list --fields=name,value --format=table
curl -sI https://example.com/wp-content/debug.log | head -1
ConstantDefaultEffect when onSafe in production
WP_DEBUGfalseE_ALL, enables the debug constantWith MODE=log only
WP_DEBUG_MODEFrom WP_DEBUGOverrides the display and log balanceYes, as log
WP_DEBUG_DISPLAYFollows modePrints errors in the response bodyNever
WP_DEBUG_LOGerror_logSets the log destinationYes, outside the web root
SCRIPT_DEBUGfalseUnminified core JavaScriptNever
WP_ENVIRONMENT_TYPENoneEnvironment label, drives core behaviourYes, as production

Never derive production configuration from an untrusted source. A .env file inside the document root, a wp-config.php on a shared host, or a wp-config-local.php readable by the web server all become credential disclosure. Debug information is itself sensitive: Site Health exposes PHP and WordPress versions to anyone who can view it, the log contains absolute paths and sometimes credentials, and a WP_DEBUG_DISPLAY of true on a crawler-reachable page puts your file system layout into a search index. Fail the deploy rather than the site: a startup assertion logging CRITICAL when WP_DEBUG_DISPLAY is on, when DISABLE_WP_CRON is missing, or when no persistent object cache is present converts three classes of outage from "discovered by a user" into "detected in the first five minutes".

12.2 Logging and error reporting

error_log() writes one line to a file or the PHP error log. It is the correct default; structured logging is the upgrade that pays for itself the first time a request spans a queue.

WordPress adds wpdb->print_error() for database failures, which in debug mode also writes to error_log — but as a formatted multi-line block, which is awkward to parse. A logging façade emitting one line of JSON per event, with a request identifier, solves most debugging problems on a WordPress site because it lets you follow one logical operation across a web request, a cron loopback, an async request and a CLI invocation. Install the correlation identifier in a must-use plugin rather than a regular one: it must exist before the earliest hook any component uses, and it must propagate into the loopback, the AJAX request and the CLI invocation through the environment.

Secrets must never reach the log, so redaction is a whitelist rather than a blacklist — a blacklist of key names misses auth, token and api_key and every typo of them. Database errors deserve particular care, because a failed query returns false and execution continues, so a page renders with missing data and no message, which is harder to diagnose than a loud failure. Hook diagnostics are the other half of the problem: did_action() and did_filter() answer whether something ran, has_filter() whether a callback is registered at all, and current_filter() tells you the stack while a callback executes — the only reliable way to distinguish a hook that is registered but not called from one called with different arguments than you expect.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);
namespace App\Debug;
final class Log
{
    public static function boot(): void
    {
        if (!defined('APP_REQUEST_ID')) {
            define('APP_REQUEST_ID', getenv('APP_REQUEST_ID') ?: bin2hex(random_bytes(8)));
        }
        add_action('shutdown', [self::class, 'fatal'], 0);
    }
    public static function write(string $level, string $message, array $context = []): void
    {
        error_log(wp_json_encode([
            'ts'    => gmdate('c'),
            'level' => $level,
            'msg'   => $message,
            'id'    => defined('APP_REQUEST_ID') ? APP_REQUEST_ID : null,
            'uid'   => function_exists('get_current_user_id') ? get_current_user_id() : 0,
            'uri'   => substr((string) ($_SERVER['REQUEST_URI'] ?? ''), 0, 200),
            'ctx'   => self::redact($context),
        ], JSON_UNESCAPED_SLASHES | JSON_PARTIAL_OUTPUT_ON_ERROR));
    }
    public static function info(string $m, array $c = []): void  { self::write('info', $m, $c); }
    public static function warn(string $m, array $c = []): void  { self::write('warn', $m, $c); }
    public static function error(string $m, array $c = []): void { self::write('error', $m, $c); }
    private static function fatal(): void
    {
        $err = error_get_last();
        if ($err === null || !in_array($err['type'], [E_ERROR, E_PARSE, E_CORE_ERROR], true)) {
            return;
        }
        self::write('fatal', 'Uncaught fatal error', [
            'type' => $err['type'], 'message' => $err['message'],
            'file' => $err['file'],    'line'   => $err['line'],
        ]);
    }
    private static function redact(array $context): array
    {
        $safe = [];
        foreach ($context as $key => $value) {
            if (preg_match('/(pass|secret|token|key|auth|cookie|session|nonce)/i', (string) $key)) {
                $safe[$key] = '[redacted]';
                continue;
            }
            $safe[$key] = match (true) {
                is_object($value)  => get_class($value),
                is_array($value)   => '[array:' . count($value) . ']',
                is_string($value) && strlen($value) > 200 => substr($value, 0, 200) . '…',
                default            => $value,
            };
        }
        return $safe;
    }
}
<?php
declare(strict_types=1);
add_action('wp_loaded', static function (): void {
    global $wpdb;
    $previous = $wpdb->suppress_errors(true);
    add_action('shutdown', static function () use ($wpdb, $previous): void {
        $wpdb->suppress_errors($previous);
        if (!empty($wpdb->last_error)) {
            App\Debug\Log::error('database_error', [
                'error'  => $wpdb->last_error,
                'query'  => $wpdb->last_query,
                'called' => $wpdb->func_call,
                'errno'  => $wpdb->last_errnop,
            ]);
        }
        if (defined('SAVEQUERIES') && SAVEQUERIES) {
            foreach (array_slice($wpdb->logs, -20) as $entry) {
                if ((float) ($entry[1] ?? 0) > 0.05) {
                    App\Debug\Log::warn('slow_query', [
                        'ms'    => round((float) $entry[1] * 1000, 1),
                        'query' => $entry[0],
                    ]);
                }
            }
        }
    });
}, 5);
add_action('app_debug_hooks', static function (): void {
    wp_send_json_success([
        'transition_post_status_fired' => did_action('transition_post_status'),
        'app_head_registered'          => has_filter('wp_head', 'app_head'),
        'wp_head_count'                => did_filter('wp_head'),
        'current_action'               => current_action(),
        'current_filter'               => current_filter(),
    ]);
});
final class AdminLog
{
    public const OPTION = 'app_debug_log';
    public static function record(string $level, string $message, array $context = []): void
    {
        if (!get_option('app_capture_log', false)) {
            return;
        }
        $log   = (array) get_option(self::OPTION, []);
        $log[] = ['t' => microtime(true), 'lvl' => $level, 'msg' => $message, 'ctx' => $context];
        update_option(self::OPTION, array_slice($log, -200), false, 'off');
    }
}

Never log the full request, the full $_POST, or a raw database row that may contain personal data. A single error_log( wp_json_encode( $_POST ) ) in a login handler writes passwords to a file typically retained for months and readable by anyone with a shell. That is a compliance incident, not a code-quality complaint, and it is trivially avoidable: log the keys you acted on, never the values you received. Log rotation is equally non-optional — a JSON line per event on a busy site is tens of megabytes a day, and an unrotated log eventually fills the disk, which on a single-partition host takes the database with it. Never write application logs inside wp-content/uploads, where a plugin might expose them or a migration might move them.

12.3 Unit testing with the WordPress test suite

The WordPress test suite runs against a real WordPress installation with a real MySQL database. That is an integration harness rather than a unit test framework, and the distinction determines what you can assert.

Three layers exist. A pure unit test tests a class with WordPress functions stubbed, runs in milliseconds and parallelises. An integration test extends WP_UnitTestCase and runs against a real database with real hooks, options and post types, at roughly forty milliseconds per test. A behavioural test is an integration test exercising a user-visible path — a REST request, an admin flow, a cron run — and is the only layer that proves the pieces are wired together. A pure unit test that calls get_option() cannot run without WordPress, which is why the class under test should take its dependencies as constructor arguments: a Config reading the database inside its constructor cannot be tested, cannot be reused in a CLI migration and cannot be reasoned about.

Mocking WordPress functions is possible and widely done, and it is a trap that arrives slowly — a mock of get_post_meta() returning a fixed array produces a test that passes while the real cache, the real maybe_unserialize() and the real type coercion are never exercised. Mock at the boundary you own and use the real implementation for everything you do not. The WP_UnitTestCase base class wraps each test in a database transaction and rolls it back, and snapshots and restores hooks so a callback registered in one test cannot leak into the next — but it does not restore the cron array, the current user, environment variables, superglobals or the object cache, which is where integration suites become order-dependent.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);
namespace App\Tests\Unit;
use App\Config\Config;
use PHPUnit\Framework\TestCase;
final class ConfigTest extends TestCase
{
    public function testResolvesDefaultsWhenNothingIsStored(): void
    {
        $config = new Config(['sync_interval' => 900], []);
        self::assertSame(900, $config->get('sync_interval'));
        self::assertSame('default', $config->source('sync_interval'));
    }
    public function testStoredValueOverridesDefault(): void
    {
        $config = new Config(['sync_interval' => 900], ['sync_interval' => 300]);
        self::assertSame(300, $config->get('sync_interval'));
        self::assertSame('db', $config->source('sync_interval'));
    }
    public function testSecretIsNeverSerialised(): void
    {
        $config = new Config(
            ['webhook_secret' => 'x'],
            ['webhook_secret' => str_repeat('a', 64)]
        );
        self::assertStringNotContainsString(
            str_repeat('a', 8),
            (string) json_encode($config, JSON_THROW_ON_ERROR)
        );
    }
}
<?php
declare(strict_types=1);
namespace App\Config;
final class Config implements \JsonSerializable
{
    public function __construct(
        private readonly array $defaults,
        private readonly array $stored,
        private readonly array $env = [],
    ) {
    }
    public function get(string $key, mixed $default = null): mixed
    {
        return array_replace($this->defaults, $this->stored, $this->env)[$key] ?? $default;
    }
    public function source(string $key): string
    {
        if (array_key_exists($key, $this->env)) {
            return 'env';
        }
        return array_key_exists($key, $this->stored) ? 'db' : 'default';
    }
    public function jsonSerialize(): array
    {
        $values = array_replace($this->defaults, $this->stored, $this->env);
        foreach (['webhook_secret', 'license_key'] as $secret) {
            if (isset($values[$secret])) {
                $values[$secret] = '••••';
            }
        }
        return ['values' => $values, 'sources' => array_map(
            fn (string $k): string => $this->source($k), array_keys($values)
        )];
    }
}
<?php
declare(strict_types=1);
namespace App\Tests\Integration;
use WP_UnitTestCase;
final class SettingsTest extends WP_UnitTestCase
{
    public function test_register_setting_installs_a_default(): void
    {
        do_action('admin_init');
        $defaults = get_option('app_settings', null);
        self::assertIsArray($defaults);
        self::assertSame(900, $defaults['sync_interval']);
    }
    public function test_sanitize_callback_clamps_the_interval(): void
    {
        do_action('admin_init');
        $sanitised = apply_filters('sanitize_option_app_settings', [
            'sync_interval'  => 5,
            'retention_days' => 99999,
            'webhook_secret' => 'not-a-hash',
        ]);
        self::assertSame(60, $sanitised['sync_interval']);
        self::assertSame(365, $sanitised['retention_days']);
        self::assertSame('', $sanitised['webhook_secret']);
    }
    public function test_auth_callback_follows_the_post_not_the_role(): void
    {
        $contributor = self::factory()->user->create(['role' => 'contributor']);
        wp_set_current_user($contributor);
        $chapter = self::factory()->post->create([
            'post_type'   => 'doc_chapter',
            'post_author' => $contributor,
            'post_status' => 'draft',
        ]);
        self::assertTrue((bool) apply_filters(
            'auth_post_meta_difficulty', true, [], $chapter, 'edit_post', $contributor
        ));
        $other = self::factory()->user->create(['role' => 'contributor']);
        self::assertFalse((bool) apply_filters(
            'auth_post_meta_difficulty', true, [], $chapter, 'edit_post', $other
        ));
    }
}
LayerBase classDatabaseDurationUse for
Pure unitTestCaseNo< 1 msValue objects, parsers, formatters
IntegrationWP_UnitTestCaseYes, transactional~40 msHooks, options, post meta, capabilities
BehaviouralWP_UnitTestCaseYes~200 msREST requests, admin flows, cron
End-to-endPlaywrightYes, sharedSecondsBlock editor, checkout, critical paths

Test the thing that broke. Every production incident of the last decade was a missing assertion about a specific behaviour: a sanitiser that accepted an out-of-range value, a capability check that ignored the object ID, a cache that never expired. Write the failing test first, from the incident, and let it stay. A suite organised around the defects you have actually had is worth more than one organised around method coverage.

12.4 Integration testing against a real database

The integration layer is where WordPress’s own behaviour is exercised: query construction, caching, option autoload, taxonomy joins and the REST controller.

The first assertion category is query behaviour, and it is the one most worth writing because query bugs are silent. A WP_Query with a meta_query returning the wrong rows, a pre_get_posts filter that fires on the admin and breaks the dashboard, an archive that shows drafts — none of these raises an error and all of them are invisible until a user sees them. The second is cache and option behaviour, because both are stateful: a test that mutates a cached value leaves the cache surviving while the transaction rolls the option back, so the next test reads a value that never existed. Multisite adds a dimension, and the two defects worth testing for are cache leakage across a blog boundary and configuration precedence between network and site options.

The suite is installed by bin/install-wp-tests.sh from develop.svn.wordpress.org, a Git mirror of core including tests/phpunit. Install against trunk to test forward compatibility with the next release, and against a tagged version to prove compatibility with production; running against trunk in CI catches the deprecation that would otherwise surface as a _doing_it_wrong notice three months after a release. Pin the character set and collation explicitly in wp-tests-config.php, and run against the exact PHP and MySQL versions production uses — a test that passes on 8.4 and fails on 8.5 is telling you something real about a deprecated function call or a strict-mode setting.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);
namespace App\Tests\Integration;
use WP_UnitTestCase;
final class ArchiveQueryTest extends WP_UnitTestCase
{
    public function set_up(): void
    {
        parent::set_up();
        add_filter('pre_get_posts', [$this, 'boundTheArchive'], 20);
    }
    public function test_archive_is_bounded_and_excludes_drafts(): void
    {
        self::factory()->post->create_many(30, ['post_type' => 'doc_chapter', 'post_status' => 'publish']);
        self::factory()->post->create_many(5, ['post_type' => 'doc_chapter', 'post_status' => 'draft']);
        $query = new \WP_Query([
            'post_type'      => 'doc_chapter',
            'post_status'    => 'publish',
            'posts_per_page' => 24,
            'no_found_rows'  => true,
        ]);
        self::assertCount(24, $query->posts);
    }
    public function test_no_found_rows_avoids_a_count_query(): void
    {
        global $wpdb;
        self::factory()->post->create_many(10, ['post_type' => 'doc_chapter']);
        $before = $wpdb->num_queries;
        new \WP_Query(['post_type' => 'doc_chapter', 'posts_per_page' => 5, 'no_found_rows' => true]);
        self::assertLessThanOrEqual(1, $wpdb->num_queries - $before);
    }
    public function test_admin_is_not_affected_by_the_front_end_filter(): void
    {
        set_current_screen('edit.php');
        $query = new \WP_Query(['post_type' => 'doc_chapter']);
        self::assertNotSame(24, $query->query_vars['posts_per_page'] ?? null);
    }
    public function boundTheArchive(\WP_Query $q): void
    {
        if (is_admin() || ! $q->is_main_query() || ! $q->is_post_type_archive('doc_chapter')) {
            return;
        }
        $q->set('posts_per_page', 24);
        $q->set('no_found_rows', true);
    }
}
<?php
declare(strict_types=1);
namespace App\Tests\Integration;
use WP_UnitTestCase;
final class CacheIntegrationTest extends WP_UnitTestCase
{
    public function test_pruning_avoids_a_query_per_row(): void
    {
        $ids = self::factory()->post->create_many(5, ['post_type' => 'doc_chapter']);
        update_meta_cache('post', $ids);   // one query for all five
        global $wpdb;
        $before = $wpdb->num_queries;
        foreach ($ids as $id) {
            get_post_meta($id, 'subtitle', true);
        }
        self::assertSame(0, $wpdb->num_queries - $before, 'meta cache was not primed');
    }
    public function test_group_flush_does_not_touch_other_groups(): void
    {
        wp_cache_set('keep', 'value', 'app/config', 300);
        wp_cache_set('drop', 'value', 'app/content', 300);
        if (function_exists('wp_cache_flush_group')) {
            wp_cache_flush_group('app/content');
        }
        self::assertSame('value', wp_cache_get('keep', 'app/config'));
        self::assertFalse(wp_cache_get('drop', 'app/content'));
    }
    public function test_autoload_off_option_is_not_in_alloptions(): void
    {
        add_option('app_big', str_repeat('x', 4096), '', 'off');
        self::assertArrayNotHasKey('app_big', wp_load_alloptions());
        self::assertSame(str_repeat('x', 4096), get_option('app_big'));
    }
    public function test_object_cache_is_scoped_per_blog(): void
    {
        $a = self::factory()->blog->create();
        $b = self::factory()->blog->create();
        switch_to_blog($a);
        wp_cache_set('marker', 'site-a', 'app/config', 300);
        restore_current_blog();
        switch_to_blog($b);
        self::assertFalse(wp_cache_get('marker', 'app/config'));
        restore_current_blog();
    }
}
<?php
declare(strict_types=1);
namespace App\Tests\Integration;
use WP_REST_Request;
use WP_UnitTestCase;
final class ChapterRestTest extends WP_UnitTestCase
{
    public function test_difficulty_is_validated_against_the_schema(): void
    {
        wp_set_current_user(self::factory()->user->create(['role' => 'editor']));
        do_action('rest_api_init');
        $request = new WP_REST_Request('POST', '/app/v1/chapters');
        $request->set_header('Content-Type', 'application/json');
        $request->set_body((string) wp_json_encode([
            'title'  => 'Caching',
            'status' => 'publish',
            'meta'   => ['difficulty' => 'impossible'],
        ]));
        self::assertSame(400, rest_get_server()->dispatch($request)->get_status());
    }
    public function test_a_contributor_cannot_publish(): void
    {
        wp_set_current_user(self::factory()->user->create(['role' => 'contributor']));
        do_action('rest_api_init');
        $request = new WP_REST_Request('POST', '/app/v1/chapters');
        $request->set_body((string) wp_json_encode([
            'title'  => 'Draft attempt',
            'status' => 'publish',
        ]));
        $status = rest_get_server()->dispatch($request)->get_status();
        self::assertContains($status, [401, 403], 'publish must be refused');
    }
}

A test that requires a shared database cannot be run in parallel safely. The suite creates a fixed wptests database and truncates tables between tests, so two concurrent PHPUnit processes corrupt each other’s fixtures. Above a few thousand tests, give each worker its own database or its own WP_TESTS_DOMAIN; the harness supports it, the default configuration does not. The same applies to assertions on query counts: a persistent object cache or a leftover cache entry from a previous test changes the number legitimately and the assertion becomes flaky, so disable the external object cache in the test bootstrap and flush it per test.

12.5 Fixtures, factories and data isolation

The factory API creates realistic objects with realistic relationships. Hand-written fixtures do not, and the difference determines whether a suite is maintainable.

WP_UnitTestCase_Factory_For_Thing provides factories for posts, users, terms, comments, attachments, categories, tags, post types, themes and blogs. Each create() returns an ID, create_many() returns an array, and the argument array is passed to the underlying function. The definition method supplies defaults, and overriding it on a shared subclass changes what "a chapter" means for the whole suite. meta_input deserves attention because it bypasses update_post_meta() and writes directly, which is much faster at volume and skips every updated_post_meta hook — correct for setup, wrong for a test whose subject is the behaviour of those hooks.

Cleanup is what decides whether a suite is trusted, and fixture placement is where it usually goes wrong. Code at file scope runs once per process rather than once per test, so a create() outside any method has its row removed by the next test’s rollback, and the second test fails with a missing fixture the first test appears to have created. All fixture creation belongs in set_up, wpSetUpBeforeClass, or a lazily-memoised static builder — and heavy fixtures belong in wpSetUpBeforeClass specifically, because the database is rolled back per test but the class-level static property is not, so a fixture built once and recorded as IDs is available to every test and each test applies only its own delta.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);
namespace App\Tests;
use WP_UnitTestCase;
abstract class IsolatedTestCase extends WP_UnitTestCase
{
