    private array $scheduled = [];
    public function set_up(): void
    {
        parent::set_up();
        $this->scheduled = (array) _get_cron_array();
    }
    public function tear_down(): void
    {
        foreach ((array) _get_cron_array() as $timestamp => $hooks) {
            foreach ((array) $hooks as $hook => $instances) {
                wp_unschedule_event($timestamp, $hook);
            }
        }
        update_option('cron', $this->scheduled ?: []);
        wp_cache_flush();                       // cache survives the rollback
        wp_set_current_user(0);                 // a logged-in test must not leak
        putenv('APP_SYNC_INTERVAL');            // environment is process state
        unset($_GET['app_locale'], $_POST['app_settings']);
        parent::tear_down();
    }
    protected function makeChapter(array $args = []): int
    {
        return self::factory()->post->create(array_replace_recursive([
            'post_type'    => 'doc_chapter',
            'post_status'  => 'publish',
            'post_title'   => 'Chapter ' . wp_generate_uuid4(),
            'post_content' => 'Content.',
        ], $args));
    }
    protected function makeGuideWithChapters(int $count): array
    {
        $guide = self::factory()->post->create([
            'post_type' => 'doc_guide', 'post_status' => 'publish', 'post_title' => 'Guide',
        ]);
        $chapters = [];
        for ($i = 0; $i < $count; $i++) {
            $chapters[] = $this->makeChapter([
                'post_title'  => "Chapter {$i}",
                'menu_order'  => $i,
                'post_parent' => $guide,
                'meta_input'  => ['document_id' => $guide],
            ]);
        }
        return [$guide, $chapters];
    }
}
<?php
declare(strict_types=1);
final class Seeder
{
    public function __construct(private readonly \wpdb $db)
    {
    }
    public function run(int $chapters = 200): array
    {
        $this->db->query('SET autocommit = 0');
        try {
            $ids = [];
            for ($i = 0; $i < $chapters; $i++) {
                $this->db->insert($this->db->posts, [
                    'post_author'       => 1,
                    'post_date'         => current_time('mysql'),
                    'post_date_gmt'     => current_time('mysql', 1),
                    'post_content'      => 'Chapter body ' . $i,
                    'post_title'        => 'Chapter ' . $i,
                    'post_status'       => 'publish',
                    'post_name'         => 'chapter-' . $i,
                    'post_type'         => 'doc_chapter',
                    'post_modified'     => current_time('mysql'),
                    'post_modified_gmt' => current_time('mysql', 1),
                ]);
                $ids[] = (int) $this->db->insert_id;
            }
            $this->db->query('COMMIT');
        } catch (\Throwable $e) {
            $this->db->query('ROLLBACK');
            throw $e;
        } finally {
            $this->db->query('SET autocommit = 1');
        }
        return $ids;
    }
}
StateRolled back by the transactionMust be cleaned manually

12.6 Profiling and diagnostic tools

Profiling a WordPress request means answering three questions in order: how much time is in the database, how much is in hooks, and how much is in the templates.

For the database the three levels are SAVEQUERIES for count and total, Query Monitor for the caller stack and duplicate detection, and MySQL’s performance_schema for statement digests across all workers. The first tells you there is a problem, the second where, and the third whether your fix worked under real concurrency — because reducing a statement’s execution time can leave the number of round trips unchanged. For hook time, marking the fixed bootstrap sequence with a small filter is more useful than a full trace, and a plugin that adds two seconds to init is immediately visible, which is almost always a remote HTTP call or an unindexed query during bootstrap.

For a slow request where the database and the hooks are both innocent, the answer is a wall-clock profiler: Blackfire samples with low overhead and is built for comparison, while Xdebug’s profiler is deterministic but changes execution speed, so its numbers are indicative. Neither belongs permanently in production; a scheduled Blackfire probe against one canary URL is the configuration most teams converge on. Command-line profiling is the fastest route to a trustworthy number, because the same URL can be run a hundred times without a browser in the loop. Beyond PHP, browser-side performance belongs in a different tool: the numbers that count are largest contentful paint and total layout shift measured as field data from real users on mid-range hardware, not a synthetic run on a fast laptop with a warm cache.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);
final class MiniProfiler
{
    private static float $start;
    private static array $marks = [];
    public static function boot(): void
    {
        self::$start = microtime(true);
        foreach (['muplugins_loaded', 'plugins_loaded', 'init',
                  'wp_loaded', 'parse_request', 'template_redirect'] as $phase) {
            add_action($phase, static fn () => self::$marks[$phase] ??= microtime(true), PHP_INT_MAX);
        }
        add_action('shutdown', [self::class, 'report'], PHP_INT_MAX);
    }
    public static function report(): void
    {
        global $wpdb, $wp_object_cache;
        $phases = ['db_time' => array_sum(array_column($wpdb->logs ?? [], 1))];
        $prev   = self::$start;
        foreach (self::$marks as $name => $at) {
            $phases[$name] = $at - $prev;
            $prev = $at;
        }
        $phases += [
            'queries'        => count($wpdb->logs ?? []),
            'cache_hits'     => $wp_object_cache->cache_hits,
            'cache_misses'   => $wp_object_cache->cache_misses,
            'peak_memory_mb' => memory_get_peak_usage(true) / 1048576,
            'total'          => microtime(true) - self::$start,
        ];
        error_log('PROFILE ' . wp_json_encode(array_map(
            static fn ($v) => is_float($v) ? round($v, 4) : $v, $phases
        )));
    }
}

Exercises 12

  1. Build a debug configuration matrix. Create a single wp-config shim driven by an APP_ENV variable covering local, ci, staging and production, and assert the six constants in each. Add the production assertion that logs CRITICAL when WP_DEBUG_DISPLAY is on for a non-local host, and demonstrate it firing on a staging copy configured wrongly and staying silent when configured correctly.
  2. Instrument and log. Implement a JSON logger with a correlation identifier that propagates into cron, AJAX and WP-CLI, whitelist-based redaction, a wpdb error handler and a fatal-error shutdown handler. Prove the identifier is identical across all four contexts in one log, and prove a request containing a password produces a log line with no trace of it.
  3. Write both test layers. Produce twenty pure unit tests for a configuration resolver and twenty integration tests against the real database covering options, post meta, capabilities and the REST controller. Measure per-test duration for each layer, and demonstrate the two production defects a unit test would miss: a sanitiser accepting an out-of-range value and a capability callback ignoring the post ID.
  4. Make the suite fast and reliable. Move a thirty-second fixture into wpSetUpBeforeClass, add manual cleanup for cache, cron, current user and environment, and run the suite in reverse order. Demonstrate it passes identically in both orders and that total duration has fallen by at least sixty per cent. Document which state each cleanup method addresses.
  5. Build a performance gate. Add a CI job running wp profile stage against a seeded database for three URL types, failing on a regression over ten per cent, and storing the profile as a build artefact. Then deliberately introduce an N+1 loop in a template, show the gate failing with correct attribution, and show it passing again after the fix.

13 · Deployment, Migrations and Operating WordPress at Scale

13.1 Environment parity and repository layout13.2 Configuration that must never be committed13.3 Database migrations with dbDelta and their limits13.4 Media, uploads and object storage13.5 Multisite at scale13.6 Hardening checklist

WordPress deployments fail for a small number of recurring reasons, and almost none of them are code defects. The database credentials and salts are in a file the web server can read but the repository should not contain. The plugin directory is owned by root while the web server runs as a deploy user, so an update half-completes. The uploads directory sits inside the deploy path, so a git clean deletes four years of media. A migration ran as part of a plugin activation and was therefore never reviewed, rehearsed or rolled back. Each is a policy failure that a template and a checklist eliminate permanently.

This chapter covers environment parity and the repository layout that makes it achievable, the strict separation between committed code and injected configuration including the wp-config-local.php pattern, database migrations via dbDelta together with the specific changes it cannot make, media and uploads including offloading to object storage, multisite at scale with the wp_blogs and wp_sitemeta hot spots, and a hardening checklist ordered by the probability and severity of the incident each item prevents.

13.1 Environment parity and repository layout

Parity means a developer can run the application locally and reproduce a production behaviour without special-casing it. Three variables determine that: the WordPress version, the PHP version, and the contents of wp-config.php.

Core, plugins and themes are all deployable artefacts. wp-includes, wp-admin and the version-matched root files are installed with wp core download --version, never committed. Beyond the licensing argument — a git repository is not a distribution channel — the decisive practical reason is that a committed wp-includes guarantees a new machine receives whatever core version the last person to commit happened to have, which is the mechanism by which version drift reaches production. The ignore file is the load-bearing artefact: everything WordPress owns at runtime must be absent from version control, because committing an uploads directory once, with thirty thousand files in it, has a lasting cost — the history carries them permanently and a clone becomes a multi-gigabyte operation for everyone thereafter.

Parity is not only about versions; it is about the constants that change behaviour. DISABLE_WP_CRON, WP_ENVIRONMENT_TYPE, WP_DEBUG and the presence of an object cache drop-in must all be in the same state locally as in production: a local site without Redis will not reproduce a cache stampede, and a local site with WP_DEBUG_DISPLAY off will not show the notice being hunted. The release-directory layout gives atomic releases, an instant rollback by re-pointing one symlink, and a migration that runs before traffic reaches the new code; it also means the document root contains a symlink, which some shared hosts disallow, and that constraint is the reason in-place updates still exist.

Object cache drop-inNoInfrastructureYes

Rolling back code without reversing the schema is how a deploy becomes an incident. A migration that renames a column, followed by a rollback to the previous code, produces an application that cannot read its own tables — with no recovery path except a database restore, which discards every write since the deploy. Migrations must remain backward compatible for at least one release: additive changes first, a deploy, then destructive changes in the following deploy. Pin everything else as well — --version=6.9.1 rather than --version=latest, a base image referenced by digest, a Composer platform requirement of "php": "8.5.*" — so a security patch becomes a one-line pull request rather than an afternoon of investigation.

13.2 Configuration that must never be committed

The repository contains code. Configuration — credentials, salts, hostnames, API keys — is injected at deploy time from a secret store or the process environment.

Four classes of value must never enter version control. Database credentials, because they grant access to the entire data set and to every replica and backup. The eight authentication salts, because they are what make password hashes resistant to a precomputed attack; a leaked salt set materially weakens a password database, and rotating them invalidates every session and every nonce, so rotation is a scheduled procedure with a backup taken first rather than an opportunistic change folded into a deploy. Third-party API keys, frequently scoped to a billable account. Environment identity — database host, site URL, table prefix, Redis endpoint — because that is precisely what differs between machines, and hard-coding it is how a production box ends up writing to a staging database.

The mechanism is a loader in a committed wp-config.php that requires a wp-config-local.php, which exists only on the server, is ignored by git, is 0600 owned by the web user, and is written by the deploy from a secret store. The committed file contains logic and a hard failure when the local file is missing, and no secrets at all — a value committed there is by definition not a secret. Environment variables are a better source than a committed file for anything that differs between environments, because they make the difference visible in the process environment and impossible to check in by accident; a .env.example listing every variable name is the discoverability mechanism.

<?php
/**
 * wp-config.php — COMMITTED. Contains no secrets.
 *
 * The salts below are the placeholders WordPress generates on a first run.
 * They are committed deliberately: a value committed here is by definition
 * not a secret. The real salts arrive via wp-config-local.php and override
 * them. Never replace these with real values.
 */

$table_prefix = 'wp_';

$appLocal = __DIR__ . '/wp-config-local.php';

if (!is_readable($appLocal)) {
    http_response_code(500);
    exit('wp-config-local.php is missing. This file is provisioned at deploy time.');
}

require_once $appLocal;

if (!defined('ABSPATH')) {
    define('ABSPATH', __DIR__ . '/');
}

require_once ABSPATH . 'wp-settings.php';

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);
add_action('muplugins_loaded', static function (): void {
    $salts = [
        'WP_AUTH_KEY', 'WP_SECURE_AUTH_KEY', 'WP_LOGGED_IN_KEY', 'WP_NONCE_KEY',
        'WP_AUTH_SALT', 'WP_SECURE_AUTH_SALT', 'WP_LOGGED_IN_SALT', 'WP_NONCE_SALT',
    ];
    $weak = [];
    $seen = [];
    foreach ($salts as $constant) {
        $value = (string) (defined($constant) ? constant($constant) : '');
        if ($value === '' || strlen($value) < 64) {
            $weak[] = $constant;
            continue;
        }
        if (isset($seen[$value])) {
            $weak[] = $constant . ' (duplicate value)';
            continue;
        }
        $seen[$value] = true;
    }
    if ($weak !== []) {
        error_log('CRITICAL: weak or duplicate salts: ' . implode(', ', $weak));
    }
    if (defined('WP_DEBUG_DISPLAY') && WP_DEBUG_DISPLAY) {
        error_log('CRITICAL: WP_DEBUG_DISPLAY is enabled on a reachable host');
    }
    if (is_file(ABSPATH . 'wp-config.php') && is_writable(ABSPATH . 'wp-config.php')) {
        error_log('CRITICAL: wp-config.php is writable by the web user');
    }
}, 0);

A committed .env is a permanent leak, not a temporary one. It must be treated as compromised: rotate every secret it contains, purge the history with git filter-repo or BFG, force-push, and require every collaborator to re-clone — because a rewritten history is not rewritten in anyone’s existing working copy. Prevention is a pre-commit hook that rejects any file matching an .env pattern and any diff line matching a secret pattern, plus an independent server-side CI check, because a hook can be bypassed with --no-verify. The Docker image’s wp-config.php can read credentials from WORDPRESS_-prefixed environment variables, which is convenient, but it does not supply the salts: a container shipping WordPress’s generated defaults is a container whose password hashes are salted with a publicly known value.

13.3 Database migrations with dbDelta and their limits

WordPress has no migration framework. It has dbDelta(), which compares a declared schema against the live one and issues the smallest set of DDL that closes the gap — plus a specific, documented set of changes it cannot make.

dbDelta() introspects the current table, parses the CREATE TABLE string you supply, and emits CREATE TABLE when the table is absent, ALTER TABLE to add missing columns and indexes, and nothing at all when the shapes already match. Its formatting rules are strict and unforgiving: two spaces after PRIMARY KEY, lowercase keywords, one space between a column name and its type, no backticks around index names, no ENGINE= clause, no COMMENT on columns, and every column carrying a default or an explicit nullability. Get the formatting wrong and the function silently does nothing — it does not error and it does not warn.

Two structural limitations follow. dbDelta knows only about the tables it is told about and keeps no record of which plugin owns which table, so an uninstalled plugin leaves its tables behind and a later plugin reusing the name finds a shape it did not expect. And it is not transactional across statements, so a failure halfway through leaves a partially migrated schema with no marker recording where it stopped — which is why the version option must be written after the dbDelta calls and why each call must be independently safe. It can create tables, add columns and add indexes; it cannot change a column type, rename or drop a column, drop a table, change a default, or add a foreign key, and each of those needs an explicit ALTER TABLE written by hand.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);
require_once ABSPATH . 'wp-admin/includes/upgrade.php';
final class Schema
{
    public const VERSION = '7';
    public static function apply(): void
    {
        dbDelta(self::subscriptionTable());
        dbDelta(self::eventTable());
        dbDelta(self::searchIndexTable());
        update_option('app_schema_version', self::VERSION, false, 'auto', 'auto-off');
    }
    private static function subscriptionTable(): string
    {
        return 'CREATE TABLE app_subscription (
            id bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL AUTO_INCREMENT,
            customer_id bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL,
            plan varchar(64) NOT NULL DEFAULT \'\',
            status varchar(20) NOT NULL DEFAULT \'active\',
            seats int(11) NOT NULL DEFAULT 1,
            amount_cents bigint(20) NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
            currency char(3) NOT NULL DEFAULT \'EUR\',
            external_ref varchar(191) NOT NULL DEFAULT \'\',
            created_at datetime NOT NULL DEFAULT \'0000-00-00 00:00:00\',
            updated_at datetime NOT NULL DEFAULT \'0000-00-00 00:00:00\',
            PRIMARY KEY  (id),
            UNIQUE KEY external_ref (external_ref),
            KEY customer_status (customer_id,status),
            KEY plan_status (plan,status)
        ) DEFAULT CHARACTER SET utf8mb4 COLLATE utf8mb4_unicode_ci;';
    }
    private static function eventTable(): string
    {
        return 'CREATE TABLE app_event (
            id bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL AUTO_INCREMENT,
            subscription_id bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL,
            type varchar(64) NOT NULL,
            payload longtext NOT NULL,
            occurred_at datetime NOT NULL DEFAULT \'0000-00-00 00:00:00\',
            PRIMARY KEY  (id),
            KEY subscription_type (subscription_id,type),
            KEY occurred_at (occurred_at)
        ) DEFAULT CHARACTER SET utf8mb4 COLLATE utf8mb4_unicode_ci;';
    }
    private static function searchIndexTable(): string
    {
        return 'CREATE TABLE app_search_index (
            object_id bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL,
            locale varchar(10) NOT NULL DEFAULT \'en_US\',
            body longtext NOT NULL,
            PRIMARY KEY  (object_id,locale)
        ) DEFAULT CHARACTER SET utf8mb4 COLLATE utf8mb4_unicode_ci;';
    }
}
<?php
declare(strict_types=1);
namespace App\Migrations;
use wpdb;
final class Runner
{
    private const MIGRATIONS = [
        '7_add_seat_index'     => [self::class, 'addSeatIndex'],
        '7_widen_currency'    => [self::class, 'widenCurrency'],
        '7_backfill_external' => [self::class, 'backfillExternalRef'],
    ];
    public function __construct(private readonly \wpdb $db)
    {
    }
    public function migrate(int $to = 7): array
    {
        $applied = (array) get_option('app_applied_migrations', []);
        $log     = [];
        foreach (self::MIGRATIONS as $version => $callback) {
            if ((int) $version <= $to || in_array($version, $applied, true)) {
                continue;
            }
            $callback($this->db);
            $applied[] = $version;
            update_option('app_applied_migrations', array_values(array_unique($applied)), false, 'auto', 'auto-off');
            $log[] = "applied {$version}";
        }
        return $log;
    }
    public function pending(int $to = 7): array
    {
        $applied = (array) get_option('app_applied_migrations', []);
        return array_values(array_filter(
            array_keys(self::MIGRATIONS),
            static fn (string $v): bool => (int) $v <= $to && !in_array($v, $applied, true)
        ));
    }
    private static function addSeatIndex(\wpdb $db): void
    {
        $db->query("ALTER TABLE {$db->prefix}app_subscription ADD KEY plan_seats (plan,seats)");
    }
    private static function widenCurrency(\wpdb $db): void
    {
        $db->query("ALTER TABLE {$db->prefix}app_subscription
                     MODIFY COLUMN currency char(3) NOT NULL DEFAULT 'EUR'");
    }
    private static function backfillExternalRef(\wpdb $db): void
    {
        $updated = $db->query("UPDATE {$db->prefix}app_subscription
                                SET external_ref = CONCAT('legacy-', id)
                                WHERE external_ref = ''");
        if ($updated === false) {
            throw new \RuntimeException('backfill failed: ' . $db->last_error);
        }
    }
}
<?php
declare(strict_types=1);
final class Backfill
{
    private const CURSOR = 'app_backfill_plan_id_cursor';
    public function run(int $chunk = 500, float $budget = 15.0): array
    {
        global $wpdb;
        $start  = microtime(true);
        $cursor = (int) get_option(self::CURSOR, 0);
        $moved  = 0;
        while (microtime(true) - $start < $budget) {
            $ids = $wpdb->get_col($wpdb->prepare(
                "SELECT s.id FROM {$wpdb->prefix}app_subscription s
                 INNER JOIN {$wpdb->prefix}app_plan p ON p.slug = s.plan
                 WHERE s.id > %d AND s.plan_id IS NULL ORDER BY s.id ASC LIMIT %d",
                $cursor, $chunk
            ));
            if ($ids === []) {
                delete_option(self::CURSOR);
                return ['complete' => true, 'moved' => $moved, 'cursor' => $cursor];
            }
            $cursor = (int) end($ids);
            $wpdb->query($wpdb->prepare(
                "UPDATE {$wpdb->prefix}app_subscription s
                 INNER JOIN {$wpdb->prefix}app_plan p ON p.slug = s.plan
                 SET s.plan_id = p.id
                 WHERE s.id IN (" . implode(',', array_map('intval', $ids)) . ')',
                $cursor
            ));
            update_option(self::CURSOR, $cursor, false, 'auto', 'auto-off');
            $moved += count($ids);
            usleep(50_000);   // yield, so production traffic is not starved
        }
        return ['complete' => false, 'moved' => $moved, 'cursor' => $cursor];
    }
}
wp db export /srv/backups/pre-migrate-$(date +%s).sql.gz --add-drop-table
wp app migrate --status
wp app migrate --dry-run
wp app migrate --to=latest
wp app migrate --rollback 7   # only when the migration is documented reversible
wp db query "SHOW CREATE TABLE wp_app_subscription\G"
wp db query "SHOW INDEX FROM wp_app_subscription"
SELECT COLUMN_NAME, COLUMN_TYPE, IS_NULLABLE, COLUMN_DEFAULT
FROM information_schema.COLUMNS
WHERE TABLE_SCHEMA = DATABASE() AND TABLE_NAME = 'wp_app_subscription'

Never run a schema migration inside a web request. A migration that times out has applied some statements and not others, with no record of where it stopped, and the next visitor may trigger a second partial run against a half-migrated schema. A migration run from the pipeline either completes or fails before the new code receives traffic — the failure mode is a failed deploy, not a broken site. If a migration must run online because the table is large, give it a resumable cursor, a documented rate limit, and a release of its own with no other change in it. Rehearse every migration against a copy of production data: synthetic data hides the problems that matter, including a row count that turns a four-minute backfill into a four-hour one and a legacy character set that dbDelta silently wants to change.

13.4 Media, uploads and object storage

The uploads directory is the one part of WordPress that grows without bound, is written at runtime, and is conventionally kept inside the deploy path.

By default wp-content/uploads is organised into dated subdirectories — 2026/03/ — with a collision hash appended to the filename. The physical path comes from the UPLOADS constant or the upload_path option; the public URL comes from upload_url_path. All of them must change together, because changing the URL alone produces a site in which every image 404s while every database row looks correct. Offloading to object storage means replacing the write path, because WordPress writes to a filesystem: hook wp_handle_upload_prefilter and wp_save_post_attachment to stream the file to the bucket and record the remote location in the attachment meta, or keep the file on the origin and replicate asynchronously, which is simpler, keeps the image editor working, and costs the local storage you were trying to avoid.

Delivery matters as much as storage. Uploads are effectively content-addressed by path — an image at 2026/03/logo-e5f2a1.png is never overwritten in place — so a one-year immutable cache lifetime is both correct and safe, for originals and generated sizes alike; the exception is anything WordPress can rewrite in place, which must be versioned in the path or given a short TTL. Image sizes are the single largest lever on both page weight and storage: the three core defaults are almost never the right three, and generating a size larger than the original produces a bigger file that looks identical. Preserve the relative path convention when you move uploads, because if the database keeps 2026/03/logo.png then every srcset stays valid and returning to local storage later is a configuration change rather than a migration.

<?php
declare(strict_types=1);
add_filter('upload_dir', static function (array $dirs): array {
    $cdn  = getenv('MEDIA_CDN_ORIGIN') ?: '';
    $path = getenv('MEDIA_UPLOAD_PATH') ?: '';
    if ($path === '') {
        return $dirs;
    }
    if ($cdn !== '') {
        $dirs['baseurl'] = $cdn;
        $dirs['url']     = trailingslashit($cdn) . ltrim($dirs['subdir'], '/');
    }
    $dirs['basedir'] = $path;
    $dirs['path']    = $path . $dirs['subdir'];
    $dirs['error']   = false;
    return $dirs;
}, 20);
add_filter('wp_generate_attachment_metadata', static function (array $metadata, int $attachmentId): array {
    $path = get_attached_file($attachmentId);
    if ($path === false || !is_readable((string) $path)) {
        return $metadata;
    }
    $basedir = wp_get_upload_dir()['basedir'];
    $key     = ltrim(str_replace($basedir, '', (string) $path), '/');
    if ($key === '' || $key === (string) $path) {
        return $metadata;
    }
    $client = new \Aws\S3\S3Client([
        'region'  => getenv('MEDIA_REGION') ?: 'eu-central-1',
        'version' => 'latest',
    ]);
    $client->putObject([
        'Bucket'       => (string) getenv('MEDIA_BUCKET'),
        'Key'          => $key,
        'Body'         => fopen((string) $path, 'r'),
        'ContentType'  => wp_check_filetype((string) $path)['type'] ?: 'application/octet-stream',
        'CacheControl' => 'public, max-age=31536000, immutable',
    ]);
    return $metadata;
}, 10, 2);
<?php
declare(strict_types=1);
add_action('after_setup_theme', static function (): void {
    remove_image_size('medium');          // 300x300, used by almost no layout
    remove_image_size('medium_large');    // 768px, too large for a card
    add_image_size('card', 640, 0, true);        // cropped card thumbnail
    add_image_size('content', 1280, 0, false);   // not cropped: hero and body
    add_image_size('content@2x', 2560, 0, false);
    set_post_thumbnail_size(960, 540, true);     // 16:9
}, 20);
add_filter('intermediate_image_sizes_advanced', static function (array $sizes): array {
    $original = wp_get_original_image_path();
    if ($original === false) {
        return $sizes;
    }
    [$width, $height] = array_slice((array) getimagesize($original), 0, 2);
    foreach ($sizes as $name => $size) {
        [$w, $h, $crop] = array_pad((array) $size, 3, false);
        if ((int) $w > (int) $width || ($crop && (int) $h > (int) $height)) {
            unset($sizes[$name]);
        }
    }
    return $sizes;
});
$ids = get_posts([
    'post_type'      => 'attachment',
    'post_parent'    => $postId,
    'post_status'    => 'inherit',
    'fields'         => 'ids',
    'posts_per_page' => 20,
    'no_found_rows'  => true,
]);
update_meta_cache('post', $ids);
<?php
declare(strict_types=1);
final class MediaJanitor
{
    public const HOOK = 'app_media_janitor';
    public function run(int $batch = 200, int $retentionDays = 180, bool $dryRun = false): array
    {
        global $wpdb;
        $cutoff = gmdate('Y-m-d H:i:s', time() - $retentionDays * DAY_IN_SECONDS);
        $ids = $wpdb->get_col($wpdb->prepare(
            "SELECT a.ID FROM {$wpdb->posts} a
             LEFT JOIN {$wpdb->postmeta} pm
                    ON pm.meta_value = a.ID AND pm.meta_key = '_thumbnail_id'
             WHERE a.post_type = 'attachment'
               AND a.post_parent = 0
               AND a.post_date < %s
               AND pm.post_id IS NULL
             ORDER BY a.ID ASC LIMIT %d",
            $cutoff, $batch
        ));
        if ($dryRun) {
            return ['would_delete' => count($ids), 'ids' => array_map('intval', $ids)];
        }
        $deleted = [];
        foreach ($ids as $id) {
            if (wp_delete_attachment((int) $id, true)) {
                $deleted[] = (int) $id;
            }
        }
        return ['deleted' => count($deleted), 'ids' => $deleted];
    }
}
wp db query "SELECT ROUND(SUM(LENGTH(meta_value))/1024/1024,2) AS mb
             FROM wp_postmeta WHERE meta_key = '_wp_attached_file'"
wp app media janitor --dry-run --retention=180 --format=table

Do not leave wp-content/uploads inside a release directory without a symlink. A git clean -fdx, a release-retention job that removes directories older than seven days, or one over-broad rm -rf will delete every uploaded file on the site, and the recovery path is a backup taken at an unknown point. Symlink uploads from a persistent volume into each release, and treat that volume as data with the same backup and restore discipline as the database. There is also a quieter media problem in the database: wp_postmeta holds one serialised _wp_attachment_metadata row per attachment, and wp_get_attachment_metadata() is not served by the post meta cache, so an archive listing twenty thumbnails issues twenty queries unless update_meta_cache() is primed.

13.5 Multisite at scale

Multisite scales differently in its network tables and its per-site tables, and the two hot spots behave differently.

wp_blogs holds one row per site and is consulted by get_sites() on most admin operations, on every plugin that enumerates the network, and on the domain resolution every request performs. wp_sitemeta holds network options keyed by site_id, where 0 is the network root, and its autoloading subset is read on every request; it has only two indexes, the primary key on meta_id and a unique key on (site_id, meta_key), and per-site rows written there are invisible to switch_blog() while accumulating in the one table every request must filter past. wp_options grows linearly with the site count, and each site’s copy of the autoload bloat from §9.2 multiplies it: a 2 MB autoloaded option per site on a 4,000-site network is 8 GB.

On a subdirectory network, resolution is a lookup on (domain, path); on a subdomain network the host is part of the key and resolution is a single indexed lookup, faster at scale and different in cookie and permalink semantics. Either way the discipline is the same: never enumerate the network on a front-end request, always look up the one site you need through a bounded, cached query with the per-site caches explicitly disabled when you are about to switch, and flush the object cache per site inside a loop, because without that flush memory grows monotonically until the worker dies and a queue stops draining after a few hundred sites. Provisioning a new site is the other hot path: wp_initialize_site runs inside the admin request that created the site, with its own transaction and its own autoloaded writes, so a provisioning hook that calls a remote API has just introduced a network round trip into a WordPress page load.

13.6 Hardening checklist

The items below are ordered by expected incident prevented per unit of effort, not by category. Each is cheap, verifiable and reversible.

The first three are configuration and take minutes: remove the file editor and the installer, disable automatic updates, and deny direct access to the PHP files that do not need to be requested. The file editor is a privilege-escalation primitive on a host that has shell access, because an authenticated administrator can write any PHP file in the document root. Next come the account and authentication controls, which prevent the largest class of incident: enforce two-factor authentication for every administrator and for network administrators on multisite, restrict application passwords to those that genuinely need them, and audit the account list — the commonest account-level finding in a mature installation is a contractor account from three years ago with administrator capabilities.

Then the monitoring controls that convert an incident from a discovery into a report: watch for authentication failures, watch for capability changes, and watch the operational state established earlier in this guide as load-bearing — the object cache’s presence, cron lag, the autoload payload, the queue depth. The remaining controls have the highest cost and the highest ceiling: a web application firewall, a read replica for reporting queries, a staging environment that runs the same migrations on the same data, a verified backup with a tested restore, and file-integrity monitoring against the WordPress.org checksums API. Each prevents a class of incident the others cannot, and each is the one most often deferred.

ControlPreventsEffortVerifiable
DISALLOW_FILE_EDITAdmin-to-RCE in one stepOne linewp config get
Delete install.phpRe-running setup against a live databaseOne lineFile absent
Deny PHP in uploadsUpload-based webshellsOne server blockUpload a .php probe
Enforce 2FA for administratorsCredential stuffingLowEnrolment report
Rotate salts on suspicionOffline hash crackingLow, with disruptionLength and uniqueness check
Health endpoint plus alertingSilent degradationLowSynthetic check
Checksum verificationUndetected webshellsLowWP-CLI output
Verified backups with a tested restoreUnrecoverable data lossMediumQuarterly restore drill
WAF or equivalent edge rulesKnown application attacksMediumRule-hit metrics
Read replica for reportingAnalytics taking down the siteHighQuery host inspection

Exercises 13

  1. Build the repository layout. Create a plugin project with the layout above, a working .gitignore, a composer.json with the installer path, and a .wp-env.json pinned to PHP 8.5 and MySQL 8.0. Prove parity by running the same WP-CLI command locally and in a production-shaped container and diffing the output. Demonstrate that no secret and no runtime artefact can be committed by attempting each in turn.
  2. Separate configuration from code. Write the committed wp-config.php loader, the wp-config-local.php template and the provisioning shell step, plus the must-use plugin validating salts, WP_DEBUG_DISPLAY and file permissions. Prove the site refuses to boot without the local file, and that the failure is a clear 500 rather than a blank page or a fatal error.
  3. Rehearse a migration. Implement a dbDelta schema plus a versioned runner, then perform one of each change type — add a column, change a type, rename, drop — on a copy of a production-shaped database. Document for each what dbDelta did and required, measure the lock behaviour of each in MySQL 8.0, and demonstrate the additive-then-destructive two-deploy sequence with a successful code rollback between the phases.
  4. Offload media. Move uploads to object storage while preserving relative paths, configure immutable cache headers, and prove no _wp_attached_file value in the database changed. Measure the storage recovered by the media janitor on a realistic dataset, and demonstrate the site is still fully functional if object storage becomes unreachable, including the image editor.
  5. Write and run the hardening audit. Implement the health endpoint and a nightly audit script covering every item in §13.6 with a pass/fail assertion each. Run it against a deliberately misconfigured staging copy — file editor enabled, installer present, PHP writable in uploads, no two-factor for an administrator, short salts, a two-year-old backup — and produce a report naming each failure, its severity, and the exact command that fixes it.
